more effectually shattered. A sergeant and five men were struck down by
this one shell—how many of these were killed I did not stop to look.

By this time the French had crowned the heights all along, and were
driving the German fusiliers down the inner slope towards us; and they
had been very quick with their artillery. Already they had got one
battery on to the _coll_ at the pleasure-garden, and another showed
its teeth through the trees of the Exercise Platz. One could see the
quick flashes in rapid succession. All Saarbrück and St. Johann were of
course fully commanded. The town was naturally in a state of intense
excitement, but the people in the houses lining the street that leads
to the height stuck to their homes like rabbits to their burrows, even
when the shells were crashing into the houses and sweeping down the
thoroughfare. There was partial safety in the cross streets, where they
were lined with houses on both sides, although occasionally a shell
would play old gooseberry among the tiles, and make one think of Fleet
Street chimney-pots in a gale of wind. But there was a space on the
road to the railway station, whither I was walking, where there were
no houses, and this had to be traversed before the road, turning with
a sweep towards the railway station, could be reached. The townspeople
were banked in masses at either verge of this _via dolorosa_, and
now and then a small boy ran the gauntlet. A burgher tried it, and
got a chassepot bullet through the fleshiest part of a very goodly
corporation.

My companion and myself got through in safety, and then were in a kind
of triangle, off which goes the road to the station on a little rising
ground, another road parallel to the frontier, and yet another across
the bridge. The base of this triangle is formed by the Hotel Hagen, in
which we sought refuge on finding that the railway station had been
selected as the spot where the Germans were to make a final stand with
the artillery which they had brought up from the bivouac. In the lee
of this Hotel Hagen were collected several detachments of infantry.
Others held the bridge over the Saar, that is to say, they occupied the
houses commanding it. A bee-line, drawn from the cannon on the Exercise
Platz to the cannon on the esplanade in front of the railway station
would pass over the Hotel Hagen, and also over the bridge. The French
were shelling the houses on the bridge; they were shelling the troops
in the triangle in front of the hotel; they were replying with shells
to the German shells from the railway station. Thus the Hotel Hagen
was rather warm quarters. There was this additional complication,—that
exit in any direction whatever seemed to involve more risk than staying
still; so we stayed still, my companion and I. We found three or four
more Englishmen there, some ladies, and a heterogeneous collection of
_Zimmermädchen_ and _Stallknechts_. The landlord of the Hagen was the
coolest customer I have ever met. His house was being knocked about his
ears, glass and crockery smashing in all directions. This exemplary
Boniface asked me coolly, under these circumstances, for four groschen,
the price of a bottle of seltzer-water that I had forgotten to pay for
the night before. He gave me change for a thaler with the profoundest
phlegm, and then grumbled that his _table d’hôte_ had been bedevilled
for the day. Bedevilled it certainly was,—at least that was the fate of
the dinner. A shell had burst right in the big square stove on which
is done all the cooking of a German hotel. Potatoes bespattered the
roof, and gravy flowed on the floor. Some of the pans yet simmered on
fragments left of the stove; others were made pancakes of against the
walls. The landlady and her maidens went off into hysterics, and we
had to carry them into the cellar under the back yard, a shell bursting
in that enclosure as we carried them down. The staircase leading to the
first floor was shattered, and communication cut off. The fronts of the
houses on the opposite side of the triangle were riddled, as indeed was
the Hotel Hagen. We here heard for the first time the mitrailleuses
at work. They plied from the Exercise Platz down on the bridge, and
the bullets spattered very closely together along the narrow track.
The report was very curious and distinctive. It sounded more like the
tearing sound of one of those diabolical contrivances which silly
people were wont to use at Greenwich Fair, and which gave one a lively
sensation of his coat being torn down the back. Any one who is too
young to remember this pleasing noise may form some conception of the
report of the mitrailleuse by listening to a police-rattle. It is
unquestionable that the mitrailleuse fire swept the bridge thoroughly;
nothing could live where its hail fell. Then the smoke began to rise
where the shells had set fire to the wood-work. A building in front,
one on the flank, and another in the rear of us, were all blazing. The
troops under our lee scuttled off under the shelter of a wall to the
railway station, and so out into the open country beyond, several of
them falling in the movement. Others took their places, coming in from
St. Arnual, and later those evacuating the town and falling back from
the bridges; and they, too, took the same course, and also suffered
more or less. All the while the German artillery from the station were
firing directly over our heads, replying to the French fire on the
heights, while the French continued to pour shells back in return.

Then the cry arose that the French had descended the slope and were
actually in Saarbrück. This was confirmed from the fact that the
German guns ceased firing, and it seemed that they were drawing off.
Clearly we were left behind. We were, it is true, in the rear of
the force to which we were attached; a safe position—but only in an
advance. It appeared certain that all St. Johann, including the railway
station, would be in the hands of the French in an hour or two. Four
Englishmen in the Hotel Hagen, acting under the advice of a German
correspondent of remarkable equanimity, concluded to wait the course
of events, in the hope that if St. Johann were taken by the French
to-day, it would be retaken by the Prussians to-morrow. The certainty
of this was not quite apparent, and I thought it my duty, looking to
the enjoyment of postal facilities, therefore, to get out of the place
with all convenient speed. We left the Englishmen playing whist in the
breakfast-room, and we—that is, an Austrian friend and myself—quitted
the rickety shelter of the hotel, and made a run direct for the sheds
near the railway station. To do this we had to cross the line of fire,
and that, too, where the shells were bursting thickest. There was no
help for it, for there was no other road. We ran—I own it. I think I
ran faster than ever I did in my life. My friend was hit on the calf
of the leg by a stone thrown up by a bursting shell, but not lamed; I
was not touched. Once at the sheds, we were safe. Traversing a deep
rocky cutting, across which the shells still flew, we emerged into the
back country, and followed first the railway and then the high road to
a village named Duttweiler, about five English miles behind Saarbrück.
On our way we saw numerous straggling soldiers, who did not know what
had happened to their regiment. They were all making for the rear. We
also passed a squadron of Uhlans drawn up on the road, and commanded
by a reserve officer, who gave me his card, from which I learned that
he was a Heidelberg professor. After our arrival at Duttweiler most
of the Uhlans and a large proportion of the infantry passed through
the village on their way to the rear. They reported that Lebach was
the rendezvous, a place some fifteen English miles back from Saarbrück
in the Trèves direction. Small detachments had been left to watch
the French and keep up the communications. The French foreposts had
occupied Saarbrück and held the bridges, but no force had crossed into
St. Johann.

It has been the fashion on all sides to make very merry over this
“military promenade” of the 2nd August. No doubt, in the light shed
upon the affair by our after-knowledge, we are entitled to set it down
as something between a spectacle and a farce. But, at the time, nobody
had either daring or data for suspecting that France had neither armies
nor generals; and, given belongings that one is entitled to assume
when a reputedly sane nation deliberately declares war, this affair of
Saarbrück was undoubtedly the winning of the first trick of the game.
German strategists have, naturally enough, made their own uses of a
fiasco which was solely due to French unpreparedness and irresolution.
It has been the fashion to argue that Saarbrück was designedly left
as it was, to delude the French leaders into a belief of German
unpreparedness, and to induce them to concentrate their forces, with
the design of incursion, at an obviously weak point, upon what seemed
an eligible track into the heart of Prussia; while in reality, had they
obeyed the lure, and ventured inland from Saarbrück, they would have
found the road barred, and the forests swarming with legions in spiked
helmets. But this _ex post facto_ special pleading would have been
swept to the winds, if the French army had been but reasonably fit,
and its commanders reasonably competent and enterprising. The fact is,
that neither on the 2nd of August, nor for several days after, were the
Germans ready to defend their frontier, much less to invade France,
had they been attacked or resisted in the manner the precedents of
France gave one the title to count on. Saarbrück was held for days “on
the bounce,” with a sublime audacity that could only have succeeded so
long, first, because the French were not ready for offensive action,
and, secondly, because their means of obtaining information were so
contemptible that they did not earlier come at the knowledge of the
weakness of the force opposed to them. No doubt the German commanders
had means whereby they ascertained the existence of both of these
conditions, and possibly presumed on this knowledge to make their
concentrations more leisurely and confidently than they would otherwise
have done; but that a grave risk was actually encountered was obvious
to any one on the spot. It has been stated that the woods in the rear
of Saarbrück swarmed with troops, which could have successfully met
an attempt of the French to penetrate further into the interior. The
truth is that on the night of the action there was no force nearer
Saarbrück than Neunkirchen, save scattered detachments of the infantry
and cavalry which had evacuated Saarbrück, and a couple of squadrons of
cuirassiers holding the roads as éclaireurs. Supports did exist further
in the rear, but scattered abroad over the face of the country, as
they pursued different lines of march, with Saarbrück as the point of
convergence. Had a French army of invasion pushed on through Saarbrück
on the 2nd, and marched rapidly into the interior, it would not have
been possible to concentrate those scattered regiments at any given
point across the hostile line of march; and my firm conviction is that
a Napoleon would have seen the Rhine without having had to fight a
pitched battle.

It is needless to multiply evidence that it was not for strategical
purposes that the Germans permitted this occupation of Saarbrück, the
barrenness of which must surely have surprised themselves. There was
not an officer within ten miles of Saarbrück who, for at least two
days after the affair, did not expect to hear from hour to hour that
the heads of the French columns were showing on the roads leading
into the interior. Inside that radius, during those two days, hardly
a reinforcement was pushed forward, simply because reinforcements
were not as yet available. On the 3rd, a working party of engineers
cut the railway below Duttweiler, that the enemy, in his expected
advance, might not be able to avail himself of the iron road. Strange
strategy, truly, to allow, if it could be prevented, the shelling of
a German town, to expose a couple of battalions to the onslaught of
a _corps d’armée_ as the gallant fellows, construing their orders to
“retire fighting” after their own hot and angry fashion, contested with
reckless fury every inch of the ground! Strange strategy, truly, to
involve that town in the all but certainty of a second shelling before
it could be retaken, and of set purpose to let the enemy get foothold
on a position of such dominance as the Bellevue height, commanding all
the area that must be traversed by a force aiming at its recapture! No,
the French had a chance, and a great chance, on this 2nd of August; and
that they did not take advantage of it is not to be ascribed to any
acts of their adversaries, but to their own inconceivable unreadiness,
and to the imbecile inaction of their leaders, assignable to ignorance,
treachery, divided councils, an organisation that was chaos, and an
Intendance that was rotten to its remotest ramification.

Criticism of the actual defence made would be absurd. The men fought
with desperate valour, and were as cool under fire as if they had been
at their dinner. One company of the Hohenzollerns bore the full fire
of two French regiments for twenty minutes, and then only retired on
receiving an order to do so. They were severely cut up, as a matter of
course. Their captain was killed and a lieutenant wounded. It is hardly
to the discredit of the little force that they allowed themselves to be
surprised, as they unquestionably were. There were hardly men enough
for sentries all along the line, to say nothing of actual defence. A
field telegraph wire along the height from Ottenhausen to St. Arnual,
and extending down into the town and the railway station, would have
been an important aid.

Consequent upon the German evacuation of Saarbrück, Duttweiler became
a curious menagerie. The Hôtel Till, the only hostelrie in the little
place, is a curious, rambling erection, rather unpromising looked at
from the street, but improving on more intimate acquaintance, and
of the most indiarubber-like capacity. There is wing after wing of
bedrooms, great bare rooms with benches, tables, and old pictures,
devoted to what use it is impossible to guess, long cavernous passages,
and a staircase you might drive a coach and six up and down. How a
place of the size comes to be located in such a village I cannot tell,
but it was found a very ark of refuge for the foreign waifs and strays
that had been driven out of Saarbrück. My friend and myself were the
first arrivals. Then came a professor in a German university, an
Englishman, whom I had last seen coolly sauntering through the fire
in rear of a company of German infantry, and who, when he had tired
of this amusement, had struck out for himself in search of a bed,
and instinctively made tracks hither. He was followed by a waggon
containing a miscellaneous load. It contained the four Englishmen whom
we had left over their game of whist, a German correspondent, and two
ladies, one of whom had been a Sister of Mercy in the campaign of 1866,
and the other had come to Saarbrück from Bohemia, on purpose to be
married to a Prussian feldwebel. He was on active duty, but a special
dispensation had been obtained, and the fräulein was to have been
wedded on the Tuesday forenoon. The ceremony was to have taken place
in the Hôtel Hagen, but the French shells postponed its performance.
The officer went one way with his regiment, the lady came the other to
be out of reach of the French. They were hardly seated when there came
in an officer of the American army, who had seen the fun out and had
then strolled out here. Presently arrived a couple of British officers
in mufti, an English student from Bonn, an undergraduate of Oxford
who had broken off a reading tour to come and see a bit of fighting,
and a real live swell, with a fearful and wonderful coat, adorned
with a girdle and several dozen pockets, with an eye-glass and long
whiskers, a dog that seemed dearer to him than life, and a drawl that
had not been perceptibly quickened by the French cannon. He—aw—had
been going eastward somewhere to—aw—Constantinople or St. Petersburg,
or—aw—somewhere, when he heard of something likely to be stirring at
Saarbrück—and had come down to view its aspect through his eye-glass.
The big hotel swallowed us all up somehow, and we assimilated into
quite a happy family, feeding together, and looked upon with eyes of
good-humoured wonderment by the honest villagers of Duttweiler.

With the only information at our command, to the effect that Lebach—far
in the rear—was head-quarters for an army of advance as well as the
little army of retreat, the position in Duttweiler seemed as good as
any, and we accordingly did not quit it. Little worthy of chronicling
occurred. In the forenoon of the 3rd a French soldier, who had been
brought in a prisoner, took it into his head to make a frantic effort
to escape. He got his hand cut off to begin with, and still this did
not conquer his repugnance to being a prisoner of war; but a couple
of pistol-shots did this very effectually. A little later there was a
grand chevy after spies. One was shot down in the rear of the Hôtel
Till, and two more were captured in a wood above the town. A villager
was torn from his home and his weeping wife. He was a Frenchman born,
but a naturalised Prussian; he had, however, been playing the traitor
to his adopted country by sending the French information.

In the evening of the 3rd a few of us bombarded-out vagrants walked
down till we gained a prominent eminence almost overhanging the valley
of the Saar, and from this we had an excellent view of the French
dispositions. The sight which we saw surprised us. Away on our right
front, on the shoulder of the slope by Stiring, was a large camp with
artillery parked close to it. There were tents on the Exercise Platz on
the Bellevue heights, with a battery of guns looking out to the right.
Far away over the Bellevue, on the crown of the height from which I
have described the French as streaming down into the valley lying
between it and the Bellevue height, was a great camp of infantry,
occupied obviously by several brigades. What lay in the valley we could
not tell, but it was hardly likely that it did not serve to conceal
a large force. On the slope above St. Arnual was another camp, but
comparatively a small one. For all this we were not unprepared, but
we were certainly not prepared for what we saw far away to the left
in the direction of Saarguemines. A good field-glass clearly made out
that on a broad plateau that must have been beyond Saarguemines, for
it was certainly distant fifteen miles, there lay extended a vast camp
that could not have been occupied by less than a division. Setting this
aside, it may be taken as a fair estimate that not less than 15,000
French troops were within the range of our vision. As I have said, what
the valley concealed we had no means of knowing. It might have been
empty; it might have held other 15,000 men.

From this coign of vantage it was more apparent than ever what
defensive advantages the vicinity of Saarbrück might have afforded had
they been utilised. The semicircular piece of table-land at our feet
almost suggested itself as a platform for heavy guns, the fire of which
might have altered the issue by playing on the heights by gaining which
the French gained the town. All that was wanted as the complement was
the cutting down of the belts of trees which encircle the Exercise
Platz. Had the fire from guns placed here not succeeded in its object,
and had the French, having occupied Saarbrück and St. Johann, made
longer maintenance of the table-land position untenable, both by the
road and by the railway were admirable facilities for withdrawal, while
the wooded slopes in rear of the table-land might have been held for
ever by a few regiments of riflemen well supplied with ammunition. Of
course the absence alike of artillery and of riflemen rendered any such
disposition an impossibility, and the observation forced from us was
that if the Prussians should have to exert themselves vigorously to
retake Saarbrück, they might easily have saved themselves the trouble
and the cost by never having allowed the French to take the place at
all.

One important effect the capture, even if temporary, should have
had. The railway from Trèves to Neunkirchen, and thence either to
Bingerbrück or through the Pfalz by Homburg and Kaiserslautern, forming
a connection with the Baden system, passes through Saarbrück, or rather
through St. Johann. As a line skirting the frontier, it was of much
the same value to Germany as was to France the Saarguemines-Hagenau
line, which Lieutenant von Voight had cut a few days previously. It
seemed incomprehensible that if the French were to be driven out of
Saarbrück, they should go without cutting this communication. Yet not
only did they do so, but they left intact the great viaduct by which
the line leading from Saarbrück to Metz crosses the Saar, and so did
the Germans a better turn than if they had evacuated the Spicheren
without a fight.

On the 4th there was a strange pause in the military operations, and
some of us dwellers in the Hôtel Till took advantage of the lull to
pay a visit to Saarbrück. The wise man who said in his heart that all
men are liars might have lived in our neighbourhood. One hour the
story was that the French had evacuated Saarbrück and were in retreat,
the next that they were in full march inland, and that we should do
well to pack up our kits and be off. Great formalities were reported
as requisite to enable us so much as to essay the task of reaching
Saarbrück. We had to leave behind our cards, our authorisations and our
money, in case of falling into the hands of the French, and being both
made prisoners of war and robbed. All that we were to take were our
passports. Then we had to visit the burgomaster and obtain from him his
written sanction—with the official seal, of course—vouching for us as
being harmless and “unsuspicious” individuals. The worthy burgomaster
granted this readily, and we left him with thanks, fighting our way out
from his door through a bevy of women whose husbands had been drawn for
the Landwehr, and who were clamouring for some means of earning their
bread. We had not gone a quarter of a mile when we came on a halted
detachment of cuirassiers, whose commanding officer gave us a voucher
to carry me through his outposts, and told us he supposed we should
have to enter St. Johann under a flag of truce. To our surprise we
found not a Frenchman in all St. Johann. I went to my old hotel, where
I found interim repairs already executed, and everything in its usual
condition. Occasionally there were little squalls of firing between the
French from above Saarbrück and the Germans, who, in scattered parties,
were in St. Johann; but the townsfolk had become familiarised to the
sound of the shots, and never so much as troubled to turn their heads.
Nevertheless, in these occasional trivial affairs lives were being lost
and plucky things were being done. Just as we came in two of the Black
Hussars made a dash to cross the old bridge into Saarbrück, not knowing
whether there were Frenchmen over the way or not. A French piquet was
in the Schloss Platz, and a few of the men under a sous-officier, ran
down the slope to the bridge head to have a crack at the hussars.
When these saw the red legs coming, they went about and rode back,
chased on to the bridge by the latter. One of the hussars’ horses fell
and threw its rider. As he lay prostrate the French sous-officier
sprang forward, his sword upraised to strike the hussar as he rose.
The gallant horseman, however, was on his legs with one bound, with
another quick movement he levelled his slung carbine, drew the trigger,
and the sous-officier was a dead man. A couple of Frenchmen sprang
forward to avenge the fate of their officer; but meanwhile from the
St. Johann side a couple of German infantrymen had covered them, and
as these two fired one Frenchman fell. This affair angered the French,
who maintained for some twenty minutes a spattering fire at everything
living that showed, but without effect.

We were again led to believe in St. Johann that if we wished to get
across into Saarbrück it would be necessary to appear with a flag of
truce, and forward to the French officer in command there a written
request for permission. As we walked on one of us had a walking-stick
and pockethandkerchief all ready. But we got on the new bridge, and no
French sentry was visible; we crossed it, and never a Frenchman was to
be seen. We reached the Platz and the post-office, and there was no
more appearance of French troops than there is in the neighbourhood
of the Horse Guards at guard mounting. I had been told that General
Frossard was living at the Post Hotel. The people there laughed in my
face when I asked the question. We traversed several streets before
encountering any Frenchmen, but at length in a back street, we came
upon a batch of the red-legged, blue-coated gentry. Harmless enough
they looked, as they came out of a baker’s shop with the long loaves
under their arms, grinning from ear to ear. A _gasthaus_ was full of
them, sitting drinking Rhine-wine and water. I went in and sat down
as a listener. The men by their dialect were chiefly from Alsace and
Lorraine, districts in which there is a very large German admixture.
Their conversation was _de omnibus rebus et quibusdam aliis_, save and
except politics, national or international. The townspeople confidently
alleged that these Alsace and Lorraine men were with Germany at heart,
but they were not so sanguine as to this after Spicheren.

After keeping the Frenchmen company for a while we paid a visit to the
Saarbrück hospitals. There were very few men desperately wounded. The
horsemen had been chiefly hurt about the legs, the infantry soldiers
about the arms or through the shoulder-parts which would be likely
to be exposed by a man taking a tree as his cover. The hospital
arrangements were on the whole very good, with one exception. The
windows were all shut, and the rooms in consequence smelt close and
stuffy, and the atmosphere was impregnated with sour tobacco smoke. Why
a free circulation of air should not have been insisted on was the more
remarkable, since the treatment in almost every case was the use of
ice. With few men in a ward and clean recent wounds, ventilation is not
so great an essential; but it struck me, were the wards crowded in hot
weather by men with sloughing wounds hospital gangrene would inevitably
arise. The long corridor in Scutari hospital would have been a
pestilential place, but for Miss Nightingale’s insistance on the freest
ventilation. I did not observe that any disinfecting fluid was used in
the Saarbrück hospitals. It was here that I first saw men wounded by
the chassepot, and one could not fail to remark what a beautiful wound
that firearm makes. It was reported after Mentana that a chassepot
bullet made but a small orifice on entering, but that from its gyration
it scooped at its exit a trumpet-like mouth. But here I saw stripped
the wound of a man who had been shot through the shoulder, and it was
impossible for a non-scientific man to tell whether the bullet had
struck in front or rear. The people of Saarbrück were behaving to
the wounded with great devotion. Several of the citizens had wounded
soldiers in their houses, whom they preferred keeping there to sending
them to the hospitals.

“Rümschöttel,” mayor of St. Johann, was a good German, and a wise and
sagacious man. We found placarded at every street-corner the following
intimation from him:—

“Looking to the interests of our town, I feel called upon to request my
fellow-citizens to avoid laying violent hands upon any French soldiers,
but to leave such duty to our troops. It is also the duty of every
well-behaved citizen to endeavour by all means in his power to prevent
any such occurrence.” Placards of a similar tenor, but uniformly
couched in less simple and plain language, one got familiar enough
afterwards in the course of this war; but never more was there occasion
for the mayor of a German town to sign such a document. The unpleasant
but necessary duty ever fell by the force of events to the lot of a
French functionary. I am not without suspicions that Rümschöttel’s sage
counsels were not taken in all cases, for the townspeople had their
backs considerably up, especially the women, who fiercely resented the
somewhat free manners of the French soldiers. But if the civilians were
commanded to hold their hands, the troops to whom the mayor grimly
alluded were displaying remarkable sagacity and alertness in “cutting
out” any straggling Frenchmen. As we came into the town, we had met
several prisoners being escorted into the country. General Frossard,
on the evening of the attack, had sent for the mayor, and told him
that his orders were very strict against straggling and marauding,
and that if any cases occurred the citizens were to take the numbers
displayed on the caps of the men so committing themselves, and report
the same, when the fellows would be severely punished. On the whole,
the Frenchmen were reported as behaving themselves pretty well, and
paying honestly enough in most cases. A brewery had, however, been laid
violent hands on, and between what was drunk and what was let run, the
brewer had lost beer to the value of some 3,000 thalers. It must not be
imagined that although the French were not in occupation of St. Johann,
it was not quite as much at their disposal as if they had.

In the early morning of the 5th, there was a considerable cannonading
from the heights held by the French above Grosbliffersdorff, and
simultaneously came a sudden alarm at Duttweiler, that the French
cavalry were advancing in force from St. Johann, driving in the German
cuirassier piquets as they came. A dropping fire of small arms in
the distance was audible, and the road became alive with additional
patrols, but in an hour all was silent again, and we never got to know
whether there really was anything in the demonstration.

On Friday morning, anxious to obtain a general authorisation from
General von Stülpnagel, of the 5th Division, who was reported to
be in command at Neunkirchen and the vicinity, I went by waggon to
Neunkirchen for this purpose. Along the route there was no great number
of troops visible; indeed, with the exception of stray patrols, we
might have been miles distant from the war. But Neunkirchen

 “Showed another sight.”

All around were battalions of foot regiments and
regiments of cavalry, batteries of foot and of horse artillery, fields
full of cattle waiting for the military butcher, parks of forage
waggons, lanes of bread carts, masses of ambulance waggons, and all the
_impedimenta_ of a great army contemplating a sustained and prolonged
advance. Troops were streaming into the place from all directions,
notably horse artillery. I watched the entrance of three batteries of
this arm of the service, and I confess I was disappointed by their
appearance, remembering the dash and carriage of our English horse
artillery. The men seemed to want vivacity, their uniforms were plain,
the horses were inclined to be soporific, and altogether there appeared
to be a want of smartness about them compared with our own Royal Horse
Artillery. It remained to be proved, as it was the next day, how truly
serviceable were the plain-looking men and horses.

Having obtained my authorisation with a courtesy on the part of the
Prussian staff such as I have almost invariably met with from that
body, I retired quietly to my quarters, with the intention of enjoying
a good night’s rest, from which, for two or three nights before, I had
been debarred. A staff officer occupying the same quarters was good
enough to furnish me with some details of the positions occupied by
the first and second Armies, which sufficed to indicate very clearly
that an invasion of France was imminent. The 1st Army, consisting of
the 7th and 8th Corps, was advancing on the Saar, through the southern
extremity of the Lower Rhine Province, mainly in a section of country
having for its western boundary a line drawn northward from Saarlouis,
for its eastern the frontier of the Pfalz. The 7th Corps (Zastrow)
had the right wing, the left consisted of von Göben’s 8th Corps. The
1st Corps (Manteuffel), which was also to form part of the 1st Army,
had not yet caught up with the advance. He had not particulars as to
the dispositions of the 1st Army during the night between the 5th and
6th of August, but those I afterwards obtained as follows:—Zastrow
had his head-quarters in Lebach, and was to march next morning (the
7th) to Dilsburg. The right division of the 7th Corps, the 13th
(Glümer) bivouacked in and around Puttlingen, feeling away further
right toward Derlen and Knausholz. This was the right flank of the
1st Army. Glümer’s orders were to advance on the 6th to the Saar at
Völklingen and Rockerkausen. Kamecke, with his division (the 14th),
spent the night in the vicinity of Guchenbach, with his advanced troops
close up on Saarbrück. The bulk of the division was not timed to
reach the frontier before the 7th. The 8th Corps stood still further
back. The halting place on the 6th for its leading division, the 16th
(Barneckow), had been fixed at Fischbach, and the 15th (Weltzien),
still further in the rear, had its billet for Holz. Thus far as regards
the 1st Army. The 2nd Army, starting from its rendezvous at Mayence,
was swarming down all over the Pfalz. Lieutenant-General Alvensleben
had the right flank of it with the 3rd Corps,[1] and it with the 5th
Cavalry Division (Rheinbaben), which was employed in covering the right
flank and the front, was the only part of Prince Frederick Charles’
command engaged in the fight of Spicheren. As regards its dispositions,
my staff officer, since he belonged to one of its divisions, could
naturally speak with greater exactitude. He believed Rheinbaben’s
horsemen were already in Saarbrück, and they were permeating all the
country intervening between that town and Alvensleben’s infantry,
while they were also covering the left front of the 1st Army. This
disposition brought Rheinbaben _en rapport_ with Kamecke, and it will
presently appear to what effect the dashing cavalry officer availed
himself of his knowledge of that general’s relative proximity. For the
rest, the 5th Division, now lying in Neunkirchen, had for its morrow’s
itinerary a route that would bring it to Duttweiler and Rentrisch,
both villages, about five miles to the north of Saarbrück, and it was
to be temporarily succeeded in its occupation of Neunkirchen by the
6th Division (Buddenbrock)—the next link in the great chain cable that
was to be warped out of the Pfalz over the frontier into Frankreich.
But Stülpnagel, although himself spending the night in Neunkirchen,
had already projected his antennæ on the road he was to travel on the
morrow—not alone such feelers as the stout squadrons of Rheinbaben,
but also the 9th Infantry Brigade, commanded by the gallant Döring.
That brigade had its bivouac for the night on the slopes to the south
of Duttweiler, while at least one regiment of the 10th Brigade (the
52nd) was forward also on the southern road, with its night halt in
St. Ingbert. Every disposition indicated the proximity of a grand and
methodical concentration in the Saar valley, with Saarbrück for its
centre; but there was an absence of urgency in the arrangements that
certainly gave no earnest of belief in the imminence of a battle.

I make no apology for interpolating here the following description of
the battle of Spicheren furnished to me by a Prussian gentleman who
enjoyed exceptional advantages both for seeing the incidents of the
fight and for obtaining accurate information as to its details. He
had been educated as a staff officer, and had served in that capacity
in the war of ’66; and having temporarily quitted the military
profession, had hurried down to Saarbrück with intent to join, at his
own charges, some cavalry regiment in which there might be a vacancy
for an officer. When Spicheren was fought he had not yet been posted
to any appointment, and was therefore free to devote himself wholly
to observation, while he could ask questions and go unchallenged to
positions not to be thought of by persons without his advantages. I
have taken pains to test his facts, and being fully impressed with
their accuracy, have used no freedom with them, although I have
ventured to take some little liberty in rendering his language.

“In the early morning of the 6th, about four o’clock, I was aroused by
the bugle blast, and an hour after came the quick march of soldiers
on their way to the front. A couple of regiments passed towards the
railway station; another regiment started along the high road. Among
the troops there was no confusion, but certainly no want of alacrity.
A visit to the railway station showed me that, with a couple of
battalions in waiting, and no train expected other than indefinitely,
the railway afforded a very precarious means of transit, so I returned
and got a place in a waggon, in which, after breakfast, I started for
Saarbrück by the southern road. Presently we passed the infantry,
striding along manfully, and I had not gone six miles when I heard in
the distance the rattling sound in the still morning air, which no man
who has ever heard it can mistake for anything else than the noise
of moving artillery. By this time I was through St. Ingbert, where
the 52nd were halting with stacked arms, and soon after, at a turn
of the road, our waggon was overtaken by two batteries of artillery,
coming along at a swinging canter. The driving boy entered into the
spirit of the thing, and I offered him double fare if he would only
keep up with the horse artillery. He tried it right manfully, but the
waggon was clumsy, and so was the harness; and in the ascent, at the
slope leading to Scheidt, he brought his team to a standstill and
struck. Spite of his remonstrances, I got the off horse out of his
harness, and, mounting the animal bare-backed, set off in pursuit of
the artillery. I overtook them a little beyond Scheidt, where they had
halted for a few minutes to give the horses their wind, and found that
they belonged to Stülpnagel’s division, and that they had been making
haste to overtake infantry that was in their front. Hurrying on (for by
this time it was nearly eleven o’clock) I overtook, just as the Saar
valley at St. Arnual opened up, the tail of a part of Döring’s brigade,
which that officer had brought forward on a reconnaissance. The head
of the column was already engaged. Looking over the meadow toward St.
Arnual, I could see how the ditches and hedges athwart it were lined
sparsely with very energetic skirmishers. The French had a battery
outside the village, sweeping the crossing of the stream, and the guns
were playing on the road by which Döring’s men were debouching into the
open. His skirmishers, by the heady way they pressed forward, seemed
eager by themselves to carry St. Arnual, and turn by the manœuvre what
was evidently the point on which rested the left flank of the French.
Away on the right, Saarbrück seemed the centre of a pandemonium. On
the Exercise Platz shells were bursting, and it seemed as if batteries
were in action on the height; but this, as it turned out, was not the
case. All up the slope leading from the town towards the Exercise Platz
were heard the crack and seen the smoke-puff of the needle-gun, as the
German infantrymen pressed on to the summit. But in our own immediate
front we had quite enough to engage our attention. Stülpnagel’s
artillerymen had unlimbered, and were blazing into St. Arnual and the
French. Suddenly the latter gave ground. Their guns limbered up, and
their infantry melted away backward into the wood. Certainly it was not
wholly the German fire that caused this sudden retreat; some order must
have come from head-quarters. No matter what was the cause of it, the
German horse artillery were in motion again like a flash of lightning.
Through the river dashed the guns, sending up the spray in jets, on to
the turf with a strain and a bound, and then with a loosened rein into
St. Arnual! Here there was another brief stand, then another limber
up, and the horse artillery at a sweeping gallop passed round the St.
Arnual bluff out of my sight, whether into the valley beyond or to
climb the ‘Winterberg’ I could not tell.

“For my part, I rode into Saarbrück on my bare-backed steed, which I
handed over to the ‘stallknecht’ of the Reinischer Hof, in St. Johann,
to be claimed by his owner. Upon the railway-station esplanade I found,
in the persons of one or two dead soldiers, the evidence that the
battle had extended thus far. On the bridges joining St. Johann and
Saarbrück lay others, and the townsfolk were attending to the wounded.
At the head of the old bridge stood a French gun, a trophy, so far,
of conquest. The steep slope up to the Exercise Platz had evidently
not been taken without some fighting, although it had been held only
by some straggling outposts of Frossard’s rear-guard belonging to the
Division Vergé. The French had obviously not been idle during the
days in which they had occupied the heights overlooking Saarbrück. A
continuous line of entrenchment had been thrown up across the crest,
with earthworks of more importance for stronger infantry detachments
at intervals. But it was evident that no serious attempt had been
made to hold this hither line. Indeed, as I learned in Saarbrück, the
brigade or division (whichever it was) that had held the heights and
plateau behind it had fallen back everywhere on the previous night
but one. On the 5th Rheinbaben’s patrols, pushing forward after their
manner, had discovered how feebly the Exercise Platz was held, and
early on the morning of the fight their explorations had not only
satisfied their leader that it had been almost wholly deserted under
cover of the darkness, but had impressed him with the conviction that
Frossard was beginning a retrograde movement, which should include the
abandonment of the yet more advantageous position on the Spicheren Berg
and the elevated ground about Stiring with the entrenched position
across the valley, stretching like a curtain between the two strong
flank-positions.” Whether Rheinbaben was not too sanguine, or at all
events premature in this conclusion, is a question that doubtless
would have occasioned more controversy if the issue of the fight
that ensued mainly upon it had been different. “He lost no time in
dispatching the intelligence to General Kamecke, who, as commanding
the division nearest to the scene of action, at once took upon himself
the responsibility of ordering an attack on what it appears certain
he considered was but the enemy’s rear-guard. He sent back notice
of his intention to Zastrow, altering by the tidings the leisurely
character of that general’s advance on Dilsburg. Kamecke himself, with
characteristic impetuosity, galloped on to overtake his leading brigade
(the 28th), which had got close to Saarbrück, when he caught up with
it. Without losing a moment, Kamecke pushed Rheinbaben’s horsemen up
the slopes to the Exercise Platz, covering the intervals between the
roads with skirmishers, thrown out from the heads of his infantry
columns, while some of his artillery unlimbered on the St. Johann
side of the stream, and plied the plateau of the Exercise Platz with
shell-fire until the advance of the cavalry and infantry compelled a
cessation. It was while this was going on that Döring’s reconnaissance
reached the valley opposite to St. Arnual. I afterwards learnt that he
sent the battalions composing it down the river to support Kamecke’s
direct attack.

“As I passed through Saarbrück, and took my way up the winding road
to the Exercise Platz, it was already past eleven. Woyna’s infantry
brigade (the 28th) and Rheinbaben’s cavalry were already over the ridge
and striking steadily down into the valley intervening between it and
the Spicheren Berg and the French position. Hurried movements, executed
by large bodies of men, were visible through the glass all along the
latter. Streams of soldiers were pouring over the horizon, and rushing
down to line the entrenched edge of the bastion-like Spicheren. On
the little quarterdeck-like plateau, just behind, the batteries had
already got into position, and the shells were dropping fast among
the Woynas and Rheinbabens as they pushed forward into the valley.
Other batteries had opened fire also from a little eminence to the
French left of Stiring. These did not have it all their own way. Four
batteries Kamecke had already got into action—three aligned with the
trees on the Forbach margin of the Exercise Platz, one on the other
side of the Saarbrück-Forbach road. The fire of these was concentrated
on the Spicheren Berg, while already down in the valley one heard the
rattle of the musketry fire, and now and then came the sharp ping of a
chassepot bullet.

“At the mouth of the scooped-out cross-road where it joins the main
road opposite the Bellevue beerhouse, sat Kamecke on horseback, not
quite so cool, perhaps, as the circumstances demanded. With abrupt
gestures and jerky messages he was hurrying up his division to the
attack. The long columns strained up the steep hill in extra quick
time, the men puffing and blowing as the top was gained, and as the
battalions marched down the further slope and took ground right and
left of the main road across the valley. Here the young soldiers got
their baptism of fire, and the veterans of ’66 had recollections
freshened that might have faded into dimness as the years of peace had
rolled over their heads. For now, not alone over their heads, but into
their ranks, hurtled the angry shells, the jets of white smoke and the
temporary disruption of the formation showing ever and anon where one
had burst. The leading battalions lay down while the deployment was in
progress, but still the shells spared them not. Kamecke’s artillery
as it came up in detachments took ground right and left, some of it
on the height, some batteries rattling down the road and opening fire
on the slope at shorter range. One or two batteries were pushed out
to the right hand corner of the Exercise Platz to give reply to the
French batteries that were in so energetic action on the knoll flanking
Stiring. But artillery, infantry, and cavalry—for all were now within
range of vision from the corner of the Exercise Platz overhanging the
steep road—made up what seemed full too weak a force to cope with what
was before it; nor was there any indications of the speedy advent of
supports.

“Presently there was a move down in the valley. The Woynas (28th
Brigade) were heading away to the German right front to engage the
French left resting on Stiring. The gallant von Francois, one of a
race of soldiers, himself that day to meet a soldier’s death, led his
stout Westphalians of the 27th brigade direct on that abrupt mountain
mass that frowned out upon the plain, its summit already marked by the
fringe of white smoke from the chassepots of the Frenchmen lining the
entrenchment. There were French skirmishers in the bottom of the valley
and on the little foothold places on the precipitous incline. On the
level the Westphalians sent these back as if they too had been smoke.
But it was no fair stand-up fight man to man. From the top of the Berg
chassepot, mitrailleuse, field-gun concentrated on the on-marching
battalions their powers of destruction. With a steady, rather slow,
step they pressed onward constantly, leaving behind them on the plain
a terrible proportion of their number. The slowness of the pace was
characteristic.

“The German is a man of method and of stubborn pith. Under this
death-hail the dressing seemed as systematic as on the parade at
Düsseldorf, the touch of man to man as accurate. Ever as a shell
crashed through a company, or the mitrailleuse opened a lane and
strewed it with dead and wounded, the men flanking the gap closed in
with a grim coolness which impressed one with the conviction that men
who could act so could not be beaten. When the foot of the Berg was
reached, the pace quickened and the formation broke. It was now like
the forlorn hope of a besieging army storming a breach rashly reported
practicable. Men scrambled upwards and forwards anyhow. The officers
threw themselves from their horses, and took perforce to climbing on
foot. Now the leader on a rugged pathway would go down, and, as he
rolled backward, he would tumble his followers with him in pell-mell
confusion. Officers were seen throwing forward their swords, men their
needle-guns, and clambering up after their weapons on their hands and
knees. There were little protected liebys here and there, and under
these shelters the men packed as you may have seen sheep huddle before
a high wind with a snow-drift borne on it. Thither crept, too, the
wounded out of the fire that spared them when wounded no more than when
they had been hale fighting men. Then when the sound men had caught
their wind again, they would dash out, and make another convulsive
scramble upward, ever upward. On the brink you might see the Frenchmen
bending over the lip of the crest to get more purchase with their
downward aim. A mitrailleuse was balanced on the edge by men clinging
to the wheels and the trail, while its honeycomb-like muzzle went down,
pointing among the stragglers, its butt end high in air. Kamecke had
asked men to do an impossibility. There are parts of the Spicheren,
notably that part just on the left of the extreme bluff nearest
Saarbrück, which are not to be surmounted without scaling ladders. Von
Francois’s men were brought to a standstill. There was no alternative
but to fall back. Fortunately there was no necessity for a retreat
right athwart the valley. Out of its centre there rise several low
detached mamelons not of sufficient elevation to give any dominance as
against artillery on the Spicheren Berg, but affording better positions
for batteries than the plain itself, and furnishing cover behind which
troops might enjoy at least a partial protection. Two of these bear the
names respectively of the Galgenberg (Gallows-hill) and the Folster
Höhe, while behind the latter is some broken ground, with a house or
two and some water, the spot bearing the name of the Drathzug. On to
these two little eminences the artillery of Kamecke’s division had been
hurried forward to attempt some support and distraction for Francois’s
brigade, and when the latter had to cease its bootless efforts to carry
the Spicheren, it fell back to reorganise itself behind the knolls
on which the gunners, themselves suffering severely, were plying the
enemy with shrapnel-shells. Kamecke’s cavalry had drawn forward in
the valley, behind the shelter afforded by the Gallows-hill and the
Drathzug, strengthened by two regiments of hussars from Rheinbaben’s
cavalry division. As the 27th Brigade drew back sulkily, the French
skirmishers dashed down from the plateau, eager to hang on the skirts
of the retreating Westphalians. There are two steep country roads that
get down from the summit into the valley by a series of slants and
zigzags, and these were extensively utilised by the French skirmishers,
who pushed down even into the valley taking advantage of what cover was
available, as ever and anon Francois’s men turned savagely at bay. But
the skirmishers’ _bon quatre d’heure_ was very short. From behind the
Gallows-hill dashed out right and left the keen-sabred troopers of the
11th and 13th Hussars. Galloping through and round their friends, they
were on the Frenchmen like a thunderbolt, and the tirailleur-nimbleness
of the plucky little Frenchmen availed but little not a few of them. So
hot was the fury of the Hussars that they recklessly charged the Berg
that had broken the hearts of the infantrymen. They were not long in
discovering that their riding-school lessons did not include practice
in crag-climbing, and they went back wiser men than before; while to
some it was not vouchsafed to apply the experience in after-life. But
the sections which formed themselves on the more westerly road up the
heights saw before them a track which looked practicable. With mad
valour the blue troopers dashed on and up, strewing the road with
living débris as they advanced. How near the summit one at least of
them must have got I never knew till next day, when I saw a dead hussar
and a dead horse tumbled over into a ravine three-fourths of the way
up. I saw them ride up. I never saw any of them ride back.

“While the 27th Brigade was re-forming under cover of the guns, Woyna,
with the 28th Brigade, having struck away to the left by the Folster
Höhe, was steadily fighting his way up toward Stiring, on which the
French left flank leant apparently in considerable strength. His
troops had fair cover in the broken woodland on either side of the
railway and the mill-dams, while the railway cutting, deep as it is in
places, and not straight for a long enough distance to be raked, also
afforded opportune protection. He had, however, to fight every step of
the ground, amid a terrific shell-fire that set alight to a terrace
of workmen’s houses on the western side of the railway, that seemed
to have been temporarily occupied by the German troops. But Woyna’s
progress was terribly slow, and the French fire never slackened.” What
his losses were, the journeyer on the railway may yet form a vague
conception as he looks out upon the numerous green hillocks in the
forest glades or the little clearings. There is a little signal-house
on the railway that must have been the focus of a fearful fire. No
shells had struck it, but when I saw it the day but one after the
battle I could not find a space the size of this page which did not
show the easily-read print of the impact of a bullet.

“Von Francois’s men were not convinced by the lesson they had received
as to the inaccessibility of the Spicheren Berg. They must have one
more useless essay at the impossible. I do not know whether Kamecke
gave the order or not, but the diminished companies came out yet again
to the open, and once more breasted the lower slopes of the hill.
By this time the fire of the division’s artillery, plied as it was
unremittingly, had made the edge of the Berg a very hot place, and the
Westphalians were hardly so much torn as they had been on their first
attempt. But the French general must have recognised how fatal to him
would be their success. They must be repulsed _coûte qui coûte_. So the
Frenchmen, standing in the shell-fire, again lined their intrenchment,
and leaning over, rained death upon the struggling bearers of the
needle-guns. Some of von Francois’s men staggered up through the storm,
till they reached the shelter of a rock which, overhanging, supports a
section of the verge of the sloping plateau above; and there, powerless
to go further, and objecting to go back, they cowered in comparative
safety, so close under their enemies that they might almost have
grasped the muzzles of the chassepots. Von Francois himself was down,
a fearful proportion of his officers had fallen, and the brigade had
to own itself foiled, and give up what it seemed to have set its heart
upon to achieve. But it went no more back behind the guns. These forced
the French to hold their hands greatly, and the German infantry men,
with a bulldog-like stubbornness, lay down among the furrows and by the
hedgerows along the foot of the Berg. If they could not carry it, at
least they would not leave it.

“It was now close on three o’clock, and Kamecke must surely, after the
example of one greater than he, have been looking often and often at
his watch, and muttering that would to God night or Zastrow would come.
But there were succours nigher at hand than any Zastrow could bring.
As it came toward its halting ground at Fischbach, the cannon-thunder
came wafted on the wind to Barneckow’s division. Von Göben was on
in advance of his corps; von Göben, to whom the _Kanonendonner_ was
ever sweet music. There was no halt for the 16th Division this long
summer day. By two the mazed people of Saarbrück saw with joy their
old friends of the 40th, as the staunch Hohenzollerns hurried over the
bridge to get on to the heights where they had already tasted blood.
Von Göben rode on to the Exercise Platz, and took in the situation at
a glance. It was truly a critical one. The 40th he sent straight out
into the plain, to support the broken left flank of the 27th Brigade,
and rode on himself to the Gallows-hill to take the command out of
Kamecke’s hands in virtue of his superior rank. The 40th Regiment
was as yet all that the 8th Army Corps had to say for itself, if we
except von Göben: but other troops than Barneckow’s division had heard
the cannon-thunder. By half-past two there was streaming round the
shoulder the mass of Döring’s brigade, the 9th, of the 5th Division
(Stülpnagel), summoned from its bivouac by Duttweiler when its general
had found that his reconnaissance was the road to a battle. It had
already done some fighting about St. Arnual, and had established the
fact that the right flank of the French was now in the forest of
Spicheren, which clothes the somewhat less abrupt slopes from the
precipitous part of the Berg eastward in the direction of Berbach.
As Döring advanced, he fell into position on the left of the 40th
Regiment, with his front looking into the dark forest that rose before
him. Other advance regiments of the 5th Division presently reached
him, and the aid, no great thing as it was, looking at the work to be
done, gave relief to the 14th Division, engaged as it had been right
up to the hilt. The attack on the Berg was renewed but in part, the
40th undertaking the less precipitous section lying next the wood of
Spicheren, while Döring’s regiments, under cover of a fire from part of
the artillery of the 3rd Corps, which had just come up, went straight
at the forest itself. The 40th had traditions of storming prowess. It
had shown the way over the fortifications of Düppel; but the Spicheren
Berg was harder than had been Düppel. What fighting it was to be sure,
all along from the nose of the bluff eastward to the right verge of the
wood! Three times did the Germans charge the hill and the wood—scarcely
less a hill; three times did the French, fighting with a stubborn fury
that was of the undegenerate days, hurl them back with a withering fire
right in their teeth almost at arm’s length. For two stricken hours
was the result dubious. But at five Alvensleben came on the ground
with fresh supports belonging to the 3rd Corps, and one more attack
was joined. Steadily climbed on the 40th up the steep green slopes.
Döring’s men were over the trench on the edge of the wood and in among
the trees. There is something horrible in the suspense of a fight in
a wood. You see little or nothing; there is an occasional flash, and
in the casual openings glimpses of troops swaying and heaving, like
sea-waves in the chasm of a cavern; then there is an interval with
nothing visible but the calm green tree-tops, which mock you when
you know that hell is raging below them; and the hell is continually
dropping its red-hot coals through the bars of the furnace, in the
shape of wounded men who come staggering out from behind the trunks,
halting often as they come. How is it to go? Are the blue-coated men
you saw go in to be vomited forth again the way they went, with the
red-breeched men hot and triumphant on their flanks, plying the devil’s
pitchfork to hoise them wherewithal outside? or has this Spicheren hell
a thoroughfare through it, and are the blue-coated men, having battled
through the furnace, to reach the grill on the further side, and draw
breaths of cooler air there? The white smoke surges up fitfully through
the dark-green boughs of the fir-trees; still the wounded men stagger
out, and still the issue is uncertain.

“But if we could not penetrate the secrets of the wood, we might, at
all events, watch the course of the Hohenzollerns on its flank. Slowly,
and evidently with arduous effort and terrible loss, they pushed on
their dogged way. At length they were over the worst in the point of
climbing, and into the worst in point of musketry fire, as they reached
a kind of natural glacis before the French entrenchment. Watching them
through the glass, one seemed to see the men straightening themselves
up and pulling themselves together; then, as it seemed, with one great
effort of their muscular loins and hips, they shot forward on to the
top of the earth-bank. For one moment it was as if they hung here,
and then fell forward, lurching down as it were on the Frenchmen who
lined the trench. They say there was no actual bayonet fighting; from
behind, it certainly looked as if for a couple of minutes there was a
hand-to-hand fight; on the near side the dogged, weighty mass of the
men in spiked helmets, leaning forward with a terrible _vis inertiæ_;
on the further, a seething, bobbing, gyrating, frantic throng of
marionnette-like men in red breeches. The spiked helmets seemed with
their sheer weight and irresistible pressure to drive back the red
breeches as a masterful wave sweeps the foam before it; and when the
berg was won, the wood was won also. Döring’s Brandenburgers were not
to be flouted by the Hohenzollerns. The wood was theirs, and cleared of
French as the latter became aligned with its edge on its western flank.
We saw little more for the time of the fight here, but the din that
came back to us proved that all was not over on the upland when the
Spicheren wood had been taken.

“As we learned afterwards, the French again and again charged the
wood with fury, and even once or twice penetrated into it. The
Hohenzollerns, dreading lest with the impetuosity of the rush they
should be swept bodily back off the plateau, had to seek something
at once of ground-anchorage and of cover among the trees. It was
impossible for infantry alone to make its way upward and onward on
the plateau. The call was for artillery, and artillery came. Two
batteries of Stülpnagel’s division, that had been firing from before
the Winterberg before the duel in the dark began in the wood, limbered
up, and made for the rough winding track by the edge of the wood. The
drivers were resolute, and the horses were strong. In ten minutes
the gunners were at “action front” in the very faces of the French
infantry, who had no alternative but to give way, for they had been
deserted by their artillery; so, fighting desperately as they went,
they fell slowly back, and the Germans pressed on, gradually making the
plateau their own. So the French right wing was turned, and the battle
of Spicheren virtually won.

“Certainly on the German right there seemed little indication that
such a result had been achieved. Zastrow, with part of the reserve
artillery of his corps, had come over by the railway bridge on to the
battle-field at half-past four, superseding in his turn Von Göben in
right of seniority. Zastrow had been trying strategical experiments
with his 13th division (Glümer), which was out of the hunt, away
by Völklingen, marching along, as we afterwards learnt, in blessed
unconsciousness of what was going on so near it, the wind setting the
other way, till an aide-de-camp from Zastrow, who reached Glümer about
five o’clock, gave him a new direction and a stimulus to speed. But
Zastrow had not at hand available troops of his own corps. The quiet
and prompt Alvensleben (who was also already in the field) had got up
nearly the whole of Stülpnagel’s division, and there were regiments
available to participate in offensive action on the right, as well as
to carry the Spicheren wood on the left. All the afternoon Woyna had
been fighting doggedly forward till he had edged the French out of
Stiring, and well across the valley from their entrenchments behind
the iron-works of Stiring Wendel, on to the Kreuzbach, which may be
called roughly a prolongation of the Spicheren-berg toward Forbach.
Zastrow sent on a brigade of infantry and some artillery to attack this
strong position; but the French, whose left had been strengthened by a
division of Bazaine’s corps, anticipated the offensive, coming down in
great force into the valley, no doubt with a design to cover Forbach at
least for a time. The German artillery, which was now in strong force
about the railway, played terrible havoc with this effort on the part
of the French, who, however, persevered with a tenacity that seemed to
partake of despair. At length Stülpnagel’s infantry were sent forward
to precipitate the action, and this they did, by driving the French up
the Kreuzbach, and storming it on their heels. This movement turned the
French left on its part, so that while they were being hustled backward
over the plateau on the right, they were being all the more shoved on
to it from the Kreuzbach toward Spicheren and Alsting from the left.
The confusion of the cross streams must have been immense, especially
as the roads along which retreat alone was possible were by no means
either good or plentiful. While Frossard held Forbach he had the key
to the main _chaussée_ to Metz; but Zastrow’s strategetical experiment
turned up a trump in the twilight. Glümer’s division (the 13th),
bearing round from Völklingen by Ludweiler and Rosseln, came down
upon Forbach from a due westerly direction, and frightened the French
out of it with hardly any fighting.” The other divisions had got all
the mauling; Glümer’s had had the furthest marching, and now came in
for the heaviest loot; for the French evacuated Forbach in hot haste,
and, after an absurd attempt to wreck the station and its multifarious
contents by sending an engine to run amuck down a siding and on to the
station, left everything as it stood, from scents and _bonbons_ to
flour and munitions of war.

“There was in the dusk a kind of miscellaneous fighting all across the
plateau, about the villages of Spicheren, Alsting, and Etzling. The
French seemed never to know when they were beaten. Although regimental
formation had become for the most part shattered in the mass which
acted as the rear guard, detachments and groups fought passionately.
It seemed as if the fight was to last all night. It became impossible
in the dusk to distinguish between friend and foe. The French were
reported to have sustained serious injury through a blunder of this
kind, in which countrymen slew countrymen. The Prussians adopted the
expedient of shouting “Hurrah Preussen!” and the hoarse, deep-throated
cry ever and anon came backward from out the din, the smoke, and the
fire-flashes with a strange weird effect. At length these detachments
and groups either fell back or went down till the front was tolerably
clear of Frenchmen. Then their artillery struck in for the last word.
It had been got into position about Kerbach to cover the retreat on
Puttelange, and was well and staunchly served to the last. The Germans
with some little over-confidence, pressed it rather too closely,
and then the French infantry yet once more showed how it carried a
sting in its tail.” It must have been a ragged array that resumed the
offensive thus in the darkness, but there is no doubt of the fact that
a determined front after a fashion was shown. Yet there might have been
some attempt at a pursuit, but that all the German army, with perhaps
the exception of Rheinbaben’s cavalry division, was jaded with long
marching and fighting. So beyond Kerbach, Frossard was let pursue in
peace his disorganised and disorderly retreat. They had left of their
goods and chattels in Spicheren, as well as in Forbach. “The firing had
not wholly died away till after nine o’clock. As I walked back over
the plateau, down the steep, across the valley, and on to the Exercise
Platz, I had constantly to pick my way very carefully. It is a dismal
sensation to stumble over a dead man. It is even a greater shock to
force unwittingly a groan of pain from a poor wounded wretch lying
helpless in his blood, waiting for the succour that, come how soon it
may, is ever over-tardy. How many, both of dead and of wounded, flecked
the brown surface of the heights I could then form no estimate; I only
knew this, that the number of both was very great. Spiked helmets and
red breeches blended together as closely as if they had been bosom
friends all their lives. The hospital corps with their ambulances were
swiftly picking up the wounded and carrying them off to be tended.
Their organisation seemed excellent. More than once I saw in the pale
ray of a moonbeam a female form swathed in black, with the white patch
and the cross on the breast, bending over a prostrate form, or flitting
ghostlike in the van of an ambulance party.” God bless the Sisters of
Mercy in a night like this, say I, whether they are Roman Catholic or
whether they be Protestant. Christianity on a battle-field can well
afford to sink sectarianism. There were days and nights of bloodshed
to come, when no Saarbrück convent should be close by to send out its
courageous sisterhood on to the brink of the _Schlachtfeld_.

The Germans had, in all, engaged about 40,000 men, including the
division Glümer, and probably Frossard, even with the reinforcement
from Bazaine, was not so strong by some 12,000. Deducting Glümer’s
division, which, although it came under fire, and therefore, strictly
speaking, must reckon as being in the field, took no real part in the
action, the hostile forces were about equally matched in point of
numbers. It is no exaggeration to say that its position represented to
the French army a value equivalent to another army of equal strength.
The German loss was terrible—over 4,300 men killed and wounded. Nearly
all the loss fell on the 14th division, only a portion of the 5th
division, and the 40th Regiment. Probably the whole loss falls to be
apportioned on a force under 20,000 men, and this would bring out the
loss as one in five—an exceptionally heavy per-centage. The loss in
officers was by far too heavy on the German side. The French left 2,000
unwounded prisoners in German hands.

The battle of Spicheren was won solely by the stubborn valour of the
German soldiers, and by the cannon-thunder. The record of it contains
no evidence of the influence of a master-mind over its dispositions and
results. General Kamecke was probably justified in his effort to make
a snap-shot for a victory, but certainly he had no right to engage
himself so closely and generally as he did, until he had far better
assurance than he had that he might reckon on supports when the time
should come for him to want them. He might have secured his end, at
far less expenditure of human life, by using his division discreetly
to keep the French in play on both flanks, instead of abusing it as
he did by an effort for the direct storm of such a position as the
Spicheren-berg. It does not appear that he had any cognizance of
Stülpnagel’s ability to come to his support in the opportune moment,
or, indeed, that he communicated at all with Alvensleben’s corps.
What succour he derived from this source—and how invaluable was that
succour, the preceding details have demonstrated—came to him from the
fortunate circumstance that Döring’s reconnaissance in the morning put
that officer on the _qui vive_, and from the accident that the wind set
in such a direction as to carry the sound of the cannonade into the
back country. The responsibility of the day’s work, in so far as it
was disastrous, rests with Kamecke; and there seems every probability
that had the first army—the support which was all that he appears
or, indeed, was entitled to have calculated on—constituted the whole
available force, the marker in the war-game would have had to score a
victory for the German arms between Saarbrück and Forbach on the 6th
of August. That Kamecke was not defeated before supports came up, was
owing solely to the stoutness of his Westphalians; and that victory
was ultimately achieved was owing to the fact that Stülpnagel and
Alvensleben were prompter than was Grouchy in marching on the sound of
the cannon. All’s well that ends well. The victory had an immense moral
effect. It must have been in the light of the experience furnished
them at Spicheren that the French huddled backward under the shells
of the forts of Metz, without attempting to utilise the many tempting
positions for a stand which the configuration of the ground afforded
them by the way. General Steinmetz has been erroneously blamed for the
waste of life at Spicheren. The fact is that the gallant old soldier
was responsible for nothing on the 6th of August, unless it might be
for the circumstance that he did not happen to be nearer the front. He
did not get into Saarbrück until the evening.

I walked out on the battle-field on Sunday afternoon. All up the steep
which rose out of the valley all the way from over against the Exercise
Platz to near Forbach, a distance of some four miles, the dead of the
German army lay thickly. At the bottom of the steepest place there was
quite a fringe of butchery that had rolled downward in obedience to
the law of gravitation, a grim alluvial deposit made by the torrent
of shot. On the little level shoulders of the steep graves were being
made, into which the burial-parties were huddling

 “Friend and foe,
 In one red burial blent.”

Some of the ravines leading on to the height, where it is indented by
the bay between the Spicheren-berg and the Kreuzberg, had been the
scenes of desperate fighting. In a little gully just at the top of one
of these I found a dozen Prussians lying dead, piled one above another
in inextricable confusion. Elsewhere they lay as thick. Inside the
intrenchments it was the turn of the Frenchmen to be found dead in the
greatest numbers. The ditch itself was full of them. For several yards
inside the intrenchments it was impossible to step without treading on
dead humanity. The fight had been equally deadly all over the plateau,
where the corn and grass were trodden into the brick-like ground that
was yet in places soft and soppy with blood.

Saarbrück, which had chafed mightily under the presence of the
Frenchmen on the heights above them and occasionally in their midst, no
sooner saw victorious Germans on the Exercise Platz than it broke out
into a spasmodic eruption of flags symbolical of victory, and generally
went half crazy with joy. But there was real work for Saarbrück to do
in manifestation of its national and indeed human sympathies, and the
flags and phlegmatic caperings were only a preliminary outburst. The
wounded were to be removed from off the battle-field, as the tide of
war surged forward and left its _débris_ behind. Right energetically
did Saarbrück rise to the occasion. I do not believe there was a man or
a woman or a boy or a girl in all the town who did not help to carry
wounded soldiers off the field. The medical arrangements were decidedly
defective. No such sudden call on the services of the department had
been anticipated, and the staff both of bearers and of surgeons was far
from strong enough, nor was there enough of hospital accommodation. The
first and the last wants Saarbrück did its best to supply. Into every
room of every house, into every shed and outbuilding, were carried
wounded men. My hotel was full of them, from the _salle à manger_
to the fortress in which Boots cleaned his knives and brushed the
clothes. Fifteen poor fellows lay all night on straw in an outhouse
without medical aid. In the morning three of them were dead. On Sunday
began the transport by railway into the interior of such as could be
moved, this being a wise provision for keeping the frontier hospitals
available for the wounded in future actions; 2,000 were so sent away
on the Sunday and Monday, both Prussian and French, their destinations
being the large towns all along the Rhine. As I left Saarbrück the
procession to the station was unbroken of litters and hand-ambulances,
of pale men with arms in slings, or limping, supported by a couple of
sympathisers. One I saw pass in a wheelbarrow.

CHAPTER III.

COURCELLES, SPICHEREN, AND GRAVELOTTE.


If one lingered long in Saarbrück, it became apparent, so rapid was
the advance, that he might find himself out of the hunt altogether.
Indeed, when I quitted Saarbrück on the afternoon of Tuesday, the 9th
of August, I was already in the rear of most of the army of Steinmetz,
and behind, likewise, no small proportion of the vast host of Prince
Frederick Charles. Instead of tramping to Forbach by the road, I took a
final walk over the scene of the battle-field of the Saturday.

By Monday at mid-day all the wounded had been removed and most of the
dead buried. The whole of the ground was thickly dotted over with
graves. The burial-parties had been pious in those their ministrations
to the dead. Few of the mounds but were surmounted with a rude cross,
made of a couple of sticks lashed together with a buckle strap. Others
were more elaborate, the cross board being flat and having inscribed
upon it the number interred, their rank and regiment. Already the
townspeople had decorated some of the graves with shrubs and flowers,
but most of the decorations consisted of bayonets and swords stuck in
the soil—tokens that soldiers lay below. Friend and foe slept at peace
with each other in one grave. The inscription over one of the largest
on the first Spicheren height I thought worth copying:—

 “Hier ruhen in Gott
 29 Preussen und 69 Franzosen.”

I walked some distance before I came to an unburied body, but in the
little clefts and gulleys leading up to the second Spicheren height
they still lay quite thick. There was no difficulty in discovering
their vicinity. The corpse of a man slain in battle does not lie
for three days exposed to the air and sun without lapsing into
decomposition, and some of the bodies were horrible sights. Some
could hardly have been known for human—so black and turgid were the
features. The deaths had chiefly been from rifle bullets. Most of the
Germans bore their death-wounds in their faces and heads, received
as they stormed the terrible ascent; the Frenchmen—those who had not
perished by the bayonet—were for the most part shot through the body,
or had their legs shattered. One scene I never can forget. By the
foot of a tree lay two dead German soldiers. It seemed as if they had
been removed thither either during or after the fight, while as yet
only wounded. Beside them were two pannikins half full of water, and
a piece of bread. One, quite a lad, lay in a crouching attitude, his
head bent downwards toward the tree. He had been shot through the
head, and the blood had streamed on to his handkerchief and the open
pocket-book in front of him. One hand grasped that of his dead comrade,
in the other was an envelope. The letter it contained may have blown
away, or the lad, in his last agony, may have thrust it in his breast;
but the envelope itself was eloquent enough. It was addressed in a
female handwriting, and bore the postmark of a little village away
near the shores of the Baltic. The writer—mother or sweetheart, I know
not—evidently had been the last thought of the lad. Will she come to
weep over his grave among the clay-stone crags of the Spicheren? Will
he, indeed, have a grave at all? The place was a lonely one, and the
straggling cowherd may, months after, have chanced upon a couple of
skeletons with arms and belts on the bones.

What a distance over the height had stretched the battle, I never
