yet free from Prince Frederick Charles’s éclaireurs. By leaving one of
his corps d’armée in position about Amanvilliers and St. Privat with
orders to hold its ground as long as possible, and make a demonstration
as if with intent to use the northern road by Brie, falling back under
the guns of Plappeville when it could keep up the delusion no longer,
he would probably have gained a day’s start before the Germans would
have found out that his whole army was not still standing before them.
But the risks of such an attempt are apparent when it is noticed that
Prince Frederick Charles, had he been beguiled into spending the whole
of the 18th fighting with a single French army corps, would on the 19th
have still by the Brie road have been equidistant from Longuion with
Bazaine on his more circuitous route, supposing that the latter had
made all possible use of his start. If we consider that Bazaine, not
so much by his own fault as by the rottenness of the French system, or
rather want of system, of obtaining intelligence, had no conception
of the strength opposed to him, but on the contrary believed that on
the 16th he had fought all the German troops that were available for
the 18th, an additional reason is found for the line of action on
which he determined, nor is there any occasion to inquire into the
truth of the allegation that he felt himself not justified in quitting
Metz in its then condition of imperfect defence. Abuse him for what
we may, we cannot deny him the selection of a fine position on which
to stand the impact of an attack in force. Its natural strength was
worth to him at least an additional Army Corps. And he had strengthened
the fortress with judicious outworks in the shape of strongly-held
and carefully-fortified posts at St. Marie-aux-Chênes, Vernéville,
Gravelotte, and in the Bois des Vaux. His whole front was studded with
rifle-pits.[2]

Von Moltke and Prince Frederick Charles had imperfect knowledge of the
dispositions just detailed. They could not assure themselves whether
Bazaine had elected to stand and fight upon his ridges, or whether he
would not rather dash out upon the northern road, offering battle as
he came. It was necessary, therefore, to make dispositions to meet
both contingencies; and since the latter contingency was the likelier,
and, if it occurred, demanded the prompter dealing with, it behoved
the German leaders to commence the game with special reference to that
eventuality. The southern road was first of all to be securely blocked.
That task was assigned to the 7th and 8th Corps of the 1st Army, the
former lying in the western portion of the Bois de Vaux and the
eastern portion of the Bois des Ognons, standing in fine due south from
Gravelotte; the latter, touching the left of the 7th Corps, occupied
the western part of the Bois des Ognons, the Bois de St. Arnould, and
the Bois de Vionville. It stood south of Rezonville. Besides caring
for the southern road, and occupying a useful position in view of a
necessity on the part of the enemy for falling back on Metz, these two
corps served as a kind of stationary anchor for the 2nd Army to sway
from, or perhaps it would be a better illustration were I to liken
them to the fixed pivot on which it was to swing round should events
justify the movement. The 2nd Army had its front in a north-easterly
direction, parallel with the line of the northern road, which was
its first objective point, having relation to the contingency that
Bazaine would attempt to march off thereby. This front was composed of,
reckoning from right to left, first, the 9th Army Corps, which touched
von Göben’s left, and with the direction given it on Caulre farm;
second, the Guards, direction Doncourt; third, the 12th Army Corps
(Royal Saxons), direction Jarny. The second line was composed of the
troops that had been so severely mauled on the 16th, viz., the 3rd and
10th Corps. The 4th Corps was on its way toward Toul; the 2nd Corps was
not up, having only quitted Pont-à-Mousson early on the morning of the
battle.

King Wilhelm had been on the field on the forenoon of the 17th, and
before returning to Pont-à-Mousson, he had issued his formal orders
for the morrow. Wellington himself could not have been more laconic
or less devout. “The 2nd Army,” so wrote Moltke in his master’s name,
“will, early in the morning, at 5 o’clock, commence operations and
advance in échelon between Ville-sur-Iron and Rezonville. The 8th
Army Corps will co-operate in this movement with the 2nd Army on its
right. The 7th Army Corps will at first have the duty of covering
the movement of the 2nd Army against any demonstrations on the part
of the enemy from the side of Metz. The King’s further orders will
depend on the action taken by the enemy. Communications for his Majesty
will next find him on the height south of Flavigny.” Prince Frederick
Charles, quitting Gorze, quartered himself for the night between the
17th and 18th in Buxières. He was stirring early. What a subject for
a painter that morning gathering of the leaders of army corps by the
poplar-trees between Vionville and Mars-la-Tour, with the Red Prince
in their midst, brusque, curt, and emphatic! Around the group, conning
over a new slaughter, lay the ghastly evidences of a past. Mild-eyed,
handsome-faced Prince of Saxony; puffy, phlegmatic, _gros_ August
of Würtemburg; Alvensleben the aristocrat, with his thin clear-cut
features and bright hawk eye; Voights-Rhetz, with the shrewd, keen
look of a lowland Scot; Manstein, grim, grey, and determined; Göben,
of the massive head and thoughtful brow; these stand, in a roughly
defined circle, with their horses’ heads turned inward; and there
addresses them in a few short crisp sentences, the tall square man on
the powerful bay; he lets his hand drop on his thigh with an audible
thud, for he is very heavy-handed in every sense, this stalwart man
with the massive hair-clad jaw, the strong wide mouth, cruel in its set
resoluteness when the features are at rest, the well-opened piercing
eye under the splendid forehead, which, broad, square, and knotted
above the eyebrows, soars inexplicably away into calm benevolence. A
man this, in the tight red tunic, cast surely by nature in her special
mould for a great military leader. You might think with yourself, as
you looked at him, that you could not fancy him as a friend; look
again, and I dare swear you will fancy him far less as an enemy. Nor is
the Red Prince without friends, warm and jealous; if the women in the
French villages use his name as the Scotch peasants of old did the name
of the Black Douglas, to hush their babes wherewithal into awe-stricken
silence, his army would die for him to the last man, and I have seen a
pretty picture of bonny children climbing in loving trustfulness about
the knees of the heavy-browed and heavy-handed man. He does not detain
his generals long under the poplar-trees. One of them afterwards gave
me his laconic parting words, “Your duty is to march forward, find the
enemy, prevent his escape, and fight him wherever you find him.” And
Alvensleben the pious added in his quiet tones, “In the name of God,”
as the generals wheeled their horses outwards, and the little council
scattered.

Modern battle-fields are not like cockpits. Were that a real battle
which is ordinarily fought on Easter Monday on the Brighton Downs,
the spectators, who squat and lunch placidly on the Red-hill, would
find that commanding elevation very hot quarters. Generals, with the
perversity of human nature, do not make the convenience of spectators
the primary consideration in their dispositions, nor has any means yet
been discovered to enable the spectators to circumvent the inattention
to their convenience on the part of the military authorities, by being
in more than one place at the same time. The science of expeditious
and comprehensive war intelligence is still in its infancy. If the
millennium does not intervene through the zealous efforts of the
Peace Society, we may anticipate gigantic strides in this department
of catering for the avidity of the public, culminating perhaps in
opposition-balloons with a war correspondent in each hanging in
the air over the centre of the battle-field, and having a “special
wire,” per favour of Mr. Scudamore, leading direct into each Foreign
Editor’s room. But such appliances are for posterity. In the late
war, especially towards its commencement, the civilian spectator who
was not fortunate enough to be attached to a head-quarter staff (and
there were only three, I believe, to whom this well-deserved favour
was accorded) had to be thankful for very small mercies indeed. I
thought myself extremely lucky in not being interfered with when I
took up what seemed an eligible position at the edge of the wood of
St. Arnould, to the south of Rezonville. The forest land here throws
itself up into a little elevation, affording some command of the
rolling plateau to the north-eastward, with Gravelotte on its bosom,
the ridge of St. Hubert and Point du Jour beyond, and away to the north
on the horizon the bushy uplands of Vernéville, with the church spire
showing above the trees; yet here, as it was not difficult to discern
even at the time, and far more easy afterwards from sad _indiciæ_, it
was possible to witness only one phase of the battle, and that neither
the most critical nor the most decisive. I propose to narrate first
the occurrences of which I was the eye-witness here on the southern
edge of the field, and, having done so, to give then a short account
of the more northern operations, from the official documents and other
information I received afterwards from the Saxons and Guards, to whose
share fell so much of the heavy fighting on the German left and left
centre.

By half-past ten o’clock the 9th Corps was no longer visible on the
plateau about Vionville. It had gone off on its northern march, and
its place was occupied by the 3rd Corps, which swarmed all over the
gentle swells and depressions. On the breast of the higher ground
south of Flavigny there was a stationary group with led horses and
mounted escort behind, the dead of the 16th flecking the ground all
around. Orderlies and aides-de-camp galloped furiously from all points
to this stationary group—galloped furiously from it north, east, and
south. For here was the King; and here was von Moltke, with all the
ends of that seemingly-tangled skein gathered inside that marvellous
brain of his, behind the downcast inscrutable eyes. The 8th Corps, on
the fringe of which I stood, was showing itself out of the wood, and
feeling its way with no great decision apparently on to Rezonville,
whence ran the straight chaussée eastward into Gravelotte, the
southern outwork of the French front. There was little or no firing.
Now and then came a shell from the French batteries about Gravelotte,
tentatively as it seemed, to experiment as to the range, but the rare
reports only served to accentuate the lurid lull before the breaking
of the storm. The clouds presently clashed together, and the thunder
rattled out, coming down clear and booming on the wind from the
bosky coverts and the church spire of Vernéville. Nothing was to be
seen save the bursting of an occasional shell on the slope, but the
bickering volleys of musketry, giving the treble to the deep bass of
the artillery, told in language that could not be misinterpreted how a
hot and close fight must be going on out of our sight to the north. It
was about half-past eleven when the air first began to throb with this
din. Now it became apparent why the 8th Corps had been cropping out on
the plateau toward Rezonville. By mid-day the heads of the columns of
Weltzien’s division (the 15th) were pushing on north-easterly towards
Malmaison and the Bois de Genivaux, to take up the alignment with the
fighting further north. Barneckow’s division (the 16th), on the right
of the 15th, was pushing across the plain toward Gravelotte, in dense
broad columns, already half-deployed into line. Now we in the south
became familiar with artillery fire through another sense than that of
hearing. The German batteries went to action from Bagnieux southward
to the edge of the Bois des Ognons, pouring their fire into Malmaison,
the wood of Genivaux, and the village of Gravelotte. Nor were the
French slow to reply. It seemed as if the wood were one huge crater,
which incessantly vomited projectiles that tore through the air and
burst in the German ranks, strewing the plain with fallen men who were
left behind unheeded as the columns pressed ever on. Gravelotte, too,
was girt about with its zone of cannon and mitrailleuses, plied with
assiduous industry by gunners who must have known what of advantage
to the cause of France rested in their successful maintenance of the
advanced position. The cannonade for long was a drawn struggle, neither
side gaining much material advantage. But the slaughter of it was
terrible, and the Germans out in the open must have lost the most
heavily. How or when Gravelotte was at length taken I could not tell.
There was a gradual collapse in the French artillery fire, a ragged
deployment of their infantrymen in front of the village, a rush of
Barneckow’s men at the double, and a hellish mêlée of fighting that
seemed as if it would last for ever. On the deadly furnace of battle
the Germans perpetually piled fresh fuel. The Bois des Ognons emptied
itself on to the plain into a fearful plunging fire from the French
batteries on the heights of Point du Jour. The regiments struggled on
through it to precipitate themselves on Gravelotte; yet so fearful was
the shell-storm that beat on the plain to the south of Gravelotte,
between it and the woods, that the Germans had to swerve out from under
it somewhat, making a circuit so as to fall upon the village from the
west instead of the south. These were von Zastrow’s soldiers of the 7th
Corps. The Barneckows—whether it was they or the former who carried the
village I know not—had slanted away further to the north-east toward
the Bois de Genivaux, roughly aligning on its right with Weltzien’s
division. The French made a desperate stand in the wood, while by this
time their batteries at the Point du Jour were firing furiously into
Gravelotte—indication that it was no longer in French hands. I was told
afterwards that the hither edge of the Bois des Genivaux was carried by
von Göben’s soldiers at the point of the bayonet, and that they had to
push the French out of the wood by sheer bodily weight. Thus much was
not apparent from my position, although I had improved it by coming up
nearer the front; but there was no difficulty in discerning that the
fighting was desperate and protracted. All the while the din was to be
heard from the north out of the range of vision, but it seemed as if it
were now further back, whence the hope became common that the Germans
were faring somewhat better in the north than fortune suffered them to
do in the south.

Yet in the south there were gleams presaging a victory to achieve
which much had yet to be done. Von Göben had seemingly gained the
wood, gaining thereby no respite from the persistent firing of the
batteries at St. Hubert and Point du Jour. Von Zastrow, with intent
as it appeared to give his friends of the 8th Corps some relief from
this, had clothed the neighbourhood of Gravelotte, as soon as it was
in German hands, with four batteries from Kamecke’s Division, but the
reply to them was so strenuous that it seemed surprising how they were
able to keep their ground, while the continuous explosion of shells in
and about the Bois des Genivaux gave evidence that the 8th were still
in purgatory. As it seemed in bitter chafing at their sufferings,
ever and anon fierce attempts were made to crush the causes of them.
Dimly and vaguely only could the movements be discerned from where
I stood, but large detachments must have burst out of the wood, and
pushing down into the dip where was the bed of the Mance, breasted the
opposite slope, and debouched against Moscow and St. Hubert. Through
the glass I could see the broken battalions as they clambered piecemeal
out of the hollow, and tumbling together into approximate formation
strove to push on up the gentler slope. Then there came heavier smoke
banks on their right flank above the batteries at Point du Jour; the
batteries of mitrailleuses at St. Hubert raged like wild cats right in
their faces; the French infantry men, in position between St. Hubert
and Moscow, fringed their ranks with spurting white smoke from their
chassepots. It seemed as if, while a portion of the German attack fell
back, the other moiety had lain down to get some cover and to bide the
spending of the storm. True they had lain down, those soldiers who
did not retreat; but it was not for them more to rear themselves into
dressed ranks. It was for them to lie deeper yet, when the time should
come, under the sod on which they were now prostrate. Effort after
effort met with a similar fate, and at length the bootless and costly
efforts were suspended. Von Zastrow had strengthened his artillery by
Gravelotte, till, as I afterwards learned, he had fifty-four guns in
position there, under whose pounding the French fire at Point du Jour
somewhat slackened, giving at length some relief to the 8th Corps in
the wood to the northward. Zastrow boldly pushed infantry forward
in support of his artillery, which to say the truth seemed to have
rather incautiously worked themselves out to the front, so as to be in
danger should the French essay a rapid and energetic push from Point
du Jour across the ravine. Not content with this, it was clear that
he had determined on beginning the offensive on his own account. This
act of Zastrow’s is the war incident of Gravelotte which has been most
canvassed and controverted. It unquestionably resulted in disaster. As
unquestionably it was done in the face of his positive orders to hold
his own, and act as the stationary pivot for the corps to north of him.
And it is certain, too, that Zastrow acted on his own responsibility
in what he did, and in virtue of a certain far from narrow discretion
which the German military policy allows to officers of any rank when
placed in a position of self-reliance. Zastrow, it appears, had
little to guide him as to the state of matters in the north. The wind
had shifted as some say, and blew away from him the sound of the
cannonade by Amanvilliers, Montigny, and Champenoise. As others have
it, there was an actual lull in the firing soon after three o’clock,
about the positions named. It is urged in favour of Zastrow that this
inaudibility of the northern cannonade, from whatever cause, gave him
some right to conclude that the French centre and right had been thrown
back, and that the strenuous defence on the left was all that was
between them and complete defeat. If these his premises had been sound,
there is no doubt that Zastrow’s conclusion, and the action he based
upon it, were eminently creditable to his generalship. Had the French
right and centre really been pushed back while the left maintained
its ground, it is plain that Bazaine would have proceeded at once
to strengthen the latter with the troops driven back on the former,
since he could afford to disregard the progress of the German left and
centre, sure ultimately to be brought up all standing on the great
rear-buttress before which the French fought—the fortress of Metz. This
in fact was what Bazaine did some hours afterwards when the Saxons
had turned his right and the Guards had smashed his centre. Zastrow,
arguing accordingly, as it seems, judged that it would be good strategy
to anticipate any such strengthening for the French left by attacking
it at once, and forcing it to participate in the retrograde movement to
which he had concluded the centre and right were abandoned. Nay more;
a decisive move on his part on the extreme French left might let him
get through to their rear communications, and once in possession of the
throat of the great chaussée about Jussy, he blocked the chief line of
French retreat, while the 8th Corps, relieved from terrible torment by
St. Hubert and the Point du Jour, might debouch from the wood on to
the plateau, and take its position in the final crushing of the French
front.

How Von Zastrow reasoned I do not know; but he acted as if he had
reasoned in the groove I have indicated. A sanguine man, he had not
counted the cost of success, even if he should gain it; he had not,
indeed, sufficiently studied the question whether success at all were
possible. He shot his bolt, a self-reliant and over-confident archer,
and he failed even to get the distance of the target. It was close
on four o’clock when the offensive movement became perceptible. From
beside Gravelotte one brigade (the 25th, of Glümer’s corps) streamed
down into the ravine inclining south-easterly, and the slackened French
fire at Point du Jour kindling up upon them as they moved. Kamecke’s
division, which was already astride of the ravine where it penetrates
the Bois de Vaux, contributed the 28th brigade, which could be seen
pressing up to debouch on to the plateau of St. Hubert, where the
skirting wood ceases nearly due south of Point du Jour. The French
seemed to catch at the significance of the movement instantaneously as
it was begun: their infantry, rushing to the left through the poplar
trees on the chaussée, formed across the southern plateau, so as to
flank as well as front the Germans when they should come staggering up
on to this new battle-field. More batteries were dashed hurriedly into
position and got into fire. Those previously there swung their trails
round to the north somewhat, and their muzzles thus looked the way the
German brigades were toiling upwards. High up as were the French, the
depth of the ravine afforded the Germans miserably little cover from
their fire. For the French gunners could almost see into its bed; and
although the inverse rise could not be reached by a direct fire, they
yet let fall upon it a plunging fire which, from the steepness of the
slope, was much more destructive than plunging fires for the most part
are. The loss must have been terrible in those ranks while they were
struggling on to the debouchment. At length the edge of the wood and
the lip of the ravine was gained. You could see groups and isolated
men straggling out into the open and falling into formation in that
momentary confusion which always attends such a reorganisation. Just
then cannon, mitrailleuse, infantry fire, as if by signal, burst into
one fell combination of outpour. Had the formation been an inanimate
structure, it must have been battered down by sheer weight of metal;
how much less then could it stand, made as it was of sentient men!
There were efforts to make head against it nevertheless; detached
rushes of rash men who were not to be convinced of the impossible till
overtaken by arguments which crushed conviction in, while they crushed
motion and life out. It was piteous to watch the efforts. I have seen
sheep swept away in a spate swirling down a Highland glen—watched their
struggles as they caught foothold on a scaur, where the rushing water
shallowed, to get out of the vortex on to the firm land on the bank
that seemed so absurdly close; how the water kelpie seized them again
as they strove, whipped them remorselessly off their legs, and sent
them down stream again battling impotently, as they span in the eddies,
or were hurried down the rapids. The sight recurred to my recollection
as I watched those soldiers struggling grimly for foothold on the edge
of that deadly plateau. They would not give it up. Ever as one attempt
was beaten back and crushed down, there were men pushing staunchly
on to make another. It seemed such a little way, such a very little
way, up to the poplar trees there and to victory; surely, with the
goal so nigh, it was not to be thought of that it should be pronounced
inaccessible. A thief might as well have tried to steal the Koh-i-Noor
from its case in the Exhibition, as these men have persisted in their
struggles to get to those poplar trees through the storm of iron and
lead, the serried ranks, the densely lined rifle-pits, which were its
protecting show-case. “_Mörderisch_” was how one that had looked into
the eye of the fire till it beat him down, described that fire to me
the same night—a quiet, unemotional, blood-depleted Feldwebel, who had
been brought into Rezonville that the _Stabsarzt_ might judge whether
it was worth while tinkering at a man who had six bullet-holes in him.
Von Zastrow’s heart must have bled as he watched his Kinder going down
by sections. I know not whether an order came that the brigades should
give up the useless persistence in courting annihilation, or whether
they drew back in realisation of the insanity of such persistence. They
did abandon it, and shrunk back off the plateau, lying down on the
inverse bank of the ravine, screened from the enemy’s direct fire, but
exposed to the plunging fire, and raked by French infantry detachments
that had pushed forward to the brink of the ravine, further northward,
opposite St. Hubert. The batteries whose duty it was to support the two
infantry brigades had to seek the plateau by the only route practicable
for wheels—the chaussée crossing the ravine by deep cuttings and steep
fall and rise. They got down, as it appeared, into the ravine. We saw
them wheel off into the road and clatter down the Gravelotte slope at
a trot. Behind them, at a swinging canter to overtake the guns, dashed
a noble regiment of Uhlans. How gallantly the horsemen rode on their
way to death! Artillery and cavalry disappeared into the ravine. Was it
bottomless, then, that they never came out? The French infantry fire
spurted out of the rifle-pits on the further brink; several batteries
of mitrailleuses, in regularly constructed emplacements, took to
grinding sedulously their cursed output; the cannon further back on
the Point du Jour made practice over the heads of the mitrailleuse men
and the sharpshooters, down into the bosom of the gorge. The German
artillerymen did, as it appeared, get into action, with the muzzles
of their pieces sticking up into the air, but guns were dismounted,
and the gun detachments had to be reinforced over and over again, from
the reserves in the rear by Gravelotte. As for the Uhlans, the morrow
furnished a chart of their doings—a chart on which the courses were
traced in by dead men and horses. They had striven to charge up the
inverse slope of the chaussée on to the plateau of St. Hubert, and
they had failed. Men and horses, not many,—it must have been a mad
forlorn hope of reckless men,—lay on the roadway in the gutters, just
on the brink of the plateau; there were none further, but very many
all the way behind. If ever horsemen were wantonly butchered by being
sent where it was staringly impossible they could have been of service
unless their horses had been winged, those Uhlans were. I believe that,
after losing nearly half their number, they were got down the narrow
bed of the Mance, somewhere behind the position where the two infantry
brigades were lying. They might as well have been in the moon.

Except an effort to cover with infantry-fire in some measure the
batteries in the ravine, by the sending forward a couple of battalions
of the infantry brigade that had been kept in reserve, an interim
paralysis was forced upon the 1st Army. The firing still continued,
both artillery and musketry, quickening now without apparent cause,
now falling away for a little into a dropping fire; but of offensive
movements on either side there were none. The game had seemingly ended
in a stale-mate. Von Zastrow had little or nothing in hand, for, as it
appeared, he had sent a brigade away on a fanciful excursion through
the Bois de Vaux with a wide circuit to come in from the south upon
Jussy and outflank the extreme French left; a rash project, which,
the direct attack having failed, whose success must surely have
been assumed in devising the flank march, ought to have resulted in
the annihilation of the detached brigade, if the French had been as
enterprising as they were stubborn. Von Göben was jammed in the Bois
des Genivaux so long as the French fire from Moscow, St. Hubert, and
the Point du Jour could sweep him off the onward path. The 1st Army was
clubbed. Von Zastrow’s intended _coup de grâce_ was arrested; in his
haste not to be too late he had raised his arm too soon, and had spent
himself prematurely.

The Germans invaded France with armies whose numbers it is easy
enough to utter glibly off the tongue, but whose magnitude it is very
difficult adequately to realise. How many Britons have ever seen
30,000 armed men in a mass? We go to an Easter Monday review, and
see some 26,000 men march past a saluting point, and come home in
the notion that we have been looking at an uncommonly large body of
troops. Let our Easter Monday Volunteer Army stand as an equivalent
to a German army corps. The former takes about an hour and a half to
defile past the commander. The Germans had fourteen of the latter
in France to make war wherewithal, besides Landwehr divisions, a
Würtemburger contingent, and fortress artillerymen, equal, on a
moderate estimate, to two army corps more. At our Brighton pace a
whole day, just twenty-four hours, would be consumed in the marching
past of this half-million of men. There are obvious advantages in
war-making when your numbers are so large that you do not require to
stand particular about the absence or presence of an odd army corps
or two, when you can comfortably leave one as a force of observation,
dispatch another on a distinct measure of precaution, and reckon with
tolerable confidence on yet a third dropping in to help you finish off
your work with the greater thoroughness. This was the happy condition
of the Germans at Gravelotte. If you care to join in the French cry,
“Over-matched by two to one!” the Germans grimly meet you with the
rejoinder, “Why be overmatched then? or, if you cannot help yourselves,
why fly in the face of all experience of war, and roar ‘À Berlin!’ at
the head of your scantier levies?” War is not a game like a school-boy
boxing-match, where the spirit of fair play insists that there shall
be some approximation to an even match between the two combatants.
And even the school-green instinct of fair play does not stand in
the way of allowing the stronger boy to put forth his whole strength
when the weaker boy denies his weakness and is the challenger. The
opportunely arriving German army corps on the 18th of August was the
2nd, whom Fransecki had marched without a halt from Pont-à-Mousson to
Buxières on the southern rear of Mars-la-Tour. There, as it seems, the
Pomeranians had cooked and eaten, and, as giants refreshed, had come
on to Rezonville, eager to have a share in the day’s amusement. Thence
the King sent them into Gravelotte, to help Zastrow to a solution of
the problem in which he had got mystified. It was a strange, grand
sight to watch these fresh and splendid soldiers marching on in a
stream that seemed endless. Nobody knew, within such ken as we had,
whence the wonderful windfall had come. Some swore it was the 5th
Corps from the Crown Prince of Prussia’s army, that there was General
von Kirschbach himself on the grey horse, and that the Crown Prince
was in colloquy with his father at Rezonville. Others had it that it
was the 4th Corps which had thus turned up promiscuously. But be it
what it might, sure it was it did not dally by the way. The leading
regiments formed in front of Gravelotte, others went north-easterly, so
as to touch von Göben’s flank in the Bois des Genivaux. By this time
it was seven o’clock, and the setting sun going down in blood,—blood
in the sky as on earth,—cast his ruddy rays slantingly on the lurid
smoke-banks that overhung the battle-field, and battled with the yet
redder-forked tongues of fire which were bursting out of the burning
farm-buildings of Malmaison, where the wounded Frenchmen were being
roasted alive in miserable helplessness. There was a general advance.
The Zastrows straightened themselves up in their layer in the ravine,
and once more essayed to get a grip of the ground on its brink.
Fransecki’s leading brigade took the chaussée and the easier dells of
the ravine towards the Bois des Genivaux. The 8th Corps pressed the
branches aside and sprang out of the wood into the open, heading for
Moscow. Behind, all along the rear of the grand movement in which some
60,000 men were concerned, the artillery of the three corps shelled
incessantly the French positions. The scene in the deepening twilight
was inexpressibly splendid—with a splendour, however, that was baleful,
and made the flesh creep and the blood turn. Right gallantly did the
French stand up against the huge living wave. Their army was fighting
not for the feeblest chance of victory: hardly any conceivable freak
of the captious goddess could have given it that. But it was fighting
for something yet more critical than victory,—for its very life; for
if the Point du Jour were carried by the Germans, then von Zastrow’s
aspirations would have tardily yet surely been accomplished. The
beaten centre and right would have had their line of retreat invaded;
the defeat would have become a chaotic slaughter till the broken and
bewildered battalions, barred from the chief line of retreat, had
huddled somehow close under the guns of Plappeville and St. Quentin.
The men who stood at Point du Jour, St. Hubert, and Moscow had it in
their keeping to avert this terrible fate—to break the fall, all too
dislocating already, of their brethren of the centre and right. Nobly
they did their devoir. In numbers they could have been but slender as
compared with their assailants; but they had a position, the merits
of which were worth many men, and they were worthy of the position.
The night fell, and still the bright streaks of flame shot westward
from the batteries at the Point du Jour, evidence that French guns and
gunners still stood there. Gradually the tongues of flames ceased to
shoot forth, gradually a hush, such a contrast to the din of the day
that it was almost painful, came over the bloody field. The dogs of war
were satiated; they had lapped blood till they might lap no more. And
still the Frenchmen stood by the Point du Jour, keeping at bay the foe
while their friends defiled into safety behind the screen thus sturdily
maintained for them. The 1st Army had fought well and squandered life
recklessly; but spite of the late reinforcements it had to submit to
the failure of its capital enterprise. The foreposts all but touched
each other through the watches of the night. Those of the Germans were
made specially strong, for the day’s work had taught the watch-setters
respect for those men on the crown of the height above them. And what
of them? One knew nothing till the morning broke, and then it was found
that the shattered churchyard-wall, and the smashed and half-burnt
wayside houses, the rifle-pits and the battery emplacements were
silent, save for the groaning of the wounded left behind, and empty,
save of those and of the dead who lay beside them. The French, having
held the position so long as it was of use, had abandoned it, and by
six o’clock Fransecki’s forepost men, creeping cautiously forward to
the crown of the ridge, saw before them the brown scarp of Fort St.
Quentin, with the beaten French host clinging round its base.

The wearied and powder-grimed Germans threw themselves down on the
field, and slept among the dead who littered it. Bivouac fires were
sparse, the men were too spent with hard fighting to care for food
or warmth. They were lighted to their slumbers by ghastly chamber
candlesticks; by the pillar of fire that yet towered heavenward from
the burning Malmaison, and by another fire that showed over the horizon
by Verneville. In the darkness between nine and ten, there burst out
the din of a furious cannonade to the north, and the tired soldiers who
had piled their arms were made to resume them. But soon this in its
turn died away, and the battle-field was left in peace.

Having now recounted what I personally saw of the battle of Gravelotte,
I proceed to give a brief narrative of the occurrences which were
not within the scope of my observation. The position of the several
corps of the 2nd Army has already been detailed. By seven the three
corps forming the 1st line were already on their north-easterly
march in gigantic echelon of army corps. Their advance in this line
was directed, as I have stated, to meet the expected contingency
that Bazaine would attempt the northernmost of the three westerly
routes toward Verdun. The German leaders had as yet no information
warranting them in concluding definitely that this design had been
abandoned, although indications pointed in that direction. These
indications became so marked towards ten o’clock that Prince Frederick
Charles judged it wise at least to arrest the march designed to meet
a contingency, the occurrence of which was improbable; he therefore
halted the Saxons, the flanking corps on the left, at Jarny, and the
Guards at Doncourt; the 9th Corps had some time previously reached
Caulre, and were halted there, cooking their breakfasts. It was not
long before the Prince obtained additional assurance that the French
were contemplating no advance move, but had elected to give him battle
on ground of their own choosing on the plateau of Amanvilliers,
and, as it appears, the King in his southerly position was early in
possession of intelligence which led him to form the same conclusion.
It would appear, however, that neither von Moltke nor Prince Frederick
Charles had any idea that the French right was prolonged so far to
the northward as it was; but on the contrary had the idea that their
right flank rested on the village of Amanvilliers. It was in the light
of this impression that the Saxons got the order to march on Batilly,
where they would be close to Bazaine’s path if he should tardily move
off by the Brie road, while in the absence of any such endeavour, their
course was thought open to make an easterly sweep, and take in flank
the supposed French right in Amanvilliers, while an attack was being
made on that position in front.

The latter attack was entrusted to the 9th Corps, supported by the
Guards. Manstein marched his men off from Caulre about half-past ten,
and headed direct on la Folie by Verneville. It was at the latter place
where he experienced the first resistance from the French holding it
as an outpost position, and as soon as he showed his columns beyond
it, began the furious cannonade, which to us in the south had been the
token that the battle was begun in earnest. He had to fight his way
hard after passing Verneville. To get at the French position he had
to march through the interval between the Bois de la Cusse and the
north end of the Bois des Genivaux. The end of this gap toward Metz is
closed in by Amanvilliers and Montigny-la-Grange; and here the French
were posted in great strength, and indeed took the offensive against
Manstein as he strove to deploy. He was not strong enough to make
much impression on the position. Part of his artillery he got forward
nearly as far as the farm of Champenoise, which was terribly mauled by
his shell-fire, and with a part of Wrangel’s division he occupied the
edge of the Bois de la Cusse fronting toward Amanvilliers, supporting
the infantry by several batteries. The Guards, in the meantime, had
in part received another direction, the result of Prince Frederick
Charles’ appreciation of the fact that the French right extended
further than he had at first reckoned upon. Von Pape marched his first
division over Jouaville to Habonville, on the north of the Bois de la
Cusse, while Budritski, whose first orders were to march his division
(the 1st) on Verneville to support Manstein, had his course presently
altered also upon Habonville. St. Privat was found strongly held by
the French, and as it would have made a gap in the German line had the
Guards gone so far north as to attack it, recourse was had to artillery
practice to drive the French out of it. From a position between St.
Ail and Habonville, Prince Hohenlohe, the General commanding the Guard
artillery, converged on St. Privat the steady fire of no fewer than
fourteen batteries of artillery, or, in other words, of 84 guns. Under
the personal orders of Prince Frederick Charles, who visited that part
of the scene of action between one and two, this pitiless fire was
persevered in for more than an hour, as a means of marking time till
the Saxons should come up on the left; and Prince Hohenlohe was able
gradually to improve the positions of his batteries directed against
St. Privat. But although the Saxons, pressing on from Jarny, were true
to time, it was found that there was other work for them still further
on the left. The French were in force also in St. Marie-au-Chênes,
and before the Saxons could co-operate with Prince August against St.
Privat, it was necessary to sweep this obstacle out of their path, and
also to ascertain exactly how far this French right, ever seemingly
elongating itself further and further, really did extend. Prince George
of Saxony with the 24th Division, supported by a brigade from von
Pape’s left, fell upon St. Marie, while Montbe, with the 24th Saxon
Division, swept round by Auboué and found that at length he was on the
right of the French flank, and had at last got their weather-gauge. It
was half-past three already when the Saxons (23rd Division) had carried
St. Marie, and were ready with the 24th, looking now straight at
Roncourt, to co-operate with the Guards in an attack on the formidable
position of St. Privat. Meanwhile Manstein with his 9th Corps was not
prospering. He had got a grip of the gap with Amanvilliers in its
throat, and of the two woods flanking that gap; but farther he might
not come. Amanvilliers was a position of great natural strength, and
the French had accumulated about it a force of artillery which fairly
coped even with the strong artillery that Manstein had brought into
opposition. Fifteen of his guns were dismounted or otherwise rendered
unserviceable by the French fire.

It was with difficulty that his infantry, by the Chantrenne Farm
and the north-east corner of the Bois des Genivaux, could maintain
themselves against the repeated and furious attacks of the French from
la Folie, raked as they were also by mitrailleuse and artillery fire
from Amanvilliers. On his right, the Hessians in the Bois de la Cusse
were lacerated by a persistent infantry and mitrailleuse fire from the
hamlet of Jerusalem, while the French artillery at St. Privat, firing
over Jerusalem, showered shells upon the wood; and, in short, so far
from being able to pursue the offensive, he had quite his work to hold
his own. But the advantage of practically unlimited reserves told in
his favour. The 3rd Army Corps had been marched on Verneville, and
halted there. Its artillery was available to support Manstein, and it
was used in this way, as also was a portion of its cavalry. Then the
Guards and the Saxons were perfecting their dispositions for storming
St. Privat, and that would relieve his pressure from the left. Already
before five the Guards were distracting the attention of the French
holders of Jerusalem. The Garde Schützen Regiment had spread itself
through the sparsely wooded Bois, and were scrambling forward through
the underbrush. Soon the whole of the 4th Brigade of the Guards was
marching on St. Privat from Habonville, and it was speedily followed by
the bulk of von Pape’s division from St. Privat. The Guards got near
St. Privat, but they could not get into it. It was held by the French
with inveterate stubbornness, and the natural strength of the position
backed their defensive efforts. Prince August of Würtemberg had to hold
his hand, and betake himself to artillery fire till assured of the
co-operation of the Saxons, who were marching southward from Roncourt.
The 10th Corps, too, was summoned close up in reserve, and, indeed, a
part of Kraatz-Koschlau’s division actually took part in the storm. At
half-past six the Guards, standing waiting on the south and west, heard
the music of the Saxon guns from the north, and then the von Papes got
the word to show of what metal were the Prussian Guards. They stormed
furiously into the place; as furiously came Montbe’s Saxons in from the
north. The double attack carried St. Privat; but after the Germans were
in it, the French refused to go out, and had to be turned out, fighting
desperately every step of the way, and causing terrible loss to their
assailants before the operation was accomplished. Thus the French had
their right flank turned, and were forced from the strong position
on which it had rested. They were rolled backward into the woods of
Jaumont and Saulny, but not utterly broken or completely disheartened.
For it seems unquestionable that Bazaine was able to march toward the
maintenance of his left-wing position at Point du Jour not only a
portion of the Imperial Guards who constituted his reserve, and of
whom he had made some, but hardly a sufficient, use during the day, but
also some of the troops who were engaged at St. Privat, and who were
moved under cover of the forced attack made on Manstein’s position from
Amanvilliers, just at nightfall.

How was it faring with Manstein while Prince August was relieving
him of one embarrassment by carrying St. Privat? He, too, had made
an offensive attempt on Amanvilliers simultaneously with von Pape’s
push at St. Privat, sending forward a brigade of the Hessians and a
part of Wrangel’s Division in combination with a detachment of the
Guards detailed from the right of that corps, the whole supported by
the artillery of the 3rd Corps. But even now Manstein could not get at
the heart of the French centre in Amanvilliers, Montigny, La Grange,
nor could his artillery get the dominance over that of the French.
Alvensleben with his 3rd Corps was close up, and prepared to back up
the labouring 9th Corps with the whole of Buddenbrock’s Division.
But Buddenbrock’s men were not to be used just then in an offensive
movement, for as they were coming the French took the initiative in
great force, and hurled large masses of infantry on the Bois des
Genivaux. It is a moot point whether the Imperial Guard participated in
this attack in any strength. Next day, behind la Folie, I saw several
dead Guardsmen, and after the capitulation of Metz an officer of the
corps told me that a whole brigade of the Guards had been in action
about Amanvilliers late in the day. If this was the case, it must
have suffered little in proportion to the line regiments engaged, for
the crop of corpses in Guard uniforms was comparatively very scanty.
This late effort on the part of the French, made in twilight that had
deepened into darkness before it was finally quelled, could scarcely
have had any other object than to mask the retirement of the beaten
right wing. It answered its purpose then not inefficiently, and there
seems no ground for concluding that the Germans succeeded at all that
night in carrying the position of Amanvilliers any more than the
southerly one at Point du Jour. But it must have been tenable only on
sufferance and by the favour of the darkness, and like Point du Jour
it was abandoned in the early morning. The Saxon cavalry, with dashing
enterprise, penetrated the northern forests the night of the battle
and cut the railway and telegraphic communications between Metz and
Thionville. By the following night the ring of Germans around Metz was
well defined.

The battle of Gravelotte was both positively and relatively less bloody
than that of Vionville. With more than double the number of troops
engaged on the former day as compared with the latter, the Germans
lost about 14,000 men; the French probably about the same number.
What force Bazaine had in the field is difficult to estimate. When he
surrendered, his strength, 173,000 men, was as surprising as it was
inexplicable on the estimates to which the Germans had access. It
seems impossible that he could have had fewer than 150,000 men at his
disposal on the 18th of August; and it seems equally impossible that a
man in possession of his senses, even without a scintilla of military
capacity, should not on such an occasion have mustered out to the last
man.

About noon on the 19th, I crossed over the ravine of Gravelotte, where
in the gutters of the chaussée the blood of the Uhlans stood in red
puddles, and pushed on past the ruins of the Auberge of St. Hubert and
up to Point du Jour. The burying parties had hardly begun their work,
the wounded were still lying on the slopes in great numbers. From the
sharp angle formed by the chaussée, where it turns sharp to the south
above St. Hubert, I went forward on the old Roman road over the flat.
The Germans had not lost a minute. Already they were reversing the
front of what the day before had been French battery emplacements and
rifle pits. The replis lay behind entrenchments shaded by boughs of
trees which the French had cut. And the French looked so strangely
near—seemingly just over the way. They were encamped in peace on the
slopes and the summit of Mont St. Quentin, the white _tentes d’abris_
clustering round the fortress, and the smiling villages on the slopes.
The scene was like a gigantic pic-nic or a gipsy camp, with the French
tents beyond, and the Germans lolling in their bowers. There was no
formal truce, but the peace was unbroken. Fort St. Quentin might have
shelled the plateau where lay the Germans—in the morning, indeed, it
had sent two shells into a group of mounted men standing before the
detached house at the sharp turn of the road; but it seemed as if this
had been merely a kind of formal protest against any supposition that
it was not solely of its own will and pleasure that the fortress was
quiet. The assertiveness was too emphatic to have been agreeable to the
two dragoons killed by one of the shells; but the Germans were quite
content to concede what they could not well have challenged, all the
more readily because St. Quentin rested satisfied with the somewhat
grim formality.

For a day or two immediately after Gravelotte there was a good deal
of confusion as regarded several matters in the army which lay on
the battle-field where so many gallant soldiers were yet without
graves. The commissariat arrangements were hardly equal to the strain
put upon them, while the great dearth of water, and the badness of
quality in what was to be found, combined with the already ripe taint
of putrefaction, the heat of the weather, and the revulsion from the
excitement of the battle, to stimulate disease among the soldiers. Men
who had to trust to the field-post for the transmission of letters, had
their tempers sorely tried. On the 19th I consigned a letter to a fussy
but civil functionary who was in charge of a field-post wagon a little
way in front of Rezonville, and who professed his expectancy to start
“_gleich_,” which experience had taught me was a very elastic word. I
found the field-post wagon was still stationary the following morning,
the civil and fussy gentleman cooking at a bivouac fire a diabolical
mess, consisting of fat bacon, onions, biscuits, minced something or
other (it might have been postage stamps), coffee-grounds, and rice,
while his horses placidly slept the sleep of the just as they stood
ready harnessed for the start, even as they had done on the previous
night. Again the courteous official professed his belief that he was
to start _gleich_, although he owned that he had not the remotest idea
what was his destination. In reply to my proposal to take my letter
away and give it to some other less indefinite field-post, he professed
his readiness to give it up, but advised me not to make bad worse. The
ultimate result was that the letter miscarried altogether, and it was
the case that letters which did arrive took longer time in reaching
England from Gravelotte and the vicinity about this period, than they
did from before Paris at a later period of the war.

On the morning of the 20th August I revisited the flat in front of the
hamlet of St. Hubert, on which were stationed the German outposts,
behind the entrenchments which they had been steadily working upon, and
from which, the day before, the French army was visible, lying on the
face of Mont St. Quentin, and around the villages of Scy, Lessy, and
Lorry. To my surprise the French had totally disappeared. On the Friday
the ground was white with their tents, around which swarmed the brisk
gentlemen in the red trousers. Now, on the Saturday, not a tent or a
pair of red trousers was visible. The surly fortification on the top
of Mont St. Quentin frowned across in its solitude, the church-spire
of Lorry smiled in the sunbeam that slanted across it, but the traces
of a great army had as completely disappeared as if the ground had
opened and swallowed up the host. I followed the concavity of the
German semicircular entrenchments round to the left through the corner
of the wood of Chatel, which extends under various names, but in one
continuity, as far as Saulny, which is nearly due north from Metz. The
Germans held the wood, and were entrenching its verge. Thus, within my
own observation, a German belt extended thus early around Metz, from
the Moselle at Moulins, in a sweep first to the west and then to the
north, till it reaches within a mile and a half of the Thionville road.
The troops which lined this belt consisted chiefly of Prince Frederick
Charles’s army. The completion of the ring was taken up by the army
of General von Steinmetz. His district, so to speak, began at the
Thionville road, and swept round the eastern semicircle of environment
to the Moselle below Metz. Of course a space of such a magnitude as
is here indicated could not in so short a time be invested with that
closeness which would have been the case had the siege been simply
that of a fortified town and citadel. But the investment was close
enough for this,—that the army which was inside could not hope to get
outside without fighting a pitched battle. Such a force as that of the
French lying in and around Metz was not like the garrison of a detached
fort, capable of an effort at cutting its way out by a sudden sortie.
It must, as it seemed, break the cordon in form, and with warning
sufficient to admit of a German concentration to oppose the endeavour.

In view of the long and weary inaction which the soldiers of the 1st
and 2nd Armies endured while summer waned into autumn, and autumn had
all but chilled into winter, it may not be uninteresting if I quote the
_ipsissima verba_ of a passage which I find in the notes which I made
on this 20th of August, as calculated to show how, even thus early,
circumstances rendered it possible to forecast the policy of the German
leaders.

“Hitherto the German army has taken the _rôle_ of aggression, and
the French army has stood solely on the defensive. On the part
of the Germans the aggressive tactics hitherto have been at once
politic and characteristic in a national sense. It was necessary as a
feature of the German programme that the French army of Metz should
be got “into chancery,” and that there should be some desperate and
decisive fighting, in which might be demonstrated the impossibility of
resistance to the fury and persistency of the German assaults. Both
these ends have been accomplished. The army of Metz is beleaguered
under its walls, the armies of Steinmetz and Prince Frederick Charles
grimly standing sentry over it. The steep of Gorze, the plains of
Vionville, of Rezonville, and of Gravelotte, the ravine beyond it,
and the plateau of St. Hubert, strewn as they are with German dead,
but left, nevertheless, in the rear of the unrelenting advance of the
German host, bear bloody but unquestionable testimony to the fury and
obstinacy of their attack. Thus the double purpose is attained. Now,
therefore, the German tactics undergo an alteration. The children of
the Fatherland will no longer spill their blood in attacks which would
be purposeless, and therefore wanton; they await in their turn the
assaults of the beleaguered French army. Sooner or later that assault
must come. Want of supplies must precipitate the assumption of the
offensive on the part of the French. But if they can manage to live
till Christmas on the traditional French wealth of resources in the way
of edible matter, it will not accord with German policy to attack them.
So long as they are surrounded as they are now, they are harmless; if
they would break the environment, and become again dangerous, they must
fight for the freedom. The German leaders will not court the affray.
Why should they, since their great object is the maintenance of the
blockade? But since this is their object, it may well be imagined how
they will resist any attempts to raise that blockade.” It afterwards
fell to my lot to be an eye-witness of the manner in which at least one
of those attempts was resisted.

And was this investment of Metz, then, with its result, to all
appearance, postponed indefinitely and uncertain, to be the end of
all the German forced marching after Spicheren; of the rapid push
across Lorraine; of the double battle above Gorze; of the German dead
whose corpses are fertilising the plains of Vionville and Gravelotte;
of the thousands of wounded who cumbered the towns and the villages
all the way in the rear to Cologne and Mannheim? No. While there was
another French army still in the field it was not for the Germans to
linger about the scenes of their victories, and take to Lord Russell’s
policy of “Rest and be thankful.” Prince Frederick Charles and General
Steinmetz were reckoned, together, capable of sitting on the brink of
the French well at Metz, and looking down on the French army in its
depths. The Crown Prince, his army rested and refreshed in la belle
Nancy, was already pushing westward, giving the contemptuous go-by to
the obdurate little Toul. Already we heard rumours that his cavalry
scouts were swarming in the valley of the Marne. About St. Marie,
Amanvilliers, the blood-stained St. Privat and the battered Jerusalem,
the picked soldiers of Germany—the army corps of the Guards, the Royal
Saxons with their lissome dash, and the slower-pulsed Saxon Province
men of the 4th Corps, were making haste to bury their dead, and were
girding up their lines, to form a new—a 4th—army under the command of
the Crown Prince of Saxony, and march westward and northward along the
river from which the army took the name under which it made itself
famous.

In a day or two the hotels of Pont-à-Mousson were to lose the custom
of the princes and counts who were on the royal staff, for the King
was to strike southward _viâ_ Commercy, and joining his son at Ligny,
accompany the 3rd Army in what seemed then was to be the straight, if
not the bloodless road to Paris. What had occurred before Metz was but
the first act of the tragedy, what should occur thenceforth in its
vicinity was in this Titanic drama only “secondary business;” the piece
was to be played on and out nearer and yet nearer to the torn heart of
France.

But enough of what then was the future. On Saturday the 20th I
traversed the whole of the battle-fields of Tuesday and Thursday, and
the outward and visible signs of slaughter more and more convinced me
that the fight of the former day had been unquestionably the fiercer
and the more hotly contested. Let me attempt to give some idea of what
may be termed the area of slaughter in the two days’ fight. Within a
space of about six miles in length by four in depth there was not a
space so large as Piccadilly Circus that did not bear some token that
one or more men had fallen upon it. Now the tokens were dead bodies,
sometimes straggling so that several yards intervened, sometimes so
close that you could hardly step without treading on the dead; again
they consisted of bloody coats, pieces of linen, helmets, cuirasses
with the tell-tale holes in them, knapsacks riddled by bullets, dead
horses all but blown into fragments by the bursting of shells. The
lines of demarcation between the German and the French dead were in
places drawn with almost mathematical accuracy. On one of the roads
leading up the steep slope out of Gorze I came on an outlying part
of the table-land where the Germans lay very thickly. It looked as
if they had advanced in a column along the road, for its margin was
quite bordered with the dead. Here it was where the column had come
up which an American of genius and fancy likened to a great anaconda,
pushing forward with long tortuous folds till a battery opened on its
hinder parts and amputated and all but destroyed its tail. Then I came
to the place where the column had struggled into deployment and had
got within the fatal 200 yards’ distance of the Frenchmen. The latter
lay far thicker then than the Germans had lain in the first space.
Burial parties were at work at this place, but seemed bewildered to
decide where to make a beginning. I advised one of the officers to
double-trench the ground all over, laying the dead along the hollow
of each successive trench, something like a celery bed; so many were
they that this market-gardening style of grave would have been, I
am convinced, the best plan for getting them out of sight; saving
transport to isolated grave-pits, and lessening the temptation to bury
too near to the surface, which is always strong with burial parties
who have excavated with labour a large pit, into which they are
anxious to crowd all the corpses in the neighbourhood, regardless of
the fact that the uppermost are so near the surface that the plough of
the next spring must uncover their remains. The surface of the little
churchyard of Gravelotte had been raised three feet all over in one
day. The church had been crowded with the most severely wounded in the
Thursday’s battle, and as the poor fellows died they had been carried
out into the churchyard. This with its loopholed wall had been the
scene of a desperate stand on the part of the French, and already had
more than its own share of corpses: so that, when I had seen it on the
Friday, the bodies, chiefly of Frenchmen, were lying two deep all over
its edge. The interior of the church was a sight I never can forget.
Most know the ornamental character of these Catholic churches on the
Continent, the meretriciously decorated altar, with the pictures, the
candles, and what not. The white altar-cloth was splashed with blood,
the floor was bedded all over with the wounded, and the atmosphere had
become tainted and malaria-laden. In the churchyard I had noticed the
little wooden memorials over the village dead, with the wreaths of
immortelles around them, sticking up through the stacks of dead. To-day
they were out of sight, covered with the earth that had been thrown
over all as the easiest and quickest method of a wholesale interment.

I have referred to the “fatal 200 yards” of the Germans. Always the
attacking force, as they had hitherto been, their fire was confessedly,
and in the nature of things, wilder than that of a body waiting
in a stationary position for them to come; and it was ever at the
long-range distances that the French did so great execution among them
with the chassepot. As to the question of the comparative accuracy
of the zund-nadelgewehr and the chassepot as weapons of precision at
long range, the Germans did not as yet appear inclined to express an
opinion, yet one might gather even thus early what a terrible respect
they had already learned for the weapon which they afterwards confessed
to be “pale death” itself. But when they got within the 200 yards,
and brought their needle-guns down to the point-blank range, then it
was that their fire was of the “fatal” character I allude to. French
prisoners with whom I spoke told with a shudder of the shower of
bullets from the needle-gun at and within this range, that fell upon
a column about the breasts and faces of the men, and against which
nothing could stand. It is only on such vantage grounds as the top of
the Spicheren or the edge of the ravine above the Gravelotte ravine
that the French stood up for any time against this fire of the Germans.

Before quitting the position before Metz, on Monday, the 22nd August,
I took a final walk along the foreposts on the Gravelotte side. The
entrenchments of the Germans were now nearly complete, and although
rough and ready, were very artistic in their formation. Sentries were
stationed on the brink of the valley lying between Mont St. Quentin
and the Chatel plateau, with strong piquets lying concealed in their
rear. From their position I could see with the glass into the mouths
of the heavy cannon of Fort St. Quentin, which looked strangely close.
Going along a little to the right, in the direction of the Moselle, I
was able to turn, so far as eyesight was concerned, the shoulder of
Mont St. Quentin, and lo! stretched out before me lay the beautiful
plain of Metz, with the river gleaming in the sunshine, the spires
and roofs of the town sparkling brightly, and the gloomy mass of Fort
Bellecroix—the finest work of Cormontaigne—grimly covering the right.
The German cordon, I was assured by a staff officer, was now completely
drawn round Metz, and Thionville was also invested. There was every
appearance that the armies of Prince Frederick Charles and Steinmetz
had quietly settled themselves down in their position. Crimean men have
been wont to assert that the French were the best constructors of huts
in the world, but I question whether the Germans do not excel them. All
along these tablelands they had built themselves dainty little leafy
habitations out of the branches and bark of the poplar trees that line
the roads, and seemed as comfortable as if they were at home in their
cottages in the Fatherland. As I passed one of the regiments its band,
standing in a circle, was practising one of Mendelssohn’s masterpieces,
the men enjoying the strains _sub tegmine fagi_, while all around the
burying parties were engaged in burying not only their own but their
enemy’s dead out of their sight.

On returning to Pont-à-Mousson, from my first start to Thiaucourt, I
had determined to walk no longer, but at once to invest in a horse, if
one was to be procured in the town. This at first seemed doubtful, for
the Germans had made a very exhaustive levy, but ultimately I chanced
on a horse-dealer whose stud had not quite been swept away. He was one
of the most marvellously stolid men I have ever seen, half German, half
French; the blended blood seemed wholly to have stagnated in his veins.
Almost the only sign of animation he evinced was a wink, but then that
wink was remarkably expressive. I was fortunate. He had an admirable
horse in his stable, sure-footed, fast, easily kept, and in fact a
compendium of equine virtues. But—bother the buts, they always crop up
in such cases—this paragon of animals had one little drawback, he was
stone-blind. Now there sometimes are advantages connected with a blind
horse—that is, if he is a reasonable animal otherwise. He trusts his
rider and will go straightforward courageously, till the rein gives a
hint to turn to the right or the left. There is no fear of his shying,
because to him there are no objects of alarm. He commonly has good
action and a light mouth. In view of all this I directed the blind
horse to be saddled and brought forth. I mounted and set him a-going.

It is cruel to be hard on the misfortunes of a fellow-creature, but
this horse must have had a mental organisation as opaque as the pupils
of his eyeballs. Three steps in any one direction were enough for
him, then he tried another, and so continued this process, totally
insensible to any suggestions on the part of his rider. He ran his
nose against a house, and then he all but fell over a plough; finally,
in one of his tacks, he broke the glass of a general’s carriage, and
brought down a “Donner wetter” or two upon my head. Tired as I was
of walking, I became fully impressed with the fact that it would be
cheaper to walk ten miles than to ride the blind horse one, even if
such a feat were possible, and, dismounting, I led him back somewhat
ignobly to the stable. The horse-dealer winked solemnly, bestowed a
caress on the abominable brute, and expressed his astonishment that I
should not have found him all that could be desired. The excuse was,
that he had always been driven alongside of another beast, and the
deprivation of his companion had demoralised him. But I was bidden be
of good cheer, the horse-dealer was still assured that he would be
able to accommodate me. After a little delay another steed was brought
forth. He was “long and low”—the true Leicestershire desiderata.
The length was principally in the neck, which resembled that of an
ostrich; the lowness was in the back, which curved downward so that
his withers and rump stood up like two sharply-pronounced hills. His
colour was a dirty-white; he had capped hocks, a pair of most villanous
recently-broken knees, and a wicked backward cast in his eye that
said very little for his temper. His appearance was certainly not in
his favour, but remembering the adage never to trust to appearances,
and being assured he was a remarkable performer, I mounted and got him
under way. He went decently enough to the end of the street; but when a
battery of artillery came along the cross street at a trot he soon gave
me a sample of his remarkable performances. He declined to go forward
at any price, but instead he was open to kicking as freely as the heart
of man could desire. He kicked the battery into the direst confusion,
wriggling about here, there, and everywhere, continually elevating that
prominent rump of his, and struggling furiously to get his head down. I
thought I should have had to choose a favourable moment and dismount,
leaving him and the battery to settle it between themselves, but at
length I got his head homewards, and then he suddenly subsided, and
walked to his stable as if he had done something to be proud of.

The horse-dealer winked again as I returned a second time, and said he
had still another beast. The past experience was not of a character
to tempt further experiments, but I wanted a horse badly, and I knew
this was my last chance; so I told them to bring him out. He was a
very conscientious-looking quadruped, slightly monumental as to his
legs, but then he had a bold Roman nose, and a tail possessed of what
may be styled the Grecian bend of horsetails. My heart warmed to him
at once, for, like myself, he was an old dragoon. He had been cast
from a French cavalry regiment in some remote age, and had since been
engaged, judging by the collar marks, as a beast of burden. Well, I
started with this animal for a third trial, and to do him justice, his
conduct did not belie his conscientious aspect. He plodded forward
steadily at a kind of compound of a walk, a trot, and a canter; he
seemed to be walking with his fore legs, trotting with one of his
hind legs, and cantering with the other. The effect was novel, if not
wholly agreeable, and then he really got over the ground very fairly.
On the day of Gravelotte he carried me uncommonly well, his ancient
military training making him stand fire like an adjutant’s horse, which
is generally too depressed in spirits to manifest emotion at anything
short of an earthquake. I began to get very sweet on him, although
the scarcity of keep was a considerable inconvenience. There was but
little bother with him on the Thursday night. When I joined the bivouac
I fastened him to the end of a row of cuirassier horses that were
picketed close by, and the men gave him of their oats and hay. But they
went away next morning, the bivouac broke up, and fresh quarters had to
be sought. In one of the houses of the deserted village of Gravelotte I
found a room which was tenanted only by two sorely wounded men and an
hospital attendant. All the other houses were packed full of wounded,
and one was in luck in getting the use of a chair, and of the common
frying-pan in which to cook a piece of bacon bought from a marketender.
But there was no room for the horse, and if there had been, he would
not have been the best company for the wounded men. I tied him up
outside while I ate, wrote, and slept, and then late in the afternoon
it struck me that he must be thirsty. I rode the brute a circuit of
some five miles in search of water, and when I got back it was all but
dark. On entering the room I found one of the wounded men lying dead,
and the other removed along with the hospital man. It was not pleasant
to occupy the same room with the poor dead fellow, and luckily an empty
stable turned up next door with plenty of hay in the loft. Having fed
the horse copiously, I adjourned among the hay to sleep, and in the
morning I found my noble steed was gone! Perhaps he had broken the
fastening, and walked away of his own accord; perhaps a passing suttler
had stolen him. Anyhow he was clear away, leaving me as a memento the
saddle and bridle. These, which were of the nature of a white elephant,
I was glad to get rid of for a box of cigars, and fortunately my
comrade turned up from Pont-à-Mousson with a carriage horse, and driver
that by some inscrutable means he had discovered there unrequisitioned.

CHAPTER IV.

SEDAN: BEFORE AND AFTER.


Having made up our minds to quit the beleaguerment of Metz and strike
off in a southerly direction with intent to hit on the march of the
Crown Prince’s army, we started off rather vaguely on the 22nd of
August, and reached St. Mihiel, a small town on the Meuse, the same
night.

Now it seems hardly to be realised how difficult it was in the times
of which I am writing to get reliable information, or to guard against
being led away by some baseless rumour. I remember we lost the whole of
the 23rd by slanting away to the north-west as far as a village called
Fresnes, by reason of a circumstantial, but untrue story, to the effect
that a battle was imminent under the walls of Verdun. When we quitted
St. Mihiel for Bar-le-Duc on the morning of the 24th we could get no
certain information whether it was as yet safe to go to Bar-le-Duc.
We travelled with a column as far as Villotte, and then the troops
struck away to the north in the direction of Vaubenoit. In answer to
questions at Villotte, it was not to be learned that any German troops
had taken the road to Bar-le-Duc, which lies through that village.
There had been fighting, the villagers lyingly said, somewhere to the
south-west, and the road to Bar-le-Duc ran south-west. Going on upon
chance, we presently came into a deeply-wooded defile, extending for
several miles. Not a German was visible; indeed, nothing human was to
be seen. The place was calculated to make one nervous, especially when
one’s driver had a fixed belief that there was a Frenchman behind every
stump. He was a Luxemburger, and therefore theoretically a neutral, but
I think he sympathised with the _grande nation_.

Possibly no reader has ever practised the art of reconnoitring from an
open carriage, and I may describe the process. You cautiously advance
till you reach an angle; then you steal round it and halt, and sweep
the whole front and flanks with your binocular. Nothing suspicious
is visible, so you trot along the straight till you approach another
turn, when you repeat the cautious tactics. You get along at the rate
of about two miles and a half an hour. If you see anything suspicious,
such as the smoke of a camp fire, you cautiously retire your machine
and make a pedestrian reconnoissance. Of course, with all this caution,
we reached Vaires in perfect safety, and found it full of Bavarian
cavalry, who told me that their army was in great force in Bar-le-Duc
and ever so much farther forward. About two hours after we got into
Bar-le-Duc we saw the old King arrive in his open travelling carriage.
He had spent the previous night at Commercy. His quarters were in the
largest house in the town, the Banque de France, on one of the charming
Boulevards by which this very pretty town is surrounded. The Bavarians
were very proud at being permitted to find him his guard, and I confess
I pitied the two sentries on the door, who had to present arms on an
average of about four times a minute. A Bavarian regimental band played
in front of the Royal quarters in the early evening, and King Wilhelm
came out and placidly smoked his cigar on the balcony, listening to
the music. The townsfolk could not understand the quiet, homely way
in which the Royal state of the Prussian monarch is conducted. When
the French Emperor passed through on his way to Metz he, too, slept
at the Banque de France, but he was surrounded by a cordon of guards,
sentries, and police spies. The people made no secret of the fact that
they liked the Prussian way of doing business best. The hotel-keepers
remarked that they got their bills paid as if the generals were
tourists, whereas the French gave promises to pay when the war should
be over. During the day about thirty thousand men of the Crown Prince’s
army passed through the town—chiefly Bavarians. The light blue of the
Bavarian uniform does not wear well in point of looks—it gets soiled
in no time and then even the officers look seedy. The helmet, too,
is a creation of intensest ugliness. It has no split crow in gold
on the front, but the letter “L” for the King’s initial, and it is
surmounted by a ridgy tuft of upstanding bear’s-skin that looks like a
dolly. But the Bavarian soldiers, spite of an ugly uniform, were fine
fellows, and right serviceable-looking soldiers. The Crown Prince had
given them some notable lessons in marching, and they had thrown away
everything not essential, the very officers carrying their own kits in
knapsacks on their backs. There were no led horses with portmanteaus,
few pack waggons, and none of the impedimenta to which Prince Frederick
Charles’s army was somewhat addicted. The men did not seem very
uniform in size, and toward the tail of the regiment some of them ran
smaller than would be admitted in the German service. But they were
smart, clean-built fellows, with an elastic spring in their step, and
bright, keen eyes, and altogether had a fighting look about them; and
Weissemburg and Wörth were evidence that their actions corroborated
their looks. Their cavalry was smart enough, but not very brilliantly
mounted. The horses seemed more of the work-animal pattern than those
of the Prussian Dragoons or Uhlans, but looked good weight-carriers,
and had lost wonderfully little in flesh considering what had been
taken out of them.

What a grand old motto is _festina lente_! In the early morning of
the 25th, in full conviction that in two or three days the King and
the Crown Prince, according to the programme then current, would be
in Châlons, we started off thither, glad in the belief that we were
so well to the front. It was, I believe, in the forenoon of the 25th
that the change of tactics was arranged consequent on the discovery
of MacMahon’s movement to the north; thus, when the troops about
Bar-le-Duc were commencing that wonderful series of marches which
brought them on the right flank of the French in the valley of the
Meuse, we were jogging contentedly towards Vitry. In the worm-eaten
old fortress we found a couple of battalions, the place having been
abandoned by the Mobile garrison on the approach of the German troops,
the curious miscellaneous assortment of guns having been partly
pitched into the moat, partly left on the ramparts. The day after the
