evacuation several hundreds of the garrison had been surrounded in
a wood by a comparative handful of Uhlans and made prisoners, after
firing a few harmless volleys. The garrison of Vitry and the troops
in the neighbourhood received orders on the 26th to march to the
northward; but no explanation of the reason accompanied those orders,
and nobody in Vitry had any knowledge as to the radical alteration in
the situation. The march to the north was regarded as simply a slight
diversion, and none doubted that MacMahon was falling back from the
camp of Châlons. The road to Châlons then seemed still the chief
thoroughfare for the advance of the German army, and off for Châlons we
set by the southern bank of the Marne. During the whole journey we saw
but one German—an officer driven in a gig by a peasant, and engaged in
noting the bridges and fords on the Marne. The timid country people,
anxious to sell their produce, continually begged of us to tell them
whether they could get into Vitry. We might have been in a land of
profound peace, so far away seemed everything warlike. When we reached
the iron gates where the municipals of Châlons collect the octroi dues,
it was matter of surprise that there stood no men in spiked helmets on
sentry there. On we drove into the town, through streets thronged with
people gossipping and gesticulating; and still never a German uniform
was visible. Relinquishing our intention of halting at a hotel, we
pushed right on, trying to look as unconcerned as possible, till we got
on the further boulevard, where a well-intentioned townsman stopped
us to say that if we went much further there were Germans still about
who were sure to requisition our vehicle. Thanking him for form sake
we pressed on till, some two miles outside of Châlons on the road to
Suippes, we overtook an officer and five men of the Black Hussars,
the last of the troops who had evacuated Châlons for the march to
the north. This party we accompanied to Suippes, where we were on
the extreme left flank of the German army. What boots it to narrate
the little good purpose to which, chasing the shadows thrown out by
defective information, we spent the days from the 27th to the 31st—how
we were carried prisoners by an over-zealous train officer to Turane,
where Prince Albrecht, commanding the cavalry covering the flank, blew
him up for his officiousness—how we tracked back toward St. Menehould
and then north to Vouziers, where we saw francs-tireurs shot, and
the villages burnt in which they had fired on the German patrols—how
on the 30th, passing through deserted camp after deserted camp of
MacMahon's army, we were within a short distance of the surprise of
Beamont without knowing it, and how at length in the afternoon we
came into Chemery by the southern road as the Crown Prince, with the
5th Army Corps, came down by the eastern from Stonne? Where the two
roads join at the end of the dismal village he halted, and regiment
after regiment, first of the 11th and then of the 5th Corps, marched
past him, dusty, fagged, but full of fight. Then there was a rattle of
wheels, and the carriages containing the King and his staff drove up,
and halted in the dusty road while Wilhelm dismounted, and stood by his
son till the last proviant waggon of the 5th Corps had filed past, and
till his uniform was so white with the dust that you might have taken
him for a venerable baker who had mounted a sword and a helmet. Then he
laid his hand on his son’s arm, and the pair walked slowly up a narrow
lane to the right between two gardens. For awhile father and son stood
talking earnestly, the senior with his hand on the other’s shoulder,
the younger holding an unfolded map in his hand and talking earnestly.
Then Von Moltke and Blumenthal were beckoned up out of the throng of
staff officers, and presently Von Roon and Podbielski. The open air
council of war lasted for nearly half an hour, and then the King left
Chemery at a brisk trot, crossed the valley to Malmy, and went off down
the further side of the canal to his head-quarters for the night at
Vendresse. In the lane between the gardens, as I afterwards learnt, it
was decided that the crushing blow should be dealt next day instead of
being postponed, as originally intended, till the 2nd of September.

It was entirely by chance that, unattached as we were to anything or
anybody, while posting letters in Vendresse about seven o’clock on the
morning of the 1st September, we heard that there was fighting going
on to the north, in the valley of the Meuse. The King had been out
of bed and away ever since the early morning—gone, one of the Royal
gendarmes sagely informed us, to visit the battle of the previous
day but one. Of course we started at once, found the road utterly
deserted, and, taking the wrong branch at the fork, came down into
the valley of the Meuse nearly as far west as Mezières, and for the
time at least out of view of the great battle that was raging as
we trotted rapidly forward. But the whole valley was resonant with
the din of battle. Away to our right we could hear the crash of the
artillery fire at Sedan. Over against Donchery, midway between Sedan
and Mezières, the blazing villages sent baleful flakes of flame up
into the bright sky. At Mezières itself the Würtembergers, from the
top of the heights between us and the town, were replying with shells
and musketry to the cannon from the bastions and the spiteful whirr of
the mitrailleuses from the glacis. As we came down into the valley we
passed through large French camps with the fires still burning. This
had evidently been the scene of a surprise. The men had left their
camp-kettles on the logs and their knapsacks in the lines; they seemed
to have hurriedly snatched their arms and made off, some indeed having
gone away without the preliminary ceremony of grasping their rifles.
Here and there, but at rare intervals, were strewn corpses of men and
horses, and the hospital flag was flying from several houses. Ere we
left the height we saw regiment after regiment of Würtembergers press
out of the village of Flize up on to the bluff which overhangs the
town, and stand there blazing away at something that seemingly lay
between them and the town, while the great guns of the fortress tried
in vain to dislodge them. Reaching Flize I found several regiments of
Würtembergers drawn up and in a high state of excitement by reason of
the easy manner in which they had driven the enemy close up under the
walls of Mezières. It had been a running skirmish for several miles in
length and breadth. The French on the previous night had broken down
a wooden suspension bridge crossing the Meuse close under the east of
Flize. The suspension cables were composed of strands of fine wire;
one of them only was cut, and the floor of the bridge had turned a
complete somersault over the still standing wire, lying upside down in
the bed of the river. The Würtembergers had, about midnight, thrown
a pontoon across in twenty-five minutes, and followed up the pursuit
on the northern river flat, while they continued to press the French
towards the town on the southern knolls and heights. About the time
we arrived the latter had been driven in a huddled mass on to the
exposed glacis of the fortress, and were struggling for entrance at
the gates. The Würtembergers from the heights, disregarding the feeble
and ill-directed fire from the guns that ought to have covered the
French retirement into the fortress, poured down volley after volley
of musketry into the chaotic mass lying below them. Then just as we
reached the height a battery opened fire with shells, and caught the
range of the entrance gate through the fortress wall at the first shot.
Two streams were meeting and battling at this entrance; the block was
complete, when a shell hurtled into its midst, and, exploding as it
fell, blew the densely-packed human mass, mangled and bleeding, in
all directions. This terrible fire of shells never ceased till the
Frenchmen were inside the great gate; and many there were that tried
to enter whom the bursting shells forbade not only to enter but to
live. The officer in command of the Würtembergers told us that he had
no intention of assailing the fortress itself till further orders,
and that he had complied with his instructions when he had chased the
French inside of it. The guns were roaring up the valley at Sedan, and
if we made haste we might yet be in time to see some fighting there. So
we went off rapidly towards Sedan along the great chaussée, and reached
a knoll behind the village of Frenois, which commands a view of the
whole valley, just in time to witness the last of a series of brilliant
cavalry charges made by the French Chasseurs d’Afrique, which went far
to annihilate that body of dashing horsemen.

Sedan, a fortress of considerable strength, with a moderate-sized
town, lies round the convex side of a northern bend of the Meuse. On
the south-east, toward Wadelincourt and Balan, there were considerable
artificially-caused inundations which materially added to the strength
of the place on that side. Further to the south-west, and on both sides
of the river, spreads out a considerable alluvial tract, probably at
one time a lake. Near the centre of the northern section of this is,
or rather was, the large village of Bazeilles. On the west side of the
town the configuration of the ground is more complicated. The river
quits the town in a north-westerly direction, leaving the suburb of
Torcy on the south, and presently trends away north-west by north for a
stretch over two miles. Doubling back with considerable abruptness it
comes back nearly due south a distance of about three miles, skirting
near the foot of this second stretch the grounds of the Chateau
Bellevue, and impinging rather abruptly on the high ground by the
village of Frenois, which diverts its course in a westerly direction
towards Donchery. By this loop a peninsula is enclosed, which a canal
across the isthmus converts wholly into an island—the island which for
several days formed the wretched prison of the captured French army.
At the angle where, on the side nearest Sedan, the canal leaves the
Meuse, stands the village of Glaire; nearer Sedan, on the south side
of the river, is the village of Torcy. On the southern side of Sedan a
range of more or less abrupt high ground quits the bank of the Meuse
above Wadelincourt (that is, on the south-eastern side of the town),
and sweeping round envelopes a plain of some size till it comes closer
to the river again at Frenois, after which it runs away parallel with
the Meuse till broken by the river Bar running down northward from the
low hills of the Argonne to join the Meuse not far from the village of
Dom-le-Mesnil. This range comprises several eminences of considerable
height: the loftiest is that just above the town of Donchery, on
the summit of which is the chateau of the same name,—the point from
which the Crown Prince directed operations on the day of the battle.
At ill-defined right angles from this range there runs out a lower
rounded spur, which gradually lessens in elevation as the river comes
to meet it at Frenois, and disappears altogether a few hundred yards
further north. On its northern shoulder is built the Chateau Bellevue,
which, from its moderate elevation, looks across the river and over
the plain to Donchery in one direction, and down upon Sedan on the
other, seeming from its position, as looked at from either place, to
stand considerably higher than it really does. On the north side of
the Meuse there is a range of high ground resembling in many points
that on the south. Commencing behind Sedan, the heights on the north
are less rugged and have their tops, as it were, shaved off, so as to
constitute a tableland sloping ever towards the river. Round the top of
the loop the heights come pretty close to the river, but they leave to
the west of it the considerable plain of Donchery, and their slopes are
easier, studded with villages and fields, alternated with woods. On the
Sedan side of the loop the belt of meadow-plain left is narrower, but
it attains a considerable width just opposite the village of Glaire.
Depressions rather than ravines serrate the high ground rectangularly,
one of these being close to the town of Sedan. Behind all rise the
forest-clad hills of the Ardennes, and the frontier is but a few miles
off among those.

The Germans had driven the French out of the country on the south
side of the Meuse, and the French lay along the northern bank. It
may be said either that they had no proper front, or that they had
three fronts. It was surely in some sense a front rather than a flank
position, the extended face shown to the Bavarians and Crown Prince of
Saxony’s army all the way from Bazeilles by Daigny back to Givonne. It
was a very deep flank, if it could be called a flank, on which the 5th
and 11th Corps fell on the west after their circuitous march to the
head of the loop. But the nominal front may be said to have stretched
along the river, with the fortress for its centre, whose fortifications
were manned, and in and about which was accumulated the French train
and other impedimenta.

Of the masses which fought so toughly with the Saxons and Bavarians
on the north-east and south-east of the town, or of the character of
the battle that raged all round that side, I can say nothing as an
eye-witness. But when I came in sight of the ground a large force of
French infantry, cavalry, and artillery held the broad, flattened
top of the bluff height next but one to the fortress on the westward
side of the town and the northern bank of the river. The great focus
of interest to me was this flattened bluff standing forward into
the valley, and bounded on the Sedan side as I have described, on
the Mezières side by a wider depression, which, rising gradually,
ultimately loses itself almost imperceptibly on the higher ground. This
depression forms a sort of bay, round which sweeps the high ground,
which then extends all the way westward as far as opposite Donchery,
more or less elevated, and crested everywhere by a dense wood. In the
very centre of this bay there rises an isolated knoll or mamelon,
nearly to an equal height with the high ground flanking the bay, and
affording a remarkable position for assailing with advantage the table
land on which stood the French; since, crowned with wood, it formed
admirable cover for any troops advancing from the west along the low
ground, who had full opportunity to make all their dispositions without
exposing so much as a helmet. And then, there was the choice either of
standing on the top of the mamelon and firing across, of going to the
left up the valley on to the height, or of debouching on to the low
river-flat to the right, and from there assailing the table land where
its slope was least abrupt, and where a road up its face was calculated
to afford material assistance to an attacking party. That the Germans
fully appreciated the strategical value of this mamelon became speedily
apparent.

In the small hours of the morning, the 11th and the 5th Army Corps,
which, after passing through Chemery, had pushed on to make their
bivouac between Dom and Donchery, had crossed the Meuse by two
pontoon bridges below their bivouac. The latter corps had struck up
at once right out of the valley to the edge of the forests cresting
the upper ground, and had then come left shoulders forward through the
upland farms and villages, having to fight their way smartly as they
progressed, and leaving a track of fire and smoke behind them. As we
came into the valley above Flize, the line of this advance was already
marked by those lurid insignia. The 11th Corps, on crossing the stream,
right inclined considerably sooner than the 5th, and kept on the lower
ground, while they bored on forward to round the top of the loop. Thus
those two corps were marching on the right flank of the French, or on
what I prefer to regard as having been its right face. Up till nine
o’clock the battle, in this direction at all events, had been confined
chiefly to artillery, but, soon after, the 5th Corps had got to close
quarters away on the further upland, and the left of the 11th Corps was
even sooner in the thick of it, nearer the river and adjacent to the
place where the depression in which stands the mamelon loses itself
on the upland. The French artillery first essayed in vain to stay the
resolute progress of the German troops. Then their cavalry charged
again and again—lancers, cuirassiers, and chasseurs-à-cheval—hurling
themselves on the ever-advancing columns, with a courage which all who
witnessed it unite in calling heroic. But the bayonets bore them back,
as bayonets, in the hands of resolute disciplined infantrymen, will
ever bear back horsemen, and then the two infantries came together, and
the engagement at this point became close and general.

While these things were being transacted on the upland, a manœuvre
was in progress for taking avail of the advantage which the isolated
mamelon rising out of the centre of the embayed depression, was
calculated to afford. There gradually massed behind it a large force
of infantry and several batteries of artillery, belonging, no doubt,
to the right division of the 11th Corps. More than one regiment and
battery occupied the summit, and fired across point blank, replying
with advantage to the direct cannon and mitrailleuse fire of the
French from opposite, by reason of their advantageous position among
the trees, while they also found opportunity to throw in a raking
fire upon the left shoulders of the French infantry engaged with the
Germans up at the head of the bay. A couple of regiments, under cover
of this fire, dashed up the bottom of the depression on to the table
land, and took the fighting Frenchmen on the flank, hurling them back,
and leaving their guns and mitrailleuses in a position from which they
had to retreat to prevent their own capture. The Germans then pressed
the French steadily backward across the table-land toward the ravine
on the side of Sedan, not without desperate fighting and heavy loss.
As they were being thus pushed back, the French flank came within the
range of two or three batteries of Bothmer’s Bavarian division, which
had from the first been stationed on the opposite side of the Meuse,
on a rising ground running out into the flat a little to the eastward
of the village of Frenois. But this was at long range, and did not do
all the execution which the leaders must have desiderated. Therefore,
from behind the mamelon in the bay, there issued to the right a German
infantry brigade of the 11th Corps and a battery of artillery. The
little force skirted the plain at the base of the high ground till it
came in a direct line with the fire of the Bavarian guns playing on the
height over their heads. There, in the meadow opposite, in and about
Floing they had a sharp brush with a regiment of French cuirassiers
of the Guard, who had been stationed in a hamlet on the verge of the
meadow. The cuirassiers, with their floating horsehair plumes and their
long, straight swords, charged home knee to knee like gallant soldiers
as they were; but we saw about half a dozen batches of white smoke,
and then a broken, straggling stream of horsemen hurriedly breasting
the steep by the terraced road that runs up it, leaving their track
chequered by fallen men and horses. The terraced road was then followed
by the battery, the brigade of infantry struggling up, regardless
of a path. The little force was wholly protected from the enemy’s
fire so long as it kept under the brow, and was further advantaged by
the Bavarian fire, which was whistling high over its head. That fire
stopped as the helmet spikes showed on to the table land, with the
battery flanking it, the whole looking straight into the right flank
of the Frenchmen retreating towards Sedan. The artillery began its
measured booming; the infantrymen plied the needle-gun. It seemed as
if the French retreat must become a rout. But there was still left
heart among the Frenchmen for an attempt to hurl at least this last
thorn in their side off the table land into the plain by the Meuse. At
a gallop through the ragged intervals in the confused masses of the
infantry came dashing the Chasseurs d’Afrique. The squadrons halted,
fronted, and then wheeled into line, at a pace and with a regularity
which would have done them credit in the Champ de Mars, and did them
double credit, executed as was the evolution under a warm fire. That
fire, as one could tell by the dying away of the smoke jets, ceased all
of a sudden, as if the trumpets which rang out the “Charge!” for the
Chasseurs had sounded also the “Cease firing!” for the German artillery
and infantry. Not a needle-gun gave fire as the splendid horsemen
crashed down the gentle slope with the velocity of an avalanche. I have
seen not a few cavalry charges, but I never saw a finer one, whether
from a spectator’s or an adjutant’s point of view, than this one of the
Chasseurs d’Afrique. It was destined to a sudden arrestment, and that
without the ceremony of the trumpets sounding the “Halt.” The horsemen
and the footmen might have seen the colour of each others’ moustaches
(to use Havelock’s favourite phrase), when along the line of the latter
there flashed out a sudden, simultaneous streak of fire. Like thunder
claps sounding over the din of a hurricane, rose the measured crash of
the battery guns, and the cloud of white smoke drifted away towards
the Chasseurs, enveloping them for the moment from one’s sight. When
it blew away, there was visible a line of bright uniforms and grey
horses struggling prostrate among the potato drills, or lying still in
death. Only a handful of all the gallant show of five minutes before
were galloping backward up the slope, leaving tokens at intervals of
their progress as they retreated. So thorough a destruction, by what
may be called a single volley probably the oldest soldier now alive
never witnessed. The cavalry blotted out, still the French infantry
found courage in despair for one last effort. It was to almost certain
death, and to absolutely certain defeat, that that straggling line
came on, irregularly firing as it pushed forward. Rushes of impotent
passion burst out from parts of the line; other parts only staggered
slowly forward, as if against a high wind. What came of the effort it
was hard to tell in the distracting medley, till the time came for
one to go on to the battle-field, after the combatants had quitted it.
Under the veil of white smoke the tattered line of infantrymen seemed
gradually to melt away, without having ever come to close quarters with
the enemy, who stood so solidly pouring his fire into its teeth; but
that some, both of the horsemen and the footmen, must have got somehow
to the brink of the slope, seemed evident from the fact that dead men
of both arms lay after the battle either on the road which slanted
up the steep, or huddled at its bottom, as if either their impetus
had overshot the mark, or they had been pitched over by the German
infantrymen, who kept so tight a hold of the position on to which they
had struggled.

Meanwhile the Bavarians from Bazeilles on the eastward of Sedan, the
Saxons by Daigny and Givonne on the north-eastward, and the Prussian
Guards edging round till they had reached due north of the town, were
steadily, and with very hard fighting, driving in the French on their
respective fronts. Pressing still to their own right, the right flank
of the Guards began to touch the ever further and further overlapping
left of the 5th Corps, till at length the junction was complete, and
there was no thoroughfare through lines composed of soldiers such
as those. And so the French host was environed and cut off from the
frontier and from escape in that direction by a semicircle round
Sedan, the chord of which was the Meuse. Then the Meuse on its south
side was lined by Bavarian and Prussian troops, and the investment
was complete. The citadel could not hold half the French infantry,
to say nothing of the cavalry, artillery, and train; the town, it
was seen from the battle-field, was densely packed even on to the
exposed side of the glacis; and it seemed, as the firing gradually
died away, almost to nothing, before three o’clock, that the battle
was surely over. A report, indeed, ran round that the place, and all
that was in it, had capitulated; but that this was premature was shown
by the recommencement of artillery fire from the south-east between
four and five. At what or at whom this fire was directed, none in our
vicinity could tell, till volumes of black smoke, rising from inside
the ramparts, gave indication that the city itself was the mark. The
French had almost abandoned the semblance of resistance, and I saw no
attempt at replying to this bombardment, which was over and past by
five o’clock, and then the stillness was something painful, in contrast
with the fearful din of the day. Before darkness fell the Germans had
drawn back off the battle-field, and had surrounded the whole town with
a cordon of artillery. The guns stood unlimbered and ready pointed at
the town—24 batteries, 144 guns—a gentle hint of what were the German
intentions in the morning if resistance should be persevered in.

My companion and myself had some expectation, judging from the aspect
of affairs, that toward the close of the afternoon the French were
concentrating for one last effort to break out in a due northerly
direction; and anxious to be in the vicinity if this should occur,
we had crossed the Meuse by a rope ferry at the village of Glaire,
and pushed across the corpse-flecked plain into the dead-littered
village of Floing at the further side. As we were clumbering up the
steep on to the upland plateau, there rose suddenly, from the dense
masses of troops collected on the southern swell, a hoarse roar of
cheering, caught up, as if by infection, by the men of the 11th Army
Corps in the bay around the mamelon, and on the plateau. From mouth
to mouth flew the tidings that the French Emperor had sent his sword
to the German King, that the whole French host were already virtually
prisoners of war, and that Sedan, with all that was in it and around
it, was formally to capitulate in the morning. From where we were we
saw nothing of the aide-de-camp with the flag of truce, or of the
grand sensational scene of his interview with the German monarch. The
ferry-boat was on the wrong side of the stream, and before we got over
once more to the south side, the King had set off on his return journey
to Vendresse, the Crown Prince had vacated his hill and had taken
the road back to Chemery, the flushed and wearied fighting men had
commenced preparations for their bivouac, and the autumn twilight was
fast descending. We came back past the Chateau Bellevue and through
Frenois to Donchery. The mean town looked like a city of the dead, for
every inhabitant seemed to have deserted it, save only the woman who
kept the hotel. I contrived to obtain quarters in it, and presently
there arrived a body of staff officers. These confirmed the tidings
we had heard, but could give no specific details. A meagre supper was
served, eked out by a few boxes of sardines, with which we happened
to be provided, and it was being eaten when suddenly a message came
that Bismarck would join the party. An officer who had constituted
himself caterer went into the kitchen to see what could be procured
for the great Count. Alas! the unhappy hostess, with many _mon Dieus_,
protested that unless they chose to eat herself, there was nothing
to be had save only eggs and bacon. On this modest fare accordingly,
washed down with champagne, Bismarck made a hearty supper; but before
he began to eat, he made a formal statement, reading from a despatch,
to the effect that the Emperor of the French had communicated to the
King the surrender of his sword. Bismarck reserved comments on this
announcement, but led off a hearty cheer, and then, like a jovial
fellow, gave the toasts of “The King,” and “The Fatherland.”

Before going to sleep I took a walk round the half-obliterated ramparts
which surround the once fortified town of Donchery. The scene was very
fine. The whole horizon was lurid with the reflection of fire. All
along the valley of the Meuse, on either side, were the bivouacs of
the German host. Two hundred thousand men lay here around their King.
On the horizon glowed the flames of the burning villages, the flicker
occasionally reflecting itself on a link of the placid Meuse. Over all
the quiet moon waded through a sky cumbered with wind clouds. What
were the Germans doing on this their night of triumph? Celebrating
their victory by wassail and riot? No. There arose from every camp one
unanimous chorus of song, but not the song of ribaldry. Verily they
are a great race these Germans—a masterful, fighting, praying people;
surely in many respects not unlike the men whom Cromwell led. The
chaunt that filled the night air was Luther’s hymn, the glorious—

 “Nun danket alle Gott,”

the Old Hundredth of Germany. To hear this great martial orchestra
singing this noble hymn under such circumstances was alone worth a
journey to Sedan, with all its vicissitudes and difficulties. We had
a domiciliary visit in our bed-room during the night from a couple of
Generals in civil but exacting search for blankets to cover them as
they lay on the floor of the _salle à manger_.

Next morning we rose with day-break, intending to get as close to
Sedan as possible, that we might watch the critical state of affairs,
and take the earliest opportunity of getting inside the city.
Whencesoever he had come—it was rumoured that he had spent the night
in Donchery—there sat on his horse at the corner of the market-place a
sad-faced French general with red eyes, as if he had not slept. This I
afterwards learned was General Count Reillé. Ever as he tried to wedge
his horse into the narrow throat of the street leading to the bridge,
and so on to the road leading to Sedan, he was baulked by the steady
ceaseless stream of proviant-waggons and munition tumbrils, and he
smiled a weary forced smile as he drew his horse back out of the press.
At length an adjutant made a way for him, and he was gone. He could not
have been across the bridge when at the same corner of the market-place
showed the tall form and square shoulders of Bismarck on his powerful
bay horse. There was no redness about his eyes; it must have been past
one in the morning when he drank his last glass of champagne in the
Hôtel de Commerce, it would be rash to say how long he had been at work
after that; and yet here he was before the Mairie clock had chimed the
hour of six, fresh, hearty, clean-shaved, steady of hand, and clear of
throat, as the voice proved in which he bade the column-men give him
space to pass. He wore a blue military cloak and the undress cap of
the cuirassier regiment of which he is the colonel. On such a morning
where Bismarck was there for sure would there be momentous doings, and
we followed in his tracks as soon as possible. We lost him, however,
on the farther side of the bridge, but learnt he had ridden on toward
Frenois, and so followed that road. About two kilometres beyond
Donchery we met a rather shabby open carriage drawn by two horses,
driven by a coachman with a gold band. It contained four French general
officers, and a fifth, the one we had seen in the market-place of
Donchery, rode behind by the side of the ubiquitous Bismarck. The hour
was ten minutes past seven. There was no escort, or anything, save the
circumstance that the occupants of the carriage were French generals,
to mark out the equipage as anything exceptional. We were barely past,
when by a common impulse my companion and myself exclaimed, “That’s
Napoleon!” We dismounted, and returned walking behind a line of wains
which were going the same way as the carriage. Sure enough, on the
right-hand side of the back seat there sat the Emperor—that grey face
of his, impassive and sphinxlike as ever, but with its lines drawn
and deepened as if by some spasm. He wore a general’s uniform, with a
cavalry cloak, which was thrown open, and showed the decorations on his
breast. In no other respect did he differ from his companions in dress.

The modest cavalcade went on about a hundred yards, and then halted in
front of a small wayside house on the left-hand side of the road as
one goes from Frenois to Donchery. The cottage into which the Emperor
went on alighting was the right-hand cottage as one looks out on to
the chaussée, one of a block of three mean two-storied brick houses
inhabited by families of weavers. The houses stand some twenty or
twenty-five feet back from the road. In their front is a narrow level
of gravelled path—at the edge of this, in front of the right-hand house
were placed the chairs for the Emperor and Bismarck—and then there is a
grassy slope down to the ditch bounding the road. It was in obedience
to a sudden request of the Emperor that the modest _cortége_ halted
at the wayside house. I saw him turn round in his seat, and heard the
request he made to the Prussian Chancellor. Immediately on alighting,
the Emperor walked hurriedly to the rear of the house, and presently
returned and went inside for, at the most, five minutes. The three
officers occupying the carriage had gone inside at once, and were
followed by Bismarck and his mounted companion. Then the Emperor and
the Chancellor came outside again and sat down on two chairs which
were fetched for them by the weaver who lived downstairs. Some chairs
were brought out also for the French generals, but not enough, so one
or two of them lay down on the grass bank in front of the left-hand
cottage, and were presently joined by several officers of the German
staff. One of the French generals, the Duke de Moskowa I think, seemed
to have been wounded, and dragged one of his legs heavily behind him.
On a table in rear of the block of houses, there had been set out a
couple of bottles of country wine with glasses, and one of the French
generals, I shall not say which, kept popping round the corner to pay
his devoirs to the sour tipple.

When the interview between the Emperor and Bismarck commenced—at twenty
minutes past seven by my watch—Bismarck stood with cap in hand, but, as
it seemed, in compliance with an injunction from Napoleon, accompanied
with a bow and a slight smile, the Chancellor covered himself and
sat down beside the other. Then an earnest conversation ensued. The
Emperor occasionally smiled and made a remark, but it was evident that
Bismarck was doing the greater part of the talking, and that, too,
energetically. From our position we could just hear the murmur of
Bismarck’s voice when he raised it at those points where he thought it
necessary to give emphasis by the gesture of bringing the fore-finger
of the left hand sharp down on the palm of the right. The occupant of
the first floor of the right-hand cottage was all the while overlooking
the pair out of his front window. Along the road streamed the ceaseless
current of a proviant column, the rude teamsters glowering greedily at
the strange scene by the road-side—a scene the like of which is not in
the history of our century—and exclaiming occasionally in their uncouth
curiosity, “_Wo ist der Napoleon, den?_”

A little after eight von Moltke drove up from the direction of
Donchery, and after joining in the conversation for a quarter of an
hour, went away again. Bismarck followed him, mounted, at a sharp trot,
and the French party was left to itself. The Generals remained seated
in a clump. The Emperor took to stalking moodily up and down a path
in the potato-plot by the side of the cottage, limping slightly as he
walked, and smoking hard. After a spell of this exercise, he came and
sat down among his Generals, still retaining an almost total silence,
while they spoke and gesticulated with considerable animation, and,
as it seemed occasionally, a little confusedness. A little after nine
there came up at the trot from Donchery a half troop of the 1st Sleswig
Cuirassier Regiment of Life Guards. The Cuirassiers, with trained
celerity, formed a semicircle around the back of the house. The burly
lieutenant dismounted two of his men, and without a glance at the
sitting party or the semblance of a salute, marched them close up to
the cottage-wall just behind the Emperor’s chair, halted them, gave
loudly the order “Draw swords,” and then gave them their orders in an
undertone. I noticed the Emperor glance backward at this arrangement,
as if he did not half like it. There was a flush on his face, the first
evidence of emotion he had manifested. At a quarter to ten Bismarck
came back among the staff, which by this time had accumulated on the
road in considerable numbers, and Moltke’s lean face was visible in
the crowd on the road. He did not seem to care about having more to
say to the Frenchmen, and never went up the slope. Bismarck now wore
his cuirassier helmet and looked quite martial, but rather heated, and
no wonder, for he had taken a stretching gallop back in the direction
of Vendresse to meet his master the King. The King and his two great
subjects, the civilian Bismarck and the soldier Moltke, met on the
road at the foot of a steep slope about four kilometres backward
from Donchery toward Vendresse and Chemery. An estaminet stands just
opposite the spot of meeting, the portly proprietor of which watched
from a distance the hurried council of war which took place in the
field by the roadside, and took the pains to mark with an axe the tree
against which the King leant when the momentous consultation was in
progress.

Wiping his hot face, Bismarck strode up to the sitting Emperor, and
as we know now, communicated the message from his master the King,
and the arrangement as to the interim disposition of the illustrious
prisoner. After a short interview he beckoned to the carriage, which
had drawn off a little, and the Emperor entered it, followed by the
Generals, and, still escorted by the Cuirassiers, the party drove off
in the direction of Sedan. I followed as fast as I could, and caught
up its track as it entered the grounds of the château appropriately
named Bellevue, which overlooks the plain on which stands Sedan. In
the grounds a regiment of Bavarians had been bivouacking, but as the
imperial party arrived they were marched out. The château consists of
a centre block, with two wings joined to the centre by conservatories
on the first floor. The chief front is towards the road. I was at the
back of the house, but then the blinds were drawn down at the front,
so I had rather the best of it. In the conservatory on the left were
the French Generals; in that on the right was the Prussian staff. In
the dining-room on the ground-floor of the central block von Moltke,
Bismarck, and de Wimpfen were debating the terms of the capitulation.
The Emperor, I gathered, was lying down on a sofa in the drawing-room
on the first floor of the central turret, or in the boudoir which opens
off it. No doubt he was weary and sick at heart, while it required no
very keen eyes to discern that he was in bodily pain. And that had
been a long trying wait there by the wayside from seven till ten, with
little truly to lighten its monotony to the stunned, crushed man. A
colloquy with Bismarck, stern of heart, sharp, if courteous of speech,
unyielding in resolve, for an hour or more; for another hour or more
a listless kicking of heels while the man who had vanquished should
pronounce what was to be done with him. He, the Emperor of the French,
the proudest monarch in Europe, kicking his heels on the roadside by a
weaver’s cottage while a Prussian count galloped to a Prussian king for
instructions! If he were not too stunned to think at all, he must have
thought at times as he lay there that surely the morning’s doings were
one ghastly dream.

The deliberations as to the negotiations seemed likely, by the rumours
which reached us, to last for some time, and quitting the precincts of
the Château Bellevue, my companion and myself utilised the interval
in visiting the battle-field. The tract charged over by the Chasseurs
d’Afrique was a scene of terrible carnage. The Arab stallions ridden
by this force had died very hard; in many cases they had with their
struggles made graves before they had died for their riders and
themselves. The burial parties did not have much trouble hereabouts in
bringing corpses to the grave-pits. The difficulty was to clear a space
on which to commence a grave-pit. Higher up on the tableland there was
fearful evidence of the power of a shell-fire at short range. I counted
five headless corpses within two hundred yards—their heads had been
blown away as clean as if they had been guillotined. Men disembowelled,
with their chests driven into fragments, with legs or arms blown away,
were common spectacles. I found one man’s leg about twenty-five paces
distant from his body. Right on the battle-field we came on a friendly
quartermaster, who was superintending the collection of biscuit barrels
and wine casks that the French had left behind them in their hurried
retreat. He placed at our service the contents of a barrel of biscuits,
the outside of which was stained by a fluid which was red, but was not
wine; but bucket staves are not sponges, and the biscuits were very
good, and we were very hungry.

After hurriedly traversing a corner of the battle-field my companion
and myself determined to take our chance of being able to visit
Sedan. We found German outposts, consisting of three men each, near
the transverse passages leading through the glacis, and close to
them bodies of Frenchmen without arms, who chatted freely with the
German sentries. We got on to the glacis of the fortress with the
greatest ease in the world, and found the soldiers lying on it to
consist chiefly of Turcos and Zouaves, dirty fellows most of them, but
certainly in better case to all appearance than the troops we found
inside. Everybody was very friendly; wine was genially pressed on us,
all the more warmly when we mentioned that we were Englishmen. One
especially greasy and strong-smelling Turco, of a full Day-and-Martin
colour, insisted vehemently on kissing us, and we had to submit to the
oleaginous hug with what grace we might. Getting into the town was
a different matter. The gates were closed, and opened only to admit
the wounded as they were brought in on waggons. By the advice of this
friendly Turco, who set us the example, we jumped on one of the waggons
and passed in without interference. As rapidly as we could for the
chaotic press, we traversed several streets of the town. We saw where
M’Mahon lay wounded, and also how full the town was of troops. They
were swarming, densely packed, everywhere. Of the wounded, some were
in the churches, the houses, and the public buildings, others lying
unheeded and jostled in the courtyards; the dead were everywhere—in the
gutters, trampled on by the living, on the swampy margin of the moat,
littering the narrow way through the glacis and the fortifications,
lying, some of them, on the steps of a church. The sight was one never
to be forgotten—a sight of misery, chaos, disorganization, and general
devilry, which assuredly was unique in this generation—an eddying sea
of humanity, trampling recklessly over the dead and the wounded, the
men now yelling for the blood of their officers, now struggling in
fierce contention for a morsel of bread.

Hastening back to the Château Bellevue, we were yet too late for the
announcement of the signature of the capitulation. We had seen nothing
of that sensational incident—if it really did occur—when von Moltke,
as is related, led outside the reluctant de Wimpfen, and weakened his
obduracy by pointing to the ring of field guns unlimbered, and with
the gunners in position, girdling the fated city, and ready at the
word to commence a bombardment which would have been the synonyme
for a butchery. But we were just in time with a sharp run to see
the King up to the gates of the château, followed by his son and
the leading members of his staff, and to see the silent greeting—if
words were spoken they must have been muttered in a low tone by men
afraid to trust their voices—between the monarchs on the gravelled
drive at the bottom of the steps. The “good brothers” turned, and
mounting the steps, disappeared inside one of the rooms in the central
block, the royal staff partly occupying one of the conservatories,
partly remaining outside. The interview, that surely must have sorely
tried the composure of both the parties to it, lasted barely some
twenty minutes, and then Wilhelm was out and off in his carriage for
a drive through his victorious Kinder bivouacking by the Meuse. He
who was left in that inner room must have heard the air throb again
with the cheering which came from German throats at the sight of the
white-haired conqueror-king. Later in the afternoon I saw the Emperor
again. He came out in front of the château to superintend the unpacking
of his travelling carriage. He looked very wan and weary, although he
still kept up the old impassible look. The imperial equipage in its
magnificence was an extraordinary contrast to the simplicity of the
modest turnout of the Prussian King. In gold and scarlet the outriders
of the former glittered profusely. He of Prussia had his postboys in
shoddy cloth, with glazed coverings to their hats to keep the dust off
the nap.

The Emperor’s barber, with whom I had a chat as the suite waited
for orders outside the château, gave me some interesting particulars
regarding the last disastrous day in Sedan. The French army, he said,
had its efficiency destroyed by socialism and a craving for a republic.
It was in a chronic state of semi-mutiny, and many regiments would
not follow their officers on any consideration. This yeasty condition
of agitation made discipline an impossibility, and accounted for the
disregard of discipline on their part, which had enabled the Germans
so often to surprise their camps and drive them whithersoever they
listed. M’Mahon was wounded in a vain attempt to excite one of these
disaffected regiments to charge. The Emperor met him in the street
as he was being carried half unconscious into his quarters. If my
informant was truthful, Napoleon bore himself worthily during the
disastrous day. Imitating his uncle at Montereau, he directed guns
with his own hand, and was continually under fire. But the French were
totally out-manœuvred and outnumbered. They had expected to have to
cope with an army of 120,000 men; instead of this, nearly double the
number started up unexpectedly, as if from the ground. The troops in
Sedan candidly admitted to me that the Germans were better soldiers
than themselves, although not braver men; and this is about the truth
of the matter. They have a sense of responsibility which the French
want; they are splendid marchers, they fight like devils, they are
strong, self-contained, self-respecting, earnest, manly fellows; they
have generals who know how to direct them, and officers who know how to
lead them, and this is why they came to go on accordingly from victory
to victory.

Next morning (the 3rd September), we tried to get through Sedan with
intent to go a little way into Belgium. There was thoroughfare only one
way and for one class of persons. The gate was surrounded by a guard of
Bavarians, and a continuous stream of French prisoners, horse, foot,
and artillery, was fast passing out. We watched it for hours—it seemed
as if it never would stint, and at last we abandoned the intention of
getting through Sedan, and turned down the stream to the rope ferry
which we had used the two previous days. When we got to the bridge
over the canal, we came again upon the stream of French prisoners,
flowing in the direction we were going. The tract between the canal
and the Meuse is an island of some extent. The rope of the ferry was
cut, and the boat on the other side. Skirting the Meuse downward for
some distance, we found several ferries, but all the boats were, like
the first, on the other side of the stream. The fact was, the Germans
had utilised the island into a prison. They had turned the disarmed
French army bodily into it to the number of some 70,000, looking to the
Meuse and the canal to do the main duty of custodianship. It is true
that here and there on the farther side there stood a company under
arms, and that towards nightfall a chain of sentries was run along the
farther banks; but I do not believe that on the island itself there was
a single German. The Frenchmen had been turned on to the island just
as they came out of the town. The Germans themselves were temporarily
short of rations, and could spare nothing for their prisoners. But
the prisoners fared better than their captors, although goodness
knows how they did it. After all nobody can achieve a savoury mess
under difficulties like a Frenchman, or build himself a house where
another man would have to be content with the heavens for his roof.
Innumerable fires had been lit, about one seemingly serving three men.
The fellows dragged out firewood from recondite corners in the most
marvellous manner; recklessly tore down houses, and burnt doors, window
frames, and rafters; broke up carts, ploughs, fences, and everything
else available. On every fire was a saucepan, and in the saucepan were
potatoes, and something else. Where the potatoes came from was plain
enough; we could see the fellows delving them, but about the “something
else,” all that one could tell was that it smelt nice. There was a
flavour of onions in the scent—I began to think Frenchmen have an onion
bed somewhere about their persons—and a _soupçon_ of garlic and a
gamey whiff that made one’s mouth water. Most of the men who were not
cooking were building unto themselves habitations for the night. About
half of them had their _tentes d’abris_, which they were rigging up
dexterously, and bedding with boughs torn from the trees. The trees
were put under heavier contributions to build wherewithal leafy bowers
of considerable architectural pretensions, and positively I do not
think that, with the exception of the extremely late-comers, a single
man of the captive French army slept this first night supperless and
without some cover. They were the civilest, cheeriest, best-humoured
set of fellows imaginable. We two, quite alone, and unable to bestow
anything—for our flasks and tobacco-pouches were but drops in the
bucket, and very soon exhausted—experienced no word but of the frankest
courtesy and the heartiest friendliness, alike from officers and men.
The officers had been allowed to retain their swords, having given
their parole, but had to accompany their men, whose disarmament had
been very thorough. On the island is a handsome château, with beautiful
and extensive grounds, and of the cover there afforded the Frenchmen
took avail extensively. I do not suppose that in the island they had
much more room than in Sedan, but they preferred it for quarters,
since they could get firewood and potatoes. A few stray barrels of
biscuit had been brought into the island on carts, and their contents
were distributed as far as they would go, but there was not a biscuit
for every twenty men of the great multitude. After some conversation
with a group of captains in the grounds of the chateau, we strolled
down towards the river, and accepted the invitation of a bivouac of
Zouaves to join them at their supper. The mess was better than good,
it was superb. It consisted of potatoes, the indescribable savoury
“something,” and flesh of some kind or other. The swarthy Zouaves, in
their red fezes and baggy canvas breeches, treated us like princes, but
evaded a direct reply to the question what was the meat in their mess.
After we had bidden the merry rascals a hearty good night, we came upon
the carcass of a horse that had been killed by a shell, and there was
a clean slice or two out of its flanks. I did not think it necessary
to go back to the Zouaves and put it to them point blank whether they
had been regaling us on horse; but, like the man in the play, I had my
own thoughts on the matter. It was late before we quitted the fires on
the island; and when we were once outside and realised the difficulty
of finding quarters, we were sorry we had not stayed and shared the
bivouac of the Zouaves. Donchery we knew to have been invaded by a
whole Army Corps; Frenois was seething full of Bavarians. As we were
discussing the probabilities of a bivouac we drove past the front of
the Château Bellevue. All was in darkness. A happy thought struck my
companion. “Let us sleep here,” he cried. The gardener seemed glad
enough to have a couple of quiet civilians for his inmates. He stabled
our horse and conducted ourselves into the chateau, one of the most
beautiful residences, both internally and externally, I have ever
seen. The dining hall, where the capitulation was signed, was a model
of taste without meretricious display. It was wholly in stained oak,
save the roof, which is inlaid with American maple. The windows were
Gothic, and in niches round the room there were alternately mirrors
and Etruscan vases. One or two large pictures were let into the
panelling, and in recesses were divans. The chairs were high backed,
in finely carved oak. Above this room, up a winding staircase, and
on the first-floor, was the bedroom occupied by the Emperor on the
previous night, just in the state he left it. Before leaving at nine in
the morning, he had breakfasted with his suite in the dining hall. On
its beauties, as on those of the rest of the charming villa, we were
free to feast; there was nothing else to satisfy the appetite. The
gardener was very hungry himself and so was his wife. Nor, according to
the gardener, was there a drop of wine in the house, and appearances
certainly seemed to corroborate his asseveration. It fell to me to
sleep in the bed occupied by the Emperor on the previous night. Sheets
and a quilt were on the bed, but one of the window hangings, with its
semicircular canopy, had been torn down, and applied as an additional
covering for the couch of the Emperor. He had apparently been reading
in bed, for a translation of Lord Lytton’s “Last of the Barons” lay
open turned on its face, on a little table by the bed-head. There
seemed something of a sombre significance in the selection.

At this time the chateau was inviolate; not so much as an ornament
had been touched. The beautiful clocks ticked on every mantel-piece,
and the pretty knick-knacks of the young ladies were sticking on the
pincushions in their abandoned bedrooms. But about five in the morning
there was an incursion of a Bavarian regiment, which during the night
had been on guard over the prisoners on the island. Beyond doubt they
did not use the place tenderly. The Bavarians are, out of sight, the
most butter-fingered of the German soldiers, and anything conveniently
portable they annexed with neatness and despatch. What the men wanted
was wine, and what seemingly wanton damage they did to the wainscot,
panelling, &c., was in quest of it, a quest in the end crowned with
success.

By the 6th of September the King of Prussia was in Rheims, the
self-constituted guest of the Most Reverend the Archbishop in that
ecclesiastical dignitary’s palace, hard by the historical old
cathedral. There had been but a short stay for the German hosts on
the field where they gained laurels so green and yet so bloody. The
blood tint had hardly died out of the Meuse, the unburied dead yet
festered in the sunheat, and the ruins of Bazeilles yet smoked and
stank, when the vanguard of the Crown Prince’s army shouldered their
packs and trudged away up the valleys of the Argonnes towards the
fertile vine-lands of Champagne, marching nearly due southward but
with their faces ever set Paris-ward, and “Nach Paris!” ever on their
lips. Royal head-quarters made a long pause in the old cathedral
city, while men all over Europe were asking one another whether the
catastrophe of Sedan had not virtually ended the war, and looking for
the white dove of peace to alight on the blood-stained battle-field.
But the Revolution had taken place in Paris, and the leaders of the new
Republic were flushed with the chimera that Republican institutions
and untrained hordes of patriots, which at the talismanic name of
“Republic” were sure to rush to arms eager to fight and proud to die
for _la Patrie_ emancipated, could cope with the generalship and
the trained and victorious armies that had crunched the disciplined
soldiers of the Empire as a boy cracks a nut. It was from the crisis
of Sedan that may be dated the falling away of British sympathy from
the German side. Wise and considerate men at home, who in the beginning
had seen and readily owned to the justice of the cause in which Germany
had fought and conquered, let their reason and their logical perception
become enfeebled by a sympathy for misfortune which did honour to their
hearts. With others the keynote to the changed feeling was kindliness
for a Republic, a gushing abstract love for an institution which they
reckoned a synonym for the truest of all national liberty. A judicious
alternative to this mild phase of mental aberration might have been
discovered in an enforced residence in Paris during the months of March
and April last. When Wilhelm went to war in the name of the German
Fatherland, he put forth that he warred not with the people but with
its ruler. That ruler gone, he was bound to look to some person or to
some body, as the successor of the captive Emperor in some kind of
right or other to dispose of the destinies of France. He could not make
peace with the French nation in the gross; that nation must set up,
whether for peace or for war, something tangible to be treated with
by the victor, if it was to be peace; to direct war, if the nation’s
fiat was for war. The Provisional Government assumed this province,
and was tacitly accepted by France. Then the nation through this, its
mouthpiece, instead of suing for peace, instead of asking what terms it
might expect if it should sit down content with the punishment it had
received, cried “War, war to the hilt, war without a thought, a dream,
a hint of negotiation!” Wilhelm had no help for it, but to accept what
was virtually a new challenge, and fight on. And that challenge gave
to him a new antagonist. He now fought, no longer with the man who was
his prisoner, but with the French nation, with whom up till then he had
proclaimed that he had no quarrel, and he fought with it, because it
had taken up the quarrel that might have gone with the _Déchéance_,
and made it its own. The German did not quit his first position; France
and the French drove him out of it whether he would or not. There was
no alternative but to march on defiant Paris. Sympathy is a beautiful
feeling; but its failing is that very often it clouds discrimination.
Under the influence of effusive sympathy for France, who has not heard
men plead—“Ah, but in the hour of her triumph Germany might have shown
her magnanimity and offered terms to poor France, instead of coldly
standing at attention waiting for her beaten foe to humiliate herself
by suing for terms.” For Germany to have acted thus would not have been
magnanimity; it would have been quixotry, and quixotry certain to have
been misinterpreted by France. Because the German arms only occupied as
a formality a corner of Paris for a couple of days, did not Paris send
up the defiantly-complacent yelp that it was fear which had made the
occupation so transient and partial? Can international history point
to a precedent in which the unquestioned victor, unweakened either by
a single reverse or by a long war, fighting in a cause acknowledged as
just by all the world, took the initiative in offering terms, either
directly or indirectly, to an adversary so stubborn and haughty that it
seemed like enough the profferer would have had them flung back in his
teeth? Who ever heard of the gladiator in the arena, or the pugilist
in the ring cry, “Hold, surely there is enough,” while the opponent
proclaimed himself still full of fighting, and refused to be led away
by what in this case was an Europeful of judicious bottle holders?

While the King abode in Rheims, waiting for what never came, and while
Europe was talking and scheming, and France was hardening her heart and
stiffening her neck, the German armies were deploying into a grand line
for the advance on Paris. Three great roads converge toward Paris from
the east of France. On the southernmost of these leading from Châlons,
through Tour de Montaimé, by Montmirail, Coulommiers, Chaulmes, and
Brie Comte Robert, the head-quarters of the Crown Prince of Prussia it
was arranged should march. The northern route is _viâ_ Laon, Soissons
and Dammartin, but the fortresses of the two first-named towns still
held out, so that the 12th Army Corps and the Prussian Guards had
tracks farther to the south perforce allotted to them. The central
road from Châlons through Epernay is cut near Dormans, by the road
that comes from Rheims, by Ville en Tardenoise, and thence continues
its course through the beautiful valley of the Marne, threading
successively, as in a string, the pretty towns of Château Thierry, La
Ferté sous Jouarre and Meaux. Meaux was a pretty town once before the
deluge came upon it. This central road was set down as common property
for the right flank of the fourth army and the left flank of the third.
Along it lay the track of the 6th Army Corps, which, spared at Sedan,
was to head the advance on Paris. Later it was to be traversed by the
2nd Bavarians, and later still by the 11th Army Corps, which had been
left in the rear when its fellows marched out of the valley of the
Meuse.

Dispositions covering a front so extensive, and having an object so
vast and so momentous, were not made in a day. The men had to be rested
after feats of fighting and marching not surpassed in the history of
the world; supplies had to be accumulated near; communications had to
be everywhere perfected and made good. Meanwhile the King took his ease
in the archbishop’s palace. Bismarck, in his yellow and white cap,
stalked about the town alone, disregarding with a grim indifference
the crowds of beggar boys who fancied they could recognise benevolence
in the fact that they were not summarily sworn at by reason of their
importunity, and only found out their mistake when they had haunted the
stern man persistently to no purpose for half the length of a street.
Moltke paced the aisles of the cathedral for hours at a time, with
set face and abstracted eye, his hands ever clasped behind his back.
Captains and subalterns, dashing Freiwilligen and gay Vicefeldwebel,
made merry over the flowing champagne in the hotels and cafés, and
wished that there was a Rheims at every halting spot. The soldiers
ate and drank, sauntered and stared; dealing justly in uncouth and
fragmentary French with the shopkeepers of the town, and essaying in
a sheepish way to make a little love to the girls, whose coquetry
overcame even the conventional hatred. But although _festina lente_
was the motto, there was no time to be wasted. On the 7th of September
General von Tümpling, commanding the 6th army corps, had already his
head-quarters in Dormans. The German Uhlans who, when they had ridden
into Rheims on the Sunday previous, had all but seen the backs of
Vinoy’s men retreating, were far up the valley feeling the front at
least two days before I reached Dormans on the 8th of September. I
wonder how many British tourists are familiar with the rich, placid
beauty of the valley of the Marne, otherwise than as seen from the
window of a railway carriage. It will amply repay a much more detailed
inspection. On the afternoon I climbed a little hill above Dormans,
which overlooks the valley for several miles in both directions, and
the view well repaid the exertion. On either side of the river lie flat
and fertile “haughs,” as the alluvial tracts are called in Scotland;
and out of them rise almost imperceptibly the swelling knolls clad
with vineyards in which the frequent villages nestle snugly. I counted
ten villages from my hill-top. The whole valley was as quiet as if
there was nothing but peace in the world. Presently, from one of the
villages over against me, there arose the swell of military music; the
band of a regiment was quartered in it, and was treating the villagers
to a concert. I do not think I ever saw such a wealth of fruit of all
kinds as there was around Dormans. For the first time I realised how
the Israelites might have been compelled to resort to a stake borne by
two men to carry wherewithal the trusses of grapes from the vines of
Eshcol. Apples and pears hung by the roadside like hips and haws in
our hedgerows at home, alternated by trees laden with delicious golden
plums. It made one glad that the troops were marching in peace through
the valley of Dormans. It would have been desecration to sully this
Eden by making it a battle-field. A quiet race were the people of the
valley, sighing for quietude and for liberty, without plebiscites, and
without severe taxation, to till their soil and live prosperously in
their quiet way. They craved a strong and moderate Government, be it
what it might—monarchy or republic. The landlady of an auberge where
I halted for a short time put the matter to me very pithily. “I don’t
care a sou about politics,” said she, “and we have been taught to abhor
the Prussians, and specially to detest Bismarck; but, oh, how heartily
do I wish that in France we had such a one as Bismarck!”

Now that the strain of the forced marching was somewhat abated, it
was instructive to note how the Germans were resuming their rigour in
little matters of detail and discipline. I saw the officer of the day
turn out the quarter-guard the evening I arrived in Dormans, and he
bullied the men as if the regiment had been in barracks, because their
buttons were not polished and their coat-cuffs not turned down. The
soldiers were everywhere brushing and beating their clothes, or mending
holes in them. In a shoemaker’s shop there was a comical-looking row of
soldiers, all sitting solemnly against the wall, each with a boot in
his hand, waiting for his turn with the funny little French shoemaker,
of whose continual jabber they understood not one word, but courteously
replied with the invariable “Ja! Ja!” The Dormans tailor was hard at
work repairing officers’ uniforms; I wonder whether he was patriotic
enough not to utilise the “cabbage.”

Von Tümpling seemed in no hurry to advance from Dormans. Had he been
dallying in Rheims, one might have apprehended that he had found his
Capua, but pleasant place as was Dormans, every one said there were
more eligible quarters and equally beautiful spots farther down the
valley of the Marne. The truth is he was waiting till the 3rd army on
his left should get approximately abreast of him. He was certainly
near enough to his own front. On the 9th we started off with intent
to make Château Thierry before we halted. About eighteen kilometres
from Dormans we came to a bridge. The bridge itself was sound enough,
but on the other side of it an embankment on which the road was made
had been cut across by a trench. This was evidently the work of an
enemy, but of an enemy destitute of the guidance of engineers, and
ignorantly eager to oppose the German progress. As might have been
expected, the pitiful trench had been no great obstacle. It had been
partially and hastily bridged, and thus we knew that friends were still
ahead. About a kilometre farther we entered the village of Crezancy.
Nobody had anything to say to us as we passed through the village; but
when we got on the top of the hill beyond it, we found a sergeant’s
guard along with an officer. The latter told us that the post was the
extreme infantry advance, and that we must not pass it. A few horsemen
were scouting farther, but he had no assurance as to the state of
the country, and he could not allow us to go on any farther. He was
a fixture, he understood, for the day, and the forward movement had
for the time been arrested. So there was no resource but to return to
Dormans, where we learned that the head-quarters of the 6th Corps were
not to move till the 11th, and perhaps not then.

Impatient of the inaction, it occurred to us on the morning of the
9th to start off in a southerly direction, inclining a little to the
rear, so that thus we should strike the head of the more southerly
advance. The place where we intersected the first east and west road
was a village called Igny-le-Jard. There were no Germans there, and
the villagers said they had seen none but a few scattered Uhlans. Our
road then lay through a fine forest, intersected with rides for hunting
purposes, and full of deer. At Mareaille, on its farther side, we
came upon an advanced infantry-post—there was nothing beyond except
cavalry, said the officer who commanded the handful of men. A few
miles farther to the south, and we reached the charming village of
Corbellieu, lying snugly by the little river Surmelin. In the environs
was a beautiful chateau, surrounded with grounds, everywhere adorned
with parterres of flowers, and intersected by artificial streams and
lakes, crossed by bridges of fanciful construction. For miles the road
lay through avenues of fine trees, on either side of which were rich
farms or beautiful villas. We had passed Corbellieu for some miles,
still edging rearward, when the towers of a castle became visible,
and soon we entered the pretty village of Montmort, at the end of
which, raised high over it on an eminence, partly natural and partly
artificial, stands the castle of the same name. At one time, I doubt
not, a castle of great strength must have occupied the summit of
the commanding eminence. The site is one which the castle-builders
of the middle ages must have thoroughly appreciated. From the wall
encircling the esplanade I dropped an apple sheer into the street,
two hundred feet below; a double moat surrounds the abrupt height,
the top of which is reached externally by winding paths; internally,
by a winding staircase up a turret built into the rock. The castle
itself is comparatively modern. It was the property and the residence
of the Marquis de Montmort, who, along with his daughter, had very
sensibly remained at home to receive the Germans instead of running
away into Paris. He had for his guests the head-quarters of a brigade
of the 5th Army Corps, the officers of which, in consideration of the
courtesy of the Marquis in doing the honours of his own house, exerted
all their efforts to prevent intrusion on his privacy and towards the
preservation of the amenities of the charming place.

Quitting Montmort we pushed on a few miles farther south to Etoges,
which is on the great road. No German sentries were posted outside of
it. We traversed it from one end to the other, and found it wholly
French. Having left our carriage at the village auberge, we strolled
towards a fine chateau in the environs, at the gate of which a
courteous French gentleman accosted us. He was the son of the owner
of the chateau, M. de Kirchener, son of an engineer officer who
distinguished himself greatly in the Prussian service in the campaigns
of 1813 and 1814. M. de Kirchener is himself married to a sister of
the Duchess de Montebello. The old gentleman himself came out to meet
us, in a little trepidation, and knotting around his waist as he came
a scarf of the “Succour for the Wounded” pattern. The father and son
courteously joined in urging our removal from the auberge to the more
pleasant quarters which the chateau afforded; and there we spent the
night with not a German soldier, so far as we knew, nearer to us than
the outposts outside Montmort. There were, however, German troops
nearer Paris than Etoges, two squadrons of cavalry having passed
through it in the morning.

Next morning (the 10th) we started westward for Montmirail, with intent
to traverse a district memorable in the first campaign of 1814 as that
in which, in a rapid series of battles within a very small space,
Napoleon destroyed the army of Blucher. Soon after quitting Etoges
we came upon the fine plain of Champ Aubert, on which was fought the
battle of the 10th February, 1814. A monument stands in the centre of
the battle-field, erected by “the Emperor Napoleon III.” A couple of
kilometres farther on we reached the village of Vauchamps, outside
which took place the battle of the same name on the 14th February,
in which Blucher’s discomfiture was consummated. Passing through the
pretty little town of Montmirail, we crossed a deep gorge and came out
on a noble plateau, almost on the verge of which stands a fine fluted
column, surmounted by a gilded eagle and bearing on its pedestal the
announcement, dated 15th August, 1866, that “this monument was erected
by the orders of the Emperor Napoleon III. to indicate the spot on
which stood the Emperor Napoleon I. when he directed the efforts of the
French army in the battle of Montmirail on the 11th February, 1814.” On
the other sides of this pretentious monument are the names of the other
victories:—Feb. 12th, Les Caquerets, Nesle, Château Thierry; Feb. 11th,
Montmirail, Marchais; Feb. 10th, Champ Aubert; Feb. 14th, Vauchamps.

At Montmirail we passed through the advance brigade of the 5th Army
Corps, which we had seen in Montmort the day before. The previous day
had been a rest day, and the troops were fresh and thoroughly in good
condition and spirits. About six miles in front of Montmirail, at a
village called Vieuxmaisons, we found the battalion quartering itself
which constituted the vanguard. There was nothing beyond except a few
cavalry outposts, and although it was ascertained that there were no
French troops in the immediate front, there was reason to believe, the
officer told us, that there were detached little parties rancorously
scouring the country, who might be more awkward customers for people
like ourselves to encounter than regular soldiery. His advice coincided
with our own opinion that we had got to the end of our tether on this
lower road. There would be a further advance next day, no doubt, but
in the meantime Vieuxmaisons afforded nothing whatever calculated to
sustain life, it having borne the brunt of a squadron of hussars on the
previous night. The officers of the 58th, a fine Silesian regiment,
made us heartily welcome to a share of their rations, but thought it
advisable to regain the valley of the Marne. So with a hearty farewell
to the good fellows of the Posener Regiment, we struck across the
low sierras to Château Thierry, crossing as we did so the scene of
the battle of Nesle and close to Château Thierry the battle-field of
the same name, where Napoleon annihilated the remains of the corps
of Sacken, which he had beaten previously at Montmirail; and on our
arrival found that Von Tümpling’s head-quarters had come on into
Château Thierry in the course of the afternoon. Château Thierry was
taken and retaken several times, and three times sacked in the campaign
of 1814, but had long lost the traces of its early misfortunes. On
the occasion of the present visit of the Prussians there was no sack,
and, indeed, very little scarcity. The people had for the most part
stayed at home, and, disregarding the lies of the French newspapers
as to the conduct of the German troops, had laid in goodly stores
of eatables and drinkables, for which they found a ready market, at
no doubt remunerative prices. A sagacious citizen told me he would
not at all mind if the Prussians were to stop a month in the place,
for they were far better customers than French soldiers, and most of
the population seemed to be much of his mind. The officers chiefly
frequented the Café du Midi, close to the Hôtel de l’Eléphant on the
esplanade, and consumed their beer and coffee much as if they had been
in the Fatherland. In this place I saw the first manifestation of a
feeling of impotent insolence on the part of Frenchmen. As most readers
are doubtless aware, it is the custom in Germany to bow to the company
on entering a café, and to remain uncovered while the stay lasts.
While the café was at its fullest two young Frenchmen of the town
entered with some noise, and without any recognition of the company
laid hold of cues and monopolised the only billiard-table. At first
they were unobserved by the German officers, but their loud laughter
and exclamations presently attracted attention. Each wore his cap
cocked on his head, and they two were the only covered persons in the
room. A German lieutenant quietly arose, and removing the cap of one
of them made him a bow, quietly said, “Pardon, Monsieur,” hung the cap
on a peg, and sat down. The young fellow looked as if he would fain be
angry if he dared, but thought better of it, and went on with his game.
His companion did not take the hint, but cocked his cap all the more
defiantly, and I heard him whisper to his companion that he would not
have stood the insult if offered to himself. A German officer sitting
near me heard this too. He got up, walked solemnly to the Frenchman,
took off his cap, chucked it out of the door, looked the Frenchman
in the face for a minute, and then went back to his seat. The young
fellow made a lame attempt to explain that he wore his cap because he
had a cold, but nobody noticed him, and the pair went off, looking as
impudent as they thought was safe.

On the 12th the head-quarters of the 6th Corps moved forward to the
village of Nogent l’Artaud; but as several regiments had gone forward,
we did so too, in hopes of being able to spend the night in La Ferté
sous Jouarre. From Nogent the valley of the Marne is exceptionally
beautiful and fertile. The villages are plentiful and full of good
houses, lavishly decked with flowers, and every slope is covered with
vineyards. At times the valley is so narrow that there is room only
for the river and the road, and the railway has to betake itself into
a tunnel; then it widens into a charming flat on which lies a pretty
village. I do not think that I ever saw a more beautiful village, both
externally and internally, than Charly, about two miles west of Nogent.
Almost all the houses were villas, and they stood embowered among trees
amid a wealth of flowers. The soldiers, of which the place was full,
seemed imbued with the influence of its beauty, and respected its
amenity as far as was possible. From Charly we went forward to Croutes,
and then on to Luzancy, where we found the 51st Regiment quartered,
and then we knew that there was no La Ferté for us that night. The
51st constitute the extreme infantry advance, and whenever one saw it
one might be sure there is nothing further but a handful of horsemen.
Luzancy, a very little place, was full to repletion. We returned,
therefore, to Croutes, and found as much crowding there; so, instead of
retracing our steps to Nogent, we crossed the Marne and made for the
railway station of Nanteuil-Saacy, which we saw opposite with a couple
of houses beside it. One of the houses was deserted and stripped. In
the other we found a man and his wife, but the lady was doing her
duty by her country in this its hour of need by adding a unit to its
population. In these circumstances we took up our quarters in the
waiting-room of the station, sleeping on the seats, and lighting our
fire to boil our coffee-water with a basketful of used tickets. Next
morning we came on to La Ferté in advance of head-quarters.

The white fog so characteristic of the Marne valley was still hanging
