over the pretty town of La Ferté, when, about half-past four in the
morning of Wednesday the 14th, we drove over the quaint old bridge on
our road forward to Meaux. All round lay the 6th Army Corps, the honest
fellows still slumbering soundly in the semi-darkness. As we drove
forward through the fog, there were but few signs of our having been
preceded by any large body of men. As day broke, the soft roadsides
showed tokens of having been trodden by cavalry, and the hoof-prints
had not been beaten out, as they invariably were, when infantry men
had come tramping after the horsemen. Seven miles beyond La Ferté,
still in the thick fog, we reached the village of Trilport, about
three miles from Meaux, and here were a few men of the 51st, and a
slender guard in front of the Mairie. The sentry shouted something
unintelligible as we passed him—it was not “Halt!” and so we did not
care to pull up. But when we had traversed quite half the fine bridge
which forms a continuation of the little town, a “Halt!” in loud and
peremptory tones, came from a dim figure half obscured by the fog.
It came again still sharper and louder as we continued to advance,
our horse having a good deal of way on him. Then the figure stretched
out an arm, and, snatching the bridle, sent the animal back on his
haunches, shouting as he did so, “Donnerwetter! can’t you see the
bridge is down?” Down it certainly was, and very near down were we,
too, in the fog. The arch nearest Meaux, one of considerable span, had
been utterly shattered by gunpowder, and the débris lay in the water,
creating quite a tempestuous little rapid. As we were thanking and
questioning the sentry, a gust of wind suddenly swept the fog away, and
there became visible, two hundred yards or so up stream, the wreck of
the fine railway bridge, all three arches of which had been blown up.
Gaunt and grim stood the shattered arches in the sombre light of the
morning, contrasting strangely in the sullen ruin of their appearance
with the busy scene in the intervening space. The pioneers of the 6th
Corps were just finishing a bridge which they had hastily improvised.
A pontoon train was hourly expected, but meanwhile the pioneers with
their axes had constructed a very creditable pile bridge. Now they were
causewaying the approaches to the timber platform with grassy sods
sliced from the bank. It was good to see the system and purpose with
which everyone worked. An officer stood by formally to superintend
operations, but he seldom needed to take the cigar from his mouth to
utter an order. The division of labour principle was carried out to
its fullest extent, and the result was speed, order, and efficiency.
As a special favour, this gentleman permitted our carriage to cross
before the bridge was quite finished, and in advance of a battery of
artillery, the horses of which were champing their bits with impatience
at the halt in the chill morning air.

The broken bridges on the Marne, at Trilport, were not the only
obstacles which the French engineers had attempted to interpose between
the Germans and Meaux. There was the tunnel at Nanteuil, a really
serious impediment. Had all the French operations on this line been
as thorough as this, the German advance would have been materially
delayed. But in other cases the engineers must either have been
consummately stupid or have built largely on a supposititious stupidity
on the part of the Germans. When they destroyed the bridges in the
direct line, they invariably left standing some convenient substitute
capable of being utilised by the easy expedient of going a few hundred
yards either right or left. Thus a mile beyond the Trilport ruin a
bridge on the canal had also been destroyed. But a wily Uhlan had
ridden down the towing-path till he came to a handy if rather narrow
wooden structure, about half a mile to the left, and then come back
on to the chaussée, and chalked on a convenient post the laconic but
intelligible word “Links.” “Links” (left) we went accordingly, crossed
the canal, and reached the outskirts of Meaux, where there was another
bridge down. This time the direction was “Rechts,” and obedience to
it soon brought us into the town, where there were nothing as yet but
a regiment of hussars and a few sergeants that had come on in advance
to take up quarters in the town and the neighbouring villages for the
troops. In the course of the afternoon the greater part of the 6th
Army Corps arrived in the neighbourhood of Meaux; comparatively few
men, however, being quartered in the town itself, in anticipation
of the accommodation requisite for Royal head-quarters, which were
expected from Château Thierry next day. General von Tümpling had
his head-quarters at Quincy Segy, a little village with a beautiful
château, about six kilometres to the south of Meaux, but divided from
it by a more serious obstacle than the trivial distance, in the shape
of a destroyed bridge on a branch of the canal. A Jäger regiment formed
the infantry foreposts in a line of villages, the centre of which was
Claye, about five miles to the front; and the 8th and 9th Regiments of
hussars were on patrol and vedette duty out to the front of the Jägers.
They had pushed reconnaissances as near to Paris as the village of
Gagny, close under Fort Nogent, and their report was that there was
no French force outside the ring of forts. But there had been a few
trifling skirmishes between detachments of French cavalry covering
the engineers in their work of destruction, and the German patrols.
Two or three German dragoons had been shot and a few French prisoners
taken. And the 5th Army Corps’ advance guard lay around Coulommiers,
considerably to the left rear. To the north, and also rather in the
rear, was the bulk of the Maas army, but its cavalry were reported
to have pushed patrols on the north-west, close up to Gonesse and
Montmorency.

On the morning of the 16th, while riding out to the cavalry foreposts
in company with a hussar officer, we suddenly came, on the great road
in front of Claye, on a little posse of French lancers, following a
civilian who carried a white flag. On the top of a little knoll the
lancers halted, and then retreated, leaving the “parlementaire” to come
forward alone. When he came within hail, it turned out that he was
the Second Secretary of the British Legation in Paris, and the bearer
of a communication from Lord Lyons to Count Bismarck. He got a mount
from one of the hussars, who vacated his saddle for the purpose, and
was escorted into the camp by a sergeant and a couple of men. Great
interest was naturally excited among the German troops when the news
spread of the advent of a “parlementaire.” A report had been current
a couple of days before that the defence of Paris was abandoned, and
although that had been contradicted, a wide-spread belief existed
that the Parisians were not prepared when confronted with the actual
realities of a siege, to let the matter go to extremities. The arrival
of the English secretary was accepted as in support of this view, and
everybody was anxious for the King and Bismarck to come on toward the
front. The Royal head-quarters arrived early in the afternoon of the
same day, and I learned, however, in the course of the evening, from
a reliable source, that Mr. Malet was the bearer of a communication
emanating, in the first instance, from Lord Granville, having for its
object that proposal relative to mediation which led to the historical
interviews at Ferrières, which Jules Favre terminated by futile
gasconade.

Mr. Malet was courteous enough to give a few details regarding the
condition of Paris, and the incidents of his journey from the capital
to the German foreposts. He described Paris as being in a seething
and excited condition, with divided councils, and a huge mass of
pseudo-soldiery, the individual bravery of each individual component
part of which might be unquestionable, but the general discipline of
which was so loose and flaccid as almost to disentitle, in his opinion,
the body of semi-armed men to the appellation of soldiers. He had spent
a night on his road, and the Gardes Mobiles where he halted had made
the cheerful suggestion that he should sleep “under the beautiful
stars.” Mr. Malet, reserving the question whether he would have any
objection to sleep “under the beautiful stars,” if they were visible,
pointed out the fact that they were not, and requested other sleeping
accommodation than that afforded by the earth as a mattress and the
sky as a canopy. This he ultimately obtained, his escort sleeping in
the adjoining shed. He saw evidences of the most flagrant disregard
of discipline on the part of the troops lying outside the defences of
Paris. The men coolly refused to get up and take their turn of duty on
guard when ordered to do so by their officers, although, to judge from
appearances, there would have been no hesitation had the order been
to get up and go into action. It says not a little for Trochu that he
could have constructed a measurably serviceable army out of materials
so unpromising.

Mr. Malet returned towards Paris next morning, leaving in the same
way as he had come, under an escort of German hussars. Those who
knew the nature of the risk he ran felt considerable apprehension
for his safety. In the course of the morning the French had fired
upon two flags of truce, and it was just a chance if Mr. Malet
should be recognized. However, he got among the defenders of Paris
without detriment, and, as we afterwards learnt, reached the British
Embassy—there was yet a British Embassy in Paris—in safety.

PART II.

METZ.

CHAPTER I.

THE SIEGE.


Instructions caused me at this juncture to quit the advance upon Paris,
and I left Meaux on the morning of the 17th September. It may not be
altogether devoid of interest to recall the position of the German
armies on that day so far as accorded with my knowledge. The troops
had hourly been pouring to the front since first the 6th Army Corps
crossed the pile-bridge at Trilport. The object, it was already known,
was completely to invest the capital. The 5th Army Corps, with the
head-quarters of the Crown Prince, striking south from Coulommiers, was
pushing on through the villages to Chaulmes, and thence to Brie, on its
road to cross the Seine at Villeneuve St. George; the pontoon train was
in front between the field artillery and the infantry. The 5th might
have marched faster, but that it had to tarry till the 2nd Bavarian
Army Corps (still on the road from La Ferté) should have got up with
it, taking the more circuitous route through the villages of Montceaux,
Vanteuil, Boutigny, and so on to the great Melun road at Segy. The
5th and the Bavarians were then to push forward round in the direction
of Versailles; the interval between their right and the Marne to be
occupied by the 6th Army Corps. The destruction of the bridge over the
Marne at Lagny had occasioned some temporary inconvenience, but this
had already been remedied by the erection of a pontoon. The artillery
of the 6th Army Corps had all gone to the south front. General Vinoy,
with a field army, mostly consisting of regular troops, was reported by
the Uhlans to be outside the cordon of forts on the south of Paris; and
it seemed as if the 3rd Army might have to fight for its passage across
the Seine. The Saxons were in force at Dammartin, touching our right,
while the 4th Army Corps, the right wing of the Maas Army, was feeling
round the north side of Paris. The King was expected to move forward in
a few days, and occupy the château of Ferrières.

The bridges over the canals as I drove back to La Ferté on my return
journey had already been repaired, but the Marne was still crossed
by the pile bridge, although there was an alternative pontoon lower
down the stream. Some little distance on the Meaux side of La Ferté
I encountered the advance of the 2nd Bavarian Corps, and I had to
strike off to the right on the road to Montmirail, till I came to a
cross road, which brought me down again into the valley of the Seine
upon Nogent, right into the rear of the Bavarians. There were no
troops on this road, but it had been extensively traversed during
the few preceding days, and the people were only just beginning to
return to their homes. The villagers were very timid, and, I may add,
very hungry, all along it, but it was wonderful how little damage the
Germans had done during their transit. The poultry still strutted
about the road through the village, and there was hardly a broken pane
of glass to be seen. Furniture and valuables the people had secreted
before the troops had come upon them, and the chief injury seemed to
have been sustained by the straw stacks, upon which the men had drawn
freely to make beds wherewithal in the empty houses. Over Nogent the
wave had passed a little earlier, and the place had proportionately
recovered more. Meat, both beef and mutton, was hanging in the open
butcher’s shop. All the way to Château Thierry was visible the eager
desire of the peasantry and farmers to overtake temporarily arrested
agricultural labour. Except for the occasional traces of a bivouac and
now and then a broken down vine stem, there was no evidence that a vast
army had so recently traversed this road. The children played around
the doors beside the old crones at their knitting, the men and women
were in the fields working with a clumsy assiduity, and it seemed to
me not uncheerfully. The vine-pickers were in the vineyards, filling
their creels with the great clusters of somewhat over-ripe grapes;
but their number was, no doubt, smaller than had peace reigned in
the land. There were cattle in the pastures and horses at the plough
and between the shafts of the carts. I do not think any one not aware
of the fact could have gathered from outward appearance that anything
of exceptional importance had recently occurred in the beautiful and
smiling valley. In Château Thierry were a handful of Bavarian bullock
drivers and a few footsore infantrymen. The people of this beautiful
town had never forsook it in numbers so great as had the inhabitants of
some other places; nor had they any reason to repent of their courage.
Now it seemed as if they had fallen again into the somnolent routine
of a provincial town. The burghers gossiped on the pavement, the young
ladies promenaded in the avenues of noble trees, the lads squatted on
the brink of the Marne, angling with a lazy pertinacity. The Hôtel de
l’Eléphant was empty, changed indeed from that Elephant which I had
seen a few days before, when Von Tümpling’s staff were buzzing the
bottles in the _salle à manger_, and the cook was in a white heat at
the multiplicity of orders. The landlord, a gentleman himself of the
elephantine pattern, confided to me, with a sage wag of the head, that
he would be heartily glad to see _Messieurs les Prussiens_ come back to
stop as long as they could make it convenient, for they were the best
customers he had ever had, never grumbled, and invariably rejected red
wine for champagne, for which they paid promptly the price demanded.

At Dormans were a considerable number of Germans, but no regular
force—only sick and convalescents, and stragglers, chiefly belonging
to the 63rd Regiment. Neither here did I find any misery. The
disconsolateness of Madame at the hotel only proceeded from the
circumstance that she had a son in MacMahon’s Chasseurs de France, of
whose fate at Sedan she knew nothing. The shops were all open, and
the show in the windows was by no means despicable. Next morning, in
and about Ville en Tardenoise, I met and passed the whole of the 11th
Army Corps on its march to the front. The men looked fresh and well,
and the horses in capital condition; but the ranks were considerably
thinner than before that terrible struggle on to the plateau above
Floing. Rheims was occupied by a division of Bavarians and another of
Würtemburgers. Although this town had been in a state of remarkable
ferment on the occasion of the first entry of the Germans, it had got
used to the situation in a very short time, and now it looked as if the
German occupation was a thing from time immemorial. It was Sunday, and
citizens and Bavarians were streaming out of the noble old cathedral
and the various churches of the city in all the apparent friendliness
of fellow-worshippers. Among the poorer classes in Rheims there had
been considerable hardship, owing to the dearness of provisions and
the obligation incumbent on them of providing food for the soldiers
billeted on them. The German authorities had, however, averted further
distress from this source, by establishing a commissariat for troops
billeted on all those unable to support them.

Although the route between Rethel and Sedan had been traversed by a
large portion of the German host on its south-western march after
Sedan, the district, at least as far as Boulzicourt, seemed to have
either suffered very little, or to have recuperated with surprising
alacrity. As in the valley of the Marne, field operations were going on
briskly. Agricultural horses seemed plentiful, and I saw several great
herds of cattle in the flat water meadows. I felt a deep interest in
the portion of road between Flize and Sedan. I had traversed it several
times before—once on the day of the battle of Sedan, when a few scared
old women, sitting crouching by their black hearths amidst the crashing
of the cannonading, and an occasional scanty group of timid-eyed men
peeping out from behind the stone dykes, were the only evidences of
resident population in villages that bore the red-fresh impress of the
fangs of the war-dog.

The people were gradually coming back to Flize, Dom de Mesnil, and
Fernois, the three villages between Boulzicourt and Sedan. In Flize it
seemed that there must be plenty of wine, as well as plenty to drink
it, for the auberge was crammed with the blue blouses. The females of
the families had fallen into the old familiar way of sitting knitting
in the sunshine outside their doors. But many houses were still closed
up, and here and there the blackened skeleton of a roofless house, a
grim memorial of the hot shell-fire. Over against Donchery the peasants
were fishing the dead horses out of the Meuse, and burying them in
great holes. It is impossible to describe the emotions which almost
every step of this road called up in my mind—associated as it was with
events fixed so indelibly in my memory. Here was the little house at
which I had seen Napoleon alight from his carriage on the morning of
the 2nd September. A child was playing on the spot where the Emperor
and Bismarck sat in that earnest colloquy of theirs which I watched so
intently. A woman was spreading clothes on the path in the potato plot,
up and down which I had seen the ruined man moodily pacing as he tugged
his dishevelled moustache, after Bismarck had drawn the white cap with
the yellow band down over his eyes, and posted off to take counsel with
his master. To the left was the ruined railway bridge over the Meuse.
In my immediate front was the Château Bellevue, before which I had seen
the perturbed meeting of the monarchs—Napoleon bowing low to hide the
workings of his face; Wilhelm studying not to hide his emotion. Away
across the low alluvial plain and the sweep of the river, that ghastly
schlacht-feld sloped upward from the low bluffs,—now brown and bare,
but, when I saw it once before, a fearful space in which surged to and
fro, in the gaps of the white smoke, the heaving masses of maddened
combatants—when I saw it yet again a ghastly expanse of death, here
chequered with dead grey horses and gay-clad dead riders, there flecked
with the more sombre uniforms of the German soldiers lying thick where
the mitrailleuse hail had beaten them to the earth. Below it was the
island, with the château in its centre, on to which I had seen the
ceaseless stream of French prisoners pouring on the 3rd—the once-gay
gardens of the château now bare, and the trees stripped of their
foliage. And farther to my right lay Sedan itself, with the German
landwehr sentry on its ramparts; and farther to the right, Bazeilles of
ghastly memory.

The scene had changed since last I looked upon it. As I drove down
the gentle slope into Sedan I met the townspeople, male and female,
the Cook’s tourists, and the relic-hunting riff-raff of Belgium,
sauntering in dilettante fashion along the road, which three weeks
ago had a ghastly fringe of corpses. The relic-hunters were picking
up the knapsacks out of the ditch, and stealing the sword bayonets
that had been set up in lieu of tombstones. In the open space between
the enceinte and the minor fortifications stood the captured French
artillery—five long straight rows of cannons, seventy-five in each
row, besides numerous mitrailleuses and tumbrils. A single sentry did
duty over the vast park. In the moats, German soldiers were punting,
and from the bridges they were bobbing for weapons of war hidden by
the water, occasionally bringing to the surface the bloated corpses
of those who had erstwhile wielded these same weapons. All the shops
in the town were open, and business seemed tolerably brisk. That the
spirit of enterprise was not wholly dead was evinced by huge posters
announcing “an immense consignment of cigars,” and every _Débit de
tabac_ was doing a roaring trade. The convalescent wounded, with pallid
faces and thin limbs, were placidly sunning themselves on the steps of
the public buildings or tottering feebly about the streets. One young
sous-officer interested me greatly. The reader may have seen the matrix
of a branch covered by a round plaster of clay to prevent the canker
from getting into the heart of the tree. This quiet, pale-faced lad had
lost his left arm at the shoulder. It seemed to me that the shoulder
itself had been taken out close to the collar-bone, and the bandages
stood out just like the clay bole to which I have referred. He must
have made a very quick recovery to be able to walk about not three
weeks after a capital operation of this nature.

The siege of Metz, to watch which was my new duty, had virtually begun
on that woeful day for the French arms when the ravine of Gravelotte
was stormed, and Bazaine, baulked in his last desperate effort to keep
hold of any of the main roads leading Parisward from Metz, was hurled
back under the guns of Fort St. Quentin. On the day after Gravelotte,
the soldiers of Prince Frederick Charles and Steinmetz had begun, in
the prompt and thorough German manner, to girdle the environs of Metz
with a series of defensive earthworks, linked together by a continuous
cincture of entrenchment, so well contrived and so formidable as to
reduce to a minimum the apparent discrepancy in strength between a vast
army free to make its concentrations on any selected point, and a host
more numerous, indeed, but necessarily attenuated as regarded any one
point by reason of the immensity of the circle around the radius-border
of which it had to show an unbroken front. That there was a time,
during which Bazaine could have hewed his way through this girdle, if
he had commenced his effort with discretion, and prosecuted it with
daring energy and regardlessness of loss, it does not appear that in
the nature of things there can be any reasonable doubt. What effect
his emancipation would have produced, had it been effected soon after
Gravelotte, is a question involving considerations so various that,
in such a work as this, I may be excused referring to them in detail.
But it is obvious that had Bazaine been a leader of originality,
self-reliance, promptness, and boldness, instead of, as I estimate him,
an honest, rather sluggish man, thankful to get his initiative from
some one else, instead of ready to take his own initiative—a military
formalist mainly, distracted between his political and his military
obligations, and utterly unversed in the rare art of regalvanising
beaten troops,—had Bazaine been another sort of man, he might surely
have seriously complicated von Moltke’s strategy.[3] Suppose that he
had cut his way out in the obviously most eligible direction, somewhere
between Plappeville and Maizières les Metz, and marched at the pace
of the first Napoleon on the track of the Maas army. The Germans can
march, but they cannot fly; the hot energy of the Red Prince could not
have accomplished the impossibility of drawing in from the fringe of
the wide circle an army strong enough to give Bazaine battle before
he had won the start by several marches. It was the Maas army which
met MacMahon in the teeth at Buzancy and Nouart as he came lumbering
through the Argonne into the Maas valley; the third army had swung
round, not in time to head him, but to take him on the right flank,
extended in dishevelled march all along the valley. But if Bazaine had
been close on the track of the Crown Prince of Saxony, the latter would
have been in a vice. He could not have fought facing both ways. Had
he elected to arrest MacMahon, Bazaine would have been upon him from
behind; had he faced about and met Bazaine, the cannon of MacMahon
would have been roaring on his new rear. His position, in fact, would
have been untenable; and it seems plain that the only course open to
him would have been to slip out of the _crux_ to the southward, and
thus to have allowed the coalescence of the army of Châlons and the
army from Metz. The outcome of a junction so formidable it would be
folly to attempt to define. The French situation would still have
been precarious—perhaps nearly as bad as it was the day before Sedan.
For on three sides three German armies would still have hemmed it in,
and there was nothing behind it but the frontier. On the other hand,
there would surely have been much possible to an army of 300,000 men,
supposing the troops in good fighting heart, and the generals worth the
skin of an orange.

But Bazaine lay supine behind his forts till he got his initiative
in the shape of the plan to co-operate with MacMahon. It seems clear
that the planners of that general’s disastrous expedition contemplated
that his march would be bloodless till he should have reached the
north-western flank of the army environing Metz. Letters from Metz,
intercepted by the besiegers, bore testimony to the fact that MacMahon
was expected to make his appearance in that vicinity about the 30th
of August. The signal for the Metz army was to have been three bombs
fired in the air by MacMahon, and then a sortie was to be made at the
same moment that the army of Châlons was to fall on the German rear.
It is actually the case that Bazaine was cautioned against allowing
the guns of the fortress to have too much elevation when fired in
support of the sortie, to obviate the risk of the projectiles falling
in the co-operating army on the further side of the Germans. But
MacMahon had other fish to fry, and the three rockets never flashed
into Metz the signal for co-operative action; so on the 31st, in
blind bewilderment, Bazaine made his first great sortie, and made it
in the direction in which success would have been most difficult to
compass—in the direction of the north-east, the church spire of St.
Barbe being seemingly his landmark. Apart from the actual difficulties
to be encountered on unfavourable ground and against exceptionally
strong opposing works, the selection of this direction for such a
sortie seems simply inexplicable, unless Bazaine contemplated, not a
junction with MacMahon, but a bold push into the Rhine provinces—a
conception no admirer has ever dreamt of attributing to him. MacMahon
was expected from the north-west. Bazaine, had he cut through on
the north-east, would have had to march to the junction round a
third of the circumference of the compass, giving time for a German
concentration in the path of his circuitous march. But he failed to
break out: it may have been for want of persistency, a faculty in
which he seems to have been weak; it may have been that he recognised
what the Germans contend was recognisable, that the longer he tried
the worse he would fail; and after this effort, while his chances of
extrication diminished daily, his prospects, were he once outside, grew
more and more precarious. He seems in a great measure to have accepted
his fate, and solaced himself with the reflection that, at all events,
he was keeping from other work the great armies which constituted his
besiegers. That not a man of those might be judged superfluous, he
made sorties from time to time during the month of September, not of a
character suggesting an attempt to break out, but in force enough to
convince the makers of his toils that the ropes by which the captive
elephant was bound were not a whit too strong. No doubt, too, although
I believe Bazaine to have been a perfectly honest and loyal Frenchman,
he was hampered in his generalship by political considerations—of all
trammels the most ruinous to daring action. The last of these partial
sorties, most of which seem to have had supplies for their object, took
place on the 28th of September, the day before I reached the front, and
from the Germans engaged in it I gleaned some particulars respecting it.

The German foreposts occupied, in no great strength, the village of
Colombey, where there are three large châteaux, in the upper stories
of which there had been left by the inhabitants a considerable store
of grain. They had taken refuge in Metz, and probably acquainted the
French of the existence of these stores. Anyhow, in the afternoon of
the 28th the French, in large numbers, and covered by the artillery of
Fort St. Julien, made a dash at Colombey, their advance followed by
a number of empty waggons. For once they surprised the comparatively
weak German foreposts, and drove them out of Colombey. Covering their
operations by throwing forward tirailleurs into the woods to the front
and towards La Planchette, they filled the waggons with the grain
and started them on the return journey. But, meanwhile, the Prussian
artillery had come to the front, and the shells fell thick among the
Frenchmen in Colombey and the convoy on the road. The former scuttled
back in great haste under the guns of St. Julien; the waggons went on
at a gallop, but out of thirty-six only fourteen succeeded in getting
safe off. The rest were arrested _in transitu_ by the influence which
the Prussian shells exercised on the animals which drew the waggons—an
influence which manifested itself in limbs blown in a variety of
directions. There was no great loss in killed or wounded on either
side. A captain of the 44th Regiment was killed under circumstances
which were a disgrace to civilized warfare. When his detachment
was in retreat he fell wounded severely, but not mortally. His men
placed him in shelter, and then left him as they fell back. When they
recovered the village, they found the corpse of their captain mangled
barbarously—his fingers cut off for the sake of the rings he wore, and
his throat cut from ear to ear. I found the Germans justly incensed at
this atrocity.

The cargo which the train carried that left Saarbrück on the morning
of the 29th of September, was of a sufficiently diversified character.
There were country-women, with great tubs of butter, which they were
taking up to the front to find a good market; officers and soldiers,
new men and convalescents, both from sickness and wounds, on their way
to join and re-join their regiments; Saarbrück merchants going up for
orders; a few Englishmen, who nourished the idea that the fighting
was chronically in progress just outside the station at Courcelles,
and that, by walking a pace or two, they would be able to command a
bird’s-eye view of the whole affair; marketenders, who had been making
investments; and a few Frenchmen on their way to the intermediate
towns, who were doing their best to seem as German as possible. Our
way to Forbach lay through a corner of the ground on which was fought
the battle of Spicheren, and, as the train passed through the woods
near Schönecken, I saw not a few graves of the men who had fallen
in the fierce fight of the 6th of August. Near St. Avold we passed
the débris of the train which had been shattered in a collision that
brought death to several French prisoners, and at this town also we
found a large depôt of military stores and provender. There was a still
larger depôt at Falquemont, where also were collected a quantity of
artillery waggons captured from the French at Gravelotte. The station
at Courcelles was a sight. It was a centre from which radiated in
every direction long lines of waggons of every description, carrying
stores of all kinds to the different army posts, north, south, east,
and west. All around the deserted little village itself are great herds
of cattle, piles of stores, whole pyramids of medical comforts, and
long lines of sick and relay horses. The bustle was immense, and yet
everything seemed orderly. There was of course an Etappen Commando
in the station, but the worthy lieutenant who represented the said
Etappen Commando knew nothing whatever about anything. All he could or
would tell me was that Prince Frederick Charles’ headquarters were at
Corny, on the Moselle, to the south of Metz, but he did not know the
distance. He had no waggons, no horses, very little temper, and less
time to gossip. The circumstances were sufficiently unpromising. One
might as well look for quartern loaves in the kennel of Fleet-street as
for quarters in or around Courcelles; and the field-post, the only and
that a precarious means of getting to Corny, did not leave till three
in the morning. I took the resolution of pursuing the policy out of
which I often found luck eventuate, of going straight to the front till
the foreposts would let me go no further. Striking northward then, and
inclining westward from Courcelles, I passed in succession through the
villages of Laquenexy, Marsilly, Ogy, and St. Agnan. All these I found
occupied by troops, constituting the second investing line environing
the fortress and town of Metz.

All the district was dotted over with the graves of the battle fought
by Steinmetz on the 14th of August, and of men who had fallen in the
various forepost fights. From Courcelles the scene was one of utter
barrenness and desolation, wastes traversed everywhere by tracks of
waggons, chequered by abandoned bivouacs, the leafy bowers of which,
plastered with mud, stood forlorn and unused, all the troops having
been got under cover except the foreposts who necessarily had to
remain in the open. Very few of the inhabitants still remained in the
district, and of these the plight was very miserable. Agricultural
occupations were at a standstill; the vineyards, traversed by waggon
roads and cut in two by entrenchments, were utterly ruined. The houses
were wrecks, many of them wholly or partially burned by fires kindled
by the shells. In Courcelles, the only living thing not German was
a mongrel dog which stuck to his kennel when his owner ran away.
The Germans had christened him “Courcelles,” and the time-serving
rogue was already more than half German—traitor that he was. In the
forepost village of Montoy, which I visited the same evening, I found a
considerable number of inhabitants. One old woman sat placidly before
the door knitting stockings, while the troops lined the entrenchment
just in her front. I suppose she had got so used to shells and
bullets that she did not mind them, or it might be that her despair
had culminated in a sort of apathy of equanimity. The poor country
folks and villagers around Metz were in cases so miserable that it
seemed they must inevitably starve the moment that the Germans should
withdraw. Though they had horses, and though the bullets had not been
sweeping their farms, yet last autumn they durst not put a spade or
a plough into the ground. Every field holds the “sacred and precious
seed” that will germinate never till the last trumpet sounds. It is
right comforting to know that good friends—Friends in more than the
mere name—came to the aid of these poor wretches, with a promptness,
tenderness, and efficiency characteristic of a sect that ever prefer
acting to speaking. But this is anticipating. From St. Agnan I
proceeded two kilometres, still in a north-westerly direction, till I
reached the village of Flanville, where lay the 4th Regiment of East
Prussian Infantry, forming part of the 2nd division of the 1st Army
Corps. The regimental staff was comfortably located in a pleasant
château, and liking the looks of the place and the friendly ways of
the officers, I asked permission to attach myself for the time to the
regiment, a civility which was frankly and unhesitatingly granted.
I was informally taken on the strength, the good old Oberst made me
free of his modest table, the officers of No. 6 company, right hearty
fellows all, assigned me six feet by four of straw in the corner of
the drawing-room, in which there was not a scrap of furniture but a
grand piano, on which a sequence of voluntaries, sometimes accompanied
by singing, sometimes by dancing, seemed constantly going on, and
everybody was anxious to show attention to the Englander who had
strayed on to the foreposts. In the afternoon Lieutenant Werth, the
officer in command of the 6th company (vice the Hauptmann shot on the
31st of August), accompanied me on a ride round a considerable radius
of the eastern forepost circuit, and the lucidity of his explanations
enabled me to arrive at what I afterwards found was a correct idea of
the position, as it was in the end of September.

Next the Moselle, and eastward therefrom, the 8th Army Corps kept watch
and ward, its left on Ars-sur-Moselle, its right interlacing, at the
village of Magny, with the 7th Army Corps. From Magny the 7th held
the line of environment lying in the village of Peltre, the château
and house of Mercy-la-Haut, shattered and burnt, and on as far as Ars
Laquenexy. It had foreposts and _Feldwachen_ a little further forward,
but not so far as they had been a few days previously, when the
foreposts were at La Grange aux Bois. The left of the 1st Army Corps
lay in Colombey, which was also considerably burnt, feeling the right
of the 7th in Mercy-la-Haut. Its second line extended to the northward
in the villages of Ogy, Flanville, Retonfay, Château Gras, and Ste.
Barbe. The first line lay in Coincy, Montoy, Noisseville, Servigny, and
Failly. The whole of this latter line was within range of the big guns
of the great Metz outwork of St. Julien, which occupies the summit of
a mamelon. Between the first and second line there was an interval of
only about a mile. To the front there lay, first, the foreposts, then
the _Feldwachen_, and lastly the single sentries, within 1,500 yards
of St. Julien. The first line occupied a section of the continuous
entrenchment which ran right round Metz. All the villages were roughly
fortified by barricades, _chevaux-de-frise_, &c.; holes had been broken
through all the houses for firing, and, indeed, every village formed a
very respectable, if rough and ready fortress. The foreposts lay either
in single houses, also well fortified by entrenchments and barricades,
or in the field behind earthworks of no inconsiderable magnitude. The
_Feldwachen_ chiefly occupied woods or the gardens of châteaux. A
_Feldwache_ which may be taken as typical, I noticed in the village of
Lauvallière on the route to Saarlouis, two kilometres nearer Metz than
Montoy. Here lay two companies, arms piled close at hand, and ready
for a sortie from the enemy at a moment’s warning. To the left, in
the hamlet of la Plancette, was another _Feldwache_ in a deep ravine.
Here I had to dismount and walk up the slope beyond to the knoll on
which the solitary sentry kept his watch within point-blank range of a
chassepot bullet from the French works at the hamlet of Belle Croix.
We could see with the naked eye the Frenchmen moving about the forts
of les Bottes and St. Julien, and circulating about the environs both
of them and of the town, while the great eminence of Mont St. Quentin,
with its mass of fortifications on the summit, shut in the scene,
dominating the whole valley from the further side of the Moselle. All
the front was pitted over with the craters of shells. The men of one
of the regiments were erecting quite a permanent barrack of wood, the
sides covered with earth, behind an isolated brewery a little way in
the rear of Lauvallière. Further north, Noisseville was densely filled
with German troops; Nouilly, which lies between it and St. Julien, was
neutral ground, occupied by neither army, while the French were in
force in Mey and Vantoux, villages still nearer the fortress. We went
forward to the edge of a wood near Mey, accompanied by two Prussian
patrols, and got a warning to go back in the shape of a sharp fusilade
from out a garden in the environs of Mey. To complete the narrative
of the localities, the environment was taken up on the right of the
1st Army Corps by the Landwehr Division, consisting of three brigades,
each six battalions strong, under the command of General von Kummer.
They covered at that time the ground round nearly to the Moselle on
the north, but were afterwards moved to the other side of the stream,
changing places with the 10th Army Corps. The General commanding the
1st Army Corps, his Excellency von Manteuffel, lay in Ste. Barbe, a
little to our north rear. Our immediate commander was General von
Pritselwitz, the chief of the Second Division of the First Army Corps,
who had his quarters two kilometres behind us, in the little village of
Puche, where likewise was the Feldpost.

Even so early as the time of which I am writing, deserters and the
German prisoners whom Bazaine had sent back, concurred in representing
the condition of the troops inside and around Metz as being very bad
in the matter of food. They were represented as living chiefly on
horseflesh, and as having neither flour, rice, nor salt—the latter
want being the subject of much complaint. The discipline of the troops
was said to be good, and the morale of a character very different to
that of the disorganised horde who threw down their arms at Sedan.
But the German returned prisoners united in abusing the domineering
greed of the French officers, who cared nothing comparatively for
their men, provided their own wants were satisfied. This selfishness
seems forcibly to have struck the honest Germans, used as they are to
treatment so different.

It could not fail to strike even the most cursory observer that the
alertness and completeness of the German forepost system was a great
feature of the army, and one of the leading causes of its success. At
night the _Feldwache_ always went forward to the post occupied during
the day by the farthest outlying sentry. Here it broke right and left
into small picquets, leaving a strong nucleus in the centre. The
front, at a distance of 200 or 300 yards, was continually traversed by
cavalry patrols, who often rode right in among the sleeping Frenchmen,
whose system of night vigilance was not brilliant. Then there would
be a pistol shot and a round of wild chassepot firing in the dark,
and the daring Uhlan would dash out through the red legs, back to
his supports. Writing of chassepots, it may be noted that the German
forepost troops were by this time extensively armed with these weapons
to enable them to cope on equal terms with the French tirailleurs. Of
the 2nd Battalion of the 4th Regiment seventy-five men were so armed,
or rather seventy-five chassepots were in use and were transferred
from _Feldwache_ to _Feldwache_, as the changes of guard were made.
This seemed to settle the question as to the relative merits of the
Chassepot and the Zundnadelgewehr in the eyes of the Germans.

On the evening of September 29th it was officially made known to the
troops that the French were constructing three bridges across the
Moselle to the westward of Fort St. Julien, it was believed with a
view to a sortie in the direction of Maison Rouge and Woippy. About
four o’clock next morning the sky became brilliantly illuminated with
the shells under the fire of which they attempted to carry out their
design, and with the return fire with which the German artillery met
it. In the thick darkness the effect as seen from our position (about
four miles away) was magnificent. Between the louder crashes of the
cannon was heard the sharper rattle of the musketry fire, singly and in
volleys. The firing became fainter towards six o’clock, and by seven
had died away altogether—the presumption being that the French attempt
had been baulked, and this we afterwards learned was the case. A few
days previously the garrison of Thionville had surprised and captured
a German provision train, which was carried into that fortress. The
food being more needed in Metz than in Thionville, it was anticipated
that an effort would be made to fight the train through the German
environment into the beleaguered fortress. In all probability the
affair of this morning was a diversion intended to facilitate this
movement. The Germans, however, were fully alive to the contingency,
and they succeeded in foiling the attempt.

I found that the Germans were in possession of detailed drawings
and full particulars of the French troops lying around Metz, the
correctness of which I was afterwards able to verify. Bazaine’s
forces—that is to say, the army driven back on Metz after the battle
of Gravelotte—were not permitted to enter the town of Metz. The whole
army was encamped on the slopes of the outlying fortifications either
of the town or of the outlying strongholds, in the intervening country,
and in the various villages which still remained in the possession of
the French. The forts were garrisoned by Mobiles and National Guards.
Bazaine’s head-quarters were at Ban St. Martin, outside the town, and
around him lay the camp of the Imperial Guards. Opposite us on the
east side lay the 3rd Army Corps of the French army—Lebœuf’s corps.
Its forepost nearest us was in the wood in front of Mey, and the chain
extended southward to the front of the hamlet Belle Croix. The mass of
the corps lay to the rear. Within the previous few days a considerable
camp had been established on the slope of St. Julien, looking toward
us. We could see the white tents, and the red-legged gentlemen
strolling about them. Their patrols, mounted and on foot, were wont to
cross our front so near that the countenances of the men could be made
out with the glass. The German _Feldwachen_ could have picked them off
quite easily if they had chosen; but there was an order against firing
at small detached parties. The French did not show the same civility.
A single man could not show himself on the advance line without being
blazed at by a regular volley, which seldom, however, took any effect,
the firing being so wild. The guard changing was wont to be a great
time for a waste of French powder and shot all along the line, and the
field guns at Little Belle Croix made a point of spitefully throwing a
few shells on the brewery on the great chaussée constituting the chief
forepost on our right front.

When the French made a sortie, Lieutenant Werth informed me, their
scheme of advance was very characteristic. They scuttled out from the
shelter of the fortresses in the loosest skirmishing order and with
great rapidity till they had reached intermediate spaces sheltered
from the German fire, whether by woods or by the configuration of the
ground. In those they accumulated their masses, and came forward in
dense columns. But they had never, save on the great sortie of the 31st
of August, and at Colombey on the 28th September, succeeded in getting
to close quarters with the first line of the Germans. The artillery of
the latter came into play with its deadly practice, and then there was
no alternative but a rapid retreat. Their demonstrations were always
accompanied by a cannonade from Belle Croix and St. Julien, but seldom
by the discharge of field artillery. This latter circumstance had led
the Germans to the conclusion that the French were short of ammunition
for their field guns, and this seemed probable, looking to the lavish
use they made of them at Gravelotte. There must have been artillery of
great calibre in the forts, for Lieutenant Werth pointed out to me, by
Lauvallière, lying in the ditch, a huge shell, which could not have
been less than a 72-pounder. It was unexploded, a fact noticeable of a
large proportion of the French shells.

The losses that had been suffered by some of the regiments of the 1st
Army Corps since it had come within hail of Metz were stupendous. On
the 14th August, and in the minor fights of the 30th and 31st of the
same month, and those during the course of September, the 4th Regiment
lost (killed and wounded) 35 officers out of a total of 60, and 1,000
men out of 3,000. The 45th Regiment, lying in its vicinity, had lost
yet more of its officers. Out of 60 no fewer than 42 had fallen. The
result had been that a large number of Vicefeldwebels and several
sergeants had received commissions. As these were comparatively
inexperienced in their new duties, they were daily receiving assiduous
instructions from their seniors. These informal lectures were highly
interesting—not a few officers of the British service would have
greatly benefited by attending them. The troops also were drilled
daily, notwithstanding their position in front of the enemy. On the
morning of the 30th I watched a whole battalion at skirmishing drill,
and a right pretty sight it was. The recruits that had come forward
since the beginning of the war spent hours daily in perfecting their
drill, and the efficiency of the whole army was sedulously maintained
at the highest pitch.

I was present the same morning at the very interesting ceremony of the
distribution to the 4th Regiment of the iron crosses awarded to members
of it for valour in the field. The ceremony took place at Puche, the
head-quarters of the general of division, who personally distributed
the crosses in the presence of the officers of the regiment. The band
played the old regimental air, the “Hohenfriedberg,” and the colours
were on the ground. The gallant 4th had earned no fewer than twenty
crosses already, and the award seemed to have been made with the most
rigid impartiality, the men taking their full share of the coveted
distinction with their officers. The 4th is one of the old regiments
of the Prussian service, and its annals record a long and gallant
career. Its regimental march, the name of which I have just mentioned,
is secured to it by a special royal order in remembrance of the valour
which it displayed at the battle of Hohenfriedberg, fought in 1745.

To understand the means of defence which the French army enclosed in
Metz had to keep wherewithal the Germans at bay, a detailed description
of the remarkable fortifications with which the virginity of La Pucelle
was guarded may not be out of place. Although Metz is no longer French,
these works still exist in their entirety, to say nothing of their
having been strengthened, and therefore respecting them the present
tense may be used.

The town of Metz, with the great Cathedral, lies for the most part
on the east side of a branch of the Moselle, which forks off from
the main stream near La Grange aux Dames, and forms the islands of
Chambière, and lower down of Sauley and St. Simphorien. A large suburb,
however, stands on the first of these islands. The inner fortifications
surround the town continuously, with the exception of one gap to the
southward, which is covered efficiently by the branch of the Moselle
which divides the islands of Sauley and St. Simphorien. On the north
of the Ile de Chambière are two important forts on the inner line, one
at the north-western angle of the enceinte, the other detached in the
plain of the island, but connected with the enceinte by a covered way.
On the south of the inner line are the Redoubt du Pate to the east, and
the Lunette d’Arcon to the west, both connected with the enceinte by a
covered way. The western side of the inner line is covered by the great
Fort Moselle on the farther side of the main stream of the Moselle, a
double crown work of immense strength and magnitude, inclosing great
magazines and arsenals. To the south of it the bastion of Ile de Sauley
sends out a long spur of fortifications, which crosses the Moselle, and
terminates in a redoubt on the farther side. Nor is the eastern side
of the inner line less strongly protected. In front of the enceinte
looms the great double crown work of Belle Croix, to the full as large
as Fort Moselle, and more highly favoured by its natural position. To
the south of it stands Fort Gisors, a minor detached fortification
which serves to complete the circuit of connection with the Redoubt du
Pate on the south. Thus the town of Metz is, in fact, environed by two
lines of fortifications, the continuous enceinte with its bastions and
what I may term the outer cordon of the inner circle having intimate
connection with the enceinte.

Of themselves these defences would make the place all but
impregnable, but they are by no means the most important defences of
this extraordinary fortress. There is an outer circle of detached
fortifications, each of which is the complement of the other, and of
which no one can be assailed without the assailant laying his account
to be enfiladed by at least two of the others. Let me begin with these
at the Moselle, on the north of the town at La Grange aux Dames, and
work round, proceeding first in an easterly direction. After climbing
the bank of the stream we come to the chaussée leading to Bouzonville,
and crossing it we almost immediately come on the great glacis of
Fort St. Julien. This is an outlying fort of the first magnitude,
covering the whole of the round summit of a natural eminence, and
having on three sides a natural glacis, therefore of great extent. It
lies about two English miles from Metz, in a north-easterly direction;
and a little to the north-east of it is the Monastery and wood of
Grimont, the former of which Bazaine’s engineers had scientifically
diverted from ecclesiastical to military purposes. About a mile and
a half due south of St. Julien, and about a mile due east from Fort
Belle Croix, is Fort les Bottes, a great bog-backed structure chiefly
of earth, which was thrown up as a precautionary measure, either
immediately before or immediately after the outbreak of the war. The
Germans, I believe, are engaged in altering it from a “provisional” to
a temporary fort. Its position is very important as a link between St.
Julien and Fort Queleu on the south. It stands a little to the south
of the great chaussée from Metz to Saarlouis and Saarbrück, while as
yet the bisection has not taken place. Forward about a mile from les
Bottes, and on the chaussée just before it bisects, is a hamlet named
Belle-Croix, which must not, however, be confounded with the fort of
the same name. Here, also, the French had strong earthworks mounted
with guns, but these, for the most part at least, were field artillery,
and paucity of ammunition kept them tolerably quiet. About a mile and a
half south of Fort les Bottes, and about the same distance from Metz,
is Fort Queleu, also an important structure, dominating the main road
to Strasburg and the adjoining flat country as far as the telegraph on
the elevated ground before Mercy-le-Haut. Turning now westward, and
inclining to the south, we come to Fort St. Privat, at a distance of
about two and a half miles both from Fort Queleu and Metz. Its value
consists in the command it has of the eastern side of the valley of
the Moselle, of the road from Nancy, and of the railway lines which
converge behind it. Leaving it, we come to the Moselle again, this
time on the south-west side of the town, and striking up nearly due
north, we have nothing in the shape of permanent fortifications,
although during the siege there were several temporary batteries, till
we climb the great hump, on the top of which stands Fort St. Quentin,
the greatest outwork of Metz. St. Quentin is a complete fortress in
itself; it could hold out were all its neighbours taken, and it may
safely be termed impregnable, looking at the combined strength of
its natural position and its skilfully devised fortifications. Not
only does it dominate the country to the south, south-west, west, and
north-west, but it frowns outward to the east, and its great guns would
play an important part in the defence against an attack on the inner
fortifications of Metz. North-west of it, about a mile on the top
of the bluffs, lies Fort Plappeville, or les Carrières, the natural
position of which is not so strong as that of St. Quentin, because
the elevation on which it stands is not isolated, while it is open in
the rear, and therefore requires St. Quentin as the complement of its
defence, a defect the Germans are remedying. It acts as a defence over
a great tract of country to the west, and keeps a besieging force at a
respectful distance, so that it cannot reach the edge of the bluffs and
pitch projectiles down into Metz. Due north from Metz, about two miles,
and standing in the middle of the plain, bounded by the Thionville
Railway on the west, and the Moselle on the east, lies the improvised
work of St. Eloy, the last and not the least important of the outworks
of Metz. Its province is in combination with St. Julien to take care
of the valley of the Moselle to the northward, and to dominate the
great road to Thionville. All these fortifications (except the little
Belle-Croix) were furnished with heavy guns of position, and the French
had no lack of ammunition for the use of those great fortress guns,
although field artillery ammunition might have been short. Besides
their field artillery, the Germans had two or three batteries of siege
12-pounders on the western side of the Moselle, and I am not aware that
during the siege they had either any more numerous or more formidable
siege artillery on the ground. Their quiescently defensive, or rather
obstructive, position, then, was dictated by sound military conditions,
since a blockade answered exactly the purpose they had in view, viz.,
to reduce the place by famine.

None of the villages around Metz are very large, and Flanville is
one of the smallest of them—consisting of a few hovels huddled by
the side of a country road, under the lee of two large châteaux.
Small as it was, it had to accommodate the whole of a battalion of
the 4th. The men were packed closely in the houses, occupying lofts,
passages, sheds, and every other available shred of cover. The officers
occupied the two châteaux, both of which had been beautiful residences
before war had laid them waste. The colonels château still had its
garden undestroyed—in this autumn time a paradise of sweet-scented
flowers, with here a fountain, and there a leafy bower. Our château
had sustained harder fortune. A company of men, as well as a number
of officers occupied its spacious chambers; on the lawn outside were
stacked the needle-guns, ready for immediate use, and the flower-beds
had been trodden down almost beyond the power of recognition. A
Corporalschaft occupied the conservatory, and the flower-garden was a
drill ground. The château had been pelted by the French in the battle
of the 1st of September—it was still inside the range of their guns—and
the roof had been shattered by shells. We occupied the drawing-room,
in which were two great mirrors reaching to the roof. The one over the
mantel-piece had been penetrated by a bullet exactly in the centre, and
fantastically starred in all directions. Here is a sketch written on
the spot, of the manner of our daily life, when the gentlemen over the
way did not happen to be bellicose.

“In the grey morning, unless we have been previously roused by firing
on the front, the soldier servants come into our drawing-room bedroom,
bringing the morning coffee. Long before, the soldiers on the other
side of the partition have been stirring, and they are the merriest,
the most noisy set of Kerle imaginable. After coffee, drill begins.
The young soldiers are at the goose-step in the flower-garden; the
older hands practising skirmishing by companies in the neighbouring
vineyards. At nine comes appel—a kind of parade without arms, at which
the clothes and accoutrements of the men are carefully inspected. The
appel only takes place occasionally, but the under officers have a
minor appel every day, at each of which in turn there is an inspection
of a separate article. Now it is arms, now boots, now cloaks—the under
officer is responsible for the condition of his Schaft or squad, and
he takes care he shall not incur a reprimand through any want of
vigilance. After appel the officers breakfast. The feeding is homely,
and eaten in a homely fashion—clean plates, or plates of any kind, not
being plentiful, and the glass-ware decidedly deficient. But there
never wants a hearty appetite; and for a guest like myself, a hearty
welcome and a frank kindliness that I never can forget. Breakfast
over, comes an interval of visiting, gossiping, and beer drinking, for
we have our own marketender on the lawn, and Saarbrück is only forty
miles away. Some write, some read, others sleep; it is astonishing how
much sleep it takes to tire some people. The men employ themselves
in tailoring, in seeing to the rations, in conducting the cooking of
the dinner, and in smoking in the bright sunshine. Dinner is rather a
discursive meal; you imagine you have finished it when you have eaten
of the rice, the soup, and the mincemeat in your own particular room,
but going the round you find another mess devoting their attention
to plums and schinken, and you join in of course. Then you go a
little farther, and find the inmates of another room topping off with
chocolate or coffee and a _petit verre_. Of course you are asked to
partake, and equally of course you do so. After dinner is the siesta.
The quarters are then as quiet as in the dead of night. In an hour or
two a sprightly officer awakens, and assiduously devotes himself to the
stirring up of his more drowsy brother officers. A pack of cards comes
to the surface somehow, and presently the round table is surrounded
by a circle of card-players, the game proceeding with a considerable
amount of noise, owing to a habit most Germans have of all speaking at
once, each at the top of his voice. Those who are not at the card-table
stroll out to visit the brother officer who is on the _repli_, drop in
upon the worthy major and colonel, whom they find placidly smoking in
their beautiful garden, or watch Fort St. Julien and the French tents
through their glasses. Deep as the interest may be in the game which is
being played at the card-table, that and all other local interest fades
into nothing at the appearance of the Underofficer who has been over to
head-quarters to the Feldpost. It is good to watch the countenances as
the letters, hurriedly opened, are being pored over with eager faces.
The eyes of the portly Dantzic premier-lieutenant on the sofa become
strangely moist as the crumpled fuchsia and rosebud drop out of his
letter, the fragrance still clinging about them as when they had been
placed there as a love token from the children he has left at home.
What is that which the handsome young lieutenant hurriedly clutches as
it falls out of the envelope, and pushes inside his tunic on the side
next his heart? I don’t think it is a bill from his tailor. He would
not smile so if it were a dunning letter he was reading, and the most
gushing man is not addicted to the practice of stealthily kissing a
letter from his tailor ere he consigns it to his breast-pocket. Depend
upon it the lieutenant is happy in the knowledge that the girl of his
heart, far away on the shores of the Baltic, has not forgotten him.
The domestic side of the German character comes out very strong in
this interval of letter-reading. Nearly all the officers of the 4th
are from the same district, and the letter of one officer commonly
contains messages to others who at home are friends and neighbours. I
was amused to-day by the amazed delight of a grizzled officer who must
have seen close on fifty years, when he looked at the superscription
of one of his letters. ‘_Gott in Himmel!_ its from the old mother,’
he cried. Then came quite a gush of interest as to the age of the old
lady, and when he told us with some pride that she was close on ninety,
the ‘Donnerwetters’ were many and hearty. After the letters come the
perusal of the newspapers and gossiping as to their contents, and the
probable designs of Bismarck—it is always Bismarck who is named. When
darkness falls we have music and dancing. One of our volunteers is a
brilliant instrumentalist, and he comes down from his lofty abode near
the roof, and plays our piano in the drawing-room. Now it is ‘Die Wacht
am Rhein,’ the officers chiming in with the words, and the men on the
farther side of the partition and out in the environs taking up the
strain, till the volume of sound must sweep over the glades and the
ruined vineyards to the French field watches outside Belle-Croix and
St. Julien. Now it is a waltz which the young volunteer plays, and then
the spectacle would greatly delight Mr. Spurgeon, who is an advocate
for male dancing with male. A nimble young officer, who, if he were not
a gallant fellow with medals on his breast, would have made a capital
dancing-master, treats us to a burlesque rendering of the cancan,
and the music gets into the head of a rotund officer of middle-age,
who executes the most fantastic capers with a suppleness, and at the
same time a gravity, that sets everybody in a roar. He infects the
rest, and for the time one might imagine he was in the company of a
congregation of dancing dervishes. But we must not keep the men awake,
and, in truth, nobody cares to be a late sitter himself. By ten o’clock
we are all in bed, the whole place wrapped in slumber, save the
sentries who surround us with a cordon through which a mouse could not
creep. No more could a mouse creep into or out of Metz. The sentries’
orders are precise to allow nobody to pass through after nightfall
on any pretext whatever. Last night an officer and myself, who had
been out on the foreposts, allowed darkness to overtake us before our
return. As we approached the chain of sentries, the stern ‘halt’ came
to us through the darkness. ‘Officer,’ was the reply, but it availed
nothing—notwithstanding that the sentry belonged to the officer’s
own regiment. We had to stand till a party came to conduct us to the
guard, and then the officer went back and praised the sentry for the
creditable manner in which he had done his duty.”

After dark on the night of the 1st October the 4th Regiment marched
forward into the first line, occupying the village of Noisseville,
a position sufficiently far advanced to make its occupation very
ticklish work. The village was within easy range of the shell fire,
either from St. Julien or Fort les Bottes, while the field guns at the
little Belle-Croix were, so to speak, just over the way. The village
constituted part and parcel of the line of the German entrenchments;
every cottage was a stronghold; in every street, alley, and gap was
an earthwork. About 300 yards to our right front, in the bottom of a
rather abrupt valley was the village of Nouilly, belonging to neither
side. Just outside of it, on the French side, and to the north-west,
begins the Bois de Mey, on the southern edge of which lies the village
of the same name, occupied by the French. The wood swarmed with their
tirailleurs; we could see their red breeches through the trees, and
they also acquainted us with their presence by firing occasionally at
our patrols when they imagined they came too near the wood.

At night I went on the farthest _Feldwache_ on our front, just behind
the village of Nouilly, and remained there during the night with the
lieutenant in command. As soon as he relieved his predecessors, he
threw forward a sergeant’s party into Nouilly, with orders to hold it
till daylight. About one o’clock in the morning our cavalry patrols,
supported by an infantry detachment, felt their way to the edge of
the Bois de Mey, and there encountered a fierce but wild discharge of
musketry. They fell back on the sergeant’s party at Nouilly, who held
the place till they could discern the advance of large detachments of
French troops. The shots had stirred up the three battalions lying in
and around Mey, and they had come out on the rampage to annihilate the
patrols and whatever else should come in their way. The German sergeant
in Nouilly, however, quietly fell back on his supporting _Feldwache_,
where the lieutenant stood fast till he could see the Frenchmen
swarming into Nouilly, and then fell back on the _repli_, which in turn
supported the _Feldwache_. It did one good to see the coolness of the
officer, a young lieutenant, not to all appearance over twenty-five,
but who wore the ’66 medal. I asked him why, looking at the seeming
strength of the approaching force, he did not give a general alarm.
The apparatus for this purpose was complete. A fusee thrown into the
bonfire on the edge of the _repli_ post would have got 40,000 men under
arms in ten minutes, and the artillery galloping to the front in a
quarter of an hour. But it was exactly the responsibility of such a
general disturbance that prevented him from crying “Wolf”—although, to
my judgment, there seemed cause enough. The event proved how wisely
Lieutenant von Verzin had learned to calculate contingencies. The
French were content temporarily to occupy Nouilly, and after a brief
stay fell back on their original position at Mey. But if he refrained
from giving the alarm, he did not allow a single precaution to escape
him. His company was extended in skirmishing order on both sides of the
post, ready to retard the French advance should it have taken place. As
it was, a great number of shots were exchanged, but no harm was done on
either side.

Around the church of Noisseville there was a _repli_ post, continually
occupied by a company. The church itself, by the way, is, or rather I
fear I must say was, a very interesting structure. Of great age and
considerable architectural pretensions, it had been pounded with shells
more severely than any building I have ever seen. The French had
pelted it on one side, the Germans on the other. The steeple had been
originally square, now it was roughly round; there were great gaps in
the walls and holes in the roof, and the birds were singing merrily
in the half ruined structure. The _curé_ had remained by his church,
and, strange to say, his residence had escaped with very little damage.
The colonel of the 4th was his guest, or rather he was the colonel’s
guest, for he had nothing to eat but what was purveyed by our worthy
_Oberst_. The good _curé_ spoke German fluently, and got a kindly
salute from everybody; he took the firing very coolly, and seemed in
remarkably good spirits, all things considered. But to return to the
_repli_ round the shattered church. The 6th Company found it on the
2nd, and I found my way into Lieutenant Werth’s shanty after coming
back from the _Feldwache_ in the grey dawn. Till about one o’clock all
was quiet—the men lay about on the straw, smoking or sleeping, and the
cavalry videttes, 100 yards to our front, circled leisurely. Suddenly
there came a spattering chassepot fire from the riflemen on the little
Belle Croix directed against an infantry party which was beating
the foreground. The bullets came whistling over the _repli_ post as
Lieutenant Werth and myself jumped up to see what was the matter. One
hit the ground a little to our front, ricochetted between us, and
lodged in the arm of a soldier who was lying down a little in our
rear. The order was for all to get under cover immediately, but Werth
had some difficulty in getting obeyed, so eager were his fellows to
see what was going on. He himself stood coolly exposed as the bullets
came whistling by, minding them no more than if they had been so many
buzzing flies. Firing with the French is infectious, and it was taken
up all along the line as far as Mey, for what reason it was impossible
to say. The Germans did not think it worth while to reply, and the
videttes phlegmatically continued their circling. In about an hour the
French fire died away, having occasioned no casualty, save the wound to
the man in our _repli_.

On the night of the 2nd of October a detachment of the 4th did a
little job in the way of fire-raising. The neutral village of Nouilly,
immediately to the front of Noisseville, has already been mentioned;
it contained—or, at least, was believed to contain—considerable stores
of provisions, which, however, were so secreted in the cellars and
elsewhere, that the Prussians could not discover them. The inhabitants,
however, had for the most part taken refuge in Metz, and it was natural
that they should inform their military countrymen of the whereabouts
of the provisions. It was equally natural that men on short commons
should do their best to obtain all the food they could, from whatever
source; and it is believed that when the French patrols had temporarily
occupied Nouilly the night before, they succeeded in carrying away
with them some stores. The place lay so directly under the fire of
Forts St. Julien and les Bottes that it was not tenable permanently by
the Germans, and, therefore, there was no resource but to burn it.

A little after eight o’clock in the evening I was sitting at supper
with Lieutenants von Hosius and Fischer, of the 5th Company of the
2nd Battalion of the 4th regiment. The quarters these good gentlemen
occupied would hardly have been considered eligible by a London
working-man looking for lodgings. The house had been burnt out twice,
and the bare walls were now roofed in with a few planks loosely covered
with straw. Door and window were both gone, but their place was not
inefficiently supplied with blankets. There happened to be left a
tolerably decent table, and one side of the apartment was littered
with straw for a bed. We ate rather promiscuously on the foreposts
round Metz. If anyone had anything exceptionally good for supper it
was sure to ooze out, and then the fortunate man had an exceptional
number of callers who “dropped in promiscuous” just as the dainty dish
was upon the table. The dainty dish on this occasion consisted of salt
herrings boiled with onions, and washed down with beer warmed with
meal, lard, and schnaps. Lieutenant Fischer understood no English, but
had a faculty for expressing his meaning pantomimically in a manner
which I never saw equalled. As a linguist Mr. von Hosius had a great
superiority over his comrade. He knew some English. It is true his
knowledge was not of a varied character. So far as ever I learned it
was confined to “Good night,” with which he was wont to take delight in
saluting me about five in the morning, and a verse of “Lucy Long,” with
which he invariably essayed to follow up the conversation. Between the
salt herrings, my German, Mr. Fischer’s pantomime, and Mr. von Hosius’s
“Lucy Long,” we were getting on as well as could be expected, when a
feldwebel entered with an order for Lieutenant von Hosius, which the
latter read aloud:—

  “Lieutenant von Hosius will parade at nine o’clock with 15 volunteers
  of his company, and will proceed forward to burn the village of
  Nouilly.”

Von Hosius was fond of salt herrings, and fond, too, of an opportunity
of distinguishing himself. The Iron Cross for Valour was already in his
button-hole. There were still herrings on the plate, and the hour was
only half-past eight. Fischer, by his direction, went outside to call
for volunteers, while he finished the herrings. The men lay about in a
couple of dismantled rooms, and in the garden at the back of the house.
“Volunteers to burn a village!” roared Fischer, into the midst of the
sleeping Kerle. Had he thrown a bomb among them he could not have
caused a more instantaneous and universal start. Volunteers! He wanted
fifteen, and here was the whole company round him like bees. He picked
his fifteen, taking the men who had no wives at home in the Fatherland,
and then came back to see if the mulled beer was done. By this time
von Hosius had demolished the last of the herrings, and as time was
short was setting about getting ready in the most systematic manner.
Having pulled on his long boots, he pulled out of his portmanteau the
little dagger which every officer wore to ward off the vultures of the
battle-field in case of being wounded, and shoved his “Adams” revolver
into his belt. Then, with a final glass of the composite beer, he
sallied out to the spot where his little band was drawn waiting for
him, and gave them a word or two of advice and precaution.

In truth, the undertaking was a most dangerous one. It partook, indeed,
not a little of the nature of a forlorn hope. A few hundred yards in
the rear of Nouilly we, it is true, had a _Feldwache_, but the French
were nearer it on the other side by Mey and the Bois de Grimont, and
had strong temptations for entering it by night. It might be that he
might encounter a force of French inside the village. In that case, of
what service would be his fifteen volunteers? It was almost certain
that, if this contingency should not occur, the party would encounter
a fierce foe in the execution of their task, and it was probable that
on their return they would be shelled both by St. Quentin and les
Bottes. I left Hosius giving his order, and went on to the _repli_
ground, through which he had to pass to get across the wine berg, and
down into the valley in which Nouilly lies. In a few minutes came the
measured tread of the little party, marching at the German quick step,
which is faster than that of any army I know. Von Hosius as he passed
hailed me laughingly with a parting “Miss Lucy, Lucy Long,” and then
disappeared in the darkness. For a while we could hear the crashing of
the little band through the vines, and then came the hoarse challenge
of the _Feldwache_ rear sentry. Then all became quiet. The group by
the watchfire in the _repli_ gave sufficient evidence of the ticklish
character of the enterprise. For a few minutes the officer in command
of the _repli_ and myself were the only persons who enjoyed its genial
warmth. Then through the gloom came stalking the major, and, after
a long look towards Nouilly, he squatted silently down by our side.
Presently another form appeared, that of the genial colonel himself,
who had been moved to forsake the society of the _curé_ to come and see
what success von Hosius should meet with. I don’t know exactly how they
came, but in half an hour nearly all the officers of the battalion were
around the bright wood fire. Everybody tried to appear unconcerned,
but it was equally certain everybody was fidgety. The regiment had
never yet failed in any duty assigned it, and if it failed now? Nobody
liked to broach the subject, but I could see it was uppermost in the
mind of all. And von Hosius was a tantalisingly long time in putting
us out of our suspense. An hour had gone—Nouilly was but ten minutes
forward from Noisseville—and the colonel’s nervousness was undisguised
as he hacked at the burning log with his naked sword, and drove his
spur into the leg of his chair. The major had taken to walking up and
down the inside of the entrenchment, and only the adjutant exhibited a
thorough nonchalance, but then he was very sleepy.

The vigilant lieutenant of the _repli_ gave a smothered shout, and we
all sprang to our feet. Flame-coloured smoke at last and plenty of
