it. But surely, suggested the major, it really cannot be so far away
as that. Why that lowering smoke is on the other side of the Bois de
Grimont, and must be a private bonfire of the French—not the one which
von Hosius was to fire. It was too true, the alarm was a false one.
The colonel sat down again moodily, and the major began to mutter
something that seemed to me very like swearing. As for the adjutant
he went to sleep, and I very nearly followed his example. One thing
was favourable, the dead silence that reigned in the valley. It was
evident that von Hosius had encountered no French in the place, else
the rattle of the musketry would have been heard long ere now, and the
battalion, which was standing to its arms at the various company posts,
would have been lining the entrenchment with the needle-guns poked over
the earthwork.

Another half hour of suspense, and then a loud “Ha!” simultaneously
from the lieutenant on duty and the sentry. This time it was Nouilly,
and no mistake. Von Hosius had taken his time, that he might do his
work thoroughly. Not from one isolated house, but in six places at
once, belched out the long streaks of flame against the black darkness,
and the separate fires made as much haste to connect themselves as ever
did commanders who were eager to feel their supports right and left. In
ten minutes the whole place was in one grand blaze, the church steeple
standing up in the midst of the sea of flame, as if it had round its
turret the old motto of the Scottish Kirk, “_Nec tamen consumebatur_.”
But, church steeple as it was, it had not the attribute of the burning
bush, and a whole pyrotechnic display of sparks bore testimony to its
fall. Here and there against the flame could be seen a human figure
in frantic flight; and on a bluff just outside the village I saw in
the strong light a woman standing wringing her hands. These were the
innocent victims of war, the victims not of the Germans, but of him in
whose ears the poor serfs would three months ago have shouted “Vive
l’Empereur.”

Presently we heard again the crashing through the vine-brake, and our
outpost sentry challenge. The watchword was returned in the hearty
voice of von Hosius, and in five minutes more the little party was
inside the entrenchment of the _repli_. Von Hosius, of rather a portly
figure, was panting with the exertion of climbing the vine-hill, but he
found breath enough to greet me with “Miss Lucy, Lucy Long.” The affair
had been singularly successful. The duty had been executed without the
necessity of exchanging a single shot. The village, which burnt till
five next morning, had been so effectually fired that all the stores
which it might have contained must have been burnt; and so coolly had
the enterprise been gone about, that a respectable old horse which was
found in one of the stables of the village was led back in triumph, as
a trophy. I suppose the French held their fire because they did not
know whither to direct it. To have shelled Nouilly would only have been
to play into the hands of the Prussians, and the destroying party might
have come from Servigny, Noisseville, or Montoy. I lay down among the
straw, and went to sleep with the echo of “Miss Lucy, Lucy Long” still
haunting my ears.

On the foreposts we came to look upon the fortresses environing the
town of Metz, much as the Naples people regard and talk of Mount
Vesuvius when that mountain is in an uneasy condition. During all the
3rd of October St. Julien had been quiet. St. Quentin had not emitted
a single puff of smoke, and les Bottes might have been a flower-garden
tended by pretty girls. The same calm reigned on the morning of the
4th up till eleven o’clock in the forenoon, and the martial population
of Noisseville were taking it as easy as discipline would permit. To
the left of the Brewery, an isolated house on the chaussée, a little
in rear of Lauvallière and parallel with Noisseville on the south,
a large body of troops were engaged in constructing large wooden
barracks just in rear of the entrenchment line. These were being built
for the accommodation of the troops holding the post of the Brewery,
which had been reduced to ruins by the many bombardments which it had
undergone. The French in les Bottes could see the progress of this
work and the number of the men engaged in it; and the idea seemed to
strike them that it might not be out of place to attempt to interrupt
the building operations. Anyhow, they commenced rather a fierce shell
fire on the unfinished barracks, and also on the unfortunate Brewery,
under the shelter of which the working party was withdrawn when the
first grenade came hurtling over the trees. The Brewery must by this
time have been thoroughly well used to the visitation of shells, and
there was this consolation for it, that had it been exploded bodily
it could not have been ruined much more utterly than it already was.
All that it was good for was that the heap of stones which once had
composed a rather imposing structure afforded a kind of shelter for
troops drawn up in its rear. But even this office the Brewery was now
rendered incapable of performing. The shells sent its loose stones
flying in all directions, and it was thus that several men were
wounded. The contagion of firing was communicated by les Bottes to
the foreposts. These began blazing away at each other in a very level
fashion, the Germans replying to the French fire with the chassepots,
the use of which they had been practising sedulously for the last few
days. Everybody had got so much used to this intermittent outpost
fire that the only notice it met with was a whistle occasionally from
some soldier moving about our village when a bullet went singing past
him, or hit the wall a yard or two above his head. From firing at the
wooden barracks and the Brewery, les Bottes took it into its head to
throw a few shells into Servigny, the village to our right, and then
St. Julien chimed in with its great cannon balls. Noisseville occupied
an intermediate position between the Brewery and Servigny; but up till
about one o’clock it had unaccountably escaped the polite attentions
of the two French forts. About that hour, however, a great cannon-ball
came whistling across from St. Julien, crashed through the wall of
a house in which most of the officers were quartered, and tumbled in
among the stacked arms in an open space in the street, making a hole
in which a horse might have been buried. Nobody was hurt; but the
paymaster was nearly smothered with the débris which fell from the wall
through which the great ball passed. Les Bottes followed St. Julien’s
example in firing into Noisseville, the latter projecting 42lb. and
64lb. shells, the former confining himself to cannon-balls. It was a
great waste of powder and shot. Nobody was hurt, and all the effect
was that a village already for the most part burnt and in ruins was
destroyed a little more. The church was terribly wrecked by the cannon
of St. Julien. Already the exterior had been knocked out of shape,
but the inside retained much of its original integrity. A shell from
St. Julien crashed right through the altar, and brought the cross on
to the pavement in splinters. The fragment of a shell from les Bottes
shivered a beautiful marble monument, splitting it right up the centre,
and cutting in two the commemoration of the merits of a respectable
defunct villager. The Virgin Mary, as she stood in marble against the
wall, had a limb amputated, and was otherwise dismembered; and if the
pastor had been in the pulpit when another shell struck it he would
never have preached more. So hot became the cross-fire from the two
fortresses that it was judged advisable to withdraw the troops out of
the village a few hundred yards to the rear. The _Feldwachen_ were
of course left in their positions, as also were two companies in the
_repli_ by the church; but the rest fell back into an orchard behind
the village. It was great fun, in a sense, to be here and listen to
the French pelting the village, under the idea, no doubt, that it was
full of Prussian troops. We could hear the crashing of the rafters and
the smashing of the walls as the huge projectiles tore through them,
the shells exploding as they struck, and sending into the air a cloud
of fragments. All this time the musketry fire on the foreposts was
unceasing along a large portion of the line, and anyone dropping in
casually upon the scene would have imagined quite an important battle
was in progress. So little was this the case that the troops supporting
Noisseville and lying in Flanville and Retonfay were not put under
arms. The whole affair was an utterly useless ignition of powder, and
its only effect was to suspend for the day the construction of the
wooden barracks. When the firing had ceased we came back into the
village, and several found that the quarters which they had quitted
at one o’clock were no longer in existence at five. A few houses more
or less ground to powder in Noisseville was, however, quite a trifle
when the whole village might have been called a ruin. It surprised me
to observe with what extraordinary force some of the projectiles had
fallen. In a field beyond the church there was a hole in the earth
that might almost have served as the shaft of a mine. It marked the
spot where had fallen a shell from les Bottes. The pioneers dug it out
in the evening. They had to go to a depth of about six feet. The huge
ugly affair had not exploded, and two men extricated it gently, and
bore it in triumph, quite a little procession following, to lay it at
the feet of the colonel. The shell was reckoned to be 110-pounder. Just
fancy a missile of this magnitude striking a man full in the chest!
It would hurl him in little pieces in all directions. Or fancy it
exploding in the centre of a group, or in the heart of a column, and
scattering its great skin, an inch and a half thickness of metal, in a
shower of morsels, each about two inches square.

During the night, as I was told in the morning, les Bottes and St.
Julien renewed the firing on Noisseville. I confess to sleeping so
soundly, that I was in blessed ignorance of the circumstance, but when
I turned out in the morning I fell into a hole that was the crater of
an exploded shell, about two paces from the place where once there
had been a door. Several other shells fell in the immediate vicinity,
and the poor church got another blow or two. The 4th Regiment, on
the morning of the 5th October, was relieved by the 44th Regiment,
from Retonfay, with whom it exchanged quarters. Retonfay is a squalid
village, which was included in the second line of environment. Close to
it was a fine château, inhabited for the time by General Zglinitzky,
who commanded the brigade. It was the turn of the 4th for a spell on
rest, and the officers who, in the front line, were not at liberty to
quit their commands for an hour, were now comparatively at large for
the time. As soon as we reached Retonfay, and had taken up quarters,
one of them was kind enough to accompany me in a ride as far as the
Moselle, on the north side of Metz. We everywhere traversed the extreme
edges of the foreposts, and the ride was a very interesting one. From
Retonfay we struck the first line at the unfortunate Servigny—that once
pretty village, at which St. Julien seemed to have so especial a spite.
There was not much of Servigny to speak of—a few gable ends of walls,
and here and there the walls of a house without a roof. A battalion
of the 45th contrived somehow to find quarters among the ruins, and a
few villagers still wandered about in a ghostly fashion. From Servigny
we passed over a considerable ridge, to the charming village of Poix,
lying snugly at the opening of a valley which tends downward toward
the Moselle. This village is surrounded in all directions by beautiful
villas, the summer residences of the wealthier inhabitants of Metz. An
isolated suburb near the top of the ridge had suffered severely from
the fire of St. Julien—indeed, occasional shells were bursting in it
as we passed, but the village itself had not been much injured, and
the German troops dad respected its amenities in a very surprising
manner. A Division General dwelt amid roses and honeysuckles, in one of
the principal chateaux, built in rustic Elizabethan, and beautifully
furnished—the Feldpost inhabited the gardener’s rustic lodge in the
grounds. Vremy lies a little to the rear in the bottom of the valley,
which here assumes the character of a gorge, and the road compels a
detour into it, which was repaid us by the observation of the admirable
manner in which its houses and churchyard walls had been converted into
defensive works, thoroughly blocking up the gorge. Leaving Vremy, and
the hospitable officers who dwelt therein, we rode down the valley to
Failly, a considerable village, in which, until a few days previous,
had been the head-quarters of General Kummer, commanding the Landwehr,
now gone across the Moselle. From Failly there is a direct road to the
Moselle at Malroy, but, by a slight detour, the opportunity offered
itself of getting very close to the Bois de Grimont, which was full of
French troops, and of inspecting, at a comparatively short distance,
the fortress of St. Julien. An officer in Failly told us laughingly,
that there were two very beautiful young women in Villiers l’Orme,
the village which is so near to the Bois de Grimont, and I am not
certain that this information did not operate with my companion as an
additional argument for visiting the place. L’Orme is a very prettily
situated little village, just on the edge of the tableland on which
stand Grimont and St. Julien. We saw nothing of the beautiful maidens,
but the place was full of inhabitants, and the children were playing
merrily about, quite regardless, to all appearance, of the fact that
the place is within three hundred yards of the French outposts. L’Orme
was unoccupied by the Germans during the day. At night a small party,
under the command of an under officer, was wont to go forward, and
take up a position behind it. Occasionally, stragglers from the French
outposts visited it, we were told, in search of food. From the shelter
of one of its houses, we could see quite easily with the naked eye, the
French outposts—the men cooking or lying down, and the sentries walking
to and fro. Behind was the forest, and then the Monastery of Grimont,
while over the whole of the quiet little scene, St. Julien frowned
grimly, casting forward his dark shadow in the afternoon sunlight.
L’Orme had wonderfully escaped the ravages of war. Both parties seemed
to have agreed to respect it, probably on account of the number of
villagers who still continued to reside in it. I was told that not a
single shell had been fired into the village. But while we were there,
the shells were flying over our heads towards Vany and Charly, and it
seemed strange to see the children playing in the street, the girls
driving home the cows, and the old women placidly spinning in front of
their doors, when the missiles were whistling through the air above
their heads.

The French might have respected l’Orme, but they assuredly had no such
tenderness for anybody happening to traverse the space which intervenes
between l’Orme and Chieulles. The track led athwart an open field, with
a hedge in the rear, and the French posts lined the wood continuously,
at a distance of about five hundred yards. The distance is about half
a mile, and, unless we chose to make a very considerable detour, there
was no alternative but to run the gauntlet. My military friend went
first, at a brisk hand canter. He had hardly shown his horse’s head
beyond the cover of the houses of l’Orme, when the Frenchmen commenced
saluting him with chassepot bullets. I followed at a short interval,
and as the bullets became thicker and faster, our canter became a
gallop. The horses were fresh, and also desperately timid; ever as a
bullet whistled past them, and cut the twigs in the hedge, they gave a
bound and a shy that necessitated very close adhesion to the pigskin.
I was by no means sorry when we got into Chieulles, a village a little
farther retired from the point-blank range of fire, and which had also,
by mutual consent, been held as a neutral territory. There were in it
plenty of inhabitants, who seemed to have got quite familiarised to
their strange position, and carried on their ordinary avocations as
if the war were miles away. The village is rather a large one, and
contains several considerable buildings. From it our road lay to the
left rear, to a _Feldwache_ outside the village of Vany, and then into
the village itself. The French outposts gave us one parting salute
as we sallied out of Chieulles, and this evoked a response from the
_Feldwache_ for which we were making. We left the hostile outposts
firing away at each other very briskly, and rode forward, accompanied
by a cavalry officer, into Charly, a beautiful village, containing
many châteaux, and standing embowered amidst orchards. Here one of
the points of the battle of the 31st August was contested with great
desperation. All around the village the graves lay thick, now German,
now French. The bayonet had been at work that day in the environs of
Charly, and in one of its alleys took place a fierce and long-continued
hand-to-hand contest. It was very pretty to see how tastefully the
German troops had decorated the graves of their dead comrades. They had
made them up with turf, and planted flowers and shrubs on the ridge,
while at the head of each stood a wooden cross, on which was painted
the name and rank of him who lay below. The French graves were not thus
tended, but neither were they neglected. Brave soldiers never draw
distinctions between the last resting-places of foes and friends. The
graves of the French had been surrounded by hedges of shrubs, and the
cross had been set up at the head of each.

From Charly we rode on to Malroy, a considerable village lying on the
Moselle, about two miles north of Fort St. Julien. It was necessary,
however, to ride up the stream some distance to find the first
pontoon bridge crossing it at Argancey. Some distance higher up, at
Hautconcourt, there was another pontoon bridge, and close to it, at
Ennery, a very large depôt of provisions and stores for the supply of
the northern portion of the besieging army. Immediately on crossing
the Moselle, we came among the Landwehr men. “These men need not fear
comparison with the finest regiments of the regular army,” that was
my first remark to my companion. So far as I could discern, the only
distinction lay in the fact that the Landwehr had more hair on their
faces, as a general rule, than the line troops. About eight-and-twenty
to two-and-thirty seemed the general age of the Landwehr men; and as
regards a martial carriage they would not suffer alongside the Guards.

Riding leisurely back by a more circuitous route than that we had
pursued in going, we were near Failly when the sun disappeared in a
glorious setting behind the yellow crest of St. Quentin. Hardly had it
disappeared when a flash came from out of the shadow over against St.
Agathe. The roar of a heavy gun came booming across the valley, and
the sound had not died away before there was another flash and another
roar. In five minutes the whole slope for a distance of nearly a mile
and a half was lit up by continual flashes of artillery fire. Then the
batteries of St. Eloy took up the reply, with a slower but steady fire
of its heavy guns, their roar coming to us deadened by the thick fog
which was rising from the Moselle. The sight in the growing darkness
was indescribably grand. We sat on horseback on the summit of the ridge
at Poix, watching and listening, till St. Julien thought fit to chime
in on his own account, and his big shells gave us notice to quit the
ridge by the suburb of Poix on which we were standing. About eight
o’clock we returned to Retonfay, on the plateau before which we found
the brigade-general, and nearly all the officers of the battalions
lying there, standing watching and listening to the gun-fire. Soon
after eight it began to slacken, and presently died away altogether;
all through the night both St. Julien and les Bottes kept the German
lines on the alert by an occasional reminder, consisting of a 64-lb.
shell.

The 6th was a quiet day on the east side, although the din came to us
of a continuous bombardment from the other side of the Moselle to the
north side. By special request of the troops stationed in Retonfay,
there was divine service in the morning in a meadow in the village. The
division chaplain officiated, and the brigadier-general was present
with his staff. It was a fine sight to see the six battalions,
numbering as many thousand men, drawn up in a hollow square, with the
clergyman and the regimental band in the centre. The service commenced
with a hymn, in which all the troops joined with fervour. Then the
minister preached a kind of informal sermon. He selected no Scripture
text, his text was the duty of a Christian soldier in war time. His
words evidently came from the heart, and as evidently went to the
heart. When he spoke of the friends at home longing for tidings from
the front and yet half afraid to hear them lest they should learn that
the loved one had fallen in battle, many were the heads bent down on
the manly chests, and many a gallant soldier held his hand before his
eyes to hide the starting tear. It was remarkable what an effect the
chaplain’s words had in stimulating correspondence when the service
was over. Round each Feldwebel there was quite a little crowd eager
to obtain the “Correspondence Karte” on which the troops mostly wrote
their brief epistles, and the post corporal had enough to do to carry
the great bag with which he went over to the Feldpost in Flanville. A
little after the service I was sitting with the paymaster and another
officer, when a knock came to the door, and there entered a great
hulking fellow from Dantzic, who sheepishly asked the paymaster to
accommodate him with paper money for his bullion. How much do you think
he had? Why, counting pfennigs and a queer collection of kreutzers,
he could make up just one thaler, and this huge remittance the great
honest manly fellow wished to send to the Frau away on the shore of the
Baltic. It was only three shillings, but it was the last stiver the man
had, and he would go without his glass of beer till next pay day came
round. Fancy an English soldier coming to his officer with a handful of
half-pence for conversion into paper money! But a German regiment is
one great brotherhood, and the paymaster pocketed the coppers and gave
the man the paper thaler with a kindly word and look, for has he not a
Frau of his own in Königsberg, whose portrait lies against that broad
chest of his?

In the evening there was a camp fire, an institution not unknown at
Wimbledon in the early days of the Volunteer movement, when as yet
the encampment was but small, and when Lord Elcho’s sonorous voice
could be heard over the whole gathering. During peace time, there
is a camp fire—or a gathering equivalent to it—once a week in every
Prussian regiment, to which the married officers bring their good
ladies, and whither also the “beauty and fashion” of the garrison
town are invited. “Beauty and fashion” were represented in Retonfay
by a few French crones of fabulous age, who confined their remarks to
muttering “Mon Dieu” continuously, and who, I suspect, would gladly
have poisoned the whole regiment if they could have done so without
risk to themselves. But nevertheless we had our camp fire, and that,
too, under quite imposing circumstances. The spot selected was a
pretty meadow by the side of a stream, about two hundred yards from
the village. An officer of taste, and with a capital eye for landscape
gardening, had devoted himself from a very early hour in the morning
to perfecting the arrangements. With a fatigue party, he had thrown a
pretty rustic bridge across the stream, and lined the path leading to
it with green boughs. With these, too, he had enclosed a large circle,
with a smaller area close by for the band, and in the centre of the
larger circle a great bonfire had been built, its foundation raised
by the earth excavated from a trench dug around it. All the chairs
in the village were collected, and most of the tables, and placed in
picturesque confusion around this central point. The officers began to
gather about five o’clock. They were all so smart that I felt ashamed
of my old tweed shooting jacket and dilapidated wideawake. The parade
coats had been dug out of the bottom of portmanteaus, and looked
quite spruce and natty. Boots had been polished to a pitch that might
have stimulated the admiration of Day and Martin. Spurs and buttons
glittered brightly in the setting sun. Then our worthy colonel—Oberst
von Dietzen-Hennig—made his appearance, accompanied by his staunch ally
the major. The colonel, too, was evidently on ceremony, for he wore the
sword which King Wilhelm had presented to him with his own hands the
evening of one of the battles of the 1866 campaign. All rose to greet
the commanding officer, and immediately that he was seated, the band
struck up. So thoroughly was the affair managed, that an elaborately
written programme of the music was laid on the colonel’s table, of
which I took a copy. It may interest English readers to know what music
a Prussian regimental band played, while the bass accompaniment was
furnished by the roar of the guns over against us on the other side of
the Moselle. Here is a copy of the programme:

  Fest Overture Fischer.
  An der schönen blauen Donau Walzer Strauss.
  La Belle Amazone Loischhorn.
  March Potpourri Bach.
  Künstler Fest Quadrille Hermann.
  Fanfare Militaire Ascher.

It was a very pretty sight. About forty officers were present,
grouped picturesquely around the tables. Military servants circulated
everywhere, serving the company with glasses of beer. The outside
of the bough-enclosed circle was lined with the men of the various
battalions, who crowded to the spot, under the attraction of the music.
More hearty good fellowship could not be imagined. The colonel and
the youngster who won his silver shoulder straps but the day before
were on the most equal footing. In an interval of the music a young
officer came into the circle who had just returned from the hospital
at Düsseldorf, whither a bullet through the foot in the battle of the
31st August had sent him. Then he had been only a Feldwebel, but he
had got his lieutenancy when lying on his back, and this was his first
appearance among his comrades since his promotion. As he came forward
the colonel rose and called for a bumper to the health of his gallant
young friend. The young fellow bowed and blushed as all obeyed the
chief’s order, following the draught with a cheer that must have been
heard at St. Julien. Then the colonel was good enough to disarrange the
programme for the introduction of “Rule Britannia” as a special token
of consideration to the Englander present. By this time the darkness
had begun to fall, and the bonfire was formally set fire to, the circle
breaking up from the tables, and re-forming around its cheerful blaze.
The band exhausted the programme, and took to playing song tunes, the
officers chiming in lustily with their voices, and the men on the
outskirts taking up the chorus. The scene was one in which a painter
would have delighted—the bright wood-fire lighting up the faces of
the circle around it, and ever and anon, as it shot up more brightly,
dissipating the thick darkness beyond, and flashing on the faces of
the men through the boughs. But no painter could give effect to what
was to me the most weird phase of the affair—the measured thud of the
cannonading sounding between every pause in the music or lull in the
din of the conversation; and it was strange, too, to reflect that,
as we sat here cheerily, we were still within the range of the great
guns of St. Julien. I could not help thinking of the effect which a
forty-two pound shell would have had had it fallen and exploded right
in the heart of the bonfire. The good fellows of the 4th, however,
troubled themselves with no such speculations. It was sufficient for
them that St. Julien was quiet, and that the fire was cheery, the
company good, and the beer reasonably plentiful, and palatable. In
the course of the evening one of the captains gave a toast, which was
received with intense enthusiasm—the health of the gallant comrades
who had won the decoration of the Iron Cross. It was on the breast
of at least half those who sat round the fire. When the cheering had
subsided the band struck up the “Paris” march, a _souvenir_ of 1813,
in remembrance of which campaign the Iron Cross was first instituted.
About half-past eight the colonel gave the toast of “Our Comrades of
the Artillery,” in honour of some gentlemen of that service who were
present, and then, after the band had played “God Save the King,” said
good night, and went away to his supper. I followed him in a very short
time, but when I came outside to smoke my cigar in the moonlight,
before turning in among the straw, I could still hear the merry chorus
down in the meadow by the camp-fire. It was then about eleven o’clock,
and how long after the last of the roisterers may have kept it up I
have not the slightest idea.

The foggy morning of the 7th October ushered in what turned out to be
the most important and determined sortie made by Bazaine since the
failure of the 31st of August. I know not whether the French gave to
the affair a name; the Germans recognized it as the “Schlacht bei
Maizières.” Battles there were in plenty in this bloody campaign
that showed a larger total of killed and wounded; but the “Schlacht
bei Maizières” made widows and orphans in the Fatherland far beyond
proportion; for the men who bore the brunt of it were husbands and
fathers,—the stout Landwehrmen of the Division Kummer. I had quitted
Retonfay early in the grey morning with the intention of making the
complete circuit of the environment of Metz before my return. The
morning was extremely misty. As I reached the eminence at Poix I could
barely see Mount St. Quentin above the fog in the valley, the sun’s
rays striking upon its warm grey peak. It seemed as if a general
armistice had been declared; even the forepost fire had dropped.
Crossing the Moselle at Argancey, I made first for the Château of
Amelange, and afterwards in a direction due Metz-ward, until I reached
the furthest _Feldwache_ at Maxe. Then I struck across the valley,
inclining rather to the rear, first to Grandes and then to Petites
Tapes, in both of which there lay only an under-officer’s guard of
nine men; and avoiding Ladonchamps, by advice, I reached St. Rémy,
where I found on duty the 59th Landwehr Regiment. Thence my journey
lay still athwart the remotest foreposts to Norroy and Plesnois, the
latter village being in the heart of the Bois de Saulny, and my road
then lay straight southward on St. Hubert, on the south-west of Mount
St. Quentin. I detail the different stages of this ride as it was right
across the theatre of the afternoon’s fight, and because the quietude
everywhere in the morning formed so strong a contrast to the sudden
hurly-burly of the afternoon.

Near Plesnois my horse cast a shoe, and as the road was rugged I was
rather in a fix, when there rode past a staff officer attached to
the head-quarters of Baron Schüler von Senden, commanding the 2nd
Division of the Landwehr. This gentleman was quartered in Maizières,
and he most courteously invited me to return with him thither and be
his guest for the night. On the return journey this gentleman gave me
some information which contributed materially to an understanding of
the events which followed. In the bombardment of the two previous days
the position occupied by the batteries of German heavy guns had been
at Frenecourt, near the commencement of the rise of the hills some
distance to the south-west of Maizières. To speak more precisely, they
were stationed on the low heights of Le Horiment, on the rising ground
behind which there stood an observatory commanding the whole plain.
But the Germans had batteries considerably to the front of Frenecourt.
Six field batteries stood mid-way between it and Sennecourt, and on
the 6th they had pushed forward a second battery as far as Sennecourt
itself. From these several positions the bombardment of St. Eloy had
been going forward intermittingly, and also a shell-fire into the
village of Ladonchamps, some little distance nearer Metz than St. Rémy,
on the line of railway. Till lately this village had been reckoned
neutral territory, but on the 2nd October the French had seized it, and
established a battery in front of it. The village itself had been burnt
the same night by German shells, but still the French had retained
the position, and the German fire in the intervening days had been
partly directed to the dislodgment of the enemy from a position which
was important, as the occupants of it could enfilade the whole front
across the valley. On the 6th nearly a thousand shells had been thrown
into Ladonchamps and the neighbourhood, and late in the evening the
French had evacuated the shattered fragments of a once smiling village.
The Germans had at once thrown forward troops in its direction,
establishing their _replis_ in its rear, and sending forward sergeants’
parties to occupy it and Grandes and Petites Tapes villages, to which
its possession was the key. St. Rémy constituted the chief support,
and here lay the 59th Regiment of the Landwehr. Maxe, close to the
river, and considerably in advance, was occupied, as I already knew,
by outposts detailed by the 10th Army Corps, from the other side of
the Moselle. The two divisions of the Landwehr stretched right athwart
the valley from the bridge at Argancey, where they touched the 10th
Army Corps, to near Marange, where they met the 3rd Corps, and to the
Landwehrmen was thus confided the duty of keeping the flat alluvial
tract on the western bank of the Moselle. This was the substance of the
information which my friend of the staff communicated to me on our ride
to Maizières. Here I found the head-quarters of General von Kummer.
About one o’clock I was sitting at lunch with two staff officers in
an arbour in his garden, when the guns of the German batteries by
Sennecourt began to give tongue. “Only a few Frenchmen loafing round
Ladonchamps,” observed one of the officers. “There will be nothing
serious to-day; there is too much mist in the valley.” Certainly it
seemed as if he must be right. When I had been on the heights at
Sennecourt I could not see the villages in the valley below, nor the
Cathedral of Metz. Fort St. Quentin, our constant landmark, had been
utterly invisible. But the roar of the guns grew louder and louder,
and then came first one great boom, and then another, from the big guns
lying behind at Frenecourt. The officers fidgeted, but still would not
own that anything was the matter. But their _nonchalance_ gave way when
an aide-de-camp came up at the gallop, spreading the alarm everywhere
as he went, and dashing on to the general’s quarters for instructions
to guide the front. In five minutes more we were all in the saddle,
and, after a short gallop, were looking out on the scene of action
from the fringe of the wood in front of the Châteaux of Brieux and
Amelange. That the reader may understand the tactics of Bazaine and
the manner in which they were foiled, it will be necessary to give a
brief description of the ground. From Metz to Maizières there is a long
trough, with a flat bottom—the alluvial margin of the Moselle. This
tract is about four English miles wide. On the west it is bounded by
the heights I have already named, and nearer Metz by those of Norroy
and Saulny. On the east it is bounded by a lower series of bluffs,
on which stand the villages of Olgy and Malroy; but between them and
the flat bottom runs the Moselle, infringing considerably on the flat
expanse just opposite Olgy. Across this bottom, at the narrowest part
thus formed, lies a series of villages—the two Tapes and St. Rémy, with
Maxe and Ladonchamps respectively slightly to the east and west front.
More or less, there were German troops in all those. The dispositions
detailed to me by the staff officer had been substantially maintained.
Bazaine had laid his scheme with great art. Covered by the dense fog,
he had made his dispositions with such adroitness that when it lifted,
a little past one, his arrangements were already all but complete. In
the first instance, he directed a strong assault on Ladonchamps. The
Landwehr outpost held the place as if they were 10,000 instead of 100
men, and the French sent their infantry swarming into it, while their
artillery played upon it. “Only an attempt to re-occupy Ladonchamps,”
quoth my friend of the staff, as he lit a fresh cigar; “the guns will
teach them the folly of that, and we can go back to our arbour.” It
seemed, truly, if any argument could be convincing, that the German
artillery must be so. The white spurts of smoke were visible all round
the valley. On the right front the batteries at Sennecourt were hard at
work, and also others nearer us on the flat, while the great guns at
Frenecourt were sending shells at a long range right over Ladonchamps
in among the advancing French. Then, on our left, before Amelange,
two other batteries were maintaining a semi-cross fire; and from the
bluffs, on the other side of the Moselle, between Olgy and Malroy,
the Prussian field artillery were also roaring. But my friend of the
staff was not, for an hour or two at all events, to see the arbour.
The attack on Ladonchamps was a diversion. Suddenly the villages of
Grandes and Petites Tapes, of St. Rémy and Maxe were overwhelmed by
a cataract of Frenchmen. The battalion of the 59th Landwehr which
held St. Rémy would not fall back, as it should have done in common
prudence, but stood up there in the street till the French having
played upon it with their artillery, and rained on it chassepot and
mitrailleuse bullets, finally pushed backwards the shattered remnant
on to the chaussée by dint of sheer numbers. The Fusilier battalion of
the 58th occupied Grandes Tapes, and it occupied Grandes Tapes when
the fight was done, but the occupation consisted of the dead and the
wounded. The battalion would not give ground, and may be said to have
been annihilated as it stood, the men with their backs to the wall and
their faces to the foe. It afterwards transpired that 300 men of this
battalion were made prisoners, after a desperate resistance, and sent
into Metz. The other battalions of the same regiment suffered terribly.
So far, then, Bazaine had succeeded. He had re-occupied the chain of
villages athwart the valley, and had got a few batteries of artillery
out to their front to reply to the German fire. But the status quo he
neither wished to, nor could retain. The German artillery, throwing
its projectiles from three sides of the parallelogram, interfered with
the realization of the latter conception. As regarded the former, it
seemed clear that Bazaine would not have done what he had if he did
not contemplate something more. That something, I have not the remotest
doubt, was a sortie to establish connections with Thionville. His
tactics were well conceived. From St. Rémy and the two Tapes he kept
the German fire engrossed, both musketry and artillery. He sent forward
from Grandes Tapes great swarms of tirailleurs, who fared extremely
ill at the hands of Landwehr men. But, besides this, he massed in the
rear what was, beyond all question, a very large force. There must have
been nearly 20,000 in all on the bank of the Moselle, and under cover
of the houses of Maxe. The head of this force he hurled forward to cut
through the German cincture where it was weakest, close to the river.
The moment was a critical one. Those regiments of the Landwehr which
had not already been sent forward against the villages were too far
to the westward to be in time to meet this attack as it ought to be
met—directly face to face. But the 10th Army Corps had been crossing
the pontoon bridge, and massing between the river and Amelange. Their
general, Voight Rhetz, was in nominal command of the days operations,
and he gave the order for several regiments to advance. It was a sight
never to be forgotten. First came the Fusiliers, extending at a rapid
run into skirmishing order, and covering the whole plain with their
long thin lines. Then the dense columns of companies of the Grenadiers,
the bands playing and the colours unfurled—unwonted sight. But all the
work was not left wholly for the infantry to do. The artillery, for the
moment, left the villages alone, and concentrated their fire on the
advancing columns of the French by the Moselle. There was but little
response from the French field artillery, but the sullen sides of St.
Julien flashed out smoke and fire, and from the earthworks of St.
Eloy came missiles at the shorter ranges. The mitrailleuse venomously
sounded its angry whirr, making the skirmishers recoil nervously as
they crossed the line of its fire, and tearing chasms in the fronts
of the solid masses of which they were the forerunners. The artillery
and the skirmishers were enough for the French. The dense columns
staggered and then broke. Through my glass I could see a continuous
_sauve qui peut_ into the village of Maxe. But when they had once got
stone and lime between them and the Germans, the French were obstinate
and would go no further. In vain the German artillery pounded into the
villages, advancing closer and closer in alternate order of batteries
with a precision and rapidity that could not have been exceeded on
Woolwich-common. That obstinate battery in front of Grandes Tapes
would not cease, and the French tirailleurs still lined the chaussée
in its front. By this time it was nearly four o’clock, and there was a
lull on our side that seemed ominous. As we stood in this suspense a
staff officer galloped along the front line with orders for a general
advance to take the villages by storm. The attack he hurriedly told me
was to consist of the four brigades of the Landwehr, with a division of
the 10th Army Corps on the left and supporting. In a few minutes more
the command came sounding along the line, and the men sprang from their
cover and went forward with that steady quick step so characteristic of
the German marching. The shells from the battery in front of Grandes
Tapes tore through the line, the mitrailleuse and chassepot bullets
poured against it their leaden hail, but still the Landwehr, silent and
stern, went steadily to the front. The oldest veteran in the field was
never in a more furious fire than that to which the centre of this line
was exposed. General von Brandenstein, commanding the third brigade
of the Landwehr, was shot down close to me, and several of his staff
were wounded. At length the entrenchments were reached, behind which
were lying the shattered remnants of the 59th and 58th Landwehr. The
fraternisation consisted in the cry of “Hurrah Preussen!” and then
“Vorwärts—immer vorwärts!” and the line threw itself to its front at a
run. The gunners from the battery, brave men and stubborn, had barely
time to run round the corner before the Landwehrmen were upon them. The
guns they left perforce. In the villages the French made a last stand,
but it had been better for them that they had run away at first. The
Landwehrmen, with less of the conventional warrior in them than the
Line, were not so much inclined to give quarter as are the professional
soldiers. With many a Frenchman this afternoon the shrift consisted of
a bayonet thrust. They fought like devils in the narrow ways of the
villages, and used the mitrailleuses with rare judgment and effect. But
then there came the steady inexorable stride forward of the Landwehr
men, the bayonet lent force with that huge thigh and back power which
is the leading characteristic in an athletic sense of the German
physique, and the villages were cleared of all save victors, dead, and
wounded.

To the Landwehr must be conceded the honour of the fray. They it was
who checked the rush of the French advance by holding the villages
till they had not a man that could stand upright and fire the needle
gun. To them also was entrusted the grand final advance which swept
the French out of the villages. I had seen the German Line soldiers
fight before this day. I had learned to believe that they could do
aught any soldiers in the world could do. But it was not till now that
I gauged the calibre of the Landwehr, how that in all things they were
the equals of their brethren of the Line. Cool in the intrenchments,
when they lay calmly in position, picking up the bullets that fell
among them—resolute, indomitable, in their steady quick march forward,
and then irresistible in the final rush with which they carried
the villages—they were troops to delight the heart of a man with a
soldierly instinct. Nothing was more observable than the cool manner
in which the wounded went to the rear in the general advance—every man
depending on himself if he could walk at all, declining the services
of assistants to help him out of the fray. Nor were they slight wounds
with which these gallant fellows struggled unaided to the rear. One,
for instance, to whom I spoke, was shot right through the lungs, and at
every step his breath came panting through the bullet-hole. It might be
that he struggled on till he reached the straw in the court-yard of the
Château of Amelange, where the doctors were toiling up to their elbows
in blood; but I fear his was one of the many bodies that checkered the
green fields behind the advance. Gallant fellows as they were, it went
to one’s heart to see the Landwehr fight and die. Not like a linesman
could they take their life in their hand and go down into the fray
conscious that nobody would hunger because they fell. For every second
Landwehrman that fell at Maizières there was a widow in the Fatherland,
and, with children of my own around my hearth, my heart swelled to
think of the number of unconscious orphans made that sunshiny autumn
afternoon in the pleasant villages and the quiet plains of Deutchland.
Pray God that, fight who may, there may be no more deadly battles in
which the Landwehr shall have to take part. Not that, as it seemed to
me, they dwelt upon the thought of Frau and Kinder. The hairy Kerl,
with the grey in his beard, and who knew how many young birds were in
his nest, went straight to the front as boldly as the sprightly young
volunteer who had only a girl to weep for him if he fell. But they
are a prayerful people those Germans, and I fancied, when the word
“Vorwärts” came sounding through the ranks, that many a man bent his
head for a moment into his hand, as if he were entering a church.

On my way to the rear, after the brunt of the fight was over, I looked
into the court-yard of the Château of Amelange, where the doctors
were hard at work. The men lay all around waiting for their turn,
and smoking like so many lime-kilns. Here already by five o’clock
there were quite two hundred wounded, and those in Amelange were
but a handful compared to the gross number that had been wounded.
The doctors, with shirt sleeves up and coat off, were toiling with
blood-smeared hands with cool but nimble dexterity. One of them seemed
something between a cannibal and a butcher as he looked up for a moment
from a case with a bloody knife-blade between his teeth.

In the evening, postal celerity being an object, I left Maizières to
ride to Courcelles. Immediately after crossing the pontoon bridge at
Argancey, a detour became necessary. The German artillery was still
firing in the moonlight from the heights above Olgy, and St. Julien
was sluggishly throwing projectiles on to the tableland, seemingly to
shut up their fire. All the ground in the rear of Charly and toward
Fany was pitted with shells that had fallen and burst during the day.
A brisk musketry fire was still going on in front of Chieulles and
l’Orme, so quiet when I had ridden through them in the morning. The
French, pouring down out of the Bois de Grimont, had made a feigned
attack in considerable force up this valley, and the old women and
children in Chieulles must have had their equanimity not a little
disturbed. All I could hear as I rode through the villages was, that
a good many had been killed and wounded; and I met a large ambulance
train coming forward into the fire from out the village of Vremy.
Poix had been shelled during the day, but all was now quiet there. A
little further on I came to the rear of Servigny and Noisseville, from
which I met my friends of the 4th returning singing triumphantly. The
French from Mey and the slope in front of St. Julien had sent three
battalions into Nouilly, and thence had attempted to storm the _repli_
in front of Noisseville, but had been repulsed with considerable loss,
the German artillery driving them out of Nouilly into the wood. I was
in time to accompany a tentative advance of the German artillery along
the chaussée right under the earthworks of Little Belle Croix, which
was chiefly remarkable from the circumstance that St. Julien made no
effort to arrest the operation. I afterwards learnt that there was
further fighting in the early part of the night in front of St. Rémy,
and that a battalion of the 81st had been forced to retake Ladonchamps
at the bayonet point, losing in the task five officers and over one
hundred men.

It is not difficult, I think, to form a tolerably correct opinion
as to the plan of Bazaine’s tactics of the 7th. I am aware that the
importance of this sortie has been belittled by testimony from the
interior of Metz, but I have a habit of believing in what I see, and
the evidence of eyesight was corroborated as well by prisoners taken
in the affair as by conversations with responsible and well-informed
men on the other side, after the capitulation of Metz. At the lowest
estimate Bazaine must have had 100,000 men, who, so far as the defence
of Metz was concerned, were superfluous, and, therefore, detrimental. A
hundred thousand men will sooner consume a given quantity of provisions
than will twenty thousand; and when the larger number reach starvation
point, the surrender of everything—field troops, garrison, fortress,
and town—must occur. But for their appreciation of this eventuality,
nothing would have pleased the Germans better than to have opened a
fair passage to Bazaine any hour in the twenty-four. He might have come
outside the cordon and welcome with 100,000 men, and in the course of
the following day, when he was holding a council of war, and debating,
_more Gallico_, whether to march on Sedan or strike southward and cut
the German communications, a hundred thousand Teutons and more would
have fallen upon him from all points of the compass and smitten him
hip and thigh. But if his field army had been chopped into fragments,
the fortress of Metz, with its garrison left inside, would still have
confronted the besiegers, and its reduction would have been postponed
in proportion to the procrastination of the starvation point effected
by the fewer mouths to feed. Of all this Bazaine, a capable man, whose
merits, within certain limits, the Germans acknowledged frankly, could
not have failed to be aware. He objected to capitulation simpliciter
in the fortress, or to the alternative of capitulation or annihilation
in the open field. But there lay the Moselle, beckoning him on to the
half-way house of Thionville, and beyond Thionville, at the distance
of a single forced march, lay the frontier. Oh! if he could only dash
out through that vexatious cordon. True, across the broad plain lay
the divisions of the Landwehr. A regular soldier himself, Bazaine had
probably learned to despise the Garde Mobile with all the contempt
of a veteran used to command veteran troops; and nothing was more
natural than for a Frenchman to reason from a false analogy that, as
the Garde Mobile, so were the Landwehr. The result gave him to digest
his misconception as best he might. But the conception of Bazaine’s
effort was creditable to his knowledge of the military art. While he
made his grand effort against the Landwehr in the valley, he did his
best to keep the other sections of the environing circle from finding
leisure to concentrate in support of them. The attack on Vany, L’Orme,
and Chieulles was well calculated to engross the 10th Army Corps; that
from Mey on Noisseville and Servigny might well perform the same office
with regard to the 1st Army Corps; the 7th Army Corps might be supposed
to have their hands pretty well full with the sortie against Peltre,
Mercy-le-Haut, and Ars-Laquenexy, and with luck this sortie, too, might
get through as far as the depôt at Courcelles, and bring back of its
abundance to the Gardes Nationaux, who were plying the big guns on the
ramparts, and to the hungry citizens. The well-conceived feint against
Ladonchamps was intended to keep the 3rd Army Corps in play, occupying
as it did the slopes between the valley and the fort of Plappeville.
And then—there was but the Landwehr to cope with, and surely 40,000
veteran French troops might crash through the lines of these citizen
soldiers, weakened as those lines were by elongation across the whole
plateau. Then, hey for Thionville! only ten miles off, where a secure
lair might be had under the guns of that fortress while the columns
caught their wind; and then, picking up by the way what superfluous
troops there might be in its garrison, who could say what next for an
army with no impedimenta? Had the design succeeded Metz would have been
a tougher nut for the Germans to crack than ever, for their policy of
starvation would have been seriously compromised.

The two grand blunders which defeated the scheme were the misconception
on Bazaine’s part of the strength, prowess, and effective dispositions
of the Landwehr, and the mistaken idea that his feints to the right and
left would so engross the 10th and 3rd Army Corps as to prevent them
from aiding the Landwehr to give him check on the plateau. His error
on the former point must have come to him when he saw his regulars
recoiling pell-mell from the Landwehrmen in the villages; the salvoes
of artillery sent by the 10th Army Corps from Malroy, Olgy, and other
heights on the east side of the Moselle, and by the 3rd Army Corps from
Frenecourt, Sennecourt, and the heights above St. Rémy, all coigns of
vantage on the bluffs on the western fringe of the valley, must have
made him comprehend that a feigned attack, even if inconsiderable, was
not capable of engrossing the full attention of a German Army Corps.
The batteries on the positions I have named enfiladed him ruinously.
Down the valley there tore a fierce direct fire from the batteries at
Amelange and those in front of Brieux, which advanced by alternate
batteries with as much coolness and precision as I have seen the troops
of a cavalry regiment working in the Phœnix Park; while from the
heights on either side came such a cross-fire that I was filled with
respect for the troops of Bazaine, when I realised the fact that they
waited for the infantry assault to dislodge them from their position.
Add to Bazaine’s disadvantages that he was miserably short of field
artillery, and the conclusion is obvious that he could not but have
failed under the circumstances, while the deduction was no less patent
that the frustration of any future attempt was equally inevitable.

On the morning of the 8th, the aspect of affairs was still threatening.
It seemed as if the turn had come round of the 1st Army Corps on the
east side of the environment. When the sun rose the French were seen
accumulated in dense masses between Fort St. Julien and the village of
Mey. Both St. Julien and les Bottes maintained a continuous if somewhat
discursive cannonade, making the stones fly all about the village of
Noisseville, whither I had hurried on the first alarm. From 7 A.M. till
early in the forenoon, there came the din of warm artillery fire from
the direction of Peltre and Mercy-le-Haut. At daylight the whole of our
first and second lines were drawn forward in support of the first line,
and the advance posts had fallen back. At nine a battery of German
field-artillery opened on the French infantry from the angle of the
church of Noisseville, and located in a cunning overbank emplacement,
was not to be dislodged by all that St. Julien and les Bottes could do
with that intent. About mid-day, however, a furious pelt of rain came
down and washed the heart out of the French, if there had been any in
them from the first. Gradually the forts, as the infantry retreated
into their rear, gave up firing, after having thrown in the course
of the morning over 100 shells into the fragments of the village of
Noisseville. The fire gradually died away to the south, and about 2
P.M. came the order for the reserves and the artillery to return to
quarters, and for the outposts to be reinstated in their old positions.

The rain which quenched the ardour of the French lasted with scarcely
any intermission for forty-eight hours, and made its impression on
the health of the besieging army. Bronchitis and diarrhœa were the
chief complaints engendered; typhus, which soon after became almost
an epidemic, now first gained serious head. One saw waggon load
after waggon load of sick men going down to Courcelles. Much might
have been done, that was not done, both by German organisation and
by those other organisations set on foot through the liberality of
nations sympathising with human suffering, to ease the hardships of
those poor fellows. It would not have been difficult to have organised
a cheap and serviceable ambulance service to have accommodated both
sick and wounded. Around Metz, in the third line, there are everywhere
practicable roads. District depôts might easily have been established,
one, say, at Ste. Barbe, another at Olgy, a third somewhere behind
Sennecourt, and a fourth at Gravelotte. To these depôts might have
been consigned the sick and wounded Germans from the several regiments
and brigades—the poor fellows whom I saw making long weary journeys to
Courcelles, in springless country carts or on foot. Between each of
these depôts there might have been a continuous service of vehicles
resembling elongated Irish jaunting cars. Such vehicles are the true
pattern of an ambulance. They would hold a man on either foot rest
slightly broadened, two more on the seats, and one above the well in
the centre. Four poles at each corner, with a tarpaulin, would make
an efficient protection from the weather. Thus, by easy stages, and
resting at intervals in the depôts, the sick and wounded might have
been brought down in ease and comfort to Courcelles, whence the trains
were ready to convey them to Germany. These hospital trains were the
most complete things of the kind I had ever seen. The carriages were
built on the American model, with entrance at either end. There were
two tiers of beds down each side, three in each tier, and the beds
had every appliance calculated to give comfort to the occupants. One
carriage was devoted to the purposes of a laboratory, another formed a
living and sleeping room for the volunteer attendants, and a third was
a kitchen. To a callousness for suffering, which must be characterised
as general, there were two exceptions which must be named. One was
the German depôt at Courcelles, of which Baron Von Kramm was the life
and soul, and which did an immensity of good without pretension. The
Baron himself was one of the most energetic men I have ever met,
and wrought day and night with the supremest contempt for the good
things of this life. The other exception was the branch of the English
ambulance, which had its head-quarters in Remilly, under the control
of Mr. Austin Lee, a young gentleman endowed with unquestionable
administrative talent and great energy; he was ably supported by his
assistant, Captain Norman, and within the sphere of my own observation
accomplished much really good work.

On the 11th, a French deserter from Metz was brought into Retonfay;
he belonged to the 59th of the Line, and to the Army Corps of which
Marshal Lebœuf had the command. His regiment, along with two others,
was lying, he reported, on the slope on which stands the village of
Vantoux, a little behind Mey. He told us that his desertion was a
matter of arrangement with a large body of the soldiery. Lots had
been drawn who should make the first attempt, and if the experiment
succeeded, the others were to desert likewise. He dodged along the
verge of the Bois de Grimont, and then came sneaking through the
vine-bergs till he reached the German _Feldwache_ behind Nouilly. Many
Frenchmen were observed apparently watching his career. The man was
an Alsatian, as, he said, were most of the men of his regiment, and he
spoke German well. He said the French soldiers lying around Metz were
tired of their lives. Nominally, the force to which he belonged lived
under canvas, but the stench in the tents was so strong, by reason of
cutaneous diseases among the men, that almost all slept in the open
air. This for several days before must have been tantamount to sleeping
in a water-butt. The skin disease was chiefly of a scorbutic character,
caused by the want of vegetables, the absence of salt, and the almost
exclusive living on horse-flesh. The poor fellow’s mouth and face were
in a very bad state from scurvy. He stated that numbers of his fellows
were similarly affected. The spirit of disaffection was, he reported,
almost universal; even the Guards were tainted with it. Since the
defeat of the 7th the troops had positively refused to make any more
sorties, and it was with difficulty that the officers could prevail on
the men to go upon outpost duty. Of bread, the deserter said, there
was considerable scarcity, and the quality was bad; the quantity of
rice served out was a tea-cup full per day between two men. This, with
the bread, was all the food besides horseflesh the men had. They had
eaten the horses of several cavalry regiments, and were now masticating
battery horses.

Confined to quarters for several days, by an accident which befel me
on the 10th October, I had leisure to note that inattention to sanitary
conditions, which was the weak point in the otherwise almost perfect
organisation of the German army. The French inhabitants around Metz
were outer barbarians in this respect. The hamlet of Tully Veolan, as
described in _Waverley_, was an elysium of cleanliness compared with
the little French villages which everywhere dot the territory around
the city, and in which were quartered the bulk of the besieging army.
Before every door a large and venerable dunghill reeked; in the back
cellars, and in the sties immediately behind the houses, dwelt colonies
of swine, whose lairs, apparently, had not been purified within the
memory of the oldest inhabitant. In dry, warm weather, these dunghills
and pigsties impregnated the air with the pungent fumes of ammonia; in
wet weather, their ooze percolated everywhere, and was indescribably
fœtid. Thus the Germans were the inheritors of nuisances not of their
own engendering. But they were by no means behindhand in the race of
nuisance manufacturing. The entrails of slaughtered cattle, instead
of being collected into one deep receptacle, and there buried deeply
after having been strewed with quicklime, were strewed at random around
the villages and in the gardens, with a little loose earth thrown
over them, through the interstices of which a piece of paunch was
often visible; and no disinfectants were used. Then the establishment
of, and the enforcement of resort to, latrines, placed in suitable
localities and assiduously disinfected, seemed to be almost wholly
neglected, and the consequences were, a state of filth in the immediate
vicinity of the houses, to allude to which further would compel the
use of plainer phraseology than the reader might stomach. It is quite
an erroneous belief that it is important only in warm weather to study
sanitary conditions. Cold, raw weather, with fog, opens the pores of
the human animal nearly as much as the most relaxing tropical climate,
and is far more depressing to the spirits. Open pores, and dulled
spirits, are the conditions exactly fitting a man for the reception
of malarious particles floating in the air; and a heavy fog, when
the ground sweats again, and the air is almost stagnant, is just the
atmosphere in which one can hardly move without coming in contact with
such particles.

Living as I did, with regimental, as contradistinguished from staff
officers, I enjoyed considerable opportunities for estimating the
thoroughness of the German system of military training and education.
A German general ought to be a good despatch-writer, considering the
training in this line which he must have undergone. He began to write
despatches as soon as he acquired a separate command, were it only of
half a company. The despatch system of the German army is perfect, and
the compilation of an accurate and detailed narrative of each campaign
ought to be very easy. Take an instance. From Retonfay, on the morning
of the 12th October were transmitted to divisional head-quarters,
the despatches from the 4th Regiment, recounting the doings of that
contingent in the action of Noisseville, on the 31st of August and
1st of September. The colonel sent in his summary of the manœuvres of
the three battalions under his command. The major of each battalion
likewise made his report of the proceedings of his own battalion,
and every captain or officer in command of a company, also sent
forward a succinct statement of his company’s doings. These reports
were accompanied by a chart on a large scale, showing the positions
of the regiment at various periods of the battle, and drawn in this
instance by a young lieutenant of our mess, the most accurate and rapid
military topographist I have ever known. To be a practical draughtsman
is imperative with every German officer. The puzzle with me was to
find out what accomplishment is not imperative. The prickles which
surround the rose of a commission are many and sharp, and, after all,
the rose (at least in a nursery-gardening sense) is no great thing.
Take the case, for instance, of my own especial comrade in the 4th,
which presented no exceptional features. He came to the regiment from
the gymnasium, in the capacity of avantageur. An avantageur is neither
more nor less than a private soldier who aspires to a commission.
On joining he had to pass an examination to demonstrate his general
scholarship. For twelve months he served in the ranks, rising meanwhile
to the non-commissioned rank of Vicefeldwebel, and then he went for
a year to the military school. On leaving this, he had to pass an
examination on everything relating to the art military, and then he
came back to the regiment as a Degen fähnrich, that is to say, as the
wearer of a sword without commissioned rank, for six months longer. The
officers of the regiment then voted him eligible for a commission, and
expressed their readiness, by the same vote, to accept him as a comrade
and a credit to the service; this step being an indispensable prelude
to a commission. Whereupon the King was graciously pleased to sign his
commission as second lieutenant, a rank which he had contentedly held
for six years, and may hold still, for aught I know to the contrary. He
wore the medals for 1866; he had commanded the company for the last two
months, because his seniors had been killed off; he was looking forward
with hope to be a premier lieutenant in the course of another year, and
a captain in perhaps ten more. His pay in war-time was about 11_l._
10_s._ a month, in peace he had not much more than about half as much.
And he was a thoroughly contented, even-minded, German gentleman, proud
of the service, proud that he bore a commission in it, very hungry for
the Iron Cross, fond of fun, and fonder still of work and fighting. I
have simply cited him as a typical example; his fellows were as like
him as peas. Who would not like to see a few peas of such a pod in the
British army?

Reluctantly obliged to quit the front, as the result of the accident
already alluded to, I spent the time from the 14th to the morning
of the 18th invalided in Saarbrück. Having partially recovered, and
reaching Remilly early in the forenoon of the 18th, I found ready
to start five fourgons belonging to the English Ambulance Company.
These were about to start for a journey round Metz, and I had the
opportunity afforded me of a seat in one of the carriages. Our journey
to Corny lay behind the foreposts through a chain of villages in the
rear of the great bulk of the Prussian regiments in the south of Metz.
As we passed Pontoy we emerged on the upland plateau lying to the
south of Forts Queleu and St. Privat, and before us to the north lay
those sulky-looking fortresses which refused to be enlivened even by
the rays of the afternoon sun which fell full on their sullen sides.
St. Privat was speaking with its great guns, firing in the direction
of the Moselle, but close to us lay a German field battery in utter
quiescence, sure token that nothing of importance was going on. At
dusk we reached Corny, and applied to the Etappen Commandant for
quarters. His answer was an imperative suggestion of the same character
as that which the Gardes Mobiles made to Mr. Malet on his way from
Paris to Meaux. We had no resource but to sleep under the beautiful
stars. They were fortunately in our case visible, and we bivouacked
not uncomfortably in an orchard outside the town, which was greatly
preferable to finding harbour in any of the houses of Corny, infected
as they were to the very ceiling with malignant typhus. A visit for
purposes of information to the fine old château occupied by Prince
Frederick Charles resulted in my being told that the parlementaire
which came out a day or two previous had returned into Metz without
any definite result, and that it was believed that Metz would now hold
out to the bitter end. Further, that there was to be no change in the
Prussian tactics. Still “masterly inactivity” was to be the policy,
and the intelligence received from Metz of a reliable character was of
a nature to indicate that this policy could not possibly, ere long,
fail to effect the desired results. Prince Frederick Charles had been
suffering seriously from dysentery, and was still far from fully
recovered, but he was able to sit his horse, and he had never devolved
his functions of commander-in-chief on any substitute.

Corny lies in a trough on the edge of the Moselle, shut out from even
a distant view of Metz by two curious conical hills, which are part of
the series of which the hill of Mousson and the range of St. Quentin
and Plappeville are also summits. The peak nearest the river and
also nearest Metz is known as the Mont St. Blaise. Its elevation is
considerable, and its position renders it highly eligible as a look-out
station. On its summit there was an observatory, and this observatory
I visited on the morning of the 19th, notwithstanding the black looks
of those in authority in Corny. The whole hilltop is honeycombed with
artificial caves, formed, I suppose, in the old days, when the ruin
on the summit was a castle inhabited by some magnate of Lorraine, who
from his eyrie here dominated the beautiful plain before him, and
the valley of the Moselle. Alongside of this ruin, and at an equal
elevation, stands a lonely farmhouse, and in front of it the Germans
had erected a wooden look-out post, open toward Metz, and mounted with
a very powerful telescope. Not a man could move within the scope of
vision that the action was not noted by the officer who constantly
sat with his eye at this instrument. A series of telegraph wires from
the several head-quarters of the different army corps converged into
a little office in the rear of this look-out post, and the intentions
of the French were thus known as soon as they proceeded to manifest
them by action. The post was occupied by two officers belonging to
the Second Army Corps—and they had at their disposal a detachment of
soldiers to act as orderlies, messengers, &c. What a glorious view
opened out before me as I stood on the little plateau in front of the
ruin of the Castle of St. Blaise, and looked Metz-ward! On the left
lay the narrow valley of the Moselle, with the slope of the vinebergs
in front of Gorze rising beyond. At our feet, to the left, lay
Ars-sur-Moselle, skirted by the railway, with the old Roman aqueduct
spanning the Moselle with its antique arches, and the new road-bridge
nearer Montigny, where is the junction of the railway lines from Nancy
and from Saarbrück. A locomotive was dodging about the junction as
I looked down upon it, and right before me stretched the rich and
beautiful plain, variegated with green and brown and yellow, according
to the tint of the crops and the foliage, and with the great twin tower
of the Cathedral of Metz standing up defiantly in its centre. The river
wound through the flat in three great stretches: one close under the
frowning bulk of Mont St. Quentin, that sulked blackly across at us
on the left front; the other twisting in a long half-loop far away to
the right. As it seemed immediately under us, Fort St. Privat peeped
spitefully out over the poplars surrounding the pretty village and
château of Cuvry, and further to my right the grim earthwork front of
Fort Queleu showed itself over the plain like a huge beaver-dam. The
prospect was vast. Carrying the eye over the spires of the cathedral,
I could see to the north of Metz the long link of the Moselle trending
eastward toward Olgy and Malroy, and with the powerful telescope in
position I could even discern St. Eloy and beyond it the blackened
ruins of St. Rémy, Ladonchamps, and Grandes and Petites Tapes, where
on the 7th I had seen the Landwehrmen die in their tracks rather than
yield the post they held, although bidden thereto by an overwhelming
number of enemies. It may be interesting to give the positions of the
hostile forces as seen from this summit, since the status quo then
visible was unaltered when the capitulation took place.

To begin at the now well-known Mercy-le-Haut. Opposite this point the
French were in force in front of Grigy, from which position they, on
the previous night, had burnt the remaining houses of Peltre. Working
westward, the French foreposts were in Bevoye, Magny, in front of
Montigny, and thence across the Moselle, at Moulins-les-Metz, which
had been burnt about a fortnight previously. From Moulins the forepost
line ran up toward St. Hubert, between St. Ruffine and Chazelles, hence
in front of Scy through the valley close under Mont St. Quentin to
Lessy, and thence northward with a slight sweep allowed by Plappeville
to Lorry, and thence to Vigneulles and Woippy. The completion of the
circle I have already detailed. The German foreposts, commencing
from the right, stood at Peltre, and thence westward in front of St.
Thiebauldt, and in la Papeterie (opposite Magny). Then there was a
sweep comparatively close to Metz, as the Moselle was neared, and the
Teuton foreposts stood impudently at Frescaty, whence the sentries must
have been able almost to discern the complexions of the gunners in St.
Privat. The line crossed the Moselle to Vaux, considerably in front of
Ars-sur-Moselle, and then made a dart forward to Jussy, right under the
guns of St. Quentin. Longeau, where there was a battery of 12-pounders,
continued the chain to Chatel St. Germain. Across the plateau and the
wood of Chatel ran the links of the strong forepost chain, giving a
wide berth to the big guns of Plappeville, behind Lorry, through the
Bois de Vigneulles to Saulny, from Saulny, still further north-east, to
Villers-le-Plesnois, and so by Norroy to the bloodstained Ladonchamps,
and the villages whose names are known in connection with the combat of
the 7th, to the Moselle at Maxe.

So much for the positions of the two hostile armies in this portion
of the investiture. A few words now as to the position of the French
inside their outposts yet outside the town of Metz, the gates of which
were sealed to Bazaine’s army as rigorously as was the road to liberty.
The French had in all, in the environed space and outside Metz, four
great lagers or camps. The first and probably the largest was on the
slope of Mont St. Quentin, looking toward Mont St. Blaise. I could
see the rows of tents athwart and athwart the slope, past the village
of Scy, and stretching almost down to Chazelles. Another great camp
almost joined the first at Longeville, a village on the west bank of
the Moselle, and this lager straggled up the river margin first to
Ban St. Martin, where Bazaine had his head-quarters, and so on to the
north as far as Devant-les-Ponts. A third camp was in front of St.
Julien, toward Vantoux, Vallieres, &c., concerning which detailed
information has already been given. The fourth camp was around Borny
and Grigy. Besides those camps of soldiers there seemed two great
collections of sick, so far as I could discern through the telescope of
the observatory. One of those was placed on the esplanade of the town
of Metz, and along the river brink. I saw the sick being carried about
on stretchers, and the curtains of the tents looped up to give air to
the inmates. Another great sick camp seemed to be on the Island of
Sauley, where it was believed the cases of typhus fever were as much as
possible isolated.

After quitting Corny on the 19th, our way lay across the river by
Noveant, the means of transit being a pontoon bridge. Just outside
Corny we came on a graveyard, which the Germans had improvised for
the bodies of those who had died in the hospitals there, whether from
sickness or wounds. They were burying the dead now in deal coffins, but
the coffins were put in the ground with very scant ceremony. I saw a
fatigue party putting a couple into a hole, the coffins lying on the
grass while the men dug their receptacle.

Gorze was very much changed for the better since the last time I had
visited it in the days of blood. From the summit of the great convent
on the hill there still was flying the hospital flag; but under it lay
few or no wounded men. The hotel was tenanted by the cheery officers of
the staff of Prince Louis of Hesse, who commanded the troops lying in
the town, consisting of several regiments of Hessian men, with the lion
instead of the eagle on their helmets. The inhabitants had all come
back to their houses, and lived not alone on amicable, but seemingly
on cordial terms with the stalwart Hessians billeted upon them. Madame
of the hotel, who in August had moved about in a stolid apathy of
despair, utterly disregarding any questions that might be put to her,
and with hardly energy enough to wring her hands, was now blithe and
sprightly, moving about briskly in response to the requirements of
her numerous guests. The stains that blood had made on the floors
were still visible—indelible probably while the boarding lasts, but
Madame’s despair was much more evanescent, and with her return to good
spirits the landlady’s instinct of charging stiff prices had returned.
In August, when I asked her whether I should pay her anything for the
quasi shelter I had had for the night under her roof, she only stared
blankly in my face, and I could not get an answer from her. Now, when I
asked her whether she could give me a bottle of good wine, she eagerly
