replied in the affirmative, and placed before me a really good bottle
of Muscat, for which she put on, with a smiling face, the fancy price
of twelve francs.

The road from Gorze to Rezonville consists, first of an ascent through
a narrow gorge into a corner of the Bois de Vionville, and then it
traverses the plain in a line with the close of the battle of Vionville
on the 16th of August. On the bleak October forenoon all that we saw
in the way of traces of the fights of August were the many graves and
occasional heaps of the _débris_ of the battle-field. But another
sense than that of sight bore testimony to tokens of carnage. Every
grave-heap had beside it an eccentric tombstone in the shape of a
barrel of chloride of lime; yet, notwithstanding this disinfectant,
the taint in the air was unmistakable and indescribably fœtid. In the
village of Rezonville were still some of the more severely wounded,
and the doctors were heartily glad to see the stores which the English
ambulance fourgons contained. At Rezonville the troops were Hessians,
belonging to the 7th Army Corps. After a short halt, to enable Mr. Lee
to distribute his stores judiciously, we pressed on to Gravelotte,
where we were most cordially received by the officers of a battery of
artillery quartered there. Afterwards we were permitted to go on to
the foreposts in front of Chatel St. Germain, looking over to Fort
St. Julien. The chain of sentries stood just where it had done on
the morning of the 20th of August, looking down into the intervening
valley. The French had a large cavalry forepost in the bottom of the
valley. On the slope around the pretty village of Scy lay the white
tents of the French camp which I had seen on the previous day from
the top of Mont St. Blaise. Walking some half a mile in the direction
of the Moselle, we came presently on a promontory that juts out into
the valley, and commands a view of the whole country. It seemed that
at our very feet lay the huge pile of the Cathedral; the tin on its
spires flashing up in a watery ray of the morning sun, and the great
mass casting a dark shadow across the Place d’Armes. On the Isle of
Sauley we could see the “isolated” crawling about the river’s edge, and
between the ramparts and the great southern camp a locomotive was at
work on the railway.

As we walked back from the extreme foreposts we passed many rifle-pits
on the crest of the rising ground forming the sentry-line, and also a
regularly-constructed battery, with earthworks in front and casemates
and magazine in the rear. On the platform were mounted six 12-pounders,
and in the rear, near Chatel, were several batteries of field
artillery. The 12-pounders looked across at Fort St. Julien in the most
impudent manner imaginable, as if to say, “Your fortress exists only on
our sufferance; we could blow you to little pieces with a round or two
if we chose, but we prefer leaving you alone, out of our tender mercy.”
And St. Quentin, on its part, seemed to look across at the 12-pounders
with something of a risus sardonicus on its grim grey features.

Returning to Gravelotte, we set out for the completion of our design
to make the circuit of the Prussian position. Our road lay past the
blackened ruins of Malmaison, in which, on the afternoon of the 18th
of August, 200 French wounded were burnt, by shells fired from French
guns. The smell in the shell, which was all that remained of a once
large mansion, was horrible. The tiles had fallen in and hidden the
charred corpses of the miserables who perished in the conflagration;
but here and there projected a ghastly blackened hand, or a gartered
leg swathed in discoloured red cloth. The place was one vast horrible
grave, never to be thought of without a shudder. In a lonely upland
valley we came upon the forlorn and ruined hamlet of Champenoise,
utterly burnt out, smashed and pounded in all directions with shells,
pitted all over with the dents of chassepot bullets, and its precincts
full of mounds, below which lay, side by side, defenders and captors.
Further to the north, in the rear of Amanvilliers, was a twin battery
to the one at Gravelotte, very daintily constructed, and fenced round
with _chevaux de frise_, and I rather think some pleasant little
surprises in the shape of mines. From Amanvilliers the road trended
away to the left to avoid the entrenchments in the vicinity of the
foreposts at Saulny, and presently reached the village of Jerusalem.
Lying in a sequestered and somewhat isolated tract, few students of
war literature have heard much about this tiny namesake of the great
Hebrew city of antiquity. Nevertheless, it was the scene of a terrible
conflict on the evening of the 18th of August. It had its Titus in
General von Pape, but there was no Josephus to chronicle the episodes
of the fray. Yet as eloquently as any pen could have described them
were they indicated now by the silent yet expressive grave-tokens sown
thickly all round the little spot, whose ruin was so utter that it
might, to complete the parallel with its namesake of old, have been
sown with salt.

Quitting the plateau lying to the north-west of Fort Plappeville,
we drove through a great wood, chiefly of underbrush, and suddenly
came out on a bluff overhanging the valley of the Moselle. Our point
was just before Feves, and the view was glorious. Moy before us in
the valley, one could easily trace the line of the German foreposts
stretching across to the river; nearer to Metz again was the French
line, and behind it the white tents of the camps, with columns of
troops moving about in the interval. We could see the zig-zags of Fort
Moselle, and of the enceinte surrounding the town. At Feves, just below
us, and in a splendid position, was a battery of 12-pounders, the same
that I had seen speaking to such good purpose on the afternoon of the
7th. Getting down into the valley, we crossed the Thionville road, and
struck for the temporary wooden barracks erected on the railway to the
right front of Maizières, in which was quartered the Fusilier battalion
of the 81st Regiment, which had distinguished itself in Ladonchamps on
the night of the 7th. In front of the Baracken was another battery of
12-pounders, and on the other side of Maizières, before the Chateau of
Amelange, stood yet another. We crossed the river at Hautconcourt, and
came down to Charly in the gloaming. Next morning I quitted my good
friends of the fourgons, and struck across for my old comrades of the
4th Regiment, whom I found quartered in Château Gras, a little village
some half-mile to the north rear of Retonfay.

Among the “kent faces” that greeted me after my absence I missed
one or two whom I had learnt to reckon friends. The adjutant of the
battalion, a joyous lad fresh from the schools, a youngster of eighteen
summers, had succumbed to the hardships of the situation. The marrow
was not “set” in the growing boy’s bones, and where seasoned men
found it not an easy matter to keep sickness from assailing them with
success, the dart had penetrated through his green frame, and he had
been compelled to go back to the Fatherland, a shattered veteran ere
he had attained his full stature. I missed, too, another face, that
of a lieutenant, whose determination to consider himself well while as
yet the fever had not left him, I had respected in one sense, while in
another I had deprecated. Wounded in the fight by Noisseville on the
1st of September, he had been sent back to St. Wendel, where in the
lazarette fever had laid hold of him. His wound healed, and the fever
in part abated, the soldier lusted to be again with his comrades in the
forefront of the battle, and he had quitted the lazarette while as yet
the dregs of the fever had not been eliminated from his frame. On the
day of his arrival he lay beside me on the straw, with the alternate
cold and hot fits working their will of him, and then I told him that
his return had been premature. My words were made good by the fact that
two days after the doctor had peremptorily insisted on his return to
St. Wendel, and so he had gained nothing by his enterprise save another
strain on his constitution.

A friend had kindly kept for me a little diary of the siege events on
this face which had occurred since my retirement to Saarbruck, but
they were of so little importance as not to call for reproduction.
The only actual sortie made by the French troops, and that in
comparatively small numbers, had been on the 16th, in the direction of
the Brewery. Of course they were driven back. They could have hoped
for nothing else. All the damage they did was to shoot through the
chest the Feldwebel of the 6th Company. King Wilhelm had no better
non-commissioned officer in all his vast army. Strauss smoked his
cigar while the doctor cut the bullet out of his back, the lieutenant
beside whom he was shot holding him up while the operation was being
performed. Then he quietly squared up his company-book, and counting
the money in his hands (the Feldwebel is the pay-sergeant of the
company), handed everything over to his successor in right trim, and
went away philosophically in the waggon to a lazarette. Deserters had
become so numerous as to engender a suspicion that there was a design
for depleting the garrison by this device, and so eking out the stores
of food. A few deserters are useful, and not to be discouraged. Assume
that none of them speak the truth; King David tells us that “all men
are liars,” and yet from our fellow-men’s utterances we contrive to
extract some grains of truth. In like manner a little wheat can be
winnowed out of the chaff of a deserter’s farragos, and one or two are
acceptable. But now that their name was legion, the order had been
issued to receive no more bodies of deserters into the lines, but
to send them back whence they came by the powerful argument of the
needle-gun. How was it that so many of the deserters were drunk when
they came in? Was strong drink on tap in the outposts round Metz, or
were the men dosed for the attempt? The latter hypothesis might seem
to be borne out by the fact, of which I was assured, not by French
prisoners alone, but by German doctors who had administered to the
French wounded, that the French tirailleurs who pushed so boldly to
the front at Maizières on the 7th were all full of brandy. Ichabod!
Ichabod! How had the glory departed from the French arms when the
leaders were compelled to infuse Dutch courage into their soldiers! The
French were the readiest to scorn the Russian serf-soldier because,
when he came up through the fog wreaths on the morning of Inkermann,
his sluggish pulses had been quickened by corn-schnaps. Now, it seemed
that they found the same unsoldier-like stimulant requisite in their
own case.

I have heard Count Bismarck compared to an elephant’s trunk, as one for
whom nothing was too great or too small. But Bismarck was not the only
German in this war who looked after all little matters as well as the
great. I was much amused by an item in one of the orders of the day
from Prince Frederick Charles’s head-quarters. It was to the effect
that whereas an unprincipled rascal of a Hamburg cigar merchant had
been selling boxes of cigars to the troops, the upper tier of which
consisted of good weeds while the lower ones were unmitigated trash,
the military authorities of any place where this astute gentleman
should present himself, were enjoined to lay hold of him and transmit
him to the head-quarters at Corny. At such an entry one may smile,
but I defy him to refrain from respecting the all-pervading care and
watchfulness which took cognisance of such a matter as this. In such
care and watchfulness lay the root of much of the Prussian success. A
campaign is not made victorious by dint of fighting alone; success more
than half depends on efficient organisation, even as regards seeming
trifles.

Château Gras must have been a beautiful village before the tide of war
came and strewed sand and shingle over its charms. A fine old chateau
is the principal building in it, the noble garden of which had been
very little injured, while the furniture had also suffered very little.
In a room on the ground floor the books still stood on the long ranges
of shelves upon the wail. So careful were the Germans to prevent injury
to property that on the door of every room occupied by them was pasted
an inventory of its contents, for which the successive occupants were
held responsible, just as in a barrack at home. The chateau till lately
had been a large Feldlazarette, occupied by the wounded of the fights
before Noisseville of the 31st of August and 1st of September. The
smell of the chloride of lime was still so powerful in the beautiful
rooms that some of the officers preferred more modest quarters in the
houses of the village. When the owner of the chateau came back he must
have shuddered at the legacy the war had left him. Of the wounded in
the great lazarette, not a few had succumbed before the grim king of
terrors. In a quiet corner of the garden, looking out through the trees
on to St. Julien, St. Quentin, les Bottes, and the great towers of the
cathedral in the centre of the plain, is a graveyard, where lie those
who died of their wounds in the lazarette. It is prettily enclosed with
a wooden paling. The one great grave, over 100 yards in length, is
banked up with green sods, and the surface trimly raked. It is flanked
up by a great wooden cross bearing the simple inscription:—“Hier ruhen
285 Preussische und 5 Französische tapfere Krieger.” There are besides
several graves of officers, which are adorned with box-wood crosses
planted in the soil and with wreaths of immortelles.

By the 25th of October the symptoms grew more and more pronounced of
a speedy ending to the siege, apart altogether from the accounts of
negociations which we had from line to head-quarters. On the night
of the 24th I had a conversation with a deserter whose information
seemed of considerable exactitude and apparent truthfulness. He spoke
of a rapid and continuous diminution in the rations served out to the
troops, and of increasing irregularity in the supply. Eight days back
the bread ration had been half a pound a day for each man; now it was
reduced to four ounces, and there was difficulty in procuring this
morsel. The man understood that the Imperial Guards who lay around
Bazaine at Ban St. Martin, received rather more food than the other
troops, and they were used by reason of their superior discipline and
their greater contentment as a means of keeping in some semblance of
order the line regiments. To each peloton a live horse was given per
day, but the man described the brutes as consisting of little else than
bones and hide. There can be little wonder that the number of sick was
greatly on the increase under such circumstances. Out of 800 men of
which this man’s battalion consisted, 200 were sick and unfit for duty.
Those who were nominally able men were, he said, in a state of great
weakness, and certainly his own appearance corroborated this statement.

Various statements appeared to have been made within the last day or
two by the French generals to their troops, to keep them from falling
into utter despondency. Another deserter brought out a cock and bull
story about a general order, to the effect that the Empress was to
arrive in Metz on the 25th, and to assume the supreme power, and that
then on the next day the whole army was to march away to the north
by permission of the Germans. The authorities seemed to interpret
these and kindred statements as giving intimation of a sortie in
force towards the north on the 26th. The line to the north of Metz
on both sides of the river was accordingly greatly strengthened,
and the troops in the third line all round were called out at five
o’clock in the morning of that day, in positions enabling them to act
efficiently as reserves and supports for the first and second lines.
These precautions were no doubt wise, yet on the face of things it
seemed that a sortie was physically impossible. It had rained with
hardly any intermission for three days, and was still raining. Except
where the macadam of the chaussées afforded a hard bottom under the
mud, it was impossible to traverse the country on foot. One floundered
helplessly in the slippery stuff till he got to a deeper place than
usual, and then had to struggle vehemently to prevent himself from
sticking outright. No regiment could have kept its formation in ground
so holding; and to move artillery or train, except on the macadamised
roads, must have been utterly impossible. Following the caution of the
Germans, I rode round early to Malroy to be on the ground, if anything,
contrary to my belief, should take place; but the alarm, as I had
anticipated, turned out to be false.

The 26th October was a great day with us in Château-Gras. Metz had not
quite capitulated. There had been no sortie. The rain was coming down
unceasingly, and the country was one huge slush. But nevertheless, I
repeat, it was a great day with us. It was our doctor’s birthday, and
there was no reason why we should not keep it, while the generals were
finishing the more serious business. Our doctor was a man unknown to
fame, but he deserved it more than many a man who has earned it. He was
of the fighting jolly style of medico; although his business was to
cure wounds, he had no objection, but the contrary, to cut out work for
himself. If you had met him in Cork, you would unhesitatingly have set
him down as a native, with his merry black eyes, wide laughing mouth,
and—how shall I put it delicately?—his nose with the Hibernian bend.
And it was the birthday of this excellent fellow.

I could not make out in the morning why _appel_ was hurried over so
rapidly; but I was soon to learn. The adjutant tucked my arm under
his, and marched me off into the chateau. Of the _salle_ the officers
had made a mess-room, and it was into this fine apartment that the
adjutant guided me about ten o’clock in the morning. We were one and
all, it seemed, the guests of the doctor on this the anniversary of his
natal morn. Nor was the doctor hospitable without the means. A certain
good lady, calling him husband, and dwelling in Königsberg, had not
forgotten the day, and, as a birthday present, had sent her good man
a great chest. The sum total of the contents of that chest, the world
through me can never know. The enumeration I heard indeed, but I lost
the tally when the doctor was about half through with it. Suffice it
to say that the table groaned under a load of delicacies. I had seen a
telegram from Berlin, to the effect that the troops before Metz were
living on mutton. The telegraphist would have opened his eyes had he
dropped in this morning. There was Schinken and boiled ham, there was
a German sausage, as big as the good heart of the doctor’s wife, there
was a snug little barrel of sardellen, pickled with bay leaves; there
was German gingerbread and rusks; there was fresh butter, and there was
compot of plums, and I don’t know what all else. Then, for drink, there
was the choice of Malaga and claret, rum, brandy, Hamburg bitters, and
absinthe, and a hearty welcome to season all. We had a curious way of
eating in the foreposts, what may be styled, a miscellaneous manner of
feeding. Sausage, gingerbread, sardines, and compot, were seen on one
plate at once, and served to season one another in a very impartial
way. I don’t think the doctor could reasonably have complained of the
appetites which were brought to his feast. He had one appetite of
which he could have had no anticipation. Just as we were in the middle
of the feast, a miserable devil of a French deserter was brought in
from the foreposts. I never before saw the wolfish glare of absolute
hunger darting from a man’s eyes. Wet, dirty, half-rotten, seemingly
from scurvy, with puffed lips, and hollow cheeks, this gaunt spectacle
of a man devoured the food with his great staring eyes. He could not
reply to any questions, so concentrated was he upon his gaze at the
meaty ham-bone, and when the Hauptmann threw it to him, it positively
made one shudder to see the dog-like style in which he fell a-worrying
of it. When all, including, I hope, the poor wretch in the lobby, had
eaten their fill, the senior Hauptmann called for full glasses, and in
a neat speech proposed the doctor’s health. The doctor was very waggish
in his reply. He suggested we should dedicate a bumper to St. Julien
and les Bottes, because they had been courteous enough not to intervene
to the detriment of the crockeryware, and expressed, further, a fervent
wish that Bazaine were making one of the company. By twelve o’clock
the doctor was drunk out—that is, his stores were—but a happy thought
struck a lieutenant concerning a barrel of beer which he had in his
room. This was fetched, and its contents went the way of the doctor’s
stores.

As we broke up, I heard the word go round that there would be a
“picnic” in the evening. One is apt to associate the idea of a picnic
with leafy glades, blue muslin, bad champagne, and lobster salad. None
of these _agréments_ had we in Château Gras. The rain was falling fast,
the winter wind was blowing bitterly, and the idea of a picnic seemed
about as wild as would have been the suggestion of an oratory. But
nevertheless the “picnic” came off, although not quite according to my
preconceived notions of this institution. It was an indoor picnic; in
point of fact, it took place in the hall where the doctor gave his
breakfast. It was on the mutual co-operation principle—everybody was
expected to bring something. My contribution was a box of sardines, a
lump of butter, and a bottle of rum. I am afraid this was not quite
up to the general mark; but what I had I gave—I could no more. I
stood in the lobby awhile, watching the arrivals and their several
contributions. One Hauptmann was accompanied by his servant carrying
half-a-dozen of red wine, a lieutenant had a leg of mutton and a bag
full of sugar under his cloak. The hall looked quite civilised. On the
great round table in the centre stood an actual moderator lamp, with a
painted shade atop of it, its pedestal resting on a mat. Talk of war
times, here was refinement with a vengeance. A great wood fire blazed
cheerfully on the hearth, and the glasses—somewhat nondescript in
shape and size, it is true—sparkled on the board. After a brief pause,
the first service of tipple was brought in, contained in a white soup
tureen and an antique china vase. The drink was very good. I regret
being unable to furnish the recipe for its composition. I may state,
however, that among the ingredients were red wine, white wine, rum,
cognac, schnapps, cloves, and cinnamon; of the proportions of each I
could learn nothing. I incline to the opinion that a “stock” pot with
the red wine was put on, and, as contributions came in, that they were
emptied into the pot promiscuously.

For about two hours conversation, alternated with story-telling, was
the order of the evening. The talk fell once upon the causes of the
French defeats, and after various explanations had been started, a
sententious premier lieutenant, who had not previously opened his
mouth, struck in with this original illustration and very sound
theory:—“The chief rabbi of the Dantzic Jews had taken a new house, and
his flock determined to stock his wine butt for him. An evening was set
apart for the affair, and one after another the Jews went down into the
cellar, and emptied each his bottle into the big vat. When the rabbi
came next day to draw off his dinner wine, he found there was nothing
in the cask but water. Each and every Jew had said to himself, that one
bottle of water could never be noticed in so great a quantity of wine,
and all acting up to this, the rabbi had not got a drop of wine in his
butt. Now it was just the same with the French army. One soldier said
to himself that it would not matter a copper if he sneaked away—in so
great a multitude he would not be missed. But the devil of it was, that
one and all took this line of reasoning, and the result was that nobody
was left to look our battalions in the face.” Everybody laughed at the
premier lieutenant’s quaint illustration, and then we had a stand-up
supper at the sideboard. That finished, singing was the order of the
evening. It surprised me not a little when one of the officers struck
up “My hearts in the Highlands,” and almost all were able to chime,
not alone with the tune, but the words. The doctor treated us to a
composition of his own, composed to illustrate passing events, for the
burden of it was—“The Bois de Grimont oh!” The Bois de Grimont was the
wood out of which the French sharp-shooters so persistently peppered
away at our foreposts. Encouraged by the success of this effort, he
gave us another song _pour l’occasion_, the chorus of which mixed up
“Mercy-le-Haut,” and “Ter Teufel,” in some mysterious manner. It may
interest the British music-hall patrons to know that a translation of
“The Captain with the whiskers” is one of the chiefest favourites with
the German officers. They rattle away at its lively chorus with great
delight. We had it to-night over and over again. At ten o’clock the
“stock” pot being empty, we broke up with great joviality, and betook
ourselves each unto his respective straw.

CHAPTER II.

PUCELLA RAPTA.


After all our long waiting from day to day in anticipation of
the capitulation of Metz, that event came upon us with a strange
suddenness. It had only been a few days previously that it was
mentioned in the general orders from head-quarters that the
negotiations had been definitively broken off; and when men heard this
they set their teeth hard, and prepared for another spell of waiting,
variegated with fighting. But there had been symptoms of the end being
imminent in the haggard deserters who came among us from day to day,
with their longing looks at our black bread and flesh. Orders had been
issued to the railway people in Saarbrück to have material in readiness
for repairing the railway communication direct into Metz; and this was
another symptom. But still, when the report ran round the regiment,
on the afternoon of the 27th, that the capitulation had actually been
negotiated, some found it difficult to realise the fact—confirmation
was wanting. It was true that the day before the generals commanding
the several army corps had been summoned to Frescaty by an order of
Prince Frederick Charles, and that some of us had the “straight tip”
under promise of the profoundest secrecy; but everything looked so much
in the ordinary way, that people could not realise the fact of the
capitulation. Men went on the field watch as usual, and the confronting
foreposts still continued to exchange shots. No white flag was visible,
and the unbelieving Thomases of the 4th shrugged their shoulders, and
would have none of the tidings. But general orders are convincing
arguments, and even the unbelieving Thomases took to clapping their
hands and capering, when the news came under the sign manual of the
chief of the staff.

At twelve o’clock on the forenoon of the 28th each of the outlying
fortresses was taken possession of by two battalions of German
infantry, a squadron of cavalry, one heavy battery without tumbrils,
100 artillerymen with the complement of officers, and an engineer
detachment. The following is the apportionment of the force of
occupation. The troops occupying

  St. Quentin were found by 18th Infantry Division.
  Plappeville  ” ” 3rd Army Corps.
  St. Julien ” ” 1st Army Corps.
  Queleu ” ” 8th Army Corps.
  St. Privat ” ” Hessian Division.

At the same hour a battalion of the 7th Army Corps marched forward, and
took possession of the Porte Serpenoise, one of the gates of Metz,
and another battalion from the same corps occupied the Porte Moselle.
Two hours before the occupation of the forts, I should observe, there
were sent forward from each of the occupying detachments an artillery
officer and a small body of under officers, accompanied by engineers,
to take over the powder magazines and the respective forts, and they
sent out the report to the effect that all was in order before the
troops were allowed to march in. This precaution was no doubt dictated
by a recollection of the catastrophe at Laon.

It was in orders that at one o’clock on the 28th the French army
should formally lay down its arms. By reason of the confusion which
reigned everywhere this disarmament was protracted and piecemeal, a
large proportion of the force not disburdening themselves of the now
useless arms till the following morning. There was no set ceremony,
yet the affair was imposing from its very simplicity. It was conducted
in detail, each corps laying down its arms in the neighbourhood of its
own station. I saw several regiments of the 3rd Corps d’Armée—that of
Lebœuf—disarm themselves. In straggling masses the men defiled past,
piling their arms in great heaps at the word of command from their own
officers, who laid down their swords as the men did their chassepots,
building quite a little heap of them to the right of the great stacks
of rifles. The disarmed French troops then returned into their
bivouacs, which they occupied for one night more, before quitting for
other bivouacs round which should stand German sentries. For this last
night they preserved a semblance of freedom. The Germans left them to
themselves. Except in the forts and at the two gates I have mentioned,
not a spiked helmet was to be seen nearer Metz than had been seen a
week before. The _Feldwachen_ were mounted as usual, the _replis_ were
established, and the troops stood fast as ever in their long and weary
watch around Metz. Indeed, in the course of the night there was a
slight skirmish somewhere between Vautoux and Mey, conducted on their
own account, as I afterwards learned, by a few men who thought proper
to repudiate the capitulation. So late as when I passed Little Belle
Croix on the forenoon of the 29th, I heard a few dropping shots on the
slope to the right, but whether they were fired in anger I never was
able to learn.

On the morning of the 29th October—sombre day for the French arms—the
Frenchmen came forth and rendered themselves to their captors—corps
by corps, and under the command of their own officers. The 6th Corps
(Canrobert’s) came from its bivouac by Woippy to Ladonchamps, where
on the 7th so many of its members had stained the soil with their
blood. Their prison-bivouac was on the plain in the rear of the wrecked
and burnt village. The 7th Corps, that of Ladmirault, came out over
the ridge between St. Quentin and Plappeville, and found quarters,
inhospitable enough I fear, on the bleak plateau of St. Maurice. The
Imperial Guard marched down to Longeville, crossed the railway bridge
at Tournebride, passed on to Frescaty to the left, and found their
bivouac in the plain between Orly and Augny. The garrison of Metz
itself, consisting of about 20,000 men, chiefly of Mobile Guards,
marched by Le Sablon, past the ruined village of Magny, and so forward
to the outskirts of St. Thiebauldt. The 2nd Corps (Frossard’s), with
Laveauconfied’s division, and the 4th brigade came out past La Posette
on the great Strasburg chaussée, and found their bivouac behind the
village of Ars Laquenexy. The 3rd Corps (that of Lebœuf) came out among
us of the 1st German Army Corps along the chaussée leading to Saarlouis.

Each corps, as it marched out, was received by the German troops
covering the respective section of the environment. They were led
by their own officers, who formally handed over their men to the
German officers, and then they were at liberty to quit their ranks
and return to Metz, there to wait the trains which should convey them
into captivity across the German Rhine. It should be mentioned that
their parole was accepted from none of the officers of the army of
Metz, a stringency adopted in consequence of the numerous defaults of
honour perpetrated by officers released on parole after having been
made prisoners. The men under the command of their under-officers were
then marched out to the bivouac places, which had previously been
partly prepared, wood for fires having been collected and a supply of
provisions being ready for distribution.

I was with the 1st Army Corps when it took up its position to receive
the corps of Lebœuf. From all the villages around, the villages
inhabited for so many weary weeks, the villages made memorable by
fights, by burnings, by graves, by sickness, and by deaths, streamed
the regiments on to the chaussée. There had been much rain during the
night, and although the sun broke out about ten o’clock, the watery
haze hung about the bottoms of the valleys and the mud splashed as
the troops strode through it. Mud! why we had had nothing but mud for
weeks, and should we mind it now, looking at the errand on which we
are bound? No. The “Wacht am Rhein” rises lustily from the pioneers in
the front, and the regiments as they come down the side roads catch
up the strain. On we go past the Brewery, with the grenade pits, the
graves, and the entrenchments around it—itself a mere heap of shattered
bricks—on to the gentle slope behind the village of Lauvallière. Then
right and left wheeled the heavy columns, leaving the chaussée open,
and a parallelogram of about 100 feet in width on each side of it. In
front of the Brewery stood the artillery, before the artillery the
serried ranks of brass-spiked helmets all the way down the gentle
slope, almost to Lauvallière. Arms were piled, and there the Germans
waited for the advent of the men with which it almost seemed, through
the length of time that the armies had confronted each other, that
they could claim a kind of personal acquaintance. While the troops
waited I pushed forward towards Metz to meet the Frenchmen. Already,
it is true, we had plenty of Frenchmen, but they were all civilians,
and there were Frenchwomen too, and French children. The road had been
opened in the morning, and they came crowding out, the poor creatures
who had abandoned their village homes and crowded into the town when
the pennons of the Uhlans had shown themselves on the fringes of the
villages. On they came, waggon load after waggon load, with their
trumpery household goods and their agricultural implements piled on
the waggons behind the women and children. They knew not what it was
that they had come out for to see. Perhaps they expected to find their
cottage homes as they had left them—not roofless, wrecked, utterly
ruined, as was the grim reality. Lauvallière seemed to give the first
shock to their cheerfulness. Gaunt and grim stood its houses, burnt
to the shell, the graves lying all around, the holes in the fields
where the shells had burst, the breastworks behind which had stood
the German foreposts. As each waggon and each knot of peasants came
into Lauvallière there was a halt, and full many a “Mon Dieu!” In
Lauvallière there was a large tannery, which must have employed many
workmen, and been an important and profitable concern. It was now a
mere shell—not a fragment of the woodwork is left. I chanced upon its
owner standing on the bluff over against his property, and gazing upon
it in moody silence. Twenty paces farther on I came on a little group,
the extreme pathos of which made my heart swell. It was a family, and
they sat in front of what had once been their home. That home was
now roofless. The stones of the walls were all that was left. The
garden was a wreck, and the whole scene was concentrated desolation.
The husband leant against the wall, his arms folded, and his head on
his chest. The wife sat on the wet ground, weeping over the babe at
her breast. Two elder children stared around them with wonder and
unconcern—too young to realise the misfortune which had overtaken all
the family. The silence of the grief-stricken group was more eloquent
than any words. No home, no food, a waggon, and a couple of starved
horses, a field with four graves in it. This is the brief summary of
the belongings and condition of the family. All were alike; although
this family was the one I happened to see, because their home lay on
my road in Lauvallière, some similar piteous sight must soon have been
visible by every house in every village all around Metz. What need for
me to occupy space by writing of what every one with a grain of human
sympathy in his composition can picture and mourn for himself?

The stream of _émigrés_ continued as I came up the slope on to the
flat, on the edge of which lay the improvised fortification of the
village of Belle Croix. Belle Croix was ghastly in its utter solitude.
Burnt and shattered by German shells, it must nevertheless have been a
very strong position. All round the entrenchment across the road, was
a moat of some depth. The angles were stockaded, the walls loopholed,
and there still stood in position a couple of field guns, which, as the
Germans had known to their cost many a time, were located with great
skill. Owing to the block caused by the entrenchment the road had been
diverted. It had been little enough used before this morning. The grass
grew thickly on the chaussée between Lauvallière and Belle Croix. As
the peasant waggons came crowding out, they stuck in the heavy mire of
this détour, and many were the _sacrés_ before they were started again,
the lean horses being too weak to pull them through the heavy ground.
When I had passed Belle Croix I could not help owning to myself that
I had never till then succeeded in forming a proper conception of the
real strength of the natural position of Metz. On the right, with a
valley intervening like a deep trench, lay the blunt-topped mountain,
crowned by Fort St. Julien. On its slope were the bivouac fires of
the troops of Lebœuf. Nothing could venture on this slope—a great
natural glacis—so long as Fort St. Julien thought proper to forbid the
passage. On my left, also on an eminence, although from the positions
whence I had previously seen it, it appeared to be on a flat, lay Fort
Queleu, square and massive, dominating the plateau to the left of the
chaussée. But this plateau is closed in Metz-ward by a fortification
all to itself—the great earthwork of les Bottes, whose grenades had so
often burst in disagreeable proximity, and which now stood solitary
and empty, with not even a sentry on its earthen summit. Over the top
of Fort les Bottes were visible the towers of the cathedral, and the
picture was framed in by the background of that great elevated ridge
of which the two chief peaks are crowned by Forts St. Quentin and
Plappeville.

It was just beyond les Bottes that I met the first French soldier—a
cavalryman of the line. The fellow was as drunk as he well could be to
be able to move at all. He staggered forward in tacks, dropping his
kit piecemeal as he came. At a bend in the road behind Four-à-Chaux,
I came upon the head of the prisoner column, marching onward to their
reception at the Brewery. The men were in a very miserable state,
covered with mud, wet, sodden seemingly, indeed, through and through
with wet, many of their faces blotched and scabbed with scurvy, and
quite one-third in a state of drunkenness. Not a few were in a state of
bestial drunkenness, their clothes disarranged, and decency utterly
disregarded. A little farther on, and right and left, lay the great
bivouac before Fort Belle Croix. The troops were being collected by
their officers in pelotons previously to being marched out, and several
columns were already converging on the great chaussée. As each body got
the order to go forward a cheer came from the slovenly and dislocated
ranks, and from the columns on the march there broke out at intervals
a fitful chorus of some lively marching song. Most of the officers
were grave, taciturn, and downcast. Surely they must have despised the
reckless throng which they nominally commanded. The men obeyed their
officers and regarded the sound of the bugle after a fashion, but it
was evident that they were utterly unaffected by the catastrophe which
had befallen the arms of their country, and were eager only to get
somewhere where a full meal of victuals awaited them. Poor wretches,
after all it is not fair to be too severe on their disorganisation.
How near actual starvation they had come to before their leader would
give up the game, their faces pale through the dirt of days, their lean
frames with the rags hanging about them, told far more powerfully than
protestations, and were the most convincing reply to the easy untruths
of those who would have had Europe believe that Metz was betrayed.

Passing the columns of prisoners at a canter, I was back again
before the Brewery ere the front of that column had reached the place
where stood the Germans waiting for them. The dismounted cavalry
came first, followed by the artillery; last of all came the infantry
of the line. The cavalry seemed to have lost all self-respect; they
greeted the Germans with cheering, and several men broke from the
ranks and slouched forward through the mud with intent to salute with
a spirituous kiss the German officers standing in front of their
companies. It was curious to notice the opposite idiosyncrasies of the
two nationalities. Grave and stern stood the German victors, with,
as it seemed, a silent pity rather than contempt for the demented
tatterdemalions who proffered them the undesirable greeting; while the
Frenchmen chattered and gesticulated, “sacréd” and spluttered, sang
and laughed, as they slouched through the gauntlet of humiliation.
The officers quitted their commands as each reached the alley fringed
by the German battalions, and returned to Metz or the vicinity. The
prisoners, comprising the whole of the 3rd Corps, till the day before
under the command of Lebœuf, and numbering about 23,000 men, were
escorted to a position between Château Gras and Retonfay, where was
fixed the site for their bivouac. The guards for the French prisoners
were contributed in rotation by the several divisions. The daily quota
for the bivouac at Château Gras was four battalions of infantry, one
battery of artillery, and one squadron of cavalry. In command of it
had been placed Colonel von Deitzen Hennig, of the 4th regiment, his
adjutant being Lieutenant von Verzin. Food had been collected for the
supply of the prisoners, who had, of course, to bivouac in the open
air. From the 30th daily drafts, each 3000 strong, were despatched
toward Germany _viâ_ Saarbrück and Saarlouis. In all the capture
consisted of 173,000 men, including three marshals, forty-seven general
officers, and 6000 regimental officers. General Kummer, the general
in chief command of the Landwehr, was appointed interim Commandant
of Metz. General von Kastrow took the control of all arrangements
connected with the transport of the prisoners and the quartermastership
generally of the district around Metz and as far as Thionville. The
German troops which had formed the besieging army were not, it was
arranged, to enter the town, except when marching through. Metz was,
with the forts, in the meantime, to be garrisoned by the Division
Kummer and the 7th Army Corps. The circle of environment was, however,
greatly reduced in size from the day after the surrender. The 2nd
Army Corps drew inward to Montigny. The 8th came on to Magny, the
10th to Woippy. The 18th infantry division, charged with the custody
of the Imperial Guard, lay now at Longeville, Moulins les Metz, St.
Ruffine, and Longeau. The 1st Army Corps came forward closer under the
wing of St. Julien. The foreposts were still to be maintained, the
chain of communications being kept up just as in the old days. It was
anticipated that by seven days all the prisoners would have been sent
away.

After I had seen Lebœuf’s command taken over by the 1st Army Corps,
I returned towards Metz, meeting on my way a stream of straggling
soldiers who had missed their corps, and were staggering out under
loads of tents, blankets, and what not. The environs of the town were
very desolate and very filthy. Mud, raw horseflesh, burnt-out bivouac
fires, huts made of the branches of trees, old women, and skeleton
horses, were the leading characteristics. There was no difficulty in
getting into the town, where I found the shops all open, but with no
great show of edibles. Meat hung in the butchers’ shops, although it
was chiefly, if not altogether, horse; the cafés were crowded, and
there appeared no exhaustion in the stocks of the shops devoted to
the sale of any material. Tobacco was plentiful, wine, spirits, and
liqueurs in great abundance. Making my way to the Hôtel de l’Europe,
I found it crowded with French officers, and I also found there one
Englishman, Dr. Ward, of the International Society for the Succour
of the Wounded, who had just been decorated by the French military
authorities for his attention to the wounded; and two Americans, Dr.
Good, of the same society, and who had also been decorated; and Mr.
Eustis, a commercial gentleman, who had spent forty-eight days of the
siege in prison under suspicion of being a spy. The spy mania had raged
very strongly in Metz. Seventy-eight persons were arrested on suspicion
in one day. Eight in all I was told had been shot as convicted spies,
including the notorious Shieull, to whose adroitness and accurate
information the French believe Von Moltke was indebted for his
knowledge of the weakness of the French dispositions on the frontier,
and for the hint which he utilised into the successes at Wörth and
Weissenburg. The following is a statement of the tariff of prices for
food ruling in Metz three days before the capitulation, supplied by the
manager of the Hôtel de l’Europe:

  Beef 8 francs per lb.
  Horseflesh 10 centimes to 2 francs per lb.; “choice
 horse,” the tender loin, fetches the latter price.
  Eggs 1 franc each; have been 1f. 20c.
  Fowls 20 francs each.
  Pike (fish) 8 francs per lb.
  Coffee 6 francs per lb.
  Ham 8 francs per lb.
  Cheese 40 francs per lb. (nominal, all done.)
  Salt 5 francs per lb.
  Geese, fat 65 francs per pair paid yesterday.
  Oats 120 francs per sack of 200lb.
  Chocolate 5 francs per lb.
  Bread White, none since 16th October; brown,
 4½ sous per lb.
  Mutton None for two months.
  Wine Plentiful, and old prices maintained;
 bottle of champagne for 7 francs.

Notwithstanding these extraordinary prices, the tariff in the Hôtel de
l’Europe was much the same as usual. I dined there in the evening at
the _table d’hôte_ for seven francs, including half a bottle of wine.
Breakfast was _à la carte_, about seven francs; bottle of champagne,
seven francs. We had four courses for dinner, the waiter said none were
horseflesh, and it is not seemly to call even a waiter a liar. The
room was crowded with French officers. I dined at the same table with
Colonel Michel Ney, a nephew of the historical Marshal. He had received
some half-dozen sabre-wounds in the cavalry fight at Vionville, and
was only half recovered. At the same table also sat General Duplessis,
a French officer of distinction, who also had been severely wounded.
In the evening there was a tremendous rush of German officers into the
hotel, and quarters became impossible at any price. Forty officers
were sent bedless away from the Hôtel de l’Europe, and I was indebted
to a good-hearted American for half his bed. The French and German
officers fraternized a little in the _salle_ after dinner. At first
the Frenchmen were a little inclined to be sulky and reserved, but
the heartiness of the Germans soon thawed the ice, and the champagne
promoted the flow of kindly feeling. I was told that in the forenoon,
when the officers of the Imperial Guard said adieu to their men,
profuse tears were shed by both. There was no such emotion manifested
by Lebœuf’s corps.

In the afternoon of the 28th, when the news of the capitulation was
made known, there had been a sort of revolutionary riot in Metz. The
National Guards made a display of refusing to give up their arms,
ran to the Place d’Armes, where they congregated in large numbers
and several shots were fired. The cathedral rang the alarm hell and
the shops put their shutters up. The editor of one of the ultra
journals was the ring-leader; mounted on a white horse, with a pistol
in his hand, and others in his belt, he hounded on the mob to yell
for Bazaine’s blood. That distinguished person, however, with great
discretion, remained in his quarters in Ban St. Martin. The mob,
composed in a large proportion of civilians, laid hold of an officer
of the Imperial Guard, and holding pistols at his head, forced him to
carry in the front the Republican flag. A hint from the mayor that
the Germans would be sent for to put down the disturbance had its
effect after the gamins had roared themselves hoarse shouting “Vive la
République!”

It seemed it had not been until ten days before the capitulation that
the troops of Bazaine’s army were forbidden to enter the town of Metz.
Up till then they had circulated freely in the town, using the cafés,
and spending their money in the shops. Until six days before it, a
ration of bread of 100 grammes had been issued to each soldier. Since
then they had had no rations, except horseflesh. The townspeople had
been on bread rations as well as the troops, the rations consisting
of 200 grammes of brown bread. Those who were able paid for their
ration, the poor receiving it gratuitously. It surprised me that Metz
had been able to hold out so long. The place had not above 50,000
inhabitants, if so many, and with no previous warning it had supported
for seventy days a gross population of 230,000 mouths. These are the
official figures. The dearness of provisions had been progressive. For
instance, on the 16th of August butter had already reached the fancy
price of 4f. per pound. On the same day chickens ranged from 6f. to
8f. At the date of the capitulation, butter could not be procured at
any price, and the same might be said of chickens. Metz had certainly
not capitulated for want of munitions of war. The fortress had ample
supplies still left. Although Metz was an arsenal, no manufacture of
cannon or projectiles had gone on during the siege. There had been no
occasion for any such work, and if there had, the want of coals would
have presented an insuperable obstacle. Powder had, however, been made
in large quantities. All the coal used in Metz comes from Saarbrück,
and there had recently been great scarcity—so much so that an order had
been issued a week before the capitulation that the gas in the town
should be extinguished at seven o’clock, under a heavy penalty. Candles
were plentiful, and the deprivation was therefore not so seriously felt.

Of sick there were altogether, in and around Metz, on the date of the
capitulation, about 20,000 soldiers, of which about 9000 were wounded
men. The latter had chiefly been wounded in the sorties. Of the wounded
in the battles of Courcelles and Gravelotte most were either recovered
or dead. Very many mutilated men were to be seen in the streets.
Latterly almost every operation had ended fatally. Pyæmia, hospital
gangrene, and typhus had their sway on frames previously debilitated by
hardship and famine. Of hospital stores the greatest lack was in the
matter of leeches. For two of these Dr. Ward had on the previous day
paid 180 francs. His colleague was down with brain fever, and he paid
this wildly fancy price to save his life.

Three or four newspapers continued to be daily printed in Metz up to
the capitulation. Since no outside information had reached the town for
so long, the newspapers had chiefly concerned themselves with local
gossip, hopes, fears, and canards. A file of one of them was a curious
sight. The stock of paper had long run out, and the journals had been
printed one day on red paper, another on blue, a third on buff, and so
on—advertisement placard paper having necessarily been had resort to.
The last issue of one journal was printed on brown packing paper.

All the afternoon of the 29th, the division of General Kummer came
pouring into the town, with bands playing and colours flying. The great
Landwehrmen stared around them with undisguised curiosity at the nut
which they have found so hard to crack. The townsfolk jabbered among
themselves as the troops defiled, and there were not a few scowling
faces. What cared the Landwehrmen for the scowling faces? From the
slush and mire of Maizières and Ladonchamps they had come into a
reasonable prospect of beds and bottles of wine. They were quartered
for the most part in the Caserne du Génie, the Hôtel de Ville, and
other public buildings. The officers were in private houses. Ye gods!
with what zest did they devote themselves to champagne in the _salle_
of the hotel! Generals, lieutenants, Vice-feldwebels, all in the lump
were there, and one and all speaking after the German manner, at the
top of their voices—the din was deafening. The French officers cleared
off early to bed, all save two, who sat on moodily in a corner, pulling
their moustaches and drinking absinthe—the poison that has sapped the
virility of the French army.

Some officious patriots had made themselves busy during the night of
the 28th, with the statue of Marshal Fabert in the Place d’Armes. When
capitulation was first mooted, a wreath of immortelles and laurel was
woven round his head, and a flag stuck in his hand. On the morning of
the 29th, the Marshal appeared draped in a full suit of crape, with a
hole in it, through which was visible the inscription—the words which
the gallant Marshal addressed to Louis XIV:

 Si pour empêcher qu’une place
 que le Roi m’a confiée
 ne tombât au pouvoir de l’ennemi,
 il fallait mettre à la brêche
 ma personne, ma famille et tout mon bien,
 je ne balancerais pas un moment à le faire.

Bazaine had not quite the grit in him to act up to the programme
chalked out by the staunch old Marshal.

Two or three days elapsed after the surrender of Metz before a regular
and reasonably quick postal service was established from the city, and
for any one to whom time was an object it was necessary to despatch
letters from Courcelles, the interim terminus of the railway. To reach
this point I quitted Metz early on the morning of the 30th October,
compelled to wriggle now right, now left, to circumvent the numerous
entrenchments still blocking up the main roads.

On my way from Colombey to Courcelles my track lay through the
prisoner-bivouac of the 2nd French Army Corps (Frossard’s). It was
a very miserable scene. The fires of the previous night had burnt
out, and the morning fires had not been lit. The miserable prisoners
lounged about in seeming utter despair, the fine rain wetting them
through and through, and keeping soft the mud with which their coats
were plastered. Some few had erected tents, but the great mass had
spent the night in the open standing round the fires, for to lie down
must have been impracticable. I saw a couple of corpses lying in the
ditch by the roadside, and was told that six or eight poor wretches
had succumbed to exhaustion and exposure during the bitter night. If
it was any consolation to the prisoners, their German guards were in
as miserable a plight as their captives. The chain of sentries posted
all round slouched backward and forward on their posts, and the fixed
corporal-schafts leaned gloomily on their weapons. In the road in
front of Ars Laquenexy a kind of market had been established, but the
dealers had hardly waked up under the tilts of the carts. They were
marketenders chiefly from the German frontier about Saarlouis, and
the bulk of their wares consisted of spirituous liquor. The French
prisoners were by no means short of money. All the cash in the bank
and military chest in Metz had been disbursed in paying up the troops’
arrears of pay, and there were plenty of francs in their pockets.

In Courcelles I saw the arrival of the first column of prisoners to be
sent down by the train to Saarbrück. There were 2000 of them, and five
trains, each containing the same number, were being despatched every
day. Poor fellows, they seemed very miserable, but they bore their
misery with a fortitude which filled me with respect. The rain poured
down upon them in a deluge as they stood in the road, which was a foot
deep in mud, of the consistency of pea-soup. Here they had to wait for
about an hour, while the arrangements for their transit were being
completed, and it was good to see the courtesy and quiet fortitude
which they manifested. I went among them in the mud. Everywhere the
poor fellows stepped aside with a half bow to let me pass. If one
accidentally jostled me, the “Pardon, Monsieur,” was prompt and warm. I
found these French prisoners here as ignorant as those whom I had seen
at Sedan. Some of the sergeants asked me for news of the outer world,
but the men seemed chiefly concerned to know whither they were going,
and what kind of country Germany was. I named several towns as among
their probable destination, leading cities of Germany, but very few had
more than heard of them, and knew nothing either of their magnitude or
of their locality. At last the prisoner-train was ready, some sixty
carriages packed as closely as the men could sit or stand. Several open
trucks had been brought into requisition, and it seemed to me as though
the men were actually stacked upon them, so thickly were they packed.
With a whistle the heavy train sluggishly set itself in motion, and
away it rolled with its freight of Frenchmen down what was once one of
the most charming valleys in France. The Frenchmen were prisoners on
their way to expatriation, the valley was a wilderness. On the ramparts
of St. Quentin, which from a distance overlooked the scene, paced the
square-built men with the brass-spiked helmets. The virgin fortress of
France, never till now profaned by the foot of an invader, was held by
the Landwehrmen, and the streets of the town echoed to the clash of
the German subaltern’s sword.

As I drove out across the glacis of Fort Belle Croix, I was struck with
the alteration which had taken place there since the previous day.
Then the whole expanse swarmed with life. Cavalrymen, artillerymen,
infantrymen, were blended in chaotic confusion, out of which it seemed
impossible to evolve order. Now the expanse lay blank and bare.
Cavalrymen, artillerymen, infantrymen, had been drafted off it. Nothing
was left but the mud and a few horses, too debilitated to be removed,
and dropping, even as I passed, into the slush out of which they should
never rise. There was a horse market in Metz on the 30th, or rather
just outside the gate. I imagine a good many horses changed hands to
which the parties who disposed of them would have had some difficulty
in proving a valid title. Prices ranged from a franc and a half up to
five pounds, but this latter was a fancy price, and only given for an
officer’s charger of breeding and substance.

After seeing the prisoner-train leave Courcelles, I drove away
northward, and after visiting the headquarter staff of the 7th Army
Corps at Puche, and my friends of the 4th regiment at Retonfay, went
into the lager of Lebœuf’s corps on the slope between Château-Gras
and Retonfay. It was getting towards evening by this time, and I was
in the right time for seeing the Frenchmen making their preparations
for bivouac. What splendid fellows they were to be sure at this kind
of work. Already the tents had a drain through each to carry off the
surface water. The great poplar trees on the chaussée—those that the
Germans had left standing—were falling fast. Those that the Germans had
felled the Frenchmen were chopping up into lengths, and carrying off
to the bivouac fire. Others stood round the bivouac fires or lay on
curious improvised beds, contrived with dexterously-placed stones to
keep their constructors out of the mud. In the blaze of the fires were
pots and saucepans; rather than part from a saucepan when he has once
secured it, I believe a French soldier would lose one of his boots.
The pots and saucepans were simmering merrily and emitting a fragrant
odour—whence it was derived I could not guess. The rations had been
meat and biscuit; nothing else save salt. The men had dug up again
the already thrice turned over potato fields and might have got a few
tubers. But potatoes, and meat, and biscuits were impotent to create
the pleasant odour, one of the recondite mysteries of French cookery.

The atmosphere after the capitulation was full of stories of the prices
paid in Metz during the siege for articles of food. Most of these were
to be taken _cum grano_, since the beleaguered, now that it was all
over, not unnaturally took a pleasure in piling on the agony. But the
one which I am going to narrate I had from an undeniable source, and I
give it as illustrative of the profligate frivolity among the wealthier
and more aristocratic officers, which has been one of the chief causes
of the ruin of the French army. There had been in Metz during the
siege a certain lady of the Parisian demi-monde, who had come to Metz
when the Emperor came, and could not get away when he retired _viâ_
Verdun to Châlons. Two officers of the Garde Impériale had been rivals
for this lady’s affections. The lady, it seemed, had a fanciful and
delicate appetite, and one day in the early part of the last month of
the siege she expressed a fervent wish to be regaled on turkey and
truffles. “Ah, you know that is an impossibility,” was the rejoinder
of one of the swains. “I don’t know that,” said the other, eager for
an opening; “I shall try what I can do.” The other, in his jealousy,
offered to bet 500 francs that he would not succeed, and the wager
was accepted. The officer came to the landlord of one of the hotels
and gave him the commission, adding that money was no object. The
head waiter went on a foraging expedition, and after two days’ search
brought the officer a turkey and truffles, with a little bill for a
thousand francs; so the gay guardsman won his bet and lost 500 francs.

As I was driving out to Courcelles my coachman quietly told me that his
wife and three children were starving. When we returned at night I went
with him to ascertain the circumstances for myself. He guided me into
a part of the town close to the middle branch of the Moselle, inhabited
only by the poorest of the poor. I had before then only been on the top
of the crust which overlay the misery caused by this siege. I had dined
and eaten dessert, I had called for wine, and lo it was forthcoming.
So far as concerned the hotels, the world seemed to wag very much as
its wont. I saw no ghastly faces in the streets pallid with want, as
were the faces of those citizens of Antwerp, who cheered the lean
Stadtholder when he swore to eat his boots before he would surrender.
But it was not because there were no pale faces and attenuated forms in
Metz that I did not see them in the streets, but because the miserable
ones cared not to make a parade of their misery. It was a sorry sight
the squalid room of the coachman presented. Of bread the family had
not had a morsel for three days; then they had received as ration two
pounds for six. One of the six—an infant two years old—wants rations no
more. The little body lay on two planks under the window, the victim of
bodily exhaustion, from deficient and improper food. How could a child
of two years survive on sinewy horseflesh, in scanty quantity, and on
a casual morsel of bread, the meal of which was mixed with sawdust and
chopped straw to make it eke out? All up and down the poverty-stricken
street the wretchedness was much the same. Horseflesh and want of salt
had killed off the children like flies. The mortality among the little
ones had been frightful. The elder children were covered with blotches
and scorbutic sores, caused by want of salt and of variety in their
food.

As regards the inhabitants of Metz itself, it seemed likely that the
misery would soon come to an end. There was no want of wealth in Metz,
and the rich citizens were reported to be public spirited. Now that
food could be bought, it was sure to be bought and distributed freely.
But no such amelioration was to be expected for the wretchedness—a
wretchedness almost without hope—of the villagers inhabiting the
country around Metz. They had nothing—not even a roof above them, in
the pittiless rain. Of their cottages nought was standing but the bare
walls, often pounded with shells. Their gardens were waste, their
cattle were gone, their furniture was wrecked, their straw was dung,
their fields lay fallow and studded with graves. A day spent in the
misery around Metz must have made as water a heart hard as the nether
millstone. But help came to the helpless. It will be long ere “the
Friends” will cease to be remembered by the simple peasant people whom
they helped through the ghastly slough of despond.

The railway into Metz having been opened on the 30th, a convoy of
300 generals and superior officers went off at five o’clock in the
afternoon. On the 31st, at 10.30, a train left for Mayence, _viâ_
Nancy, for the accommodation of generals and chief staff officers. A
regular series of trains was organised on the 1st of November for
regimental officers. The first, leaving at 9.30, accommodated the
officers of the garrison; the second, at 1.30, for the officers of
the Imperial Guard, cavalry of the reserve, and engineers. At 1.30,
the officers of Canrobert’s corps (the 6th), and division of Forton.
At 4.15 a train took off the officers of the 2nd Corps (Frossard’s),
and the brigade Lapasset; and at 6.15, the 3rd Corps (Lebœuf’s). Great
consideration was invariably shown by the German authorities for the
officers, and careful arrangements were made for the wounded and sick
French, chiefly under the supervision of German doctors.

I spent the afternoon of the 31st in inspecting, so far as time would
permit, the fortifications of the town of Metz, and those constituting
its exterior defences. What I had already gathered concerning the
natural and artificial strength of the forts of St. Quentin, St.
Julien, Plappeville, St. Eloy, and Queleu I found more than borne out
by personal investigation. Whoever holds Metz holds an impregnable
fortress. Nothing can so much as get at the fortress of Metz proper
while the outworks hold out, and there is no reason that, properly
garrisoned and provisioned, they should not hold out for ever. It might
be possible to batter down St. Eloy or Queleu, the others cannot be
got at advantageously in any way on account of the strength of their
natural position. The weak point of the fortress is towards the east,
between Queleu and St. Julien; but the complement of the defence on
that side is made up by the “provisional” earthwork of les Bottes.
From what I heard I think it probable that the day after the 14th of
August, the Germans, if they had made a dash, and had been prepared
for severe losses, might have effected a lodgment in front of Fort
Belle Croix in this direction, and, indeed, if all tales were true,
might have pushed their way into the town itself. We all know how easy
it is to be wise after the event. The English army in the Crimea was
abused for not going straight into Sebastopol two days after the Alma,
instead of sitting down on the bleak plateau over against the fortress.
Had its leaders known the state of the fortifications, no doubt the
weary winter and the heaps of dead might have been spared to Britain.
So with the Germans before Metz. I was assured that so confident were
the French of success—so ridiculous did they think the idea that it
was possible to invest Metz—that it was not till after the battle of
Forbach that serious efforts had been made to place the fortress in an
efficient state of defence. The guns were not mounted till then behind
the embrasures. Les Bottes was unfinished—a mere heap of shapeless
earth. Even now it was not properly finished, and looked as slovenly
a piece of fortification work as I ever saw put out of hand by troops
who asserted they had engineers in their ranks. Nevertheless, it had
a front, and guns mounted on that front. The Germans, ever masterful
and prompt, would not, I was satisfied, delay long to alter les Bottes
from a “provisional” into a permanent fort. Subsequent events bore out
this anticipation; and, with their efficient and thorough system of
administration, Metz, if impregnable hitherto, will be henceforth, if
such a thing can be, doubly impregnable.

From what I learned, there had been a good deal more of huckstering
than patriotism among the civilian population of Metz. There was a
reason for the _émeute_ on the evening of the 28th. The wily burghers
had been keeping back their stores for a market, while their poorer
fellows hungered because of the high prices, and the army starved.
Horses were dying daily for want of forage; yet the Germans found
stores of hay and corn quietly stowed away in unlikely places. It was
the same with other articles of consumption, and the administration
seemed to have been either not strong enough or not cunning enough
to make everything forthcoming for the common good. Napoleon called
us a nation of shopkeepers, but I take leave to doubt whether our
shopkeepering idiosyncrasy would have developed itself in such a
manner. I could not help having a quiet chuckle at the douce burghers,
whose market Bazaine had spoiled. How they hated him for it! And they
spoke their minds right venomously after the French manner.

If one listened to all he heard, he was bound to accept the conclusion
that Bazaine was a renegade and traitor of the largest possible
calibre. It seemed, according to the gossip of the natives, savage
that their canny little scheme for putting money in their purses had
failed, that Bazaine nourished the design of having himself declared
Regent of France, and that he only relinquished this ambitious project
for the consideration of a fabulous subsidy, contributed I could
not exactly learn by whom. It was useless to point out to the angry
and disappointed cits that on the face of it this was rubbish, and
that Bazaine kept his upper lip straight and his face to the foe so
long as, and indeed longer than, he had an army capable of fighting,
marching, or standing up to be shot down. If he had had subordinates
honest and capable of co-operating with him, had he been unfettered at
the outset, and had the discipline and _moral_ of his army not been
deteriorated before he took the command of it, it might have been that
the chroniclers of this war would have had a different tale to tell.

It was with a deep interest that I listened at the table d’hôte the
same evening to the despondent remarks of a venerable French gentleman,
who had been a colonel at Waterloo. The old man had the water in his
eyes as he spoke of the depreciation in the character of the forces
of his country. The cause that had wrought the curse of deteriorated
discipline was, according to him, the pernicious itch for popularity
with which all the French superior officers were bitten. Canrobert, he
told me, had been the great initiator of this popularity-hunting fever.
In his rough, bluff way he used familiarly to appeal to the private
soldiers as “his children” whether their rations were what they liked,
and if an ill-conditioned fellow, thus encouraged, vented a grumble, he
would turn on the colonel and blow him up sky-high. One very pregnant
observation he made, a remark which I had thought of all the evening,
and doubly significant as coming from a Frenchman and an officer.
“Surely,” said I, “this will bring the French to their senses, and we
must have peace in a very short time. And just as surely that peace,” I
continued, “must be a permanent one, for the French army, with all its
craving for _la gloire_, must realise the fact that there is not much
in the way of _la gloire_ to be got by being greedy for the Rhine.”

“I don’t know,” said the old man, mournfully. “Logically you are
right, no doubt; but severe as the lesson has been, I question of
its effect. It is the officers of the French army who chiefly are
the clamourers—senseless and frothy clamourers—after _la gloire_.
They shrieked for war, enervated with lust and absinthe as they were,
ignorant of their duties, their minds uncultivated, and debilitated
with luxury. I fear they are too far gone, too utterly vitiated and
emasculated mentally, to be able to draw the conclusion from the
premises; and I should not be surprised if, a year hence, you may
hear the parole loafers who are swinging about the streets to-day,
yelling for war again, as unprepared to pursue it to success as they
were when this one was entered into, and as reckless and regardless of
consequences as they were then and are now.”

Just conceive what France must have come to when one of her _anciens
militaires_ could speak thus! That the officers of his army were, in
his opinion, so utterly spineless as to be unable to recognise the fact
that they had been beaten, the causes which had led to their defeat,
and the utter impossibility that unless they purged and lived cleanly
they could make even a fair fight of it against the cool-headed and
heavy-handed German! The whirligig of time brings round many curious
changes, but it is utterly outside the nature of things that Frenchmen
as they are, as I saw them in this war from day to day, can ever,
given what advantages they may, be a match in warfare for a people so
constituted as those which dwell in the Vaterland. The hot iron of
adversity brands deep, and has a wonderful power to cure ailments, but
if the surface is so calloused that the iron will not mark, how can the
beneficial result be achieved?

The old gentleman was specially severe on the Zouaves. When these
regiments were first constituted, he said the ranks were really full
of fighting men. But as their fame grew they became fashionable, and
then their ruin set in. The ranks were crowded by young sprigs who
had no other thought than a picturesque uniform, waxed moustaches, the
demi-monde, and absinthe; and the once stout and capable regiments had
lost their prowess and become enervated and flaccid. This view may be
exaggerated—the _ancien militaire_ of the table d’hôte was not the
first man that ever put forth the opinion that “the service is going
to the dogs, by Gad;” but it is in a measure confirmed by the events
of this war, in which the French infantry of the line displayed far
greater valour and patient endurance than the pet regiments.

One might have been surprised on entering Metz for the first time since
the capitulation how little sickness was apparently existing in the
town. One strolled on to the Place Royale, and there saw the streets
of railway waggons, which in the early days had been brought into the
town from the station—300 of them—and diverted from the carriage of
luggage or merchandise to be hospitals for the reception of the sick
and wounded. Here and there a convalescent limped about on crutches,
or a pallid-faced man glided past, from whom you instinctively
shrank with a curious feeling that the taint of the fever must be
hanging about him still. But of sweeping pestilence there seemed no
traces. One had to look for the deadly sickness in quiet corners in
the little improvised hospitals, where there were only a few cases,
or in the neglected villages around the town where hygiene had been
comparatively neglected. There it was and there alone, where you could
find forms which you were told were those of men, but passed out of
all recognition as such, discoloured and bloated in the last stages of
the foul black small-pox. It might be that you chanced on a lazarette
into which the terrible demon of hospital gangrene had entered and
fixed his relentless fang, never to be withdrawn till the earth should
have been heaped on his victim. A few cases of this terrible disorder,
and only a few, I saw. Not alone was the sense of sight called into
requisition; no cordial in Metz seemed to have the virtue of washing
the horribly fœtid odour out of my throat, where it stuck as if it were
something solid. I trust no reader will ever be called to look upon the
corpse of one who has died of pyæmia. One took his life in his hand
going among such spectacles. Here is a house in the Isle de Saulcy in
which there are five sick men and two corpses. There is a doctor with
them—a lone man, for the very women, with their tender hearts hardened
by the terror of the dread infection, have run away. The doctor is a
mere youth in years, but there is grey already in his hair, and he
tells me that the last six weeks have made him feel as an old man. With
every breath he inhales he risks his life nearly as much as if he held
his fist into the cage of a cobra capella. What words can describe
the colour of these semicomatose forms which he handles so tenderly?
Have they been blown up in an explosion, and had their skins dusted
over to ease the smart of the burn, and to hide the swarthy brand of
the powder? No, they are the victims of a far worse misfortune. They
lie smitten to the marrow with the terrible _Typhus exanthematicus_,
the floury typhus, which is happily so rare that many a respectable
and capable general practitioner in Britain has never heard of it.
The flour which the exanthematicus powders on the skin of its victim,
seldom washes off. Almost invariably face and flour dust are alike
hidden in the grave.

The railway station outside the Porte Serpenoise of Metz was a curious
sight. It was the depôt of all the vast train of that Corps-Francs
organized by the Chemin de fer de l’Est, with a military constitution,
and having a material that had cost fifteen millions of francs. The
corps numbered 1200 men, and they, with all their material, their
bridges, their stalls, their waggons, their pontoons, and their vast
timber blocks, were destined to act as the pioneers of the French army
on its march “à Berlin,” and to repair the damage to the communications
wrought by the Germans, falling back beaten and demoralised across the
Rhine on their capital. There was a bridge here in pieces, specially
constructed to be thrown across the Rhine. The bridge had never quitted
the locality where it was constructed. It was the prize of the army
that had not only kept the watch fast and true on the German Rhine,
but that was standing sentry on the Moselle, the Marne, the Seine,
and the Loire. And to-day in the drizzle of the small rain, the bold
Corps-Francs were slowly jogging it on the prisoner-train towards the
Rhine which they had thought to approach in array so different.

Many a day it will take Metz to recover from the handiwork of the
men who undertook to defend it. The tourist who in the early summer
came to this virgin queen of the Moselle might have noticed outside
the fortifications a belt of buildings about half an English mile in
breadth. The trim villas of the wealthy citizens had stood here in the
dainty gardens; the pretentious house of the mayor had been here behind
its red-brick walls. There had been snug little cottages _ornées_,
where dwelt ladies, about whose antecedents few knew anything, but
who had fair faces and who dressed magnificently. Not one stone now
remained upon another of all the villas, the mansions, the cottages.
All was blank desolation. Here and there the stump of a fountain,
erstwhile standing in a pretty garden, asserted itself disconsolately,
its once superincumbent Cupid or Neptune half battered into the mud.
The ground was churned into slush by the tread of many footsteps, and
fetid odours blew over it from the dead horses that were rotting on its
surface. This belt of desolation was the _zone militaire_ of Metz—the
space outside the fortifications, where buildings were allowed to be
