erected solely on the condition that if it should be necessary at any
time for military exigencies to destroy them, the owners should not
be entitled to claim compensation. The military exigency came in July
last, and now the portly mayor might bite his thumb, and the owners of
the villas and the cottages grope about the ruins for salvage, for no
consolation was forthcoming for them in the shape of compensation.

On the 1st of November I continued my investigations as to the
condition of the French sick who had been left by the capitulation as
a legacy to the Germans. In those inquiries I derived great assistance
from the courteous kindness of Dr. Leithold, of the division of General
Kummer, on whom devolved the supervision of all the medical and
sanitary arrangements. Dr. Leithold had in all inherited about 19,000
sick men, among whom were included only sixty German prisoners—all the
rest were Frenchmen. This included the sick in the field hospitals
and the various forts. The diseases chiefly raging were typhus and
dysentery, the latter with a strong tendency to assume the phase of
the former. There were not many cases, however, of spotted or floury
typhus. Small-pox was far from rare, and in not a few cases it was
of the most virulent form. A good deal of scurvy was also existing.
I fancy that a great quantity of scurvy cases of a mild type had not
been allowed to enter the hospitals, for of the prisoners in the
lager by Retonfay I saw a considerable number bearing the evidences
of suffering from this disease. With the good and varied rations now
being distributed, a disease which had been superinduced by their
absence was sure soon to disappear. In order at once to clear for the
occupation of troops the barracks in Metz, hitherto used as hospitals,
and to provide a change into higher and purer air, all the sick able to
move were being sent out into the highlying forts surrounding the town.
This step, it was hoped, would stimulate healthy action in systems
which had become low, degenerate, and stagnant, and good nourishment,
which was being provided with a lavish hand, would naturally co-operate
greatly in the setting up of this healthy action. His Excellency von
Kummer deserves to be held in honour by all humanity for the prompt
and solicitous arrangements which he caused to be instituted for
relieving the pressure on the sick of the pre-capitulation condition
of things. A great bakery of white bread was at once set up in the
Caserne des Chasseurs, and orders were circulated that all medical
officers in charge of lazarettes should have carte blanche to make
requisitions for bread. Stores were hurried in of coffee, chocolate,
soup extract, and other comforts, and by Sunday morning there were very
few sick who were not the better of the advent of the Germans. Prior
to the establishment of the regular system of supply, the Berliner
Hülfsverein, represented by Herren Adolph Schwabe and E. Minlos, was,
with the English Ambulance, among the first to commence a distribution
of comforts. Britons may be glad to know that they too helped towards
the Liebesgaben with which were packed the fourgons of the Berliner
Hülfsverein, since the British National Society helped its funds with a
donation of 1000_l._, the administration of which was confided to Mr.
Schwabe. As General von Senden and his staff entered the town a scene
occurred, pathetic enough in one aspect to move the deepest sympathies
of those who love the Fatherland and its children, and yet not without
a certain grotesqueness that only affords another illustration of the
narrow gap which exists between tears and laughter. As the General rode
down the Rue de la Prison Militaire, he passed the prison of the Maison
d’Arrêt, where the German prisoners taken on the 7th at Maizières were
confined. As the Landwehrmen on that day had met the foe with shouts of
“Hurrah Preussen!” and “Immer vorwärts!” so now, gaunt, hollow-eyed,
and squalid, the great gallant fellows cheered their countrymen as
they came on. The cheer was hardly so full-volumed as when it had
echoed through the gables of Ladonchamps; scanty rations exclusively
of horseflesh were not quite the class of feeding off which to cheer
lustily. But von Senden, not unfamiliar with the cheers of his Landwehr
men, heard the “Hurrah Preussen!” from behind the bars, and, halting,
called out to his Kinder to know how they fared. The hungry men told
their plight, and the general turning to the Intendant who rode behind
him, asked when he could have food up for the wants of the prisoned
Landwehrmen. “Not for an hour at least,” was the reply. Then von Senden
turned to Mr. Schwabe, the bearer of the Liebesgaben, and asked whether
he could do nothing till the Intendant got up his column. The fourgons
of the Berliner Hülfsverein were not a furlong behind, and they came up
at the trot. The bread tumbled out of the waggons, and in at the prison
windows. But the waggons contained other provender than bread, and the
question was asked of the prisoners as they munched their bread, what
relish they would have to it. Who that knows the German palate but can
anticipate the reply. “Haben Sie Wurst?” was the response, as if from
one man, and then from behind came a piping voice, it must have been an
epicurean boy who spoke—“Oder Schinken?” Wurst is the German sausage,
suggestive to the British mind of trichinæ, and other nameless horrors,
but dearer to the German heart than any other edible. Then they got
cigars—the prisoners—and von Senden and the Berliner Hülfsverein passed
on, the former to take up his head-quarters, the latter to distribute
their arrowroot, tea, woollen garments, winter beds, biscuits, and what
not of Liebesgaben to the wretched French sick.

On returning to the Hôtel de l’Europe, after a round of the hospitals,
I found in the _salle_ an officer from Retonfay who had ridden in to
tell me that the General in command of the brigade had sent to desire
me to call upon him. It was very well calling on generals if one went
of his own accord, but a summons to attend was calculated to make
one nervous. Had I been writing of the positions without sufficient
discretion, I anxiously asked of myself, or had any order been issued
to forbid correspondents from frequenting the foreposts? Speculation
was useless, and I went straight out in the moonlight to the big
château inhabited by the General, in the outskirts of Retonfay. There
was no frown on the features of the gallant old Pole; my portion
was a warm grip of the hand, and a cordial salutation. The General,
instead of having a wigging for me, wished to ask a favour of the
journal I represented. The General’s children are half English, his
wife is an English lady, and his mother-in-law, who, to judge from
the enthusiastic manner in which the General spoke of her, is utterly
unlike mothers-in-law in general, had thoughtfully sent her fighting
son-in-law a box of concentrated soups all the way from a western
suburb of London. The gift had moved the General to tender thanks, if
he might, through the columns of an English journal, and to make of
the box of soups a peg on which to hang a compliment to the British
nation. The General pondered, as he ate his soup, on the form which
this communication should take, apologising to me for his silence from
the circumstance that he desired “to make a little wit.” When he had
got to the salad, he found the spirit move him, and he dictated as
follows:—“I thank you, dear grandmamma, for your delicious soups; the
vermicelli and the tail ox are delicious. The North German Ambassador
has made a mistake when he said that the English nation has only
manifested a warm-heartedness towards the French. As I eat my dinner,
I am a witness to the contrary.—Your loving son,—Von Zglinitzki,
Major-General.” The good General slightly distorted the relation
existing between him and the relative he desired to address; but I
supposed he had taken the word from his children.

On the 2nd of November the whole of the 10th Army Corps defiled through
Metz on its southward march towards Troyes. Its General, Voight Rhetz,
sat in the saddle in the Place Royale, with Kummer beside him, and a
background formed of the trucks devoted to hospital purposes. On the
other side of the broad thoroughfare stood groups of French officers
looking on critically as the men went past with whom they had so often
been engaged. To have seen the different regiments pass the saluting
point with everything about them as neat as if they had come out of
barracks, you would never have thought they could have been doing
forepost work and fighting for two months. Fresh, stout, clean, and
supple, the great stalwart fellows came past with the German quick
step in one long continuous stream for two hours, and long before all
had passed the Frenchmen had sunk into a dull silence that seemed to
betoken acquiescence in an inevitable fate. There was no cheering or
singing; the bands played as they passed the General, but the “Wacht
am Rhein” was kept for the country roads. Besides the 10th, the 3rd
Army Corps was on the march for Troyes. The 7th for the present was to
remain in Metz; the 2nd had started Paris-ward before the capitulation;
and the 1st, after seeing off the last of the prisoners, was destined
for the northern march toward Lille and Amiens, along with the 8th
Corps. General Kummer, leaving the commandership of the town for an
active command, was succeeded by General von Löwenfeld. General Kummer,
during his brief tenure of a very difficult and delicate office, had
earned a well-deserved popularity. His urbanity and consideration to
the French officers were appreciated warmly by them all, and not a few
of them referred to the subject in my hearing with deep feeling. I have
omitted to mention before, that when the report of the capitulation
became spread abroad throughout the army on the evening of the 28th
Oct., a great number of regimental officers had a sort of caucus
meeting, in which they discussed the matter. Universal indignation at
the conduct of Bazaine was expressed, and it having been estimated that
the officers present were able to rely on the fidelity of a following
of some 25,000 men, it was resolved that a desperate effort should be
made that same night to cut a way through the German lines. The meeting
separated, and one of its members came to the Hôtel de l’Europe, where,
over a bottle of champagne, he made his last will and testament, and
with a tear in his eye, committed it to his friend my informant, and
went forth with set teeth on his desperate emprise. Next morning he
came back to breakfast, blythe as a bird, and reclaimed his will. It
seemed there had been a miscalculation. Instead of the following of
25,000 men on which the officers had relied, they could not get a man
to follow them. The realisation of the misconception prevented the
attempt being made.

I never tired of passing my time in watching the Place Royale and
the Esplanade, on which were quartered the wounded. On the former
were the railway car hospitals, on the latter are fine roomy tents,
holding twelve men each comfortably. What a variety of mutilations
you noticed among the convalescents. Men who had lost a leg, others a
foot, others an arm; the gamut of mutilation was run up and down till
you almost expected to see a man walking about without a head. It was
a listless, weary promenade which these unfortunates executed. Some
few formed little groups for conversation, and others made belief to
play at a kind of outdoor billiards on a wooden frame on the ground,
but most patrolled languidly backwards and forwards, with a dejected
listlessness that was the more painful to witness because it is so
un-Frenchlike. I suppose they must have been tired of the promenade
at the top of the Esplanade over against the river, for there were
seldom more than a few there. And yet the view from it is one of which
I think it must take a long time to get weary. If I could think the
inhabitants of the Esplanade possessed of so much depth of feeling, I
would fain have imagined that they shun the promenade on the ramparts
that the sight of so much of beauty lost to France might not wound
them too poignantly. Behind, among the tents, are the statues, and the
still bright flower-beds. In front, at one’s feet, are the outworks,
and then the broad stream of the river, with the fertile meadows of
Simphorien beyond. On the right, across the stream, is the Isle de
Sauley, the island of the weeping willows, with its powder-magazines
all but hidden among the graceful willows and the tall poplars. On the
left is the citadel, with its green earthworks and glacis, and beyond
the beautiful suburb of Montigny, with the bend of the stream farther
on ending in the railway bridge, the horizon to the south, bounded by
the range of heights, of which the highest is Mont St. Blaise, with its
ruined château and the Observatory, from which I had looked down on the
plain and city. Over St. Simphorien is the other branch of the Moselle,
fringed by the nestling villages of Moulin, St. Ruffine, Ban St.
Martin, and Devant-les-Ponts, wearing a very peaceful aspect but for
the absence of smoke from the chimneys. Over them towers abruptly the
rugged Mont St. Quentin, with the vineyards on its shoulders, lovingly
hugging the white villages, then the bare interval of red scarp, and
then the summit, crowned by the great spreading fortress, its warm grey
tint brought out in the slanting sunbeams. Well may St. Quentin be
called “the eagle of Metz,” soaring over the valley, as it does, up in
the very sky. Alas! for France and Metz, the wings of the eagle were
shorn. On its ramparts there was an eagle still, it is true, but it was
the eagle on the helmet of the Prussian sentry, who as he paused in
his beat, and looked down on what a week before deserved the name its
inhabitants so fondly gave it, “La Pucelle,” may be trying how he can
adapt the German song, “Oh Strassburg, oh Strassburg, Du wunderschön
stadt,” to the new conquest. Verily, it is a wonderful city, a city for
the possession of which to stake everything and to fight to the last
man and the last ditch. Now that the German army had seen it—few who
have not studied him know what an instinctive eye the German soldier
has for the beauties of nature—he would not be a discreet man who
should have suggested that the prize should be let slip. Nothing so
much strengthened Bismarck’s hand in his demand for Lorraine, as the
opportunity the German army had for seeing and admiring Metz.

The very heavens seemed to smile on the capture of la Pucelle. For
days before Bazaine had sent in his capitulation it had seemed as if a
repetition of the deluge of Noah was in progress. All the low country
was inundated; where the waters had not yet reached, the ground was a
thin, spongy, quagmire of mud. On the day that the Germans marched in
and the French marched out, the heavens still wept copiously, as if in
lamentation over a spectacle so full of woe for France. But when the
German sentry had taken his post on the gates, and when the black and
white flag floated out over the huge brown works of Plappeville, out
came the sun in a joyous radiance. As if by magic, the waters abated.
On St. Simphorien, where but the day before the dove could have found
no rest for the sole of her foot, an army might now have manœuvred.
The mud dried up, and the roads become hard again—those wonderful
roads that stubbornly resisted deterioration by the grinding wheels
of the cannon and proviant columns, and which ought of themselves to
prevent the name of the Third Napoleon, their constructor, from being
held in execration by the French. Under the bracing influence of the
bright sun and the frosty wind, everybody and everything seemed to
regain cheerfulness. The French prisoners yet remaining in Retonfay
had somehow got the mud off their clothes, and with the mud had gone
the moodiness. The German troops had heel-balled the eagle on their
helmets, and polished after a fashion the long boots that for days
before had been caked with successive layers of mud. The townspeople,
in all their sullenness, felt and yielded to the influence of the
fine weather. The ladies of the place, of whom hitherto we had only
caught glimpses as a pretty face peered out from behind a window-blind,
came out into the streets, and tripped along, daintily booted and
delicately gloved, among the dashing German subalterns, who might
have been excused for glances of undisguised admiration, and among
the throng of common soldiers, who seemed unimpressible, but were as
courteously civil as if they had served an apprenticeship as masters of
the ceremonies. Metz seemed to have no objection to any amount of what
it no doubt considered the mammon of unrighteousness in the shape of
Prussian thalers. All the shops were open, and had got stocked with a
rapidity that might have been accounted for either by concealed stores
or by wonderful enterprise in getting supplies speedily from Saarbrück
or Nancy. The cafés swarmed with German officers, with French officers,
with French civilians. It was already two days since the departure of
the last train had been notified for the conveyance of French officers
to Germany, but there must still have been close on 1800 of them left
in Metz. I suppose the greater proportion of them must have been on the
sick or convalescent list, but I saw comparatively few who appeared
seriously ailing. The diverse propensities of the two armies in the
matter of drinking could be seen at a glance by a visit to a café.
The German officer, trim, neat, buttoned up to the throat, dressed as
regimentally as if on parade, and with a carriage that might suggest
his having swallowed a ramrod, entered, saluted the room all round with
a stiff-necked bow and a chink together of his spurred heels; then
stalked to a seat, and called for a “Glas Bier.” The French officer,
his frogged and furred undress pilot coat open at the throat, and
showing a slice of shirt-collar and of front—a dickey, for aught I
know—and with the invariably white or brightly-coloured cravat, thrown
loosely round his neck, lounged in with his hands in his pockets,
nodded to a friend, tumbled in a heap on to a settee—if he ever
swallowed a ramrod the gastric juice must have made pulp of it long
ago—and called for a _petit verre_ of cognac or absinthe. He took his
tipple neat, fiery as it is. As the German plunged his moustache into
the froth of his beer flagon, he gave one the idea that he was drinking
because he had thirst and wanted to quench it. It was impossible to
think the same of the Frenchman as he took a sip out of the little
glass, and rolled the spirit round his mouth and under his tongue with
as much gusto as if it had been a sin.

As I was strolling on the Esplanade, on the morning of the 4th, I met
a comfortable-looking old gentleman, in gaiters and woollen gloves.
A remark as to the scenery served as a mutual introduction, and he
told me he was an old officer of artillery who, during the greater
part of the siege, had held the position of Commandant of Fort Queleu,
and who, when capitulation seemed imminent, had returned to the civil
life, out of which the siege had temporarily called him, so as to
escape expatriation as a prisoner of war. The good old gentleman spoke
very frankly as to the events of the siege, and had no hesitation in
speaking his mind as to Marshal Bazaine, and the motives by which, from
first to last, he had been actuated. When the Army of the Rhine first
fell back around the fortifications of Metz, after the battle of the
18th of August, instead of making efforts which he thought undoubtedly
would have been successful, to push onward for a junction with the rest
of the French forces, Bazaine’s explanation to his principal officers
for his policy was to the effect that the forts of Metz were in a
defensive condition so backward that he dared not, consistently with
his duty to France and his impression as to the importance of Metz as
the premier fortress of the Empire, abandon it in its then inchoate
condition to a defence to be carried on solely by its garrison. In
this view the commander of Fort Queleu declared that he completely
coincided. Comparatively trivial efforts would, he believed, have
sufficed to reduce Metz immediately after the 18th of August, had
there been no army to support the garrison, and to strengthen the
fortifications, and had the Germans taken immediate advantage of the
state of weakness. Bazaine, he continued, with a zeal and industry
beyond all praise, had put the forts into a state of efficient defence
by the end of the month. It was then, according to my informant’s
views, that his presence for the first time became _de trop_ in Metz,
and then it was that he ought, with all the vigour he could exert, to
have burst through the German cordon and got at large. That he could
have done this, whether in prosecution of the temporarily energetic
sortie which lasted over two days—31st August and 1st September—in the
direction of Nosseville, Servigny, and St. Barbe, or in the better
line down the valley of the Moselle, my interlocutor had no doubt;
and it is fair to him to mention that I found many German officers
who could not conscientiously differ from this view. After the middle
of September, however, Bazaine’s position, in view of breaking-out
tactics, had become hopeless. Sickness was decimating his ranks; his
troops had already eaten deep into the means for transporting artillery
and train. I asked the old commandant how, in his opinion, it was
that Bazaine did not strike a decisive blow for extrication after his
presence in Metz for good had ceased. The reply, to my thinking, was
very pregnant. Bazaine, a capable general, a gallant man, and a sincere
patriot, hung balanced between two stools. Instead of looking with a
single eye to his duties as a military commander, he became distracted
with political complications. In the early days he was studying the
interests of the Emperor, and the means of conserving for Imperialistic
uses the army under his command. Later on, the poison of personal
ambition had impregnated the system of the hitherto straightforward
soldier. He had nourished dreams of seeing himself Regent of France,
and made ducks and drakes of his reputation without attaining the
personal _quid pro quo_ he hankered after.

What a strange kaleidoscopic scene in those early post-capitulation
times did the great room of the Hôtel de l’Europe present from day
to day! Yesterday it had been thronged by officers of the 7th Army
Corps; to-day it was the 8th; on the morrow it would be the turn of
the first to snatch a glass of the “sparkling” before the route is
taken for the champagneless villages on the line of march. As I sit in
my quiet corner, there comes clinking and clattering in a divisional
staff; hussars blue, hussars green, hussars red, hussars brown, and
hussars black—the last with the grinning death’s head and the crossed
thigh-bones in silver on the busbies. All are speaking at once, most
at the top of their voices, and the clamour is for champagne, of which
400 bottles were drunk in one day in the room in which I sit. If I
had the portrait edition of the _Almanach de Gotha_, I might identify
the bearers of some of the proudest names in the Fatherland; for the
wearers of the showy uniforms are many of them princes and poins. Of
Grafs and Freiherrs, the crop is too large to take any account of.
This young slip of a lad with the pale face and the slight moustache
is of the blood royal of Russia, and not many removes off the throne
of the Czar. He is studying the art of war, attached to the staff of
General Manteuffel. If grim Nicholas—he of the Roman nose and strong
jaw—were alive to-day, with what interest would he be watching this
contest, and how searching would be his questioning of his young
kinsman on his return to the banks of the Neva! Who is this straight,
square man, stiff of figure and massive of head, that comes quietly
into the room alone? All rise respectfully as the door frames, as in
a picture, that powerful figure. The sparse hair on the face is but
slightly grizzled; surely the man with the few medals on his broad
breast, and the simple General’s star at his button-hole cannot be
a personage of great distinction. You may search in vain the roll
of the German army to find a name more honoured or more deserving
of honour. The big quiet man is General von Göben, the commander of
the 8th Army Corps, the youngest commander of an army corps in the
Prussian service, the one who has seen the most fighting. So gluttonous
is he for fighting, that when there was peace within the borders of
the Fatherland this modern Bayard was fighting in the fierce internal
strifes of Spain. In this war he has borne the brunt of the hardest
fighting. At Spicheren he had relieved Kamecke of the command; on the
rugged slope of Gorze, and in the thickets of the Bois des Genivaux,
he saw full many of his Rhinelanders go down. Von Göben seats himself
beside a burly officer with a bushy beard, clad in the white and green
of the Cuirassiers. The pallor of the big officer’s face contrasts
markedly with his jetty beard. Well may he be pale. A furrow of that
field at the north of Vionville soaked in not a little of his blood
as he lay there on the night of the 16th of August, a chassepot ball
in his chest and a sword-cut across that hard, bullet head of his. It
was he who led the Cuirassiers, diminished from a strong regiment to
a weak squadron in that last death-charge against the French battery.
He took the cigar out of his mouth to shout the “Vorwärts,” and then
sat down in his saddle and spurred the big bay into a charging gallop.
It was his luck and his hard head, he tells you, that pulled him
through when so many of his brother officers fell to rise no more.
And now he is a Rittmeister, no less, and has a good chance of the
order _pour merite_. At a side-table there sit together an Oberst of
reserve Uhlans, and a quiet little officer in a general’s uniform. The
chubby Oberst ought to have an army corps at least, if, indeed, he
should not supersede von Moltke, to judge of the energetic and fluent
manner in which he is demonstrating how a battle should be won to the
quiet little taciturn man beside him, who has a queer half comical
look in those grey eyes of his. The Oberst is, to use a vulgar phrase,
teaching his grandmother to suck eggs. The little General commands a
division, and has before to-day directed the operations of an army.
He is the author of a standard work on military strategy; whereas the
Oberst for the last dozen years has been tilling his acres away in
Pomerania. Nearer to me is a group of subalterns of a Hohenzollern
regiment, the youngsters drinking Rhine wine when all others in the
room are votaries of champagne. The Hohenzollerns affect “a deep deep
draught of the good Rhine wine,” for they are a Rhineland Regiment, and
it has always been a regimental tradition to stick to the Rudesheim.
In the group is a blithe young face I know right well. Many a hearty
clink of glasses has passed between us, not a few mornings have we
patrolled together the foreposts in the valley on the French side of
Saarbrück. Under the silky moustache you may faintly trace two slight
weals. How they came there is a story. The lad was among the foremost
up the steep of the Spicheren-berg. So difficult was the climb that he
had to take to his hands and knees, and, as you may see a butcher do,
he stuck his sword between his teeth. Like a silly fellow, he turned
the edge inwards instead of outwards, and when, after his manner, he
took to roaring “Vorwärts,” the keen blade enlarged the naturally by no
means diminutive orifice of his mouth. At another table sits Kummer,
the chief of the Landwehr men, with a certain Landwehr, fatherly look
about himself. In the corner opposite me is the sole group out of
uniform. Two are the almoners of the Berliner Hülfsverein, the third is
Professor Kollmann, of Munich, who has thrown aside the toga and the
doctor’s cap to take the charge of a lazarette. The fourth is, what
think you?—a lady. In the military history of Europe the name of von
Alten is not unknown to fame. It can be unfamiliar to no student of
Waterloo literature. The male von Altens of to-day are all soldiers,
and the lady at the table with the Professor is their sister, and a
sick nurse in an hospital at Chalons. In the salons of Berlin there was
no fairer face than that of Agnes von Alten; now the salons know it no
more; but when the soldier awakes from out of his delirium, or when
the chloroform-influence leaves him after an operation, he finds her
bending over him in tender solicitude. There are those, I fancy, in the
gay staff coats at the centre table, who would gladly hail a wound if
they could only choose their nurse.

The forenoon of the 5th I devoted to a careful examination of the
French line of defence which opposed the German environment on the
west. A description of its characteristics may, I fear, be but dull
reading, but I do not feel myself entitled on that account to refrain
from writing it, looking at the fact that the salient points of this
line of defence probably disappeared within a few weeks after, and
that some details with regard to it may not be useless in a humble
way to the Alison or the Kinglake of this stupendous war. Crossing
the river and the Thionville Railroad, on which a train of repairing
materials was moving forward with a tentative slowness that reminded
me of a Highland pony’s movements in a bog, I traversed a flat expanse
of considerable size, which had been occupied as the French Guards’
camp, and then passed through Ban St. Martin, noticing on the way
the château in which Bazaine had had his head-quarters. The abrupt
rise of St. Quentin began two steps after I had cleared the lanes of
Ban St. Martin. Looking at this hill from where you will, you cannot
question its steepness; but how steep it is on the face looking towards
the town is only to be appreciated by whoever essays to climb it on
a frosty morning, when the air is rarefied, and when the ground has
yielded its hardness caused by the frost into superficial slipperiness
caused by the sun’s heat. There was no road but a faint footpath
through the vineyards, which soon broke into what may be called a spray
of still tinier tracks, and then utterly disappeared. As I toiled
slowly and laboriously up the slippery steep, it became apparent to
me that a position such as that of Fort St. Quentin, so advantageous
because of its isolation and the great area which its height allows
it to dominate, may yet have elements of weakness caused by its very
isolation and height. Looking upward as I clambered, I could see no
grim earthworks on the crest, no cannon mouths depressed so as to sweep
this ascent. The hill shoulders up very steeply for three-fourths its
height, and then “tumbles home” so rapidly to the summit on which
stands the fort, that its base, as well as the lower steeps, are
protected from the fire of the fort by the convexity of the more upland
configuration. This fire could not be brought to bear upon these spaces
directly, no matter by what expedient, and if once, therefore, an
enemy’s force could get across the outside area swept by the guns of
the fort, it might nestle snugly, if audaciously, right under the lee
of the fortress, keeping itself safe much in the same manner as we read
of a nigger sticking on the back of a crocodile while the monster snaps
his jaws and lashes his tail to no purpose. All that St. Quentin could
do in the way of dislodgment must be by an indirect and plunging fire,
and the bay of safety is so close, that its artillery essaying any such
fire would have to fire at an elevation closely approaching to the
perpendicularly upwards. But a glance backward as one climbs forcibly
impresses on one the care and skill with which the several parts of
the defence of Metz are made to dovetail one into the other. Where the
configuration of the ground draws the teeth of St. Quentin, the bare
tusks of the citadel of Metz show themselves dangerously. Its guns look
across the Simphorien on to the alluvial plateau of Ban St. Martin and
the lower steeps of Mont St. Quentin, and change the “bay of safety”
into a position utterly untenable, while the batteries of Montigny
and Longeville, one face pointing up the river, the other athwart it,
opposed at once a direct and enfilading fire to any body of troops
rash enough to essay the path leading to this same untenable bay of
safety. Nor was this all that has been done to guard this access, and
to negative the feasibility of this place of lodgment. Out of the round
shoulder of Mont St. Quentin, just where the “tumbling home” begins,
there rises an abrupt, isolated peak, a kind of junior Mont St. Quentin
in itself. On the summit of this Bazaine’s engineers had knocked up a
hasty, but most useful outwork. A strong loopholed palisading enclosed
bomb-proof subterranean quarters for about half a battalion, and there
were evidences that on the top of this bomb-proof a couple of pieces
of artillery had been mounted. From the slits in the palisading the
infantry men standing on the tread could have searched with the muzzles
of their chassepots the “bay of safety,” while the shells from the
depressed pieces of artillery could have torn down through the steeps
of the vineyards, or pelted into the pretty châteaux and snug cottages
of Ban St. Martin in the level bottom.

Traversing the interval between this useful outwork, and the almost
upright substructure of granite blocks which forms the eastern
foundation of Fort St. Quentin, I came upon a deep cutting, crossed
by a foot-bridge. At the bottom of the cutting was a railway worked by
wire ropes. At the summit of the incline, a little to my right, was the
house of the stationary engine that had puffed so assiduously during
the siege, but that was now silent and deserted. On the left the track
seemed to tumble over a precipice into the valley of the Moselle, near
to Longeville—the declivity must in truth be very steep. This line,
used for the transport of building materials and munitions of war for
Fort St. Quentin, and a continuation of which had performed the same
function for Fort Plappeville, was constructed, it may be interesting
for railway readers to know, on the same gauge as the well-known
Festiniog line. It must have been of extraordinary importance during
the building and siege operations. Crossing the flying bridge of the
railway, I climbed up on to the plateau on which stands the fort,
through a perfect litter of chassepot guns and chassepot ammunitions,
which had been strewed there by the French troops to whom the order had
been given to lay down their arms within the fortification. I skirted
its left, making for the brow of the mount, looking out over against
the plateau of St. Hubert and Châtel St. Germain, anxious, as on the
morning of the 19th of August I had seen the French army lying on this
brow, to see how from it the Prussian position must have appeared.

Fort St. Quentin by no means occupies the whole of the summit of the
hill of the same name. It stands on an almost imperceptibly raised
space on the extreme verge of the eastern extremity. To the westward
extends a rugged flat of about a mile in length, by half as much in
breadth. The appearance the hill presents was as if some Titan of old
had shaved off flat its pointed top, and then strewed boulders over
the surface. These boulders, as well as the space between them, are
now covered with a short, half-heathy sward. It was then on this flat
that the French had sought refuge when at daylight on the 19th of
August we looked out from the miserable bivouac on the blood-stained
field of Gravelotte, and saw their white tents between us and the dull
heavy mass of Fort St. Quentin, as if for safety they were throwing
their bare arms around the protecting fortress. On the flat were yet
visible the traces of the trenches they had dug around the tents, and
their _al fresco_ kitchen fires had left their marks on the sward. I
was not a little surprised on pressing forward to the edge of this
flat to find its margin bordered by a substantial and systematically
constructed entrenchment. This line of entrenchment sprang up the
steep side of the hill between the top and the village of Scy, which
is just outside its cordon. If constructed at all during the siege, it
must have been made in the early days. The face was banked up with an
earthen slope now grass grown; inside was the inner face of an upright
wall of stones uncemented by mortar. This entrenchment was regularly
constructed with salient and re-entering angles, and with bastion-like
projections at intervals, for sweeping the face laterally. Close behind
were, at intervals, bomb-proof half-underground constructions for the
protection of troops not actually lining the entrenchment, and that
it was not meant as a place of occasional resort, but was habitually
held by defenders, was evidenced by the durable construction of the
kitchens, as well as by other less savoury tokens of the continuous
presence of troops. In the rear of the bomb-proofs, at frequent
intervals, were the earthworks thrown up for batteries. Encircling, as
this entrenchment did, the extreme edge of the plateau, its defenders
could look right down into the valley below, as well as across at the
lower and less abrupt heights opposite which constitute the fringe of
the plateau of St. Hubert; and with proper appliances and moderate
determination, could, to all appearance, have prevented anything from
living in, much less successfully traversing, that valley, to make an
assault. This defence work of the summit of Mont St. Quentin, which
may be called the key of Metz, presented an appearance so formidable
as to seem almost to reduce into a superfluity the great fort in its
rear; although of course the utility of the latter became obvious for
all round firing at long ranges in co-operation with the other forts
for the protection of Metz. This line ran away, still as strong as
ever, down into the dip between the summit of Mont St. Quentin and the
longer hog-backed ridge of Plappeville. As it came on upon the latter,
instead of climbing directly up the face and so passing close under the
greater fortification, it took a sweep westward, skirting the eminence
near its base, and was continued right round to the northern front of
Plappeville, putting an effectual check upon any sudden surprise from
the Bois de Châtel or the Bois de Saulny.

How deceptive are appearances! From the shattered hamlet of St.
Hubert, on the grand chaussée, I had looked over to St. Quentin on
the morning of the 19th August, and had thought I could have knocked
at the gates of the fort after a short half-hour’s walk. Now, looking
from St. Quentin to St. Hubert, I could barely see the ruined hamlet,
and the poplar trees on the chaussée stood up against the horizon.
Yet the distance could not have been so great after all, since a shot
from Fort St. Quentin on the morning I refer to had killed two German
soldiers mounted at the hamlet on the chaussée. Now on the horizon
were visible the long low huts which the Germans had erected as their
temporary barracks. Much closer on the left, and near the chaussée,
where it passes through Roserieulles on its way into the Moselle
valley, I noticed the trim square earthwork, so completely constructed
as to have been sodded up with turf, of a German battery. In this
battery had stood the 12-pounder guns which had burned the village
of Moulin les Metz in the early days of October. What the gunners in
St. Quentin were thinking of to allow the erection of this earthwork
in such a position I could not comprehend. To pound it to pieces they
need scarcely have taken the trouble to elevate the muzzles of the
great guns on the top of the casemates. A steady cannonade must have
made its construction too arduous for the Germans, not given, save in
rare instances, to squander lives wantonly. Nay, more, at a quarter
of a mile closer still, at La Maison Blanche, just behind Longeau,
there stood an impudent little affair of an improvised battery, thrown
up for a few field pieces. I wondered if the gunners there, whose
lives were not worth five minutes’ purchase had St. Quentin been in
earnest to dislodge the cocky little concern, ever sent a six-pound
shell flying across the valley at the big guns of the fort, in mere
bravado. Quitting the entrenchment, I plunged down the slope, about
midway between the villages of Lessy and Scy. Now I was among what
had been the French outposts, guarding the country roads and the hill
paths. The inartistic character of the field defences, on the French
side, surprised me not a little. There was a trench across the path
with the earth thrown up on the farther side, and that was all. There
was no cover for retreat, there were no means of pouring in a cross
fire, there was no continuation of the cordon of entrenchment, so
as to give cover for a thin line of riflemen connecting the posts.
It was the same with the position for a field watch in front of
Plappeville, between the entrenchments and the Bois de Châtel. The
half-moon ditch and paltry earthen breastwork stood there utterly
isolated and disconnected. Under a hot fire from the adjacent wood,
no relief could have reached it, nor could its occupants have fallen
back on their supports in the inner entrenchments, without ruinous
loss. It was another story when, having crossed the bottom, I came upon
the German foreposts on the opposite slope. On one side of the narrow
path there looked down on me a venomous little work, behind which some
half-a-dozen marksmen might have stood. Half-a-dozen paces forward, on
the other side, there was a duplicate. It made one uncomfortable, even
in the solitude, to move about in the continual consciousness that,
look which way one might, there was one of these posts looking one all
but full in the face, while at least a couple of others were squinting
at one on the flanks. Then right in front came an obstruction of
felled trees, then the _chevaux de frise_ of vineyard stakes stuck in
the ground, and last of all the deep trench across the road, with its
substantial parapet running away on either side in an unbroken cordon.
It was not one road that was so protected on the German side, but all.
A comparison of the field works of the rival armies impressed me, in
short, with the conviction that the French works were constructed by
soldiers, the German by engineers; and this surprised me the more,
because the engineering ability displayed on the part of the French in
the choice of sites for the forts, and in their construction, was of a
very high order, an opinion, I am sure, which will be confirmed by our
highest military critics.

Hark! I hear the quiet tramp of the German infantrymen marching down
the narrow street. It is the 2nd Division of the 1st Army Corps on
its way through Metz. Ha! here comes the 45th, fresh, clean, and
confident. Can you see any gaps in those dense files of fours, although
it was the 45th that furnished the 285 _tapfere Krieger_ that lie in
one long grave in the quiet garden by Château Gras? No, the depôt
of the 45th has given over 1400 recruits to the Regiment which it
feeds, and if more are wanted (and more were wanted), I doubt not
the depôt is equal to the demand. And here comes the sister regiment
of the brigade, the gallant 5th, the blood of whose linesmen ran in
the gutters of Servigny in that “Schlacht bei Noisseville” on the
afternoon of the 31st August. Here is the dashing Hauptmann, who one
night, having sitten a little in the sun before it went down, took it
into his head to pay a visit to the fair maidens of Villiers l’Orme.
The Hauptmann lost his way, and only found it when he was under the
nose of a French field watch. He lost, too, his horse, and had a
night’s rest in the open in the vine-berg; but he does not look much
the worse for it, does he? Crash! bang! Surely I know that tune! It is
the old “Hohenfriedburg.” Faugh-a-ballagh there for the fighting 4th.
If the obstruction consisted of men with arms in their hands, instead
of a posse of _gamins_, the 4th would do their own work in the way of
Faugh-a-ballagh. At the head of the brigade rides General Zglinitski,
full, let us hope, of “vermicelli and tail ox.” He looks quite fresh
and rosy. I wish his “grandmamma” could see him as he jogs through the
Port de France. Ha! here comes the good Oberst, with the cares of that
horrible lager of French prisoners off his mind; and now he is on the
road again with a reasonable prospect of a fight. Behind him comes the
1st Battalion, marching splendidly; the houses echo again to their firm
tread; and the 2nd Battalion follows, with the portly Mecklenburgher
Major at its head, and young von Verzen, his adjutant, by his side.
Here comes Puttkamer, “the good old man,” as we used to call him,
who speaks English with a Scotch accent, because he learnt it from
a certain Jessie of northern extraction. What do I hear? Can it be?
Yes; it is the familiar, although still far-off, salutation of “Miss
Lucy, Lucy Long.” Verily there is von Hosius in the flesh, and a good
deal of that same, too. At his heels his Scotch terrier “Phipps,” and
by his side his trusty Fïscher, adept at pantomime and lover of salt
herrings. “_Kommen Sie mit?_” is the hearty question that greets me on
every side. _Ach nein_, worse luck, old friends. To the 2nd succeeds
the Fusilier Battalion, with the stalwart senior Hauptmann at its head,
its major is detached on another command. Company after company passes
me, the honest Kerle in the ranks grinning a kindly greeting to one
who had shared with them their roughs and smooths, chiefly the former,
goodness knows, for not a few weeks. Near the tail of all comes the
11th Company, my own, with stately Ertmann marching in its front. There
is a little darn on the right breast of his coat; you would scarcely
guess, to see his sturdy step and upright carriage, that it marks the
spot where entered the bullet that went through him on the 14th of
August. _Mein leiber Werth_, best of chums! truest of comrades! Shall
we ever meet again, I wonder? And as I live, here is Feldwebel Strauss
mended again after that unlucky bullet at the Brewery, and with the
company account-book sticking out of his breast as of old. They turn
the corner over the bridge, and my Metz friends are out of sight. Good
luck and plenty of fighting be their lot wheresoever the fortune of war
may carry them, and a glad home-coming when the _Friede_ comes at last!


END OF VOL. I.


BRADBURY, EVANS, AND CO., PRINTERS, WHITEFRIARS.

FOOTNOTES:

[1] Gustave von Alvensleben who commanded the 4th Corps is a full
general.

[2] I observe it has been asserted that the French in those earlier
battles did not resort to rifle-pits. Anybody asserting this who was
in sight of the operations must have been stone-blind. I have been in
dozens of them. The story of 800 and odd corpses having been found in
one after the battle of the 14th of August must have been a “goak”
perpetrated by some youngster on the staff eager to feed Germany
with sensationalism. There were two varieties of what the Germans
call schützen-graben. Of one the churchyard at Point du Jour is a
good example—a triangle surrounded with a low wall, which had been
loopholed, and with a trench inside for additional cover. The other
pattern consisted of a small half-moon excavation—the convexity toward
the enemy—the earth from the hole breasted up in front, over which the
marksmen fired in a kneeling position. The maximum garrison of one of
these rifle-pits must have been about twelve men, half of them firing
while the other half rested behind the shelter. To have packed throngs
of men into large rifle-pits would simply have been to provide so many
charnel-houses. Doubtless numbers of the little half-moons are still to
the fore on the battle-fields. It is easy to tell the French works from
the German by the objective of their convexity. The Germans, however,
converted many of them.

[3] I give no heed to the balderdash written about Bazaine’s
abandonment to sensual indulgence, of which I have been unable to find
a scintilla of unprejudiced and impartial confirmation.

 NEW COPYRIGHT EDITIONS OF SHAKESPEARE’S PLAYS
 AND POEMS.

 _Shakespeare’s Plays and Poems._

 EDITED BY

 CHARLES AND MARY COWDEN CLARKE

 In Four Volumes, Demy Octavo, price £1 11_s._ 6_d._

 ALSO,

 _The Same Text_.

 In One Volume, Royal Octavo, price 12_s._

 LONDON: BICKERS & SON, 1 LEICESTER SQUARE.


The PUBLISHERS have much pleasure in announcing the publication of the
above Editions of Shakespeare’s Plays and Poems. Mrs Cowden Clarke’s
Edition of Shakespeare, recently published by Messrs Appleton & Co.
of New York, has been made the basis of Mr and Mrs Cowden Clarke’s
joint labours. The original Edition, produced after long and careful
preparation, has met with great and deserved success in America. As
the present issue, in addition to the careful revision of Mrs Cowden
Clarke, has had the benefit of the labours of Mr Charles Cowden Clarke,
one of the most zealous and successful illustrators of the great
Dramatist now living, they believe they are presenting Editions of
Shakespeare’s Works distinguished by an amount of mature judgment in
collating the earlier copies which will vindicate their claim to the
rank of STANDARD EDITIONS.

In projecting Editions of Shakespeare without note or comment, the
PUBLISHERS believe that they will be acceptable to the general public
who desire to become acquainted with the writings of the author,
free from the distraction which a multiplicity of notes is so apt to
produce, and to those also who prefer being their own interpreters of
the meaning of the passages which appear obscure.

As the value of these Editions will lie in the discrimination exercised
in the selection of the text by the Editors, it will be conceded that
the life-long devotion of Mr and Mrs Cowden Clarke to the study of
Shakespeare, their thorough knowledge of the various readings, and
their ability to adopt in all cases the reading which appears to be
in keeping with the style and general treatment of his subject by the
author, form a guarantee that the Editions will present, as nearly as
possible, a pure text.

Both Editions will include reprints from the American Edition, of Mrs
Cowden Clarke’s valuable Introductory Essay, Glossary, &c., carefully
revised and amplified. The Four-volume Edition will be printed from a
new fount of Longprimer Ancient type, on fine toned paper, and will
form four compact and handsome volumes. The One-volume edition will
be printed from a new fount of Brevier Ancient type, on toned paper,
and will be the most compact and readable edition of Shakespeare
ever issued in a single volume. The PUBLISHERS are confident that
no Copyright Editions of Shakespeare, of corresponding value and
importance, have ever been offered to the public at such moderate
prices.

TRANSCRIBER’S NOTE

Except for those changes noted below, all misspellings in the text, and
inconsistent or archaic usage, have been retained.

Inconsistent hyphenations have been left as is.

  Page 17. “petty” replaced by “pretty”.
  Page 93. “eft” replaced by “left”.
  Page 315. The scanned image was of poor quality for the phrase “About a mile and a half due south of”.
  Page 433. The scanned image was of poor quality for the phrase most likely to be “top of the crust”.
  Page 463. ““Vorwärts,’” replaced by ““Vorwärts,”” Divine
  Emblems advertisement. “3s. 6a” replaced by “3s. 6d”.
